<meta>This page transitions from the printer's colophon and library stamps to the editor's preface, which describes the source manuscripts for the treatises contained in this volume.</meta>

->## PREFACE.<-

THE two treatises now published for the first time, along with a third one entitled *De Sacramentis Ecclesiæ*, are contained in a manuscript volume in the Library of St. Paul's School. This volume has already been described in an edition of the work just mentioned; so that, besides stating that it is a clean copy in a single consistent handwriting, there is no need to give any further account of it here.

The original of the first treatise, on *the Celestial Hierarchy*, is found in the manuscript marked Gg. iv. 26 in the Cambridge University Library; and this has provided the means to correct the text in some places, as well as to supply one significant omission.

The second treatise, on the *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*, cannot be found anywhere else, as far as I am aware. <margin>PR 2235 CHH 1869</margin> Although, therefore, this is the one on which the author evidently bestowed the most care, the fact that it exists in only one copy (and that made, as I would infer, by a scribe who was not very intelligent) is a disadvantage that may be offered as an excuse for occasional errors.

One slight change in the spelling I have made

### PREFACE.

I wish now to offer my sincere thanks to the Court of the Worshipful Company of Mercers <note>The Mercers' Company is the premier Livery Company of the City of London and has served as the trustee of St. Paul's School since its founding.</note> for their permission to publish the contents of the school manuscript. I also thank the Senate of the University of Cambridge for allowing me to borrow the valuable manuscript mentioned earlier, and the Librarian, Henry Bradshaw, Esq., for his great courtesy in providing me with ready access to it.

To Mr. Seebohm, the author of *The Oxford Reformers of 1498*, my obligations are more than can be expressed in a preface. I am indebted to him even less for the information I have received—great though that has been—than for the high standard of work which he has set before me. That the life and writings of Dean Colet <note>John Colet (c. 1467–1519) was a pioneering English humanist, priest, and the founder of St. Paul's School.</note> should hold such an attraction today for someone with no local interest in his memory is, I think, a happy omen. It suggests that Colet may finally obtain the recognition he deserves, which his faithful adherence to his own maxim—"If you wish to be like God, remain hidden as God is" <note>original: *Si vis divinus esse, late ut Deus*</note>—has until now prevented him from receiving.

->St. Paul's School,<-
->March 17th, 1869.<-

<meta>This introduction follows the Table of Contents and begins the editor's preface to the work, establishing the historical context of Dean Colet’s reputation.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

->§ 1.<-

IN some respects, time has not yet done justice to the memory of Dean Colet. With the present year, three and a half centuries will be completed since he passed to his rest; and though the recollection of him has never faded away during that long interval, it has been but a faint and shadowy recollection. Men have seen him, not by his own light, but as reflected by Erasmus. As the founder of St. Paul's School, indeed—wise in his enactments no less than liberal in his endowment—he has always been held in honor by some in each generation; people who were, themselves, neither few nor undistinguished. But while various contemporaries of his have enjoyed a European reputation, his fame has been considered only local. Those who knew enough of his history to describe him as the friend of Erasmus undoubtedly meant by that term someone whom Erasmus honored with his friendship. They did not dream that his words were not yet ended; that written, if not spoken, they would still be listened to; and that chiefly by those words, as the direct expression of his mind, his place must finally be awarded to him among the thinkers of his time.

This comparative obscurity has been the result of several causes, chief among which I would mention the peculiar circumstances attending his biography, and the seclusion of his writings.

->§ 2.—*On the Biographies of Dean Colet.*<-

THAT Colet was fortunate in having Erasmus to give the world an account of him cannot be doubted. If his portrait is but a miniature, it is from the hand of a <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This introduction addresses the historical obscurity of Dean Colet, arguing that his reputation has been unfairly overshadowed by his association with Erasmus. The author suggests that Colet's own writings, rather than just his role as the founder of St. Paul's School, should define his place among great thinkers.</summary>
<keywords>Dean Colet, Erasmus, St. Paul's School, biography, intellectual history, 16th century</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION.

in one half of the subject has diminished the clearness and effect of the whole.

After the lapse of nearly a hundred and fifty years, in 1661 preparations were being made by Thomas Smith of Christ's College, Cambridge—a former student of St. Paul's School—to publish some of Colet’s *Treatises*;¹ but the project was never accomplished. The destruction of the School and its library in the Great Fire of 1666 probably put a final stop to it, if it had not been abandoned before.

At the end of another half-century, Mr. John Postlethwayte, the High Master of St. Paul's School, intended to have published a Life of Colet; but his death in 1713 prevented that project as well.²

Thus two hundred years had passed away, carrying with them into oblivion many memories and traditions—if not actual records of Colet's own writing—which might have enriched the account of such a man. And meanwhile, little or nothing had been added to the sketch left by Erasmus. It had been translated and illustrated with notes by Smith, and repeated, in more or less modified form, by Holland and Fuller; his *Convocation Sermon* had been reprinted in the *Phoenix*; and some few additional details of his life and writings might be found in Anthony à Wood and Pitseus, in Polydore Vergil, Leland, and George Lily. But still, Erasmus could be said to remain the only biographer of Dean Colet.

In the next few years, however, much was done in this direction. Dr. White Kennett, afterwards Bishop of Peterborough—the records of whose boundless industry, as they stand among the volumes of the Lansdowne manuscripts, I can never remember without veneration—had labored hard in the mine of Erasmus's letters; and from there, and

## INTRODUCTION.

in his surviving compositions a certain inaccuracy of language,¹ which he seems to have felt himself, and which, Erasmus thought, had restrained him from committing his reflections to writing. “And I wish,” adds Erasmus, “that this had not been the case; for I should be glad of the thoughts of John Colet, in whatever language expressed.”²

By his Will, dated August 22nd, 1519, Colet thus disposed of such manuscripts as he had preserved: “Also, the New Testament, and others of my own making, written on parchment, such as commentaries on Paul's Epistles and summaries, along with many others like them, I desire shall be disposed of at the discretion of my executors, which disposal I leave to their judgment.”³

It does not appear that anything was published by them; and some of the manuscripts, through purchase or otherwise, eventually came into the possession of Matthew Parker—a youth of fifteen at the time of Colet’s death, and later Archbishop of Canterbury. He bequeathed to his own college, Corpus Christi, the volume (No. 355) containing Colet’s Exposition of the *Epistle to the Romans*, and a portion of his Letters to Radulphus on the Mosaic account of the Creation.⁴ In the Emmanuel College Library is another volume, the gift of Dr. Anthony Tuckney, containing a similar Exposition of the *First Epistle to the Corinthians*.⁵ The originals of both these, which are in the handwriting of

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to John Colet’s life and works, transitioning from the discussion of his surviving manuscripts to the intellectual influences—specifically mysticism—that shaped his thought.</meta>

<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

<margin>P's ed. 4.</margin>
<margin>Payne</margin>
Payne is probably right in saying that mysticism chiefly springs up and flourishes when the established forms of religion have begun to lose their hold on men; when the instinctive longing of the soul for the immortal and divine remains, but can find nothing to satisfy it in the external rites of a failing church. "From the hard and arid system of Peter the Lombard," says Milman,¹ "the profound devotion of the Middle Ages took refuge in mysticism." At such a transitional period as the end of the fifteenth century—even without taking into account reasons to be mentioned shortly—it was natural enough for any educated Englishman to be attracted to the writings of Dionysius. And though Erasmus does not speak of Colet having begun the study of that author until he had gone abroad, about the end of 1493, yet in his mention of Plato and Plotinus being carefully read by Colet while at Oxford, he shows that he had made a good preparation for such a study.

But I think that, in the present case, there are faint yet perceptible traces of a personal history, by following which we can connect Dean Colet almost immediately with the Dionysian writings. Without attempting to surmise what turn may have been given to his thoughts by discussions with Grocyn and Linacre—both of whom had returned from Italy before he started—it seems to me highly probable that he owed to Mirandola and Ficino much of his early preference for those mystic writings, if not his first acquaintance with them; and that connecting links with Mirandola and Ficino may be found in Robert Gaguin, former ambassador to England for Charles VIII, and Germain de Ganay.

It was the sight of a letter from Erasmus to Robert Gaguin, the French historian, that first introduced Erasmus to Colet's notice.² The practice of handing letters about as evidence of scholarship seems to have been common at that time; and Gaguin had in this manner shown to Colet one received by him from Erasmus. The fact would seem

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the intellectual links between John Colet, Robert Gaguin, and Marsilio Ficino, exploring how these connections influenced Colet's exposure to Platonic and Dionysian thought during his time in Paris.</meta>

## INTRODUCTION.

<margin>Gaguin</margin> Gaguin to suggest a certain amount of intimacy between Gaguin and Colet. Now Gaguin, a man of letters and the enviable owner of a well-stocked library,<note>Thus Erasmus writes to ask for the loan of Macrobius (Epistle iv. 26), of Trapezuntius and Quintilian (v. 16).</note> was in correspondence with Ficino. In his collected *Letters* there is one addressed to the Florentine scholar, dated September 1, 1496,<note>Robert Gaguin's *Letters* (1498) folio xlv.—"Because of all these things, many of our scholars are eager to know you by sight and to gaze upon the man from whom such noble monuments of learning are produced," etc.</note> in which he compliments Ficino on the extent to which his writings <margin>of Colet Plotinus Sep 12</margin> were then read in Paris. His translation of Plotinus, he says, and other treatises of his, were so popular and so highly esteemed, that many a French scholar was eager “to know by sight and to gaze upon a man, from whom issued forth such noble works of learning.” One such person he mentions by name, who had resolved on a journey to Italy for that purpose, with an ardor like that which carried Plato into Egypt, or brought the Spaniard from Cadiz to Rome for the sake of seeing Livy. This being the case, there seems nothing improbable in supposing that Colet, <margin>2) to</margin> who had previously read Plotinus at Oxford, should be interested in the writings of Ficino, through Gaguin, and possibly enough through many other channels in Paris, and <margin>Malvini</margin> so be led up to the sources from which Ficino had drawn some of his varied learning.

Again, with respect to Germain de Ganay. He too was a friend and correspondent of Ficino’s. In one letter<note>Dated Oct. 16th, 1494.—*Ficini Op.* (1641) i. p. 984.—"You would have seen the Areopagite at the same time, the peak of Platonists, if you could have approached him now in that attire which became both him and you. The negligence of the writer and bookseller hindered the access."</note> <margin>3 for</margin> Ficino sends him word to expect shortly some of his works, which would have been sent off before but for the transcriber’s slowness; and among them “the Areopagite, the <margin>X</margin> highest of all Platonists.” He was the friend also of the elder Pico della Mirandola; on whose untimely death Ficino wrote to Ganay with that news, as one who would be

According to Greswell (*Memoirs of Politian*, 2nd Ed., p. 357), this Ganay was Rector of the University of Paris; though in another letter of Ficino’s (*Works* 1641, i. p. 987, *b*), his brother John is styled "President at Paris." Greswell criticizes the style of this letter quite severely.</note> This Germain de Ganay was Bishop of Orleans; and from a passage in a letter from Francis Deloine to Erasmus,<note>*Epistles*, i. 13:—"He [Colet] who, as you write, reminded you of an old friendship and intimacy, which was no common one between me and him when we were at Orleans for the sake of our studies."</note> it appears that Colet, when abroad, spent some time there in study. Orleans would naturally be on the route from Paris to Italy; and we find, as a matter of fact, that Erasmus went that way on another occasion.<note>"For to no one, when I was formerly at Orleans, about to go to Italy."—*Epistle* i. 14.</note> Now, it is of course a mere matter of speculation that Colet, staying there for the purpose of study, would enjoy the society of its learned Bishop;<note>There is still in the British Museum a beautiful manuscript copy of Ficino's *Comments on the Romans* (Harleian, 4695), at the end of which is written, "The book belongs to master Germain de Ganay;" and after this, in a later hand, "in his lifetime, Bishop of Orleans, brother of Jean de Ganay, chancellor of France, and brother of my maternal great-grandmother."</note> but there is at least nothing improbable in it. If this were so, then through another channel, Colet would be brought into communication with the writings of Ficino and Mirandola, and through them again be pointed toward Dionysius.

But, not to pause longer on these inconclusive, though interesting, traces of Colet's progress, the present treatises prove beyond all doubt that he had read at least some of the writings of Mirandola and Ficino. From the *Apologia* of the former, he quotes a passage of some length with scarcely a word changed;<note>See below, p. 109, *n.*</note> while the identity of several phrases and the general tone of thought in another place<note>*Celestial Hierarchy*, p. 36, *n.*</note> reveal a study of the *Dialogus inter Paulum et animam* of the latter. <header>## INTRODUCTION.</header>

<margin>May</margin>
<margin>preface</margin>

...been attracted to the study of Dionysius by many other causes than the influence of these two Florentine scholars. Soon after he started on his travels, the great ecclesiastical dictionary of the day was published: the *Liber de Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis* of John Trittenheim.<note>The first edition was published at Basel in 1494 and speaks of Pico, Politian, and others as still living.</note> This included notices of the life and works not only of the ancient Fathers of the Church, but of eminent contemporaries—such as Ficino, Mirandola, Reuchlin, and many more. Supposing now that Colet had taken up this new work of the learned Abbot of Spanheim with the curiosity of a young student and the deference naturally paid to the latest authority, how would he have found Dionysius spoken of? As the convert of St. Paul; a man of surpassing holiness and incomparable learning; as probably the first Bishop of Athens, and certainly the apostle of France; and lastly, as the author of many illustrious works.<note>Leaf 2, *b*.</note> With such language as this passing current as the best information of the day on the subject of Dionysius, it is almost unnecessary to inquire particularly how Colet’s attention came to be turned to his writings. But still, as the evidence of his connection with the Florentine school is unmistakable, and as that school had a very great influence—not only in making the study of the so-called Areopagite popular, but in infusing much of his spirit into the Christianity of the time—it seems desirable to notice, in passing, at least two of the great Neo-Platonists of Florence: Pico della Mirandola and Marsiglio Ficino.

### § 5.—On the *Neo-Platonists of Florence*.

<meta>This page continues the introduction to John Colet’s intellectual influences, moving from the specific works of Dionysius to the broader context of the Platonic revival in Italy and the establishment of the Platonic Academy in Florence.</meta>

<page-num>xx</page-num>

## INTRODUCTION.

<margin>No</margin>
Philosophy was introduced once more into Europe.¹ But in reality, Plato had been known to Italians a century earlier. Petrarch, who died in 1374 and to whose zeal in collecting manuscripts the cause of learning was indebted in no small degree, had obtained several copies of Plato's works, partly in Latin translations and partly in the original Greek.² His guide in this pursuit was Barlaam, a Calabrian monk and the fortunate possessor of even more treatises by Plato. Boccaccio followed in these studies and read Plato in the library of his friend Petrarch.³ But the flame, if it was ever vigorous, seems to have died out soon. The Academy does not appear to have gained any significant new disciple in Italy until the event mentioned above—the Council of Florence—brought a number of learned Greeks to that country. The conversation of these men, especially of George, surnamed Gemistus or Pletho,⁴ filled Cosmo de' Medici with an ardent desire to transplant the philosophy of Plato to Italian soil; and the foundation of the Platonic Academy at Florence must be attributed to his efforts.

In some respects, the Platonic revival is open to the charge of extravagance and even childishness. In the enthusiasm of entering such a vast and tempting unexplored territory, men were not likely to proceed soberly and with measured steps. But we must remember what those days were like and how utterly new and untried many domains of knowledge still remained. They were the days in which Politian, forced to take shelter from the rain in a random workshop, could be urged by his fellow refugees to read the works of a Roman poet aloud to them, and could not

<note>³ Bandinius, *ibid.*; who refers, as authority, to Boccaccio's Commentary on Dante (1724), p. 231.</note>

## INTRODUCTION.

pacify them until he had read and explained the whole.¹ Those were the days in which Poggio could rescue the only surviving manuscript of Quintilian from the hands of a German grocer, just as it was about to be torn up to make wrappers for his goods.² We can judge leniently, therefore, the excesses men might fall into when the fruits of the tree of knowledge were being showered down in such varied abundance at their feet, and their inability to always judge rightly the relative value of what they gathered. No doubt much nonsense was spoken at the Banquets, revived after a twelve-hundred-year interval to honor the anniversary of Plato's birth;³ and questions were raised that were far more trivial than the one that occupied the company at the table of Hermolaus Barbarus in Rome, as to whether the old ship of Theseus, when its decaying timbers had all been gradually replaced, was the same ship or a different one.⁴ But the freedom of thought fostered in this way was like the healthy action of a child at play. It was the capricious but vigorous exercise of the mind, finally able to shake off the numbness that a previous system of education had caused.⁵

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

“From this period,” says M. Delécluze, “freedom of thought in Europe arose; and from this crude mixture of deep learning and mystic philosophizing sprang the taste for the study of the moral sciences and general literature, which opened the way to philosophy and the sciences.”¹ And he adds that from such intellectual gatherings as those in the house of Bandinus at Careggi came the first blows aimed at the scholasticism of the age.

The remarks made above apply with special force to Mirandola and Ficino. In each, we see a force of intellect reveling in its own abundance. At times they seem occupied with topics almost as trivial as those that amused the Della Cruscans of Florence three centuries later; and again, we seem to be reading, in the language of Cicero, arguments more sublime than the New Academy ever reached.

Giovanni Pico, Count of Mirandola, was a few years older than Colet, having been born in 1463. In his twenty-fourth year, he had already completed a course of reading so extensive as to embolden him to publish at Rome a series of theses for disputation, enough to startle the most experienced logician there. Far outstripping Longfellow’s Scholastic at Salern, who could say,

> “There, that is my gauntlet, my banner, my shield,
> Hung up as a challenge to all the field:
> One hundred and twenty-five propositions,
> Which I will maintain with the sword of the tongue
> Against all disputants, old and young,”

Mirandola could boast of nine hundred *Questions*, on all conceivable subjects, as the literary challenges he had thrown down. It would be equally outside my purpose to stop to investigate these, and presumptuous to try to judge the actual knowledge they represent;² suffice it to say, that pro-

<note>...culis, that is, for students; as if to say, this work is not written for the advanced, but for the unlearned”—(*Ed.* 1482).</note>

Who can compare this with Holt’s *Lac Puerorum*, or with Lily’s Grammar, and not feel that another generation had risen up?

->INTRODUCTION.<-

propositions from Plotinus, Porphyry, Iamblichus, and Proclus are included among the number at this early period of 1486.

The three great objects of Mirandola's literary labors were, as Ficino informs us,¹ the reconciliation of Platonic philosophy with Aristotelian; the explanation of the Holy Scriptures; and the refutation of astrology. He often alludes to the first of these goals in his surviving letters. Thus, in one dated 1484, he speaks of having recently passed from the camp of Aristotle to that of the Academy, not as a deserter, but as a spy.² Six years later we find him writing to his Carmelite friend Baptista Mantuanus and giving an account of his studies. “I am busily occupied,” he says, “with the reconciliation of Plato and Aristotle. I devote my entire mornings to that pursuit. I give my afternoon hours to my friends, to relaxation, and at times to the poets and orators and other such lighter studies. Sleep and Holy Scripture share the night between them.”³ His nephew, John Francis Pico, repeatedly mentions this as a goal that the elder Mirandola kept before him. In one passage of his major work,⁴ for instance, after describing the increase of Aristotle's influence—which he attributes in part to the influx of the works of Averroës and Avicenna, made easier by the conquests of Ferdinand and Isabella—he details the efforts made by Pletho and Bessarion to draw attention to Plato; and then he contrasts his relative’s endeavor to reconcile the two systems with his own purpose of devaluing

---

of Mirandola. “These propositions,” he says, “were after all very insignificant, and substantially contained nothing of any importance. . . It must be confessed that his learning was not very profound, and that he was far inferior in erudition to Politian, and in philosophy to Ficino.” —*History of Savonarola*, translated by Horner, i. p. 81. In the case of someone who died in his thirty-second year, an inferiority to Politian and Ficino in their respective fields may easily be granted; yet enough remains, it seems to me, to almost justify the praise of Erasmus and the elder Scaliger.

pagan philosophy altogether, when brought into rivalry with the doctrines of Christianity.

In his love for Plato, Mirandola found a kindred spirit in one who, in personal appearance and fortune, was most unlike him: Marsiglio Ficino. Born in 1433, and therefore thirty years his senior, Ficino nevertheless addressed the other constantly as his partner in the search for wisdom;<note>“His fellow philosopher.”</note> and in the touching letter in which he describes his death, he speaks of him as “in age a son, in intimacy a brother, and in affection a second self.”<note>“For in age Pico was a son to me, in intimacy a brother, and in love truly a second self.” —Mirand. *Op.* p. 275.</note> To be near his friend, Mirandola rented a neighboring house—though one beneath his rank—and lived in it for nearly three years.<note>“That famous Pico della Mirandola... when he had come to Florence, rented a house near Marsilio, quite humble; which he nevertheless lived in for nearly three years.” —*Corsii Vita*, p. 192.</note> It was because of his pleading that Ficino undertook the translation of Plotinus; an author whom Mirandola described as worthy not only of constant reading, but of being learned by heart,<note>Corsius, p. 189, and Mirand. *Op.* pp. 79, 250.</note> and who appears to have led Ficino naturally on to Dionysius, as a translation of some of his works was the next task he undertook.<note>Corsius, p. 190.</note>

<margin><unclear>letter in Opera 153</unclear></margin>
There is one letter from Ficino to Mirandola, unfortunately undated, which describes so pleasantly the spirit in which they both worked that I will provide it in full.

> “You send me,” he writes, “most welcome news, my worthy friend, that you are daily advising many, and have already succeeded in persuading some, to abandon the irreligion of Epicurus, or lay aside certain notions they may have gained from Averroes, and embrace the devout sentiments of our Plato concerning the soul and God. For through this, as through an intervening road, they may finally reach the religion of Christ. Heaven speed you then, true fisher of men!

> of men. These perhaps are the ‘great fishes’ named in the Gospel, which the net enclosed, yet for all that it was not broken. Our net, Mirandola, is now the system of Plato; for if this is only rightly drawn beneath Christian truth, it does not break, but remains whole while it is filled. You have read that no philosophers in former days embraced the Christian religion except Platonists.<note>So Bouillet, *The Enneads of Plotinus* (1857), *Preface* p. xxxi:—“And, indeed, several of the early Fathers and most zealous Confessors of the faith—Saint Justin, Athenagoras, Clement of Alexandria—were, as is known, converted Platonists.”</note> Rightly therefore with Platonic nets, so to speak, you always fish for the highest intellects for Christ. Would that our religion had even just three such fishers in its service, so that no great fishes might be left in the sea! I said that it was unfortunate that this letter is not dated, as it would be desirable to know at what period of his life Ficino wrote in such terms. For the question that arises from it is one of considerable interest in its wider scope; as it amounts to this:—Were Mirandola and Ficino consistently, through at least a considerable portion of their lives, men of such a religious spirit as the letter above would seem to imply; or were they almost entirely indifferent to Christianity until a certain event arrested and changed their course—which event was the preaching of Savonarola?

Now far be it from me to detract from the just renown of that wonderful man. We seem to behold in him one set free from the ties that bind ordinary men to the earth, and able to hurl himself against the strongholds of iniquity. There was that fire of the Spirit in him for which “enthusiasm” sounds like a cold name—a fire that can inflame the lukewarm and melt the stubborn, and, in Colet’s language,

For the question that arises from it is one, in its wider extent, of considerable interest; as it amounts to this: Were Mirandola and Ficino uniformly, through at least a considerable portion of their lives, men of such a religious spirit as the letter above would seem to imply; or were they almost wholly indifferent to Christianity, until a certain event arrested and changed their course, which event was the preaching of Savonarola?

Now far be it from me to detract from the just renown of that wonderful man. We seem to behold in him one set free from the ties that bind ordinary men to the earth, and able to hurl himself against the strongholds of iniquity. There was that fire of the Spirit in him for which "enthusiasm" sounds but a cold name, which can inflame the lukewarm, and fuse the stubborn, and, in Colet’s language,

---

² <note>Ficino’s *Works* (1641), i. p. 956.</note>

When he speaks, in his *Triumph of the Cross*,¹ of "men of science, skilled in all forms of learning, who, having drunk freely at the pure fountain of Holy Scripture, and tasted the sweetness of Christ, have abandoned their sciences, and only found happiness in His teaching," he is merely describing the effects of his own words upon many in Florence.

<margin>50 min +</margin>
But still, I think it is unjust to the fame of two illustrious men to ascribe the formation of a Christian character in them to the influence even of Savonarola.

To speak first of Ficino. We are told by one writer that the inclination of Ficino and others "to turn to the fanciful theories of Plato, instead of to the cross of Christ for comfort, was probably a consequence of hidden infidelity," produced by the corruptions of the Church at that period.² And again, "Spondanus assures us that, under the preaching and influence of the celebrated monk Savonarola, he [Ficino] became in his later days a humble and devout learner in the school of Christ."³

<margin>a</margin>
There are two facts which seem to me difficult to reconcile with this. One is the almost complete absence of any mention of Savonarola in Ficino's numerous letters,⁴ which

Corsius, who was a disciple of Ficino’s, speaks of a depression of mind to which he was subject in the early part of his life, fostered partly by peculiarities of his constitution, and of the fruitless remedies by which he sought to alleviate it. He then speaks of a conviction growing upon him that he had forgotten Christianity in his Platonism, and of the works in which he sought to direct his philosophy to its right use, and concludes by describing him as having,
> “when he had now completed forty-two years of age, changed from a pagan into a soldier of Christ.”¹

Ficino was forty-two years old in 1475—the very year in which Savonarola, then aged twenty-three, left his home at Ferrara to join the Dominican convent in Bologna. Three years later, in 1478, we find him writing as follows to Hieronymus Rossius, <margin>1478</margin> concerning his own treatise *On the Christian Religion*:—
> “If you find in it anything worthy of praise, give God the praise, without whose gift nothing is in truth worthy to be praised. If anything should happen to displease you, take care that you are not displeased on that account with Religion herself. Do not measure the loftiness of divine things by the lowliness of my poor intellect; for the divine does not depend on the human, but the human on the divine.”²

No doubt many extravagances can be found in the writings of Ficino;³ probably he was unable to shake

In the case of Mirandola, as in that of his friend, a distinct change of life is recorded by one who knew him well. His nephew previously mentioned, in the *Life* prefixed to his collected works, distinctly says that the disappointment he met with in finding his *Propositions* censured by the Pope was the bitter lesson which sobered his thoughts and turned him aside from the lure of youthful ambition to listen to the voice of Christ. before this he had been both desirous of glory and kindled with vain love. . . . But after he was once awakened by this disagreement, he drew back his mind from indulging in excess, and turned it to Christ.” This would be shortly after 1486. His letter of fatherly counsel to his nephew,¹ and the fervent language with which he spoke with him in the orchard of Ferrara, have often been quoted as examples of Mirandola’s warm and enthusiastic piety, and of the change that some cause or other had worked in his character. That this change was “produced, at least in part, by Savonarola’s influence,” I would not attempt to deny. There seems to be no doubt that Mirandola met Savonarola at Reggio when a Chapter of Dominicans was held there, and that he was so impressed by what he saw of him that he persuaded Lorenzo de’ Medici to invite him back to Florence.² The younger Pico also mentions that, after his uncle’s death, Savonarola spoke of that event in a sermon at Florence and recounted how he had urged Mirandola for two years to enter a religious Order. “He was accustomed to be in conversation with me,” he said, “and to reveal to me the secrets of his heart, in which I perceived that he was called by God to a religious life by private inspiration. Therefore, he often intended to obey this inspiration and follow his calling.”³ The great preacher then expatiated on the loss to Mirandola himself, through not pursuing the course he had marked out for him.

The religious characters of both of them were formed before the "second Amos" began to speak out against the sins of a "second Samaria."<note>A metaphorical reference to Savonarola acting as a biblical prophet rebuking the corruption of Florence. See Stanley’s *Lectures on the Jewish Church*, Part ii. (1865), p. 358.</note>

It may be thought that an undue share of attention is being bestowed on these eminent men, connected as they are only indirectly with the history of Colet. But their writings are not only quoted in these present treatises, but also very materially directed the current of thought which at that time drew the rising generation of scholars along with it. Without even going beyond the bounds of their writings, we can trace the main stream in which the speculations of the age flowed. If the study of Cicero, which was eagerly pursued by Cicero’s countrymen at the revival of literature,<note>This is pointed out by Van Heusde, in his *Characterismi princip. Philosoph. vet.* (1839), p. 181.</note> encouraged the study of Plato, then Plato naturally led the way to Plotinus. And to the Christian reader of Plotinus, the writings of Dionysius—who was then not doubted to be the Areopagite—would seem to bid him welcome. “His walls,” says Creuzer, “are inlaid with Plotinian mosaic. . . . The streams that water his garden plots are drawn from the fountainhead of Plotinus.”<note>*Annotationes in Plotini de Pulcr.* (1814), pp. 198, 205.</note> We have seen above what a reader and admirer of Plotinus Mirandola was, and that his works were translated by Ficino. It is no surprise, therefore, to find Dionysius a familiar name with both of them.<note>In Savonarola, too, there are passages which read very much like the *Celestial Hierarchy*. See especially the *Compendium Revelationum* (1495), leaves E 6 and 7:—“Looking up into heaven, I saw nine choirs of angels,” etc.</note> And to the Platonist who had made his way through Dionysius, what would seem so fitting a completion of his journey as St. Paul? dominant Aristotelianism, they had had enough of doctrine and of definition. Weary of this intellectual captivity, it was well for them that they had found a more open country, so to speak, into which their minds could emigrate, and where their reason, no longer confined, could wander in purer air and more ample space. Those who think otherwise may tell how Ficino was said to keep a lamp always burning in his study before an image of Plato;<note>Villari, *i.* 59.</note> or how Lorenzo de' Medici rejoiced at the discovery among the ruins of Athens of a long-sought bust of the same philosopher.<note>*Life of Lorenzo de' Medici*, by Nicolaus Valorius (1749), p. 18. Valorius was a disciple of Ficino’s, along with his brother Philip. His work, though written in 1492, was not published until the year mentioned above, when it was edited by Mehus.</note> But those distinguished men knew what Plato had done for them. And if Lorenzo could declare his belief that, without Platonic training, a man could not easily be either a good citizen or skilled in Christian doctrine,<note>Valorius, *ibid.* p. 12:—“The same Ficino said that Lorenzo used to say that without Platonic discipline, no one would easily become either a good citizen or skilled in Christian doctrine.”</note> it is only fair to assume that he regarded Christianity as the ultimate goal after all, and that his support of the Platonic Academy was something better than a mere revival of paganism.

In Colet's fondness for Dionysius, in his somewhat argumentative turn of mind, and in his low opinion of the schoolmen, we may see the influence of the Florentine philosophy; and he probably would have returned to England less equipped than he was to search for and fight for the truth, and to be the great champion of a spiritual Christianity, if he had never encountered the works of Mirandola and Ficino.

<margin>X</margin>Erasmus, in the note on Acts 17:34 in his *Annotationes*. There he points out, following Laurentius Valla, that they are mentioned by none of the ancient Fathers, whether Greek or Latin.<note>“Furthermore, it is a wonder, if he were so ancient an author and wrote so much, that he was cited by none of the ancients, whether Greek or Latin,” etc.</note> Neither Eusebius, in his *Ecclesiastical History*, nor Jerome, in his *Catalogue of Ecclesiastical Writers*, specifies any such works; though Jerome, who “left nothing untouched,” includes two people named Dionysius in his list. Whatever conclusions may be drawn from these facts, the facts themselves seem indisputable. Thus, while some have asserted that these writings are quoted by Origen, Peter Halloix himself—the zealous defender of their authenticity—frankly admits that the *Homilies* where the quotation is found (*Homilia I. in Joannem*) are not by Origen.<note>*Quæstiones IV*, appended to Migne’s Edition of Dionysius, vol. iv. p. 893.</note> And Bellarmine plainly says that Dionysius “was altogether unknown to Ambrose, Augustine, Chrysostom, and other Fathers, so far as can be gathered from their works.”<note>“He was unknown to Ambrose, Augustine, Chrysostom, and other fathers, as far as can be gathered from their works.”—*De Monachis*, ii. 5, quoted in Hakewill’s *Apologie* (1630), p. 215.</note> And elsewhere he acknowledges that the citations claimed to be made by Chrysostom and Cyril of Alexandria are not found in the passages referred to.<note>*De Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis*, quoted in the same place.</note> Nor does it seem a sufficient answer to say that the omission is accidental, since on some occasions a reference to them would have been most appropriate. For instance, how unaccountable the lack of allusion to them seems when <margin>D</margin>Dionysius, Bishop of Corinth, discusses the conversion of his namesake by St. Paul.<note>This is pointed out by Ussher in his *Dissertatio de scriptis Dionysio suppositis* (1690), p. 285.</note> And how strange the oversight of such a weapon as these writings would have provided when the difficult struggle against the Arians was being waged.<note>Ussher, *ibid.*, p. 281.</note>

Hence, the only way to escape the conclusion that these—

<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the authenticity of the works attributed to Dionysius the Areopagite, focusing on internal contradictions and anachronistic terminology that suggest a later origin than the Apostolic age.</meta>

<page-num>xxxvi</page-num>

To this may be added the further difficulty that in both the *Hierarchies*, which are dedicated to Timothy,¹ there are frequent references to the Gospel of St. John. But this, according to the common account, was not written until a period later than the death of Timothy.

Moreover, the precepts laid down by Dionysius regarding Infant Baptism are said by him to be derived from “ancient tradition;”² and this, it is held, is language inconsistent with his being a contemporary of the Apostles.

Finally, it is alleged that there is similar evidence of a late origin in the frequent mention of altars, temples, sponsors in Baptism, and the like;³ in the general inflation and obscurity of style, savoring little of the lively realities of an Apostolic Christianity;⁴ in the use of particular terms, which only after a lapse of time acquired the technical sense in which he employs them;⁵ and in the very nature of the subject on which he wrote. Fault is found with his “curious speculations into the secrets of heaven, as if he had been its surveyor, or had taken a muster of all the heavenly host of blessed spirits within it; whereas St. Paul himself, though he had been caught up into the third heaven, even into the paradise of God, yet, returning back, neither dared to nor did utter any such thing.”⁶

Some of these objections admit of at least a partial answer. What seems irresistible is the cumulative force which they possess. Thus Halloix can maintain, with considerable ingenuity, that the quotation from Ignatius is an interpolation;

that the passages from St. John's Gospel were added by the author himself after the death of Timothy;$^1$ Chifflet can point out that "ancient" and "recent" are relative terms, and that Dionysius, who lived to a great age, might properly speak of "ancient tradition";$^2$ and so on. But when every single objection can only be dismissed by what may fairly be called a stretch of probability, the cumulative force, as I said, appears hardly weakened. And though some eminent names may even yet be counted as supporters <margin>what is the meaning?</margin> of the theory of authenticity,$^3$ their opinions are not likely to do more than win for them credit for their ingenuity.

Assuming now that good reasons have been shown for rejecting the notion that these writings are really by the Areopagite, it remains to be considered at what time they probably appeared. The two chief opinions (omitting, for the sake of brevity, all less supported hypotheses) are those which refer them to the third and to the fifth centuries.

A principal supporter of the former theory is Baumgarten-Crusius, who thinks that the period of their composition was the age in which Christianity was occupied with extricating itself from Gnosticism and adopting a fixed system of doctrine. He judges this period to be the third century. From the general tone of the writings, he concludes them to be the product, not of the monastery, but of the school, and that school an Alexandrian one. In keeping with this view, he disputes the inferences drawn from the supposed lateness of certain words and phrases, and pronounces them to be mere Platonisms.$^4$ One singular theory is advanced by this author, which has had the effect of somewhat discounting his conclusions in general. He thinks that the writer, whoever he was, sought to embody in Christ—

[Christ]ianity the secret teachings of the ancient Dionysian mysteries; and that for this reason he took the name of Dionysius. For this, he argues, is clearly a symbolic name, like Hierotheus and others; since, if the writer had merely wished to pass for a disciple of St. Paul, he would not have selected a name to hide under which—however famous in other respects—had no philosophical reputation attached to it.<note>Footnote marker 1 is on the original image but no corresponding note is visible in the transcription area immediately after the word. I am inserting it based on typical formatting.</note>

But the theory which has generally found the most favor is that which dates them to the late fifth or early sixth century. Such is the opinion of Montet,² and of Gieseler;³ and it is accepted by Mr. Westcott, who concludes his research into the subject of the date by saying that “the error cannot be great if it is conjectured that they were composed A.D. 480—520, either at Edessa, or under the influence of the Edessene school.”⁴ Mr. Westcott agrees with Baratier in thinking that there is no reason to consider the writings to be a forgery, though he does not go so far as to clear them of being pseudonymous.⁵ In any case, the real name of the author will probably still remain unknown. Time has, in one respect, dealt with these remarkable monuments of the Alexandrian mind (if they are indeed such) as it dealt with the Pharos, which was Alexandria’s physical monument. In both cases, time has shown the name outwardly inscribed on the work to be temporary; but it has not yet revealed the name of the true creator carved underneath.⁶

---

<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

## INTRODUCTION.

2. In approaching the study of the *Hierarchies* themselves, it is difficult to restrain a vague feeling of wonder. As none but a poet can understand a poet, so to read <term>Dionysius</term> rightly, there is need of something of that old <term>Dionysiac</term> fervor, in which the mind of the devotee was transported beyond itself and lost all individuality in an adoration of what it worshipped.¹

To trace the first origin of <term>mysticism</term>, we should no doubt have to travel, as <term>Meier</term> says,² to the banks of the <term>Ganges</term> or the <term>Hydaspes</term>. But I think that, with a less remote search, a point may be reached from which—though not as from a parent lake—the stream of the <term>Dionysian</term> writings and others akin to them may be derived. I refer to that part of <term>Plato's</term> teaching in which he treats of the communication between <term>God</term> and man; and especially to the passage in the <term>Symposium</term>, where <term>Diotima</term> relates the birth and office of <term>Eros</term>, or <term>Love</term>. The child of <term>Poros</term> and <term>Penia</term>, himself neither a mortal nor an immortal, but a spirit between the two, he is ever busy in “interpreting and conveying to the gods what comes from men, and to men what comes from the gods; on the part of the one, their prayers and sacrifices, and on the part of the other, their commands and returns for the sacrifices of men. And being midway between both, he fills every void, so that the universe is firmly joined together.”³

The thought was one which appears to have worked in the minds of <term>Mirandola</term>,⁴ and other leaders of the <term>Florentine</term> school, and which had been embraced and developed by the <term>Neo-Platonists</term> of an earlier generation.

---

Truly, to achieve that same thing easily, a divine madness is absolutely necessary for us."</note>
² *Dionysii et Mysticorum doctrinæ* (1845), p. 1.

This philosophical lineage is connected to both ancient Neo-Platonists and the later Florentine school of the Renaissance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction, moving from the conceptual origins of mysticism to the specific ways that philosophers like Plato, Plotinus, and Dionysius proposed for the human soul to ascend toward the Divine. <page-num>xl</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

How does the Supreme Being communicate himself to man? By what stages of ascent can man raise himself up to God? These are the problems which have occupied thoughtful people in all ages. In the allegory of the birth of <term>Eros</term>, <term>Plato</term> foreshadowed one solution to them; and the *Hierarchies* of <term>Dionysius</term> seem to be primarily a contribution toward the same result.

The writings of the <term>Neo-Platonists</term> all testify to the extent to which these problems absorbed men's minds. Thus, while <term>Plato</term> had spoken of the lives of the Musician, the Lover, and the Philosopher as being three ways by which the human soul might rise to the Divine, <term>Plotinus</term> had likewise marked out three ways, almost identical to those, for reaching the same goal: namely, those of <term>Music</term>, <term>Love</term>, and <term>Reason</term>.¹ Just as one who is “moved by concord of sweet sounds” may learn to withdraw his mind from the external aspects of music and gradually absorb that harmony of the *intelligible world* with which the mechanism of this sensible world vibrates in unison, so the Lover may rise from the admiration of material beauty to the immaterial. Finally, by Reason, or <term>Metaphysics</term>, the mind may be entirely lifted above the sensible world and enjoy uninterrupted communion with the invisible and eternal.

In some passages <term>Plotinus</term> speaks of the <term>Divine Vision</term>, which the mind thus elevated can enjoy, in language that strongly reminds us of <term>Dionysius</term>. “*He is not unfortunate*,” he exclaims, “*who does not find beauty of complexion, or fairness of person, or power, or dominions, or a kingdom; but he who fails of this Divine Vision alone. For the obtaining of which it would be fitting to abandon kingdoms and dominion over every land—yes, and over every sea, and over heaven itself—if only, by forsaking and despising these, one might see and turn to that*.”² And elsewhere he has a striking comparison about man's purification to the truly beautiful. Just as a sculptor, he says, chips away from the

² *De Pulchritudine*, chapter vii.

He writes in one place,^1 when describing the successive hindrances to be overcome in our ascent to the Divine: “we must shun the multitude of mankind, who go in droves, and must neither participate in their lives nor their opinions. We must avoid the manifold desires which distract us while attending to the body, and which cause us to pursue first one external object and then another—now irrational pleasures, and now undefined and conflicting actions. For these fill us with regrets and troubles.” And as the spiritual athlete is thus to shun that “multiplicity” which <term>Dionysius</term>—and <term>Colet</term> after him—speaks of so often, so is he commanded by every means to strive after unity.^2 The life of <term>Proclus</term> himself, as related by <term>Marinus</term>, was an exhibition of such a struggle. And we are at once reminded of the familiar “<term>purification</term>, <term>illumination</term>, and <term>perfection</term>” when we read of the three stages through which he passed—of political, purgatorial, and contemplative virtues.^3 That <term>theurgic</term> state, which his biographer declares <term>Proclus</term> attained by this triple ascent, may correspond to the state of <term>beatific vision</term>, which the Christian philosopher sets forth as the highest elevation to which the mind of man can soar.

Not without reason, then, does <term>Fabricius</term> conclude that <term>Proclus</term> and <term>Dionysius</term> “ploughed with one and the same <term>Neo-platonic</term> heifer.”^4 And in reading the following pages, the reader will hardly fail to be struck by the resemblance in tone between <term>Dionysius</term> and what has just been quoted. In the <term>Jacob’s ladder</term>, which this author would describe as linking all earth with heaven, he will see something more real than any mere flight of fancy; he will see a mind laboring—cumbersomely, no doubt, but powerfully—to make plain the way between God and man, and to teach how man, “rightly apprehending his own position in the chain of

of it. In the words of Dean Milman, “The triple earthly priestly order had its model in heaven, and the Celestial Orders had their counterpart on earth. Just as there were three degrees of attainment—Light, Purity, and Knowledge (or the Divine Vision)—so there were three Orders of the Earthly Hierarchy: Bishops, Priests, and Deacons; three Sacraments: Baptism, the Eucharist, and the Holy Chrism; and three classes: the Baptized, the Communicants, and the Monks. I would only ask the reader not to lose sight, throughout the various details, of what has been pointed out as the main object of <term>Dionysius</term> in his *Hierarchies*;³ namely, to show the soul's relation to God, and the means by which it may be raised from its earthly prison to the unclouded glory of the Divine presence.

Neither in what he writes himself, nor in the mystical extract which he proceeds to quote from his preceptor <term>Hierotheus</term>,¹ is there anything explicit on the subject of the Incarnation and Redemption of mankind.

In truth, convenient as it may be to speak of these doctrines as "sufficiently set forth elsewhere," the Dionysian system hardly admits the Scriptural doctrine of man's Redemption. The Orders of the two Hierarchies fill up all the space between God and the humblest human soul; the chain is too complete to admit such an interruption as the work of a personal Savior. "The office that Christ should have discharged," says <term>Meier</term>, "is discharged by this order of Hierarchies."²

<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>

Dionysius is, of course, unable to deny that there is something in the world which we call evil; but since he cannot account for the presence of an active, antagonistic principle—after starting with a First Cause that is perfectly wise, good, and powerful, from whom all things proceed—he is driven back toward optimism. He maintains that even the devils "are called bad because of weakness in respect to their natural energy."¹ In other words, all things were created perfectly good; and if any beings display opposite qualities, it must be through their failure to live up to their proper standard of goodness.
<margin>compare Lovejoy</margin>
Evil is a failure, a defect, not an active, intelligent power working in opposition to God.²

This is very different from the language of Colet referred to above, and from the equally strong expressions in the *De Sacramentis* concerning the mischief continually wrought in the world by those angels "who apostatized through their own wickedness; the one who, from an Angel of Light, became an Angel of Darkness: the Devil and his ministers."³
<margin>See page 39</margin>

In conclusion, it may be observed that Colet plainly held up the Dionysian writings as a mirror to his own times. Again and again, he returns from the ideal portrayed in the *Hierarchies* to the realities around him, ending with some indignant exclamation about the contrast they present.⁴
<margin>123/126/136/151</margin>
It might have been expected that more attention would be given in this Introduction to such an aspect of the subject; that, considering the disputes now widespread regarding matters of ecclesiastical doctrine and ritual, an effort would be made to gather opinions on those points from the present treatises.

# CH. I. ON EMANATION FROM GOD.

...imitate the angels in their bodies and bodily actions; so that they, who in spirit had been unable, through their bodily dullness, might at any rate in a figurative manner—in their flesh and body itself, as far as is possible—exhibit God. This corporeal and fleshly hierarchy and order among men, purifying, illuminating, and perfecting (or rather signifying purification, illumination, and perfection), was (under Moses, the teacher of the Hebrews) the Synagogue. It imitated the angels by bodily objects and sensible signs, not by intellectual and spiritual ones. 

Yet Moses himself was not ignorant of the truth, according to the pattern of which he arranged the Hebrews. Nor was he himself unaware, moreover, that there would one day be a Man in whom not an angel, but the Truth itself should speak; and who would, in spirit, advance men to the truth which he, Moses, had foreshadowed in their bodies. This is our most blessed Jesus, "the Sun of Righteousness," who has not only shown us what was the meaning of those shadowy ordinances of Moses, but has also enlightened our understanding to believe what is revealed. 

Meanwhile, however, it was brought about that on earth there should only be visible that empty shadow of order and of some hierarchy, so that the advancement and translation of mankind to an angelic state might appear to proceed in a just order; namely, from the shadow to the image, and from the image to the truth. Under Moses there was the shadow; in Christ there now exists on earth the luminous image; the reality we shall hereafter have in heaven. Under Moses, the system of the Hebrews and their Synagogue was such as to contain a measure of light within much shade. While, on the other hand, we who are here in the Church of Christ have very great light, but at the same time along with it some duskiness and shade; yet it is still so overpowered by the light that it should rather be called color than shade. For color consists of darkness and light.

In truth the Church, while we are here in the body,

<header>CH. II. *ON THE USE OF SYMBOLS.*</header>

## CHAPTER II.
->*On the Use of Symbolic Acts and Words.*<-

THEOLOGY, which is the revelation of divine truth and the language of the prophets,¹ takes great account of human weakness; and when it seeks to transform that weakness into a heavenly state, it causes heavenly and spiritual natures to descend in some measure, bringing them down to the lowly state of men to be objects of their senses to some degree. It describes the condition of divine things and the ranks of the blessed spirits through poetic fictions. But this is not done through what is entirely fictitious—lest a mere fable should have too little authority—but rather through the persons of actual living men and their actions with one another. Thus, there may first exist a kind of stage, a crude display, and an indistinct representation; although this is not the absolute truth itself, it is still a figure of the truth that is to come. In this way, the truth may later be more brilliantly revealed and more clearly reproduce the reality itself, its shadow being brought out into the light, and the previous darkness being, as it were, illuminated with colors. For, as I have said before, the Synagogue of the Jews is like a shadow; the Church, with Christ as its Sun, is like a shade variously illuminated. And this Church, like some object beautifully colored by receiving the light of the Sun, was foreshadowed on earth by that entire system and

Erasmus says in his Letter to Justus Jonas of Colet's fondness for children:— "In boys and girls, the purity and simplicity of nature delighted him; Christ calls his followers to imitate this, being accustomed to compare them to Angels." It is strange to observe in what an unquestioning spirit the anecdote of Erasmus about barbarity in schools ("I knew a certain theologian," etc., *Works* i. 441) has been assumed to refer to Dean Colet above all people. Mr. Seebohm has satisfactorily refuted this application of the story (*Oxford Reformers*, p. 144), and I will not impair the freshness of his arguments by repeating them. But it may be instructive to notice how widely such base coin circulates before it can be finally detected and destroyed. Thus, in the present case, we have Tytler, in his *Life of Henry VIII.* (p. 42), relating how "the amiable Erasmus . . . has left us a picture of the manner in which Colet used to superintend the flagellations, and the good will with which Lilly administered them, which is at once ludicrous and revolting." And to the same effect we may read in the *Quarterly Review*, vol. xxxix. p. 104; *MacMillan's Magazine*, No. lxxxi. p. 401; *Report of the Commissioners on Colleges*, etc. vol. i. p. 194; and many other places. And all this is because Dr. Knight says that "it will be a hard task to apply [the story] to any other than to them." Kennett, from whose collections Knight took the passage, speaks more diffidently: "I think there can be no other application of it." And he sets down the extract from Erasmus in an evidently half-jesting mood:— "I leave it as a task to be translated by some ill-deserving Paul's scholar."— *Lansdowne MS.* 1030, *folio* 132 *b.* Such are the scanty materials from which this tissue of error has been spun.

...are all things in full measure, which Moses sought either to teach in righteousness or to establish in religion; which things had first to be foreshadowed, and then afterwards in due time to be set in a clearer light, so that at length, at the end of the world, they may all be made perfect. In these foreshadowings and signs, metaphors are borrowed from all quarters by Moses, a theologian and observer of nature of the deepest insight; because there are no words suitable to express the divine attributes. For nothing is fit to signify God himself, who is not only unutterable, but even inconceivable. Therefore, he is most truly expressed by negations, since you may state what he is not, but not what he is; for whatever positive statement you make concerning him, you err, seeing that he is none of those things that you can say. Still, because a hidden principle of the Deity resides in all things, on account of that faint resemblance, the sacred writers have endeavored to indicate him by the names of all objects, not only of the better but also of the worse kind; lest the less intelligent, attracted by the beauty of the fairer objects, should think God to be that very thing which he is called.¹ You ought to speak me-

---

Scholastic Lecture on Psalm 116. The passage from Isaiah 10:23 is taken as the text; and the Prophet's language is said to suggest four causes of the Psalm: namely, the Material, Formal, Active, and Final. The Material Cause—that which furnished its matter or subject to the Psalm—was the "mercy and truth" of God, as displayed in the incarnate Word. This will explain Colet's use of *word*. By *shortened* is said to be signified the shortness of the Psalm. The whole composition is a most singular one.
¹ The soundness of this reasoning is pointed out by Mr. Westcott, in his Paper on Dionysius in the "Contemporary Review" (May, 1867), p. 10. "And here Dionysius acutely adds, that those positive affirmations are to be preferred which, while they convey a partial truth, yet convey it in such a form as to avoid any appearance of expressing a complete truth. Thus there is little danger in describing the angels under the likeness of beasts and birds, because no one could suppose that the likeness extended beyond the single point of comparison, while many may be deceived by the nobler imagery which describes 'the beings of heaven as creatures of light in human form, of dazzling brightness, and exquisite beauty, arrayed in glittering robes, and flashing forth the radiance of harmless fire.'" The same principle is laid down by Marsilio Ficino (whom Colet more directly quotes afterwards, at the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the use of metaphors and symbols in theology, specifically explaining how Dionysius and other sacred writers use physical imagery to represent divine realities.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>CELESTIAL HIERARCHY. CH. II.</header>

<margin><unclear>re all habits</unclear></margin>
...metaphorically in such a way that you may be considered to have sought an expression from other sources, rather than having spoken in accordance with reality itself; and so that it may be openly acknowledged that you are using similitudes, not what is strictly appropriate. On this account, divine attributes are expressed not only by the names of the sun, sky, light, life, intelligence, mind, or whatever else might be superior, but also by the names of birds, four-footed beasts, plants, stones, elements, and <margin><unclear>(Is miss OT)</unclear></margin> creeping things. For God is all in all; and that which is lesser dimly represents that which is greater. Furthermore, all things represent God as far as they are able; from whom, by whom, and in whom all things exist.

<margin><unclear>X quins</unclear></margin>
This kind of writing was employed by the sacred writers of old—both patriarchs and prophets—as being both necessary and useful. It was necessary because they could not have spoken of God otherwise; and it was useful because through it, the weaker vision is attracted and led to behold brighter things, while the ungodly and profane are kept in darkness. The veil created by Moses is of such a kind as to conceal the precious things of God from the ungodly, while still teaching the good something; though it is not the truth itself, it is at least some shadow of the truth. And regarding what Dionysius discusses concerning divine things, he solemnly charges Timothy¹ to conceal and keep them hidden, and not to divulge them except to the worthy, so that they are not profaned by the eyes of the uninitiated.

## CHAPTER III.

### *On the Angelic Hierarchy, and its threefold Office of Purifying, Illuminating, and Perfecting.*

<margin>remodel</margin>
<margin>Eph. i. 6.</margin>
<margin>all parts of the works of the church</margin>
GOD<gloss>1</gloss>, who is one, <gloss>2</gloss>beautiful and <gloss>3</gloss>good; Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; the Trinity which created all things; is at once the purification of things toward unity, their illumination toward what is beautiful, and their perfection toward what is good. That God, by pouring forth his light which purifies, illuminates, and perfects, unspeakably deifies and makes godlike the threefold hierarchy of angels; among whom, though all things are shared by all alike (for purity, light, and likeness to God are inseparably connected), yet there are, as it were, personal attributes; so that perfection is the property of the first hierarchy, illumination of the second, and purification of the third. Likewise, in the first hierarchy, the office of perfecting is specially assigned to the order which is first; that of illuminating to the second; and that of purifying to the third. In this threefold way, the threefold hierarchy resembles the triune God. The whole endeavor of all <insert>(</insert>spiritual beings is to <insert>(</insert>represent God.<insert>)</insert> God first, by his <insert>x</insert> power, makes those beings who are near him like himself; then they, in turn, make others like them. Thus, there proceeds a diffusion of the Deity from order to order, from hierarchy to hierarchy, and from superior creatures to inferior ones, according to each one’s capacity, for the making godlike of all; “to <insert>x</insert> the praise,” in St. Paul’s words, “of the glory of the grace of God.” In this allotted task, moreover, are men, so that they may be refashioned in a hierarchy representative of God.<insert>x</insert> And when, in reducing them to order, the angels were <insert>(</insert>exhausted,<insert>)</insert> then did Jesus Christ—who is himself Order and the incarnate beauty of God, “fairer than the children of men”—come to their aid, establishing <insert>(</insert>in himself three hierarchies, and in each one of these the threefold virtues of purifying, illuminating, and perfecting; in order that men also, being fellow-workers in Christ for the glorifying of<insert>)</insert>

## CHAPTER V.

### *On the general and special application of the name of Angel.*

Angels have their name of "Messengers" from the messages they bear, seeing that the higher orders announce the things of God to the lower, even down to men. But the greatest messenger to man was Jesus Christ; why he is 
<margin>Isai. ix. 6.</margin>

<header>CELESTIAL HIERARCHY. CH. VI.</header>

<margin>! Speak of?</margin>
by no means so for the lower orders. These names were not invented by man, but borrowed from Holy Scripture. And the variety of them in the Sacred Volume necessarily indicates a variety of spirits in the heavenly regions, the order of which Dionysius has arranged. The lowest angels work upon our bishops and priests, and refine and raise them to what is spiritual; so that they may become wholly spiritual, and may in turn spiritualize others beneath them, each according to his capacity; so that from among men, as far as is possible, there may be in Christ a fourth and highly spiritual hierarchy, who will one day be true spirits and angels, their spiritualization being continued by God in Christ, through angels and angelic men.

God, who is the founder of all things and of every order, alone surely knows all things exactly; and he made known by his angels to the prophets (whom Dionysius also rightly calls Divine Teachers, since he is rightly and truly a divine teacher who speaks out what is revealed by God);—he made known to them in some measure, I say, what they in turn, as far as was

### CH. VII. THE HIGHEST THREE

"Lift up your heads,¹ O you gates, and be lifted up, you everlasting doors, and the King of glory shall come in." And to their question they make answer: "The Lord strong and mighty, the Lord mighty in battle; the Lord of hosts, he is the king of glory."

<margin>Ps. 24:3–6.</margin> It is he who has "ascended into the hill of the Lord," having "clean hands and a pure heart; who has not lifted up his soul to vanity, nor sworn to deceive his neighbor." He is followed by "the generation of those who seek him, who seek the face of the God of Jacob." The highest angels, as they contemplate Jesus, ask one another in doubt: "Who is this who comes from <margin>Is. 63:1–3.</margin> Edom, with dyed garments from Bozrah?" To whom Jesus makes answer, "I who speak in righteousness." Then the angels, encouraged by his condescension, ask, "Why is your clothing red?" <note>original: "Wherefore art thou red in thine apparel?"</note> Jesus answers, "I have trodden the wine-press alone." In this way, they learn the mystery of the incarnation <note>The doctrine of God becoming human in the form of Jesus.</note> more fully from the glorified Jesus and teach those beneath them; at once purifying, illuminating, and perfecting. For these operations go on together. But those highest spirits, resting² in the most blessed happiness, sing in sweetest harmony, "Holy, holy, holy, Lord God of Hosts <note>original: "Sabaoth," a Hebrew word meaning "armies" or "hosts."</note>."

***

¹ It may make the sense somewhat clearer here to notice that in the Vulgate <note>The standard Latin translation of the Bible used in the Western Church.</note>, from which Dean Colet quotes, the word *Princes* <note>original: *Principes*</note> is used differently than the English version would lead us to suppose. It there implies "Lift up your gates, **you princes**," by which designation the angels may be intended; not "Lift up your **heads**, O you **gates**," which, as Hammond <note>Henry Hammond (1605–1660), a prominent English churchman and scholar.</note> observes, is applicable to the raising of a portcullis <note>A heavy iron gate that slides up and down to protect a castle entrance.</note>. The former interpretation is seen in Wycliffe's version <note>The first complete translation of the Bible into Middle English (c. 1380s).</note>: "You princes, take up your gates, and you everlasting gates be raised; and the king of glory shall enter."

² Hakewill, in his *Apology* <note>original: *Apologie* (1630)</note>, page 224, makes this a mark of discrepancy between Dionysius and St. Paul, whom he professes to be his teacher: "This Dionysius, in various chapters of the same *Hierarchy*, teaches that the higher orders of angels are always assisting around the throne of God, never employed in foreign messages; directly contrary to that of his Master, 'Are they not all ministering spirits,' **not some**, but all, 'sent forth to minister for their sakes that shall be heirs of salvation?'"

## CHAPTER VIII. <insert>2nd</insert>

### *On the Dominations, Virtues, and Powers.*

<margin>General</margin>
There are nine orders of angels, <gloss>images</gloss> figures of the nine <gloss>archetypes</gloss> in God. And each one obtains a name corresponding to the property in God which it exhibits. Hence the <gloss>1</gloss> first have love, the <gloss>2</gloss> second have knowledge, and the <gloss>3</gloss> third have a throne as their characteristic; since these recall the steadfastness, wisdom, and love in God.

<margin>Dominion</margin>
In the second hierarchy, the <gloss>4</gloss> first have the name of Dominations, being an express image of the true and archetypal dominion in God. For the dominion in them is simple and unmixed, and free from all subjection, ruling over all, useful to all, a true and pure liberty of exercising power, according to the form and pattern of God. And they not only strive to exhibit in themselves this divine dominion, but they also strive most earnestly to draw all things everywhere to a true dominion and to an imitation of the dominion in God; so that whatever takes upon itself the character of dominion may exercise true lordship in God.

<margin><unclear>S Paul</unclear></margin>
The Lord himself, Jesus Christ, in his human form, has plainly taught us how we poor men are to imitate the dominion of God; from him we learn that dominion among men is a free subjection and a willing obedience to God and to men, so that they too may obey God.

<margin>x</margin>
To the <gloss>5</gloss> second order, the name of Virtues is applied; by which word Dionysius intends to signify a certain manly, masculine, and unshaken strength in them, an unconquerable and invincible valor, and a marvelous imitation of the divine virtue itself, in which nothing is done feebly, weakly, or cowardly.

<margin>x</margin>
In these valors and virtues, there is zeal, care, and energy, so that all things in God may be strongly and manfully valiant in pure and masculine virtue; so that in virtue also God may be everywhere displayed. Jesus Christ, the very "power of <margin>1 Corinthians 1:24. <br/> (1 Cor. 1:24-25)</margin> God and the wisdom of God," as St. Paul calls him, when in our human form, taught us poor men, who are made strong in him—

## CHAPTER IX.

### *On the Principalities, Archangels, and Angels.*

<gloss>3rd angelic hierarchy</gloss>
The last hierarchy is that of the Principalities, Archangels, and Angels. These Orders also have their models in God himself—the <gloss>divine ideas and patterns</gloss>—which they represent. <margin>7.</margin> For what else is principality than <gloss>an aspect</gloss> (an image of the true and exalted principality in God), and than a spiritual effort to draw all things throughout the world that hold principality toward an imitation of the true and predominant principality of God; in order that <margin>God as</margin> whatever is in the chief place may exercise lordship with all love, and may join love with lordship?

Angels, who hold the last place in the series of divine spirits and are closer to worldly and physical objects, and who specifically receive the name of "Angels" because they are primarily and most clearly occupied with the task of bringing news to the world—they, I say, specifically express the generous and beneficial messages of God. They apply themselves truly and holily in announcing, as far as they can, among one another, and then in turn to mankind, the things that are derived from God through a long series of angels. As their nature is more like our human minds and more closely connected with them, so their life is more familiar to us.

Between these Orders is placed that of the Archangels, as it is called; who have by nature, power, office, and operation, as well as by position, an intermediate existence between the Principalities and the Angels; representing and imitating in God a certain supreme, wise, and virtuous power of bringing news.

These Orders, like the others in the remaining hierarchies, are distinguished by their specific properties, and yet at the <margin>as is the Deity (all)</margin> same time (as Dionysius constantly acknowledges) they are coequal;¹ which I judge to be because they represent

---

<margin>x</margin>...the Trinity, in which the Three Persons are equal. Finally, it is the constant business of the order of Angels to introduce among men those attributes of the Deity that are expressed within their order, and to lead men also to imitate them and God. This is so that beneath the angels there may exist a threefold hierarchy—namely, of men on earth—corresponding to the one among the angels. The angels attempted this in vain for a long time because the unfitness of mankind was an obstacle, until the mighty Angel came to their aid—the <margin>Isai. ix. 6.</margin> "messenger of the great counsel," as Isaiah calls him, Jesus Christ. At his birth, the angels sang in gladness, <margin>Luke ii. 14.</margin> "Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace toward men."

He, the Son of the Virgin, established a hierarchy after the model of the angels, according to the pattern of his own truth. He built it up from those he chose, as living stones; he not only arranged them in order but also gave them life in himself, so that as living members they might be arranged in him, who is Order itself.

Now, the writings of the prophets testify that the angels are leaders and officers of mankind to guide their obedience to God. <margin>Dan. x. 20, 21.</margin> For in Daniel, there is a Prince of the Persians and a Prince of the Grecians, and Michael is called the Prince of the Jewish nation; for "the Most High appointed the <margin>angels</margin> boundaries of nations according to the number of the angels of God."¹ Michael, as Vicegerent, held the province of the Hebrews, which was more obedient to him. In all the other nations, the wickedness of mankind resisted so much—and everyone followed their own will and crooked desires so much—that nothing could be brought into order or even the appearance of order. As Dionysius says, the cause of this evil was each person's self-love and pride. But Michael found at least some shadow of truth among the Hebrews and trained

### CHAPTER X.

*Recapitulatory; on the dependence of the several Orders, one upon another.*

<margin>purity</margin>
Of the spirits who contemplate God, whom we have just discussed, the first Hierarchy both penetrates more deeply and is more inflamed in God than the rest, being wholly purified, wholly enlightened, and wholly perfected. This primarily produces firmness, knowledge, and love in the human Hierarchy, so that the foremost among men, being raised on high, may have a pure, clear, and glowing vision of all things along with them. This first shining and dazzling Hierarchy irradiates the second hierarchy next beneath it, so that being enlightened, it may illuminate the third; and the third in order may beautifully color darkened men, causing them in some measure to imitate and reproduce the beauty of God—for to Him all glory is credited as due. And as this diffusion proceeds and advances, new names in turn
<margin>by his power</margin>

<margin>Good disposition</margin>

<header>CH. XVI. DISTINCTIONS IN OFFICE.</header>
