<margin>Romans 11:36.</margin>
completed and perfected. This is what St. Paul says in his Epistle to the Romans, in these words: “For of him, and through him, and to him are all things: to whom be glory for ever.” Since of himself, and after his own pattern, and by his own grace, he forms, disposes, and perfects all things. In so far as he fashions by his power, he is the Father; in so far as he disposes by his wisdom, he is the Son; in so far as he graciously perfects, he is the Holy Ghost. Hence comes existence, order, and aim among things; hence all things both are, and are beautiful, and are good. This existence, beauty, and goodness are wholly in the Beginning; and in it, it is an undivided essence, in which all things are the same; and this First Cause, through its infinite power, begets in its own depths an infinite offspring, that is infinite in the infinite.) This is the eternal, beautiful Son of the eternal, omnipotent Father, between whom of necessity there springs an eternal and infinite love. And these three, on account of their common infinity, are both equal one with another, and most like, and their essence is one and undivided. In the case of these, a man may not rightly speak otherwise than of three Persons and one God, the beginning, middle, and end of all. In whom, except for their relationship, whatever is in each, is in all. For the Father is Power, wise and good; the Son is Wisdom, powerful and good; the Holy Ghost is Goodness, powerful and wise. By this Trinity in Unity all things whatever are powerfully, wisely, and well established; and in them there is universally, in like manner, propagation and love, essence, sense and aim. For all things consist in themselves of beginning, middle, and end; and exist steadfastly in themselves, and are resplendent in beauty, and do well in Him, from whom they received being, form, and goodness.

Upon that unspeakable Trinity the blessed spirits, whom we call angels, unceasingly turn the eyes of their mind. And all of them indeed gaze earnestly upon the three Persons, but individually in a varying manner; yet so that all contemplate everything, and all the hidden principles of things.

<header>CELESTIAL HIERARCHY. CH. XVI.</header>
<page-num>40</page-num>

perfection in being. In the middle Hierarchy, those of the first Order care most for being in form; those of the second for form itself; those of the third for perfection in form. In the third and highest, those of the first Order care most for being in perfection; those of the second for form in perfection; those of the third for perfection itself.

<margin>all this through the angels</margin>
Thus by the ministry of angels, all things have their being, form, and completion.

Moreover, for the work of founding a church of Christ for a time beneath the angels—composed of men who shall be purified, illuminated, and perfected under Jesus Christ—the angels are ministering spirits. The first Hierarchy purifies, and as it were begets men anew to exist by hope in God. The middle one reveals and illuminates, so that men by faith may have clear insight into God. The last inspires love, so that men by love may be made perfect in God. But in the first Hierarchy, the Angels chiefly make it their business that men may be purified and established in hope; the Archangels produce light and faith in hope; the Princedoms love in hope. In the middle Hierarchy, the Powers work hope in faith; the Virtues faith itself; the Dominations love in faith. The Thrones, moreover, in the third, work hope in love; the Cherubs faith in love; the Seraphs love itself, the perfecter of all.

<margin>open <unclear>her</unclear></margin>
In this manner, this threefold Hierarchy, in a threefold way, in a triune God, constantly works out a threefold Christianity among men, subsisting by faith, hope, and charity. <margin>x</margin> <insert>(1)</insert> The Thrones, and in them the Powers, and in them again the Angels,¹ diligently work out, in the power of God, purification and a pure, simple, and steadfast hope; so that man may come forth from multiplicity to simplicity, from base despair to pure hope in God, and may stand firm in it; and by hope in the powerful ray of God, may at length have a spiritual existence <margin>x</margin> in God and Christ. Next, the wise Cherubic spirits, and in them the Virtues, and in them again the Archangels, in the...

---

¹ These are the first, or lowest, Orders in the three Hierarchies, just as the Cherubim, Virtues, and Archangels are the middle ones, and so on, counting downwards.

<header>CH. XVI. DISTINCTIONS IN OFFICE.</header>

<margin>Logos</margin>
all-disposing wisdom of God, reveal the reasons of things and the fair truths of the Word of God; and by revealing, they illuminate man, who now hopes in God through the faith of Jesus Christ, who is true Reason, the Word, and the Messenger of truth. In Him, every announcement of light comes in order from the angels midway down to men, so that, being now purified for hope, they may believe in that Word of God.

<margin>X 1. C. 13.</margin>
The Jews seem to have had <note>A handwritten bracket in the margin marks the following phrase</note> hope rather than faith before the coming of Christ. The Christians, in like manner, during the lifetime of Christ upon earth, had faith rather than charity, which is the gift of the Holy Spirit.

<margin>distinction between heat and light</margin>
Lastly, the Seraphic spirits—in goodness, love, and the Holy Spirit—are wholly inflamed with love itself; and the Dominations are wholly inflamed with wisdom; and the Princedoms are wholly inflamed with the power of God. One within another in order, the lower in the higher, and all in the heat of the Spirit of God, they fill men (now clear and bright through faith) fully, weightily, sweetly, and perfectly with the love of God. Thus, being purified by Angels, Powers, and Thrones, and illuminated by Archangels, Virtues, and Cherubs, they may finally be perfected in the Spirit of God by Princedoms, Dominations, and Seraphs.

As there are nine orders of angels, so there are likewise nine orders of men, through Christ in God and by the ministry of the angels. And in these, all things are in all, since everyone hopes in, believes in, and loves God in Christ. It neither is, nor can be, otherwise in this threefold ecclesiastical hierarchy.

<header>CH. XVI. ON THE FALLEN ANGELS.</header>

<margin>in the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy</margin>
...hope in faith; the fifth are those who especially believe; the sixth those who love in faith; the seventh, as it were Thrones, are those who firmly hope in love; the eighth are those who believe in love; the ninth and last are those who especially and wholly love.¹

<margin>for example</margin>
Those who are without hope, faith, and love are in a state of total disorder, coldness, darkness, and death. Among them there is no order, but an everlasting wandering. Still, there are degrees of disorder, unhappiness, and misery. And accordingly, set down on the other side nine bands of men under the Devil, the prince of darkness, and under his ministering satellites; who, in their state of utmost confusion, defect, and privation of all goodness, are distributed into nine most horrible troops. Set down, I say, beneath them nine bands of men who, under their chieftain the Devil, beneath the standards of death and in the armor of darkness, are all fighting with the sword of impiety and hatred—each one, alas, most miserably to his own everlasting destruction.

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 18:1</margin>
<margin>of an empyreal heaven</margin>
<margin>St. Augustine</margin>
According to the testimony of Solomon, the angelic nature, and every spiritual nature, was created at the same time as the corporeal. For he says, "He who lives forever created all things in general." These spirits were created as inhabitants of the empyrean heaven,² which shines like fire above the firmament. They were created in innocence; but a great part of them, by their own will, not long afterward—wishing in a guilty pride to exalt themselves—fell from the highest to the lowest. Our Savior Jesus Christ bore

<header>CELESTIAL HIERARCHY. CH. XVI.</header>

<margin>Luke 10:18.</margin> bears witness to this, when he said, “I beheld Satan fall from heaven like lightning.” Satan also did not stand fast in the truth. The rest, looking up to God, partly through their own <margin><unclear>avy</unclear> <unclear>ment</unclear>?</margin> will and partly through grace working with them, fortunately remained in the truth; not through their own merits, but through grace being made blessed in God. On the other <margin>St. Augustine</margin> hand, Lucifer with his followers fell not through God’s disdain, but by his own fault. These wretched and lost angels inhabit the dense and murky atmosphere around us,¹ and they are harassers of men, so that men do not grow sluggish through neglect and apathy; they are destined to be more miserable hereafter after the last judgment. St. Paul teaches us that we wrestle against “the prince <margin>Ephesians 2:2.</margin> of the power of the air.” For they unceasingly endeavor to make us like themselves, so that we may suffer a punishment similar to theirs. But during our life here, angels are assigned, by the mercy of God, to the protection of mankind. <margin>guardian angel</margin> For each person has his own angel as his guardian and preserver, and also as his constant monitor and one who prompts what is good; otherwise, weak humanity would be able neither to persist in the good nor to oppose the evil. It is the opinion of some that their advancement in blessedness will correspond to their advancement in labor; which these disputants call accidental merit.² All the angels care for us; but those to whom we are entrusted as guardians <column-break/>

¹ “And by *Tartarus* here (2 Peter 2:4), in all probability is meant this lower, dark air, or atmosphere of the earth, according to what St. Augustine says concerning these angels: ‘After their sin they were thrust down into this darkness, where the air also is.’” —Cudworth, *Intellectual System* (1678), p. 817.
² I am not sure whether the passage which Colet had in mind was Article 3 of Question 93 in the Supplement to the *Summa* of Aquinas. The chief texts on which both rely are John 14:2 and 1 Corinthians 15:41. It will be noticed that Colet speaks with very little respect for the author or authors from whom he quotes.

# CH. XVI. ON THE FALLEN ANGELS.

<gloss>so Origen</gloss>

The devils, unclean spirits, possessed of reason, subtle and envious, strive to injure the human race; they long to beguile them, delude their senses, and corrupt their affections. Whether sleeping or waking, they unsettle the world and disturb the elements. As hypocrites, they often “transform themselves into angels of light;” in the form of idols they claim for themselves the majesty of God; in their pride, they aim for empire.<insert>)</insert> They are incessantly waging war against men, both by openly assaulting them and by secretly plotting against them. If victorious, they exult; if conquered, they retreat in confusion, so as not to venture again to tempt in the same way and to the same sin. <margin>see</margin> Of these malignant natures David makes unceasing complaint <margin>X</margin> <margin>a prayer</margin> in his hymns; and as though always in danger, he implores aid for himself and invokes curses upon those evil spirits.

<margin>Ps. xl. 13-15.</margin>
“Be pleased, O Lord, to deliver me: O Lord, make haste to help me. Let them be ashamed and confounded together who seek after my soul to destroy it. Let them be driven backward and put to shame who wish me evil. Let them be desolate as a reward for their shame.” Again in another <margin>Ps. lxx. 1, 2.</margin> Psalm: “Make haste, O God, to deliver me; make haste to <margin>Psalm</margin> help me, O Lord. Let them be ashamed and confounded who seek after my soul.” In truth, he does scarcely anything else in all his Psalms than pray to God in Christ that, among so many and great enemies, he may come out victorious and be saved in God. Men are called to this perilous war by God through Jesus Christ. But we must not proceed to it rashly or heedlessly, but weigh our strength first, <margin>X</margin> and consider the risk beforehand. Indeed, before anyone becomes a Christian, or a priest and member of a religious order in Christianity, <margin>can he do it</margin> let him first consider carefully with himself what he will be able to do; lest, if he be vanquished, the devil, now become

an open enemy, would deal more harshly and miserably with him than if he had remained a subject and tributary of Satan. For this reason Jesus Christ, the leader and captain in this war, when summoning his fellow soldiers to him (as St. Luke relates), although he would have all people to be saved, yet—so that they might not, in avoiding Scylla, fall into Charybdis, nor in search of safety rush into greater condemnation—warned the multitudes to be cautious and look carefully to what they were about to undertake. He preferred that they should live as servants to the Devil than be slain in open war as enemies, and be condemned with a lesser rather than a greater condemnation. And so he said to them: “What king, going to make war against another king, does not first sit down and consult whether he is able with ten thousand to meet him who comes against him with twenty thousand? Or else, while the other is still a great way off, he sends an embassy and asks for conditions of peace.” When we have made a profession of any better life in Christ, then the Devil prepares himself with a more numerous and better-equipped army, so that he may assault and trouble us; and if he conquers us in rebellion, he will torment us more cruelly as a tyrant. So it is that, as St. Paul writes to the Hebrews,¹ “It would have been better not to have known the way of righteousness, than to turn back again.”

envy
</margin>
The devils are subtle by nature, observing the natural world with a keen eye, skillful through long use and experience, and moreover, diviners of the future and at times well-versed in former revelations. But, being evil themselves, they use everything for evil, plotting above all else the everlasting destruction of mankind. In their envy, they cannot endure the glory that people are to have in Christ; they suggest evil under the guise of good, and if at any time they prompt anything that is good, they do it for an evil purpose. They always discourage true good; and if ever they have discouraged evil, it is so that they may lead into a greater evil. At times

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>CH. I. SENSE OF SCRIPTURE.</header>

<margin>Leo I</margin>
> "We must not doubt, dearly beloved, that every Christian observance is from God's teaching, and that whatever has been devoutly received as a custom by the Church proceeds from the tradition of the Apostles and from the teaching of the Holy Spirit, who even now rules over the hearts of the faithful by his ordinances, to the end that they should be both obediently kept and wisely understood by all."

<margin>440-461</margin>
Thus spoke Leo the First, who sat on the papal throne four hundred and fifty years after Christ.¹ This is the way of explaining the Scriptures—not so much by words as by acts, not so much by teaching and writing as in reality—according to the appointment and command of the Apostles, which Saint Dionysius undertakes to write about to Timothy. Before doing so, he earnestly charges him² in many words not to wickedly disclose the principles of sacred rites, most holy things, and the mysteries of God and of divine worship to the <gloss>common</gloss> multitude, nor to impart them to anyone but men like himself, of the greatest holiness, keeping in mind that precept of Jesus Christ:

<margin>Matt. vii. 6.</margin>
> "Do not give what is holy to the dogs, nor cast your pearls before swine."

He bids him rather to keep all things in sacred and serious privacy, and to store them up and tend them religiously, with all love and reverence, in the depths of his own heart. In this, he is imitating Jesus, the author of all priesthood and sacred rites, who taught the mysteries to none but his own disciples, and did so in private; and even to them, he did not teach everything, as they were not yet perfect and spiritually minded, nor able to bear it all. <margin>disciples from the apostles</margin> That same Jesus now, as an indescribable Light at the right hand of the Father, shines more brightly and more fully upon the angels—beings of a far higher nature than man—and bestows on them a more abundant revelation; then in turn, through them, he shines upon those men who tower aloft by detachment and separation from the body, and who approach more nearly the

And the greater he is, the more he should serve in the office of love and ministry. <margin>2</margin> Then, in the next place, those who are second perform this task, and transmit what they have received to the third. And thus the work goes on in order, and the imbuing of men with truth in Christ proceeds; all striving upwards, and delivering to those beneath them what they have drawn from above, that they may be perfect members in Christ, through a yearning love of God and their neighbor. <header>CH. I. IMITATION OF CHRIST.</header>
<meta>handwritten "X" marks in margins; pencil annotations throughout</meta>

...so, being fashioned in righteousness by <gloss><unclear>value</unclear></gloss> love on either side, they may be ministers of the grace of God, from whom are all things in Christ. (For in him each person ought to be so ready to give what he has received, as to appear to have received it for no other reason but to give; so that in the giving, the receiving may be set forth, and the love of God in the love of our neighbor; since we then declare that we love Him, if, as St. John teaches in his Epistle, we love our neighbor. <margin>1 John 4:20.</margin> “If a man says, I love God, and hates his brother, he is a liar.”

In this mutual love consists all order, duty, and office in the Church; and the whole Ecclesiastical Hierarchy in it <margin><unclear>Studies Vol. 1 p. 34</unclear></margin> rests on the love of God and of our neighbor; in receiving and giving; in an imitation of Jesus its head, who is all love; in a desire for each person to sanctify himself with the fire of love, so that he in turn may sanctify others; so that, in this office of sanctifying, the whole Church may copy Jesus, to the end that at length they may be made wholly like him; as St. John writes:—“Every man that <margin>1 John 3:3. <unclear>of those pure</unclear></margin> has this hope in him, purifies himself, even as he is pure.” And again he says:—“He that says he abides in him, <margin>Ibid. 2:6.</margin> ought himself also <gloss>to do</gloss> so to walk, even as he walked.” In this ecclesiastical chorus Jesus has himself begun the sacred dance; that all may follow him with linked hands.¹ <margin><unclear>x 110</unclear></margin> He was the fullness of righteousness, that all in him should jointly imitate his righteousness, which consists in giving and in loving, because from love we have received. For what other perfection is there in the angels, than the giving what they have received? In this giving they more resemble God than in receiving. For there is no perfection in receiving. God receives nothing; but he gives in the <margin><unclear>born differs</unclear></margin> highest degree, and by giving he is in the highest degree set forth. Therefore St. Paul says that “it is more blessed <margin>Acts 20:35.</margin> to give than to receive.” In Christ therefore, in whom are all things, who is the highest principle and limit of order and of our hierarchy, who has received nothing from us, who gives all things, who has placed us in a course of truth,

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. I.</header>

<margin><unclear>others</unclear></margin>
...who has raised us above the law of Moses to the light of the gospel and of revelation; who has made us to know by faith and to act on our faith; who by his Spirit has brought us forth into a better state; so that in the law of the Spirit we should rightly imitate the blessed angels, in whose virtue we begin to be spiritual; so that being at length perfected, we may become spirits. The endeavor to imitate their super-celestial life of bliss led St. Paul to say that <margin>Philippians 3:20.</margin> "our citizenship is in heaven"—yet in this happy state in Jesus Christ, because we are still men to outward view, and while this nature of ours is at any rate unfamiliar to us, its powers and operations are both profitably declared and assured to us by a groundwork of sensible signs; so that they may be reminders to us and inducements to the things that are not seen. For so long as we are in a world of <gloss><unclear>unreality</unclear></gloss> figures, and <margin>1 Corinthians 13:12.</margin> "see through a glass darkly," no training, fashioning, or forming of us, and no working and imitating of spiritual beings, can <margin><unclear>mirror</unclear> <unclear>language</unclear> <unclear>divine</unclear> <unclear>sense</unclear></margin> dispense with corporeal figures. For as long as we are in the body, our sacraments and ecclesiastical system must be in some degree corporeal. 

Under Moses, the legal hierarchy was wholly corporeal and figurative; under Christ, in whom we are now imitating the truth which spirits possess, there has been established an ecclesiastical hierarchy that is partly corporeal and partly spiritual. But in the hierarchy triumphant, all will be spiritual, since our animal bodies will have been all transformed into spiritual bodies. Then shall we burst forth from these signs and allurements of grace to the unveiled face and truth; so that with the angels we may then truly, and in our true nature, behold truth herself "face to face." All ought to aim at one object and, as far as possible, assume its form. Now the one prize of our Church is Jesus, towards whom we all run, so that we may wholly obtain him. Every person ought to represent him within himself and to have a taste and savor of him; although not all in the same manner and in equal measure, yet each as far as he can, and as much as is given to each: <margin>Romans 12:3.</margin> "according as God has dealt to every man the measure of faith," each as he thinks that he is able should strive to represent him who is our Head and is undivided. ->But let us now discourse of the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy.<-

<margin>exp</margin> Every hierarchy is a system and summary of sacred things. In the Christian hierarchy the office and duty of the Bishop is to comprise within himself and possess all sacred things. For the Bishop is a true sacrament and a summary of all that follows after him in the Church. He understands and represents fully and clearly in himself the whole priesthood; so that there is nothing in any inferior minister given by

<meta>This page continues the discussion of imitating Christ, transitioning from the individual's spiritual obligation to the structural role of the Bishop and the divine origins of the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. I.</header>

There is nothing of God that does not exist in Christ our Bishop<gloss>1</gloss> more substantially, clearly, and in an even more perfect manner. Indeed, it follows that through him all spiritual and divine gifts must be transmitted to all the orders of the Church; and that from him nothing goes forth in the Church which is not first contained in him, the Bishop; and that nothing praiseworthy and divine exists in anyone beneath the Bishop that is not recognized as having proceeded from him. For in truth, if he were lacking in anything proceeding from God that is found in any inferior person—such as holiness, wisdom, or justice—he assuredly is not the one who should occupy the seat of the Bishop.

The creator and founder of our hierarchy is that same one and all-powerful Jesus. Through him the Father, with the Holy Spirit, the adorable Trinity, by His goodness, is the cause of the existence of this human hierarchy, warring under Christ its leader, beneath the standard of truth, with spiritual weapons. That is the cause also of its well-being and perfection. For it was the will of the good God that man should be saved; and that cannot be unless men are made godlike, refashioned to a divine condition, and become gods by being made like God, so that they may represent God.<gloss>2</gloss> For to that end all things strive earnestly and most ardently: that they may be fashioned after the divine image. To that end, all who are called in Christ are consecrated to God, so that while living here they may represent God as far as they are able. And how far men are able, Jesus, who is God and man, has taught us. For he willed to be made man, so that he might show among men a godlike way of living;<gloss>2</gloss> whose likeness everyone in him ought to strive for. <margin>1 John 2:6.</margin> For "he who says he abides in him, ought himself also to walk, even as he walked." He is the head of both the celestial and the human hierarchy; in him the world had a pattern for imitation, and now the angels also in heaven...

Indeed, there ought to be this wholesome struggle on the part of everyone in the Church: that all men should in all things have an appearance, a sound, a fragrance, a taste, and a representation of Christ.

God makes us godlike by imparting himself to the angels directly and individually; (to men He does so indirectly, and through a diverse system of images with which Holy Scripture is filled (which <margin>X</margin> Dionysius calls the substance¹ of our priesthood), especially the Old Testament; and the New Testament, added to it and written by evangelists and apostles, is not lacking in such figures.) In the time of Dionysius, these books were placed by the authority of the early Church among the sacred and canonical writings and were approved with all reverence. To these was added a more hidden system of wisdom, not committed to writing by the Apostles, but kept sacredly and religiously in their minds and hearts. As worthy men, they learned this from Jesus Christ and afterward passed it down only to those who were worthy and like themselves, commanding them not to commit these matters to writing, lest <margin>Matt. 7:6.</margin> divine things should be held cheap, and "that which is holy be given to the dogs, and pearls to swine." The sublime mind of St. John used his own allegories to portray what he saw of this kind in such a way that almost no one but himself can understand it,² unless they are moved by the same spirit of prophecy. For ceremonies commonly practiced in the Church and transmitted to us from the Apostles themselves were not committed to writing by them, but were en- <margin>not only explain X</margin> trusted to the practice of the common people, while the <margin>X</margin>

<margin>X <gloss>principles</gloss></margin>the reasons for them were retained by the chief men only in the Church.

<margin>X</margin>These reasons they did not disclose, except to those whom they knew to be of purer natures and advanced to the episcopal rank; and by them, the undisguised principles of things <margin>from the Pentateuch</margin> and of the sacraments were understood. By the wisdom of the Apostles, it was brought about that a draft of these should be given to the common people under a figurative system, as it were; so that, being uneducated, they might drink <margin>from Ruth</margin> and feed on coarser food. For, as St. Paul says, "there is <margin>1 Cor. 8:7.</margin> not in every man that knowledge." Holy bread, according to our Savior’s testimony, is not to be "given to the dogs." <margin>Matt. 7:6.</margin> Of the fine flour<sup>1</sup> of sincerity, the bread of the priests alone <margin>Y</margin> is made. But that on which common men feed must necessarily have something of the <gloss><unclear>wramer</unclear></gloss> bran of figures and images; lest by the naked truth their eyes should be too much dazzled, or they be stirred up to vain thoughts. Hence sacraments and ceremonies—whether purgatorial, illuminating, or perfecting—were instituted for the common people by the Apostles themselves, of excellent inner meaning and denoted by the choicest images. The reasons for them, however, were committed not to writing but to the minds of <margin>X</margin><margin>from recent</margin> the holy Bishops; so that, just as the signs follow their course among the common people, so the reasons for them should follow in the minds of the Bishops. The unfolding of these reasons has at length been attempted, after very many years, by William, Bishop of Mende;<sup>2</sup> who in this pursuit divines

In all subjects of this kind, whether written with such a physical nature in the emblems or adapted to habit and custom, we need

> "...renaissance, pagan in the arts, in letters, in morals; finally the eighteenth century, with its laughter as treacherous as it was insane, and which, after having broken all the bonds that connected man to God, bent him toward the earth and tore from him, in the fatal embraces of philosophy, the abjuration of his memories—the only plank of salvation that remained to him."

There is some truth in this; and the poetic taste of the late Dr. Neale, aided by his fellow-translator, discovered something that is valuable, as well as merely curious, in Durandus. What, for instance, to a man of Dean Colet's strong sense, could have seemed the value of the following piece of symbolizing, which occurs in Book 1, chapter 4, on *Church Bells*:

> "Moreover, as the rope begins from the wood on which the bell hangs—by which wood the Cross of our Lord is understood—it is a fit sign of Holy Scripture coming down from the wood of the Cross. And as the rope consists of three strands, so also does Scripture consist of a threefold strain, namely, History, Allegory and Morality. And so the descent of the rope from the wood, in the hands of the priest, is the descent of Scripture from its mystery in the mouth of the preacher."—"Ad hæc cum funis etc."

While men were altogether unlike God, and like themselves, it pleased the good God <gloss><u>to be like man</u></gloss>,<gloss>✕</gloss> so that he might render men like God.<note>It is notable how Colet here expands and brings into due prominence the work of Christ’s atonement, as compared with Dionysius. The latter, in the passage corresponding to this, briefly says that the Bishop, desiring the salvation of men by their being made like to God, “proclaims to all the gospel, truly so called, how that God, in mercy to those on earth, of his own natural goodness, deigned himself to come to us, in keeping with his love to man, and by a union with himself to assimilate, as fire does, what is so united, according to men’s several capacity for being made godlike.” This, with the quotation of St. John 1:12, which follows, is all that Dionysius has in this place corresponding to what Colet dwells on so fully and scripturally. It has been elsewhere said that the work of the atonement is left very much in the background in the system of Dionysius.</note> “The Word was made flesh, and dwelt among us.” “He who was in the form of God, made himself of no reputation, and took upon him the form of a servant, and was made in the likeness of men. And being found in fashion as a man, he humbled himself, and became obedient to death, even the death of the cross.” “He has made him to be sin for us, who knew no sin, that we might be made the righteousness of God in him.” This was the eternal Son of God, whom the Father “sending in the <gloss><u>likeness</u></gloss> of sinful flesh, and for sin, condemned sin in the flesh; that the righteousness of the law might be fulfilled in us, who walk not after the flesh, but after <gloss><u>the Spirit</u></gloss>,” in the acknowledgment and worship of the true God. <gloss>Christ’s</gloss> <gloss><u>His</u></gloss> office on earth the Bishops everywhere <gloss><u>discharge</u></gloss>, and in <gloss><u>him</u></gloss> act as he acted, and with like zeal strive

Now this fire is, I do not doubt, the holy love of God, which Bishops worthy of love diffuse over the world,¹ being themselves lovers of men in God, so that they may in turn love God again, and being born anew by the love of God, may live in God, believing in the Son whom he has sent, and placing all their hope in God. For love is the source of begetting; holy love, of begetting holiness; and the love of God, of godliness. Now the messenger of this goodness, compassion, love, and tenderness of God, was his lovely Son Jesus Christ, who first in wisdom brought down love to men, that they, being born anew by love, might in turn love their heavenly Father along with him. It was, indeed, a custom and the practice of all those who introduced anyone to the Church of Christ, into the temple of God for the heavenly warfare, first to hesitate and shrink back in thought. At last, however, they led them in with good faith and hope in God, and with the utterance of many prayers, so that the outcome might be successful and all might end well and happily for the one who wished and desired to be initiated into the Christian rites. But the Bishop, inwardly rejoicing over the gain for Christ, returns thanks to God and, along with the rest of the priesthood, sings hymns in a festive strain. The Bishop then again marks him with the ointment and receives him as a participant in the Holy Communion; so that, being now in the mystical body, he may daily go forward toward Christ and proceed onward, so that he is not pronounced lazy in the march he has undertaken and <gloss>way of living</gloss> the form of life he has professed.

> ¹ In the *Apostolic Constitutions*, ii. 61, the reason given for turning towards the East during prayer during the Holy Communion is the memory of paradise: “in memory of the ancient possession of Paradise situated in the East, from where the first man, having despised the commandment and obeyed the counsel of the serpent, was expelled.”
> ² From this passage, a slight argument may be made for the relatively late date of Dionysius. Bingham says, “It is clear from Tertullian that neither of these [anointings] were given before baptism; but when men had come out of the water, then they were anointed with the holy anointing, and had the laying on of hands in order to receive the Holy Spirit. Therefore, I think Daillé’s theory is very just and reasonable: that the anointing preceding baptism is of a later date and was not yet adopted among the ceremonies of baptism in the time of Tertullian. But the writers of the following ages speak distinctly of two anointings, the one before and the other after baptism.” — *Antiquities* (1855), vol. iv. p. 158.

<margin>X 2.3.</margin>
<margin>Thus</margin>
<margin>best judgment before? Editor Crown 3</margin>
Such was the custom and ceremony of Baptism and the washing of regeneration in the early Church, established by the holy Apostles, by which the more excellent baptism of the inner man is signified. And this form differs very greatly from the one we use in this age. And here I confess that I marvel how it is that in one and the same old-established religion, there can be such a different sacred rite; since it would seem that we ought to be more careful in preserving our ceremonies than the Jews were in theirs, in proportion as ours are more perfect than theirs.

<margin>Eph. iv. 5.</margin>
For, as St. Paul writes to the Ephesians, “There is one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one body;” so in like manner there certainly ought to be one manner and rite of the sacraments, without adding to or taking away from the ancient and venerable institution of the Apostles;

<margin>X apostle source</margin>
the changing of their ordinances in sacred matters is in truth a serious crime. For they, being fully taught by Jesus Christ, knew well what are suitable symbols and appropriate signs for the mysteries; so that one may suspect either rashness or neglect on the part of their successors regarding what has been added to or taken from their ordinances.¹

---

<note>¹ It is interesting to compare with this the complaint which Erasmus makes of the innovations in the Service of the Mass which had been made in his day. He says, “In many churches water is publicly consecrated, and a response is sung for the entrance of the Mass. The Psalm which used to be sung in its entirety is shortened. Before the Gospel, prose pieces are sung—sometimes unlearned ones—and the Creed is omitted. Before the canon of the Mass, an expanded preface is sung; during the consecration, the *Sanctus* is sung; when the mysteries are displayed, a song is sung imploring the help of the Blessed Virgin or St. Roch, and the Lord’s Prayer is suppressed. These things are not impious, but they depart from the gravity of the ancient worship, and if no one resists, they lead toward superstition.” —*Annotations*, on Luke 2:14.</note>

<summary>The author compares the baptismal rites of the early Church with those of his own time, arguing that the original apostolic ceremonies should be strictly preserved without innovation. He references St. Paul’s call for unity in faith and Erasmus's criticisms of contemporary changes to the Mass.</summary>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. II. 3.</header>

# CHAPTER II.

## PART III.

### *On the Spiritual Contemplation of Baptism.*

<margin>MP</margin>
<margin>Mal. iv. 2.</margin>
Besides the purification of life, which is fittingly signified by the washing of water mentioned above, there is also an elevation in it, and an indication of a higher meaning. For as <gloss>invisible things</gloss> are the archetypes of sensible things, so are sensible sacraments the tokens of things invisible; and they are joined to spiritual sacraments, as the body is to the soul. The good and bountiful God is the “Sun of Righteousness,” who shines upon spiritual natures, spreading Himself equally over all without ceasing, and who stands at the doors and windows of the soul, knocking so that He may be admitted. Under these rays of the heavenly sun are two kinds of men: one, who does not want all the wisdom he can have; the other, who would be wise even beyond his power. The former, basely and willfully pleased with evil and darkness, refuses the light; the latter over-proudly exceeds the measure of his light. From this it follows that these people not only do not receive what they desire, but also lose what they once possessed.

<margin>Rom. xii. 3.</margin>
<margin>Rom. i. 22.</margin>
<margin>Eph. v. 14.</margin>
For, as St. Paul warns us “not to think of ourselves more highly than we ought to think, but to think soberly,” lest, “professing ourselves to be wise, we become fools;” so if, on receiving the divine light, you trust <gloss>in yourself</gloss> and begin to form plans by yourself, you then extinguish it all. Increase what you have by the same means as you received it; I mean, trust faithfully in God, and in no way in yourself. Allow the heavenly ray to increase in you; be subject to it, humble and patient; this is to lay yourself open to the light. If, relying on yourself, you boast in any way in the light, you are closing yourself against the entry of the light and walking in your own darkness, not knowing where you go. The Bishop who is illuminated by Christ, like the sun, shines from the pulpit with the light of truth and makes the word of the gospel stream forth equally upon all. He cries, “Awake, you who sleep, and arise from the dead, and Christ shall give you light.” His words are

<summary>This chapter explores the spiritual symbolism of baptism as an enlightenment from the "Sun of Righteousness," warning that true wisdom requires humility and a reliance on God rather than oneself. It describes the Bishop's role as a conduit for this divine light, calling the spiritually dead to awaken through the Gospel.</summary>

<keywords>Baptism, Spiritual Contemplation, Sun of Righteousness, Illumination, Humility, Bishop, St. Paul, Divine Light, Sensible Sacraments</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual contemplation of the rite of baptism, specifically focusing on the role of the Bishop and the significance of the "sealing" and enrollment of the believer.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>CH. II. 3. CONTEMPLATION OF BAPTISM.</header>

<margin>Psalm 12:7.</margin>
> “pure words, even as the silver which is tried, and purified seven times in the fire.”

The Bishop himself is made into fire by God; he possesses the light of truth and the warmth of goodness; in loving-kindness, he teaches everyone.

<margin>Ephesians 5:13.</margin>
Now, he who has learned that he is in darkness (
> “all things that are exposed are made manifest by the light: for whatever makes things manifest is light”
), when he, I say, recognizing his own state, is being enlightened, then seeks to be carried onward even higher into the light and goes to some believer more illuminated than himself.

<margin>Ephesians 1:13, 14.</margin>
But in this material order of things, be mindful also of a spiritual order. To apply in this way to a believer<note>See above, page 65.</note> is humility and a submission of the heart to the heavenly ray. And when this is done submissively and sincerely, such a man is at once touched with the divine light and sealed to the portion of the Saints. For he receives a portion of the light as a sign and token that he belongs to the Lord’s flock. This marking, as it were, of the sheep of God the Shepherd, is called by St. Paul in his Epistle to the Ephesians,
> “the pledge of our inheritance.” “In whom you also,” he says, “after you heard the word of God and of truth, the gospel of your salvation, received it: in whom also, after you believed, you were sealed with that Holy Spirit of promise, which is the pledge of our inheritance until the redemption of the purchased possession, to the praise of his glory.”

This marking is represented by the laying on of the Bishop’s hand. For just as he is touched by the hand of the Bishop, so is he at the same time touched and sealed by the right hand of God, which is the Son of God, whose finger on the man’s head is the Holy Spirit. Then the man is fortunately among those who are enrolled for salvation; the inscription of his name in the list of the faithful by the priests is a symbol of this.

In the early Church, all who had been sealed to the faith through grace were enrolled. But later, due to the influx of a multitude of people who took the name rather than the reality of Christianity, this practice—like many other customs that had once been general—began to be maintained only in the purer branch of the Church alone.

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual symbolism of the Bishop as a source of divine light and fire, and the significance of the laying on of hands as a "seal" of salvation. It also notes the historical shift in the practice of enrolling the faithful as Christianity grew in numbers but decreased in individual devotion.</summary>

<margin>
Matthew 18:13.

The godfather, whom Dionysius calls <gloss>(Evangelist)</gloss> *Sponsor*, is also enrolled: (the name is inscribed both for him who offers himself, and for him who offers another, to God;) because our Father in heaven "rejoices more over the recovery of the hundredth sheep, than over the ninety and nine which went not astray."

<margin>
Luke 9:62.
Luke 16:13.
</margin>

He, being newly admitted into the singleness of Christ, must profess a single and undivided mode of life—the life above, only. For, as Dionysius says, it is not lawful that he, who has received a share in that which is one, should have a divided life. He must proceed in one direction only. For "no man, having put his hand to the plough, and looking back, is fit for the Kingdom of God." "No servant can serve two masters: for either he will hate the one, and love the other; or else he will hold to the one, and despise the other." He cannot be divided and single. Christianity is a profession of singleness; and man is drawn to it, as if from multiplicity to simplicity. A single Christ does not endure a double coat.<note>1</note> If you wish to be in his wedding garment, you must make arrangements to come to him naked, so that you may put it on; and to lay aside your former manner of living, so that you may enter into that which is Christ's. This is the meaning and significance of the act of stripping off all garments by him who commits himself to Christ; of his spitting and blowing towards the west, and renouncing with a solemn vow whatever is of iniquity. The meaning of his turning to the east, when now quite stripped, and exposing himself to the rays of the risen "Sun of Righteousness," is that he, being now purified and single, should receive the single and pure ray, and put on the robe of light and righteousness, which the grace of the Holy Spirit in Christ has woven; so that, shining in the light with the bright clothing of righteousness, he may walk in heavenly majesty

<margin>
2 Corinthians 6:7.
</margin>

through this gloomy vale, "by the armour of righteousness on the right hand and on the left;" holding out to all his

---

<note>1</note> This would seem to be an allegorical interpretation of the "two coats" in Matthew 10:10 and Mark 6:9. Erasmus, in his *Annotations* on the latter passage, does not mention any such interpretation, but has a long and exceedingly sensible note on the folly of justifying begging by any such texts.

It details the ritual of stripping off old garments and renouncing the west to turn toward the "Sun of Righteousness" in the east.</summary>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>CH. II. 3. CONTEMPLATION OF BAPTISM.</header>

undefiled hands, full of goodness; he himself being ever good and consistent with himself, unable to do anything except to benefit others; guileless toward the guilty; unconquered in patience during troubles; so that by a continual doing of good he may seem to have kept his robe of righteousness untarnished. This is the white robe which is put on by everyone who is washed in the basin of holy regeneration, the robe of a holy and unspotted righteousness. In this we must always walk toward the east, to earn more and more light; with no looking back toward the west, the region of death, <margin>Gen. xix. 26.</margin> lest, like Lot’s wife, we be “turned into a pillar of salt.”

His being anointed all over by the priests signifies the contest and wrestling that we have <margin>Eph. vi. 12.</margin> “not against flesh and blood, but against the rulers of the darkness of this world, against spiritual wickedness in high places,” in the armor of God, which is signified by that anointing—even the Holy Spirit—so that our limbs may be made more vigorous. You must strive, so that you may conquer; you must conquer, so that you may be crowned. Fight in him, who fights in you and prevails, even Jesus Christ, who has declared war against Death, and fights in everyone. It is he who overcomes in us, and we in him, when anointed with the Holy Spirit; he who, when he shall have subdued and overthrown the empire of Death, is himself to be all in all in the kingdom of light. It is the rule of combat that we should imitate our leader, who is <margin>Ps. xxiv. 8.</margin> “the Lord strong and mighty in battle.” Nor may Christians undertake any other manner of warring than he has himself taught; for we have no enemies and opponents except sin (which is ever against us), and the evil spirits that tempt us to sin. When these are vanquished in ourselves, then let us, armed with the armor of God, help others in charity; even though they may not tolerate us, even though in their folly they do not see their bondage, even though they would put their deliverers to death. To love man so much as to die while caring for his salvation is most blessed.

The threefold immersion, with an invocation of the Trinity, is a beautiful image of death,<note>1 “Baptism is therefore given into the death of Jesus; water serves for burial;”</note> and of the putting off the flesh,

And this is signified by the plunging of the <gloss>2</gloss>whole man beneath the waters, by which men are reminded that they are dead with Christ, and as it were buried with Christ for three days.) This is the meaning of that <gloss>1</gloss>triple immersion; that, with the putting off of Christ’s body, all our bodily life should be put off, so that we may rise again in righteousness; preparing to die in Christ the righteous, so that at length we may in the same body also rise again to glory. We must first come forth to a life of righteousness; then we must rise to a <gloss>1</gloss>life of glory. Death destroys death; grace produces righteousness; righteousness brings forth glory. (So that we may die unto sin, it is necessary that a <gloss>2</gloss>righteous one die for us. And so that we may live unto righteousness, it is necessary that we be sustained by the <gloss>3</gloss>grace of the righteous one rising again.) And again, (so that <gloss>1</gloss>we ourselves when dead may rise again in righteousness, it is necessary that we be reanimated by the <gloss>2</gloss>virtue and power of God.) They truly rise again, who having put off their sins in the washing of regeneration, in the death of Christ, walk with perseverance until the end, in the white robe of righteousness without spot; and who do not frustrate the anointing of confirmation, which is finally performed by the Bishop,¹ to signify that a man is confirmed in <gloss>3</gloss>grace and

<header>CH. II. 3.  CONTEMPLATION OF BAPTISM.  75</header>

<gloss>continuation</gloss>
perfected. To the one who is thus perfected as a member of Christ in his own degree, the sacred food of the Eucharist is administered for his union with the Body and his nourishment as a member. By this, it is understood that he is both in the Body and is fed and spiritually nourished within that Body. No one is fully a member of Christ until he has been a partaker of the Holy Communion and the food of life; by participating in this, he grows to be one with the Body. In the early Church, all who were baptized and at the same time confirmed by the holy anointing were invited without delay to the Communion of the heavenly food.

It is to be observed that Dionysius speaks of Confirmation in such a way as to teach, not that it is a distinct sacrament, but something for the completion of baptism; so that it and baptism are only one sacrament. It is also no less to be remarked that it was customary in the early Church for all the baptized to receive communion at once, in order that, through a common nourishment, they might be accounted part of the mystical body of Christ. Otherwise, although baptized, they are not considered to be of the body. For such participation in Communion gathers and binds them together by a common nourishment and perfects them by a final completion. On this account, it was at one time given even to baptized infants; <note>1 Aquinas argues that children, being as yet without the use of reason, should not have the Eucharist administered to them; and he says that the opposite has been incorrectly inferred from Dionysius, because he believes Dionysius is speaking of the baptism of *adults*:—“*Not understanding that Dionysius there speaks of the baptism of adults.*” *Part iii. Question 80. Article 9.* But he takes no note of the fact that Dionysius, in the last chapter of the *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy* § 11, speaks clearly of children as partaking of this Sacrament:—“*But as for the fact that children also, who cannot yet understand divine things, are made partakers of the symbols of holy baptism and the most sacred communion, this indeed seems to the profane, as you said, to be reasonably laughed at.*” In Cyprian’s *On the Lapsed* (*Op. Ed. 1617, p. 145*) is a remarkable story of what befell a little girl on receiving the sacramental cup, which shows that in that time and country the practice was not unknown. And so Pamelius, in his note on the passage, concludes from those words, citing the authority of Dionysius; but he thinks that the custom of young children receiving communion survived no</note>

Gracious God! Here one may perceive how cleansed and how pure he who professes Christ ought to be; how inwardly and thoroughly washed; how white, how shining, how utterly without blemish and spot; in short, how perfected and filled, according to his measure, with Christ himself; so that in him he may afterwards be vigorous and strong, a member of him whole and sound. For Christ, the head of the Church, is health and perfection itself; "from whom," as St. Paul writes to the Colossians, "all the body, by joints and ligaments having nourishment supplied, and knit together, increases with the increase of God;" to the intent that in everyone, being simple, still, and firm, God may be reflected; so that with steadfastness, clarity, and perfection, men in Christ may now recognize and worship God; and that each one may so savor of and represent Christ alone in himself, that Christ is seen to be all in all. Being dead, let us seek our life, which is "hidden with Christ in God," which is the glory that shall appear to us when Christ, in whom we have hope, shall appear in glory. ## CHAPTER III.

### PART I.

->*Introduction to the Sacrament of <term>Synaxis</term>, or Holy Communion.*<-

THIS communion in the body and blood of Christ is the consummation of all the sacraments. All the sacraments indeed lead to a communion, but in no degree compared to this, in which there is a wonderful communication and fellowship, since many, by participation in one, become themselves one. For this, men are prepared by other sacraments which go before, so that by this they may be fulfilled. All the sacraments have for their object the developing of unity, likeness, and simplicity among men.

Towards this we are brought by other sacraments; by the <term>Eucharist</term> and <term>Synaxis</term> we are perfected. Therefore, whatever great mystery is being performed, it is accustomed to be completed by this sacrament; without which all the mysteries in which men are initiated are undeveloped and imperfect. And since the effect of them all is that men may be formed into one body, by being themselves united to one, and since the <term>synaxis</term> especially attains this end, it has on that account had this name specifically allotted to it;¹ just as to the sacra-

---

<note>¹ The passage of Dionysius on which this is based formed a point of dispute between Harding and Bishop Jewel. The former argued that the words of Dionysius, "This most worthy Sacrament is of such excellence that it passes all other Sacraments, and for that cause it is"</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Sacrament of Holy Communion (Synaxis), defining it as the ultimate fulfillment and perfection of all Christian sacraments. It explains that while other rites prepare the believer, the Eucharist specifically achieves the goal of unifying the faithful into one body in Christ.</summary>

<keywords>Synaxis, Holy Communion, Eucharist, Dionysius, Harding, Bishop Jewel, sacraments, communication, unity, Christian initiation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sacraments as means of unification and perfection, specifically addressing how the sacrament of baptism (regeneration) serves as illumination, while the Eucharist (synaxis) serves as the final perfection of the believer.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. III. 2.</header>

the sacrament of regeneration, the name of Illumination is uniquely suited. In all the sacraments there are the three qualities of purifying, illumining, and perfecting; but perfection is the attribute of the <term>synaxis</term>, while illumination belongs to the sacrament of regeneration. For in the latter, man first receives light, by which he begins to perceive the truth. Hence baptism is called by Dionysius the sacrament of illumination. For he who is touched by the heavenly ray is called back and looks back. This is signified by the laying on of the bishop's hand. He looks back upon the light by returning to God; <note>This seems to be the general sense of the passage, but I cannot understand the Latin *resiliente in Deum*.</note> as David 
<margin>Psalm 36:9.</margin>
says, "In your light shall we see light."

### CHAPTER III.

->PART II.<-

->*§ 1. On the ante-Communion Service; § 2. On the celebration of Holy Communion.*<-

### § 1.

The Bishop first offers at the altar a holy prayer, which I suppose to have been the Lord's Prayer. He incenses the altar, and then the whole temple. Next, returning to the altar along with the rest of the priests, he sings hymns and the psalms of David. The ministers and deacons, one after another, then read the Lessons, as they are called, from Holy Scripture. They then dismiss from the temple the <term>Catechumens</term> and the <term>Energumens</term> and the

---

<note>only called the Communion. For although every Sacrament is such that it gathers our lives—which are divided in many ways—into that one state by which we are joined to God, yet the name of Communion is fitting and appropriate for this Sacrament especially and uniquely, more than any other"—this implied that the Communion, or <term>Synaxis</term> (that is, *Gathering*) was so named by way of distinction, because of its gathering and uniting us to God. Jewel shows that there was another equally sound reason for the name, which was that it joins Christians together in a common fellowship.—*Reply* (1611), p. 20.</note>

Penitents, since they are not worthy to be present at the mysteries.

<margin>Gal. 6:6.</margin>
(The Catechumens are those who, not yet having been initiated in the sacred rites, nor baptized, are being instructed for baptism. For *Catechize* signifies *instruct*; from which those who are being instructed, so that they may properly approach the mysteries, are called by a Greek word *Catechumens*. Concerning these, St. Paul says in his Epistle to the Galatians, “Let him that is taught<note>“Him that is taught” is expressed by the single term *Catechumen*.</note> in the word, share with him that teaches, in all good things.”)

<margin>Eph. 6:12.</margin>
(The Energumens are those who are driven and harassed by evil spirits,<note>“The word Energumens,” says Bingham, “in its broadest sense denotes persons who are under the influence and operation of any spirit, whether good or bad; but in a more restricted sense it is used by Church writers for persons whose bodies are seized or possessed by an evil spirit.”—*Antiquities* Bk. iii. c. iv. § 6.—A form of prayer for them is given in the *Apostolical Constitutions*, viii. 8.</note> in whom the power of darkness largely dwells; from whose force and working and operation they are called Energumens. For the Greek word *energeo* signifies *I work*. Under this name are not only those who are driven into a demonic fury and pitiably distracted; but moreover all men of the deepest guilt, who either disguise or deny their religion, such as Apostates; or, when they have made a profession of Christ, yet lead a life of the greatest wickedness, and stained with notorious crimes, such as fornicators, poisoners, murderers, blasphemers, and all such like; who, though Christians in name, lead the life of profane heathens, and, though called after Christ whom they falsely profess, are in reality the subjects, even the servants, of darkness. In them the princes of darkness and “spiritual wickedness in high places” have more power than Christ; and they are but a few degrees removed from those who are goaded to madness by evil spirits and the followers of the devil.)

For they are in the very state, and under the very influence, of the devil; and the life of those wicked ones, whoever they be, in the devil, is a kind of madness. And for them to be utterly deranged, and altogether out of their

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. III. 2.</header>

minds, nothing is lacking except that, for the completion of their madness, a few steps be taken by the evil spirit who began that falling away from the light. In the falling away toward what is worse, a beginning is made by the devil as the prime mover, and this moves toward completion; just as, in growing perfect toward what is better, there is such a beginning from God. And as the latter ends in a heavenly enthusiasm, so the former ends in a devilish madness. But heavenly enthusiasm is wisdom and health in Christ; and those who have fallen away from him toward themselves, into a lapse toward darkness, and those who are pleased with their own ways because they are then forsaken by God and are immediately and easily led on by evil spirits to every kind of guilt, may be called Energumens—that is, Demoniacs. He who is not moved by the Spirit of God to be a child of God is necessarily moved by the spirit of the devil to be a child of the devil. For on either side, man is being drawn continuously to follow whichever he chooses. And toward whichever side he inclines, there are constantly present those who advance his steps when he is so drawn and who perfect him as he advances. It is man’s own part to choose where he will go, to listen to prompters (good or evil), and to follow the inspirations of one or the other. But toward whichever direction he tends, there are spiritual beings on all sides who guide him where he wishes.¹

<margin>Psalm 93:5</margin>
<margin>Matthew 7:20</margin>
When, therefore, the heavenly mysteries in Christ are being celebrated, no one may be present except those who are moved by the Spirit of God and who are fully alive. But the profane—as all Energumens may be called—should be driven far away from the sacred rites and mysteries. For, as David sang, “holiness becomes the house of God.” Accordingly, all these whose wicked lives have declared them to be in the power of the devil (for “by their fruits you shall know them”) used to be kept far away from the sacred rites in the early Church, so that this honor might not be

bestowed upon the devil, namely, that some of his subjects should be present at them.

<margin>Ezek. xxxiii. 11.</margin>
The Penitents were those who repented of this wandering and lost life, and who with a change of purpose had returned to the Church. These, in humble prostration at the feet of the Church, implored the mercy and grace of God, mourning their sins and seeking the penalties and punishments for their sins, so that they might publicly regret their errors as an example to all, and by temporal punishment might avoid eternal death and regain eternal life. The Church—which acts in the place of Christ, and which has whatever it looses or binds on earth loosed or bound in heaven, and which “has no pleasure in the death of the wicked, but that he turn from his way and live”—receives them lovingly again into the Church when they have finished the penance assigned to them and are sufficiently penitent. She cherishes them and, so far as possible, preserves them in that state. But meanwhile, as long as they are disciplining themselves in their allotted penance, they are considered to be not yet sufficiently purified; and along with the Catechumens and Energumens, they are driven far away and shut out.<note>1 “Dionysius Areopagita says, ‘That after the reading of the Old and New Testament, the learners of the faith before they were baptized, madmen, and those who were joined to penance for their faults, were shut out of the Church, and only those remained who would receive [communion].’ Chrysostom also witnesses that these three sorts were shut out from the Communion. Therefore Durant writes, ‘That the mass of the learners is from the Introit until after the Offertory, which is called *missa*, *misse*, or a sending out: Because, when the priest begins to consecrate the sacrament, the learners are sent out of the Church. The *miss* or sending out of the faithful is from the offering until after Communion; and is named *missa*, a sending out, because, when it is ended, then each member of the faithful is sent forth to his own business.’”—Guest to Sir Wm. Cecyl, in *Strype’s Annals of the Reformation* (1709), App. p. 38.</note>

§ 2. When these are excluded, and the gates of the temple are watchfully guarded by the door-keepers and porters so that no profane person may enter, then, each deacon and minister carrying out his own office, the chief deacons—along with the priests who are around the bishop at the altar—diligently wait upon him, and place on the altar the holy

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. III. 2.</header>

bread and the cup of blessing. Meanwhile, as the whole body of clergy chant together the praise and confession¹ of the sacred office, the bishop, having the bread and the cup now before him, utters a sacred prayer, which I believe to have been the Lord's Prayer. Then, after a blessing has been pronounced by the bishop on everyone together, they all in turn greet and kiss each other. Next, after reciting the mystic (names² of the saints), the bishop stands along with the priests, with washed hands, at the middle of the altar, closely surrounded by the priests and chosen deacons, and proceeds to utter the other things that tend toward the praise and confession of the sacrament, and at length, by the <margin>X of the</margin> sacramental words, to complete the divine and saving act.

When this is done, he displays the consecrated sacrament to them all with all reverence; and then, turning to the altar, he first partakes of it, and afterward invites <margin><unclear>him</unclear></margin> all the rest to partake and communicate in the same sacrament; so that in one sacrament, all may be made one with the bishop. When everyone together has partaken, the bishop concludes by giving thanks; understanding well the meaning of those sensible signs, and seeing <gloss>the idea of division</gloss> the reason for the whole.

---

<note>¹ It might seem as if two distinct portions of the Service were intended here, corresponding to the *Gloria in excelsis* and the *Credo* of the Roman Office. But the Greek words (which I provide, as the Latin version is not precise) are *proomologetheises hypo pantos tou tes ecclesias pleromatos tes catholices hymnologias*, and appear to signify only the singing of a hymn as a confession of faith by the whole assembled congregation before the placing of the bread and wine upon the altar.</note>
<note>² I am uncertain whether, by *mysticis verbis sanctorum*, Colet meant what I have provided, or "the mystic words of the holy books." In any case, it is clear what the custom referred to was. "Some time before they made an offering for the dead," writes Bingham, "it was customary in certain eras to recite the names of such eminent Bishops, Saints, or Martyrs as were specifically to be mentioned in this part of the Service. For this purpose, they had certain books which they called their holy Books, and commonly their Diptychs—from their being folded together—wherein the names of such persons were written so that the Deacon might recite them as occasion required during Divine Service. . . . It appears from this author [Dionysius] that these Diptychs were then read before the Consecration, immediately after the Kiss of Peace." —*Ecclesiastical Antiquities* xv. iii. § 17. A further account of the custom may be found in Rock's *Hierurgia* (1851), p. 477.</note>

<vocab>Ecclesiastical Hierarchy, sacrament, Lord's Prayer, Kiss of Peace, Diptychs, Dionysius, Colet, Bingham, Rock's Hierurgia</vocab>

To this, beyond doubt, all priests are obliged and bound whose part it is to perform the office of the divine sacrament: that they may have more understanding of that heavenly mystery than the people.

<margin>?</margin> In the primitive church, as St. Jerome¹ proves by good evidence, those whose office it was to make the Body of the Lord were the chief of all; and at first all, being equal by virtue of this office, were on a level in rank. <margin>college of presbyters</margin> For rank is known by office and action. The most excellent office, demanding the most excellent and perfect men, is the consecration of the Lord’s Body. <margin>Presbyters</margin> From the whole priesthood, none can be chosen or selected for a more excellent office than this. Still, from the number of the priests, though they were equal in office and rank, the first disciples and followers of the Apostles, immediately after the apostolic age, chose <margin>one?</margin> one person and placed him at the head to settle disputes, appease strifes, and end <margin><unclear>Aug. Episc</unclear></margin> contentions by his opinion and judgment, so that the Church might remain in harmony. To him, an authority was delegated by the universal Church, so that those who were at variance should abide by his decision. <margin>By</margin> And he is not so

### CHAPTER III.

#### PART III.

->*On the symbolic truth of the Holy Eucharist.*¹<-

First of all, chants and lessons from the <gloss>circle around "sacred writings"</gloss> sacred writings are heard by the people and by the whole Church together, so that they may learn <gloss>circle around "a holy life"</gloss> a holy life in the <gloss>scripts</gloss> <gloss>circle around "records"</gloss> records where it is taught. This is what St. Paul writes to the Ephesians; <margin>Eph. 5:18-20.</margin> instructing them to "be filled with the Spirit; speaking to one another in psalms, hymns, and spiritual songs, singing and making melody in your heart to the Lord, giving thanks always for all things to God the Father, in the name <margin>Col. 3:16.</margin> of our Lord Jesus Christ." And to the Colossians:—"Let the word of Christ dwell in you richly in all wisdom; teaching and admonishing one another in psalms, and

<meta>This page continues the symbolic contemplation of the Holy Eucharist, moving from the reading of sacred scriptures to the meaning of the communal participation in the bread and cup as established by Christ at the Last Supper.</meta>

hymns, and spiritual songs, singing with grace in your hearts."
(From this has come the custom of reading passages from the sacred scriptures in the Church, so that people may learn from them how to live well. For Holy Scripture is the teacher of life and the rule of truth.)

Likewise, the people and the unlearned multitude are rightly and appropriately reminded, by the presentation of one bread and one cup and by everyone sharing the same nourishment, that all, being fed and nourished by one, should grow together into one, in harmony and brotherly agreement, under God their Father, at the table of God.

They are also commanded to understand that, just as the bread is one and the cup is one and the same which they all taste, so they themselves also ought to be all one and the same among themselves, bound together in a union of charity, which is the "bond of peace."

For this Communion provides a solemn representation of that holy supper of the Lord with his disciples, in which he gave himself to them to eat, so that all might be united in him and, being joined together, might form one body with him.

(St. Matthew the Evangelist describes this hallowed and life-giving supper when he tells us that at the Passover feast Jesus set the bread and cup before his disciples.

<margin>Matthew 26:26-28.</margin>
For "as they were eating," he says, "Jesus took bread, blessed it, broke it, and gave it to the disciples, and said, 'Take, eat; this is my body.' And he took the cup, gave thanks, and gave it to them, saying, 'Drink all of you from it; for this is my blood of the new testament which is shed for many for the forgiveness of sins.'

What was given by Jesus Christ to his disciples is, in turn, distributed in the same way by all bishops to the faithful; so that, in memory of that supper, they may be united in Christ with those who were first united in Christ. In this way, the life-giving work that was begun may continue to advance, through the operation of the sacred mysteries, toward the fullness of Christ, in whom are both the dead and the living, and all who shall believe in the future.) For they are sustained in Christ by the Holy Communion and the heavenly food in the exalted company of God, being guests now not at the unclean table of demons, but at the spotless table of God.

It emphasizes that participating in these mysteries incorporates the faithful into the body of Christ, alongside both the living and the dead.</summary>

The Bishop acts the part of God as he burns incense at the altar, in the sight of God, with a sweet odor, which is a sign of the Almighty’s most fragrant love. For as God pours forth His sweet goodness abundantly, and Jesus His compassion, so that it may return again to Him along with the great salvation of mankind—to Him, I say, who, though remaining still within Himself, yet marvelously extends Himself to others, so that by His fragrant grace He may draw them together to Himself—just like Peter’s net, which, when let down into the sea, is brought back by him into the ship full of fish—in nearly the same manner, so that what is least may imitate what is greatest, the bishop and vicar of God, the head of the Church and its most vital part, communicates himself and his own divinity, or rather the godhead of Christ which is in him, bountifully and divinely to others, without in any way departing from himself or lessening his own majesty; so that as many as possible may be made godlike with him in Christ. This certainly the burning of incense, beginning from the altar, and proceeding from there through the whole temple, and returning to the place where it started, signifies in a sacred and solemn manner to the unlearned; that in this, if they are able, they may perceive that sweet-smelling grace <margin>bishop</margin> is diffused far and wide over all from the high place of God; in order that, being captivated by the fragrance of the heavenly sweetness, they may eagerly follow the grace<note>The metaphor here is a somewhat bold one; the idea being that as the outward senses of the worshiper follow the incense in its course from the Bishop at the altar, through the congregation, and back to the Bishop again, so his mind may be drawn by the grace which comes forth from God and may ascend back again, as it were, with that grace, to the Holy of Holies from which it proceeded. <margin>2 Timothy 2:19.</margin>
When the sacred bread is placed, covered, upon the altar along with the cup of blessing, those present salute each other in turn with a mutual kiss and recall the names of those who have departed in Christ. The mutual salutation of the living and the commemoration of the dead,<note>See above, p. 82.</note> which occurs during the presentation of that one bread of which all may partake, signify the unity in Christ of both the living and the dead. It represents their agreement, likeness, and uniform way of living according to the example of Christ—which all who worthily partake of His sacred rites profess to imitate. Moreover, while reciting the names of the saints, we call to mind how great was the virtue of those whom we remember, so that we may be stirred to a similar virtue by their example. In the few whose names are heard, the rest are implied—those whose names cannot be repeated due to their multitude. These are recited in certain hope, as if they were surely living; for they, as our Savior has testified, have “passed from death into life” and are held in the mind of Him who well “knows those who are His,” as it is written in the Book of Life. Upon the presentation of the Savior under the forms of bread and wine, the commemoration of the saints follows immediately, so that they, being united in Jesus, may be perceived as one with Him.

<margin>John 13:10.</margin>

that if any taint of sin, however slight, remains, it may be utterly scoured away,¹ and that the Bishop with those about him may in all sweetness and purity enter on the most holy rites, so as to purely and devoutly conduct and complete the divine office in the sacrament. <gloss>of concupiscence</gloss> For it is hard to live so chastely and uprightly that no tinge of passion, though slight, appears to cloud our brightness. Hence Jesus washed the extremities of St. Peter's feet. Hence the Bishop, when about to perform the sacred rites, washes the extremities of his hands; a custom derived from that washing of feet, so that even the remaining taint of sin, whatever it may be, on the tips of his fingers, may be removed by the water of grace. <margin>Ps. 26:6.</margin> At which act it is now the custom to repeat “I will wash my hands in innocence.” <margin><unclear>1 Rule of the Creed</unclear></margin> But no one is so innocent as not to have need to wash the tips of his fingers at least, if he is performing any sacred office; that is, to lay aside the slight passions from which it is hard to keep aloof, and the relics of sins that have been, as it were, rooted out.
