# CHAPTER IV.

## PARTS I. II.

->*On the consecration of the Chrism.*<-

IN all the Sacraments there is a derivation from the one to the many, from the single to the manifold, and from invisible unity to sensible signs; so that by these related principles men may be taken and led from multiplicity to unity. Thus it is clear that all the sacraments are appropriate means between God, who is one, and men, who are many; so that the many may be made godlike in God.

The consecration of the Chrism,<note>This rite occupies an important place in Dionysius and is placed by him on a level with the Holy Eucharist. His term for the sacred ointment, or Chrism, is *muron*; and he speaks of it as used in Baptism, in Confirmation (or the completion of Baptism), and in the consecration of the Altar. However, this actual reference to its use is very brief and occupies a small space in comparison with his meditation on it as symbolic of Christ. Below, in the Seventh Chapter, he speaks of the anointing after death; but nowhere, I believe, does Dionysius make any allusion to what is called Extreme Unction. In the Discourse attributed to Cyprian *On the Unction of the Chrism*, much is said about this Chrism and its consecration on the Thursday of Holy Week. One passage reminds us of what Colet writes below: "That just as Christ was named from the 'chrism,' because God anointed him with oil of singular excellence, so also his participants, as many as they are, may be sharers of both the anointing and the name, and be called Christians from Christ." In the later Latin Church, there were three kinds of ointment employed: "holy oil, chrism oil, and sick men's oil," which were kept in separate flasks in a vessel called a Chrismatory.—See Peacock's *English Church Furniture* (1866) p. 161 n.</note> which the priestly office employs for almost all its services, has nearly the same ceremonies as the Communion; namely, incense, psalms, lessons, and chants. During the consecration of it also, Catechumens, Apostates, Energumens, and Penitents are dismissed. The concealment and covering of the Chrism indicates and signifies the keeping secret in the religious

<summary>This page introduces the consecration of the Chrism, explaining how the sacraments act as a bridge between the unity of God and the multiplicity of humanity. It notes that the Chrism ceremony shares many rituals with the Holy Eucharist, including the dismissal of those not yet fully admitted to the mysteries.</summary>
<keywords>Chrism, Sacraments, Dionysius, Consecration, Holy Eucharist, muron, ritual symbolism, unity and multiplicity</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the consecration of the Chrism in Chapter IV, focusing on the symbolism of the ointment's fragrance as a representation of divine grace that must be kept secret and protected within the soul.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. IV. 1, 2.</header>

the mind influenced by divine inspirations; which ought neither to be inappropriately displayed nor recklessly publicized, lest, in that case, they lose their precious fragrance. The fragrance of the substance and the brightness of the divine grace ought alike to be shielded by the covering of a religious guardianship. For nothing is either more precious or more fragrant than the gift implanted by God into the mind of man. And if a man holds fast to this indwelling, the degrees of grace are intensified, so that his mind at length becomes not unlike the image of God. God depicts His own full image, if man will strive wholly and untiringly for it. But let him not make public the share of the divine nature which he has received, lest the scent of the fragrance of the heavenly gift grow weak and faint.

<margin>Mat. xxiii. 5.</margin>
<margin>Col. iii. 1–3.</margin>
<margin>Matth. v. 16.</margin>
By their own private right, divine things choose to be kept secret; for if they are revealed, they lose their divinity. For God does not will to communicate Himself to many, since there are not many to whom He can communicate Himself. If you make public His secret gifts, their brightness, beauty, power, and fragrance are gone; and you ought to conceal them so as not only to refrain from publishing wisdom and what you know by revelation, but even from "doing your works" publicly, according to our Savior's command, "to be seen by men." For as God is in secret, so are divine men withdrawn, secret, solitary, and inwardly self-contained, being widely separated from the multitude; and so is their whole wisdom, life, and works hidden, being seen by God and the angels, not by men. They "seek and set their affections on things above, where Christ sits at the right hand of God, not on things on the earth. They are dead, and their life is hidden in Christ." Hence they love solitude; and, like God, they desire to do all their good deeds in secret, so that God may be praised, not themselves; and that their works may "shine before men," not themselves, and "their Father who is in heaven may be glorified," not themselves. If you would be godlike, remain hidden, as God does; be manifest in heaven, and in heaven let your way of life be. If you are in the truth itself, you do not need sensible sacraments <note>Sacraments that can be perceived by the senses, such as the physical oil or bread.</note>; you possess the grace of God itself, which

<summary>This page explores the necessity of keeping divine grace and spiritual insights secret to preserve their "fragrance" and power. It emphasizes that truly godlike individuals live inwardly and solitary lives, hidden in Christ, seeking only the glory of God rather than human recognition.</summary>
<keywords>divine inspiration, fragrance, secrecy, image of God, solitude, hidden life, Matthew 5:16, Colossians 3:1-3, sensible sacraments</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the consecration of the Chrism in Chapter IV, specifically regarding how it represents the goal of all sacraments and the necessity of divine secrecy.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>CH. IV. 1, 2. ON THE HOLY CHRISM.</header>

...is the object of all sacraments. The monks in Egypt¹ once possessed this, even though they did not use all the sacraments we have for the purpose of being drawn toward God; because, since they were already drawn to God, they did not need them.

To help us understand that divine things should be secret and concealed, the Chrism, through which every priestly office is performed and fulfilled, is shrouded by wings. For the sacred Chrism is almost on a level with the Eucharist, and it is equal to it in use and necessity. In its consecration, there are the same solemn ceremonies by which men are also drawn through many colors toward the pure light itself—a light that is luminous to its very core and does not become colored when passing through dim signs. In the case of the Chrism, just as with the Eucharist (in which the truth of God is seen through signs by the physical eyes), the simple truth as it streams from the Sun of Righteousness is beheld by the spiritual eye. Those who cannot look at the sun itself—since the radiance of its powerful light blinds a vision made delicate by the shade—can nevertheless endure tempered colors.

The ointment and sacred Chrism I describe are composed of many varied and fragrant materials, so that men anointed with it may have a good and sweet scent, refreshing and pleasant to the nostrils. This Chrism is Jesus Christ himself, our heavenly anointing and fragrance; in him, a scent arising from a blend of all virtues breathes with unspeakable pleasantness upon spiritual men,² and refreshes

---

<note>¹ I do not know whether Colet is referring to anything more specific in this than the solitary asceticism in which the lives of the earliest monks were spent, which necessarily barred them from many religious rites. "Monachism certainly took its rise in Egypt," says Dr. Burton (*Lectures etc.* 1845, p. 227), who traces its origin to the ancient Therapeutæ. In the persecution under Decius, many fled to such retreats as the cavern St. Jerome describes (*Epist.* iii. 1) in the Thebaid, which in the days of Antony and Cleopatra had been the resort of counterfeiters.</note>

<note>² Compare with this figure what Ignatius writes to the Ephesians: "For this reason, our Lord received the anointing on his head, so that he might breathe incorruption upon his Church." *Propter hoc, unguentum* etc. (Ed. 1708) p. 53.</note>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. IV. 1, 2.</header>

<margin>
2 Cor. i. 21, 22.
1 John ii. 20, 27.
</margin>
the nostrils of their mind. This very Jesus, our Chrism, saturates us with himself; and anoints us with his own life-giving oil of gladness, so that we may be vigorous, strong, shining, and sweet. This is the oil about which St. Paul writes in his second letter to the Corinthians: "Now he who establishes us with you in Christ, and has anointed us, is God; who has also sealed us, and given the pledge of the Spirit in our hearts." It is this Spirit of Christ which is the true Chrism; and where that is, it breathes the sweetest of odors. This is the "anointing from the Holy One," which "teaches all things," as St. John writes: we are Christians in Christ, that is, anointed in anointing itself. God is all softness, gentleness, sweetness, pleasure, delight, flavor, fragrance, harmony, light, and exulting joy. Jesus, God and man, satisfies our spiritual senses with every pleasure; and through an excess of pleasure, he transports his bride, the soul, which unites with him, into ecstasy; so that, in the pleasure of God, it may be entirely pleasure—no longer merely feeling pleasure, but being pleasure itself, and instead offering pleasure to others. This softness filling the touch, flavor regaling the taste, fragrance delighting the smell, harmony soothing the ears, and light refreshing the eyes, is Jesus, our spouse, who is entirely delightful. He who stands nearer to him has the more fragrant odor. We should all be spreaders and followers of this fragrance, so that we may be like a sweet-scented temple of God, being sweet-scented ourselves; so that, having been made fragrant in Christ with a pleasant odor, the whole temple may be redolent of God. And so our Chrism is Christ, with whom we have been saturated and made fragrant.

<margin>
Isai. vi. 3.
</margin>
The wings of twelve Seraphs cover Christ and the Chrism, and guard them from the profane. Those spirits themselves also first, and without ceasing, derive their fragrance from Jesus. Therefore they are portrayed around Christ and the Chrism. All things come to us in succession from Jesus through the angels, who constantly and unceasingly refresh themselves in the fullness of Jesus, and rejoice without interruption, singing evermore *Holy, Holy, Holy*; because of unspeakable joy, they are unable to restrain themselves; thus they rejoice most greatly in the highest.

<summary>The author describes Jesus as the true Chrism who saturates the soul with divine sweetness and pleasure, transforming the believer into a fragrant temple of God. # CH. IV. 1, 2. ON THE HOLY CHRISM. 99

<margin>Ps. xlv. 7.</margin>
When you read of twelve Seraphs' wings, understand two Seraphs;¹ the six wings of one and the six wings of the other conceal what is deep and profound, reveal what is midway, and incline towards the things of God that are midway. They in turn cry aloud to one another, so that they may in turn impart glory. They stand by the anointing Jesus, so that we may understand that Christ, by being incarnate, did not withdraw from his own majesty and from the angels; and that he who was anointed and sanctified “above his companions,” was the very one who anoints and sanctifies. Christ Jesus is the very anointing which the angels adore; and therefore this is rightly used in all sacred rites. To the spiritual nostrils, it is such as the word of God is to the ears, and the Eucharist to the eyes.

* hears God in the Word of the Gospel.
* smells God in the Sacred Unguent.
</image-desc>

<image-desc>
In these three, Jesus Christ is the very sacrament of the:
* Eucharist.
* Gospel.
* Anointing.

<margin>Rom. vi. 3, 4.</margin>
The signs of the cross also, made with the sacred unguent, are always a sacrament to us of Christ and our Chrism being crucified and dead for us. In the baptistery, the oil is poured on the baptized in the form of a cross, by which it is signified that the Anointed himself was crucified and died for those who are baptized in him; and that the baptized die on the Cross of Christ, and sink into his death, so that they may come forth to his life. This indeed is what St. Paul writes to the Romans:—“Do you not know, brothers, that as many of us as were baptized into Christ Jesus, were baptized into his death” and cross, who was in the flesh for us, so that we in the flesh might lay aside our sinful life, and be sunk in the waters of his death, “being buried with him by baptism into death.” In this baptism, by faith in the cross of Christ, we are as it were crucified along with him,

<meta>This page begins the introduction to Chapter 5, focusing on Holy Orders. It explores the relationship between the angelic and earthly hierarchies and details the four traditional senses of Scriptural interpretation.</meta>

### CH. V. I. INTROD. TO HOLY ORDERS.

His rays, by means of the angelic hierarchy, cast a shadow of that same hierarchy upon the earth. At length, in his own good time, the Sun himself descended, to brighten with colors that shadow of the angelic hierarchy by his own immediate presence and to shed upon it the light of his truth; so that from there, a colored reflection of the angelic city might also be more clearly seen upon earth. And finally, when the shadow is removed, the unclouded light will one day appear in the glory of Christ, <margin>Col. iii. 3, 4.</margin> "who is our life," hidden in the heavens; which "shall appear, when he shall have appeared, in glory." Meanwhile, <margin>Rom. viii. 22, 23.</margin> "the whole creation groans and labors in pain together until now, waiting for the adoption" of God.

**The Hierarchy is typified by:**
*   **The Heavenly:** Anagoge, namely, the more hidden depth.
*   **The Christian:** Allegory—The whole.
*   **The Individual:** The Moral sense—The part.

**The Mosaic Hierarchy is wholly symbolic in its acts of:**
*   The Heavenly truth.
*   The Christian image.
*   The righteous life of a Christian man.

**The act is a shadow of three things:**
*   Light.
*   The Whole.
*   The Part.

From this order, anyone may perceive the reasons for the four senses¹ in the Old Law, which are customary in the

---

<note>
¹ From the tenor of Colet's response to Erasmus on the subject of Christ's agony in the Garden, it has been thought that he rejected the theory of fourfold senses much more decidedly than would appear from this passage. As the subject is an interesting one, I will endeavor to state it clearly for the reader, though at the risk of being long-winded.
From the *Bibliotheca* of Sixtus Senensis (Ed. 1610, p. 134), a full view of the method may be gained as it was regarded by its advocates. He says that there are two chief lines of Scriptural interpretation: (1) the Historical (which Colet calls the Literal), and (2) the *Mystic*. This latter, again, is subdivided into the Allegorical, Tropological (or Moral), and *Anagogical*. Sometimes, he argues, the same word or phrase may bear all four meanings in different passages. For example, take the term "waters." In Gen. 1:9, "Let the waters under the heaven," etc., it is in its literal sense. In Isaiah 43:2, "When you pass through the waters, I will be with you," it is in the *moral* sense. In Ezekiel 36:25, "Then will I sprinkle clean water upon you" (understood as baptism), it is in the allegorical sense. And lastly, in Jeremiah 2:13, "They have forsaken me, the fountain of living waters," it is in the anagogical or elevating sense;
</note>

<summary>This page describes how the earthly hierarchy reflects the angelic hierarchy through the light of Christ and explains the four traditional levels of Scriptural interpretation: literal, allegorical, moral, and anagogical. It contrasts the symbolic "shadow" of the Mosaic Law with the truth revealed in the Christian life.</summary>
<keywords>angelic hierarchy, Holy Orders, Christ, Mosaic Hierarchy, four senses of Scripture, Allegory, Anagoge, Tropology, Colet, Erasmus, Sixtus Senensis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the four senses of Scriptural interpretation (literal, allegorical, anagogic, and moral) as applied to the Church and the Old Law, providing historical context and opposing scholarly views on this hermeneutic method.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. V. I.</header>

Church. The literal sense is when the actions of the men of ancient times are related. When you think of the image—even of the Christian Church which the Law foreshadows—then you grasp the allegorical sense. When you are raised aloft, so as to conceive of the reality from the shadow that both represent, then the *anagogic* sense dawns upon you. And when from signs you observe the instruction of the individual man, then everything has a moral tone for you.

In the writings of the New Testament, except when it pleased the Lord Jesus and his Apostles to speak in parables, as Christ often does in the gospels, and Saint John 

<note>
by which, that is, our thoughts are raised from the earthly to the heavenly. Mirandola goes to great lengths in this direction. Thus he says in a passage of his *Heptaplus* that the Waters above the Firmament, the Firmament itself, and the Waters under the Firmament, signify the three Hierarchies of Angels (Op. 1601, p. 18). Erasmus, who may fairly be classed as one of the same school in this respect, goes so far as to say that we might as well read the story of Livy as the Book of Judges, or many other parts of the Old Testament, if we leave out of sight their allegorical meaning (*Enchiridion*, 1523, leaf g). Savonarola in his sermons, besides the literal interpretation, "divided almost every passage in the Bible under four other heads, namely, *Spiritual, Moral, Allegorical, Anagogical.*" (Villari's Hist. tr. by Horner, i. 114.) So also Bishop Longland, a friend of Colet's, when preaching before the University of Oxford on the occasion of Henry VIII's founding a new College there in 1525, took for his text Proverbs 9:1, 2; and explained the words "She has also furnished her table," to represent the four dishes, or courses, of History, Morality, Allegory, and Anagogy, which Wisdom set forth on the table of her Scriptural banquet. (*Concio*, 1525, fol. 23).

These extracts may serve to show the principle as it was maintained by some at that period, and those too, men whose opinion Dean Colet would respect.

The opposite view may be taken as it is held by a learned scholar of Dean Colet's school, Whitaker, the opponent of Bellarmine. In his *Disputation on Holy Scripture* (Parker Soc. 1849, p. 405) he says: "As to those three spiritual senses, it is surely foolish to say that there are as many senses of Scripture as the words themselves may be transferred and accommodated to bear. For although the words may be *applied* and accommodated tropologically, allegorically, anagogically, or any other way; yet there are not therefore various senses, various interpretations, and explanations of Scripture, but there is only one sense, and that is the literal, which may be variously accommodated, and from which various things may be collected." He then goes on to explain even the well-known text in Galatians 4:24 ("Which things are an allegory") on this principle;
</note>

<summary>This page defines the four senses of Scripture (literal, allegorical, anagogic, and moral) and provides a detailed historical note on the debate surrounding these interpretations among scholars like Erasmus, Savonarola, and Whitaker.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on scriptural interpretation, specifically how the literal and mystical senses coexist in the New Testament and the writings of theologians like Colet, Erasmus, and Thomas Aquinas.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>CH. V. 1. INTROD. TO HOLY ORDERS.</header>

throughout in the Revelation), all the rest of the discourse, in which either the Saviour teaches his disciples more plainly, or the Apostles instruct the Churches, has the sense that appears on the surface; nor is one thing said and another meant, but the very thing is meant that is said, and the sense is wholly literal. Still, because the Church of God is figurative, always conceive of an *anagoge* in what you hear in the doctrines of the Church; the meaning of which will not cease until the figure has become the truth. From this, moreover, conclude that where the literal sense is, the allegorical is not always along with it; but, on the other hand, where there is an allegorical sense, the literal sense is always underlying it.¹

---

(although he is driven to admit that “the whole entire sense is not in the words taken strictly, but part in the type and part in the transaction itself. In either of these, considered separately and by itself, only part of the meaning is contained; and by both taken together, the full and perfect meaning is completed.”)

It seems to me that this last admission concedes as much as Colet assumed in the guarded sentence with which he ends the paragraph, and that there may be a middle ground on which the contending parties could unite. The keen mind of Thomas Aquinas appears to me to detect the truth when he writes: “The author of Holy Scripture is God, in whose power it is not only to adapt words to have a meaning (as man also can do), but facts themselves as well. And so, while in all other sciences words are significant of things, the science of Theology has this peculiar property: that the very things implied by words have themselves also a significance” (*Summa, Pars I. Art. X. Conclusion*). Without going so far as to maintain, as some expositors do, that the very names of places, such as Bethesda, Bethsaida, and the like, were given through the workings of Providence to harmonize with events afterward to be performed there, I still am unable to think that the meaning of such an act as the lifting up of the Serpent in the wilderness is exhausted in the record of it as an historical fact. Doubtless the only meaning of the writer who narrates it is the simple literal meaning which his words bear; but I would look on the act itself as a word spoken in the then unknown language of God, whose meaning became revealed in later times.

On such a principle as this, I think it possible that what Colet wrote to Erasmus against the scholastic system of interpretation may be reconciled with what he lays down in the text.

---

<note>¹ “For the historical sense, like an underlying foundation, does not exclude but supports the mystical sense.” — Erasmus, *Exposition on Psalm 86*.</note>

**Day beneath the Sun.**—The day is that *true* day of the Lord, when the Sun of Righteousness enlightens all things.

In Night {
* Stars shining in heaven, under Christ; namely, saints enlightened by the Sun.—Conduct in heaven.
* Stars shining in the water, under the Law; a watery image of the Church.

Night of darkness, completely without light, before the Law.

For while we are living here, in the hours of morning and forenoon, we are journeying toward the midday sun to dine with our Lord. Starting from a northern region, we are traveling toward the south.

The Day {
* Of the Lord in its fullness, when all clouds are dispersed and all enemies subdued, and God shall be all in all.
* Begun in Christians: people beholding the rising of the Sun of Righteousness.

The Night {
* Prophets, shining as stars in heaven; understanding the meaning of the symbols.
* The people, shining in the water; clinging to the symbols.
* The Gentiles, completely without light, wandering “in the vanity of their mind.”

# CH. V. 1. INTRODUCTION TO HOLY ORDERS.

<page-num>109</page-num>

<margin>Pico</margin>
Moses¹ truly received from God upon Mount Sinai not only the Law, which he left to those who followed him written down in five books, but also its hidden and true explanation and interpretation, and its spiritual meaning when revealed. God commanded him not to commit this to writing in any way, but to disclose and entrust it to Joshua the son of Nun only; who should in turn communicate it to the chief priests, who were likely to maintain a sacred, strict, and religious silence. <margin><unclear>Pico</unclear></margin> But he was commanded by God to publish the written law to the people; in which, through a simple history, there is shown at once the power of God—now his wrath against the wicked, again his mercy toward the good, and his justice toward all everywhere—so that the people might acknowledge and revere a powerful, good, and righteous God. In this Law the multitude is also trained, by wholesome and divine precepts, to live well and happily, and in the service of true religion. And thus, in the written Law, it is seen only how the goodness and power of God are visible in so vast a series of events; how each one should live; and in what manner one should worship God. But as for those more hidden mysteries, and those that lay concealed beneath the rugged bark of the Law and its rough covering of words—namely, those secrets of the high divinity—if they had been disclosed to the foolish multitude by the men of old; what else, I ask, would it have been, than to

ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. V. I.

*fleshly, physical, and sensory symbolism.* For as long as we are human, even the most spiritual among us cannot entirely do without symbols. The lower Hierarchy resembles the higher in its own measure, as much as it can. All things that exist in the heavenly Hierarchy also exist in the Christian Hierarchy in a figurative manner; and all things in the Christian Hierarchy existed previously in the Legal Hierarchy in a shadowy manner.

| Hierarchy | Nature and Characteristics | Position |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Heavenly | Angelic. Enlightened. | **Higher.** |
| Christian | Angelic; enlightened.—The spirit; God.—Heaven. <br> Human; shadowy.—The flesh; man.—Earth. | **Middle.** |
| Legal | Human. Shadowy. | **Lower.** |

<margin>Rom. xiii. 10.</margin>
<gloss><unclear>seven orders</unclear></gloss>
We may consider, therefore, that there are three Hierarchies in the Christian Church, and these are furthermore threefold; so that there are nine Orders among us, just as there are among the angels, in imitation of the angelic ranks—the shadows of which came before in the literal Law. The entire purpose of these is the work of sanctification, so that men may be made like God, who is holy; and among them are various classes of holy people: the first and highest are those who sanctify; the last are those who are sanctified; and others, midway between these, are at once sanctified and sanctifying. But all are ministers of God for the spread of divinity, for the eventual full sanctification of mankind. All strive to imitate the angelic Orders, so that the first triad may purify, the second enlighten, and the third perfect and complete; that the whole Church may be purified, enlightened, and perfected in God. 

Purification consists in putting off whatever was our own, even our greatest reasons for self-confidence, so that we may turn to God and hope in Him. Illumination consists of faith in what is revealed, which is our wisdom. Perfection consists of the fullness of charity, which is “the fulfilling of the law” and of righteousness. The highest purify, enlighten, and perfect; the lowest are purified, enlightened, and perfected, having now been initiated into the sacred rites, baptized, and confirmed. The intermediate ones also both receive and bestow purification; being already enlightened themselves, they enlighten the purified for their completion. Com-

<summary>This page describes the structure of the Christian Hierarchy as a bridge between the Legal and Heavenly Hierarchies, organized into nine orders that mirror the angelic degrees. It outlines the three-stage process of spiritual progress: purification from self-reliance, illumination through faith, and perfection through charity.</summary>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the nine orders of the Christian Hierarchy, detailing how the higher ranks facilitate the spiritual growth of the lower ranks through purification, illumination, and perfection.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>

<header>CH. V. I. INTROD. TO HOLY ORDERS.</header>

Completion and purification are the work of the highest in the Church, in whom there ought to be the greatest power to establish men in hope, to illuminate them in faith, and lastly, to perfect them in love. Through them—namely, by those leaders in the Church—the same work is performed as by the intermediate ranks, and through the intermediate ones, the same as by the lowest. For among all of them alike, men’s purification, illumination, and perfection are carried out together. For these three operations, produced as they are by a single cause, cannot be separated; but the same acts are performed by the intermediate ranks as by the lowest, only on a more glorious scale, and finally by those last and highest ranks on the most glorious scale of all. 

The whole of this divine working among the heavenly beings tends toward their assimilation to God and toward the representation of the Trinity—its unity, beauty, and goodness. Hence, there are three operations: purification, illumination, and perfection. The effect of purification is unity, simplicity, power, constancy, and stability; which in them is, as it were, a single faculty by which they stand firm in God. The effect of illumination is a fair variety, truth, order, beauty, wisdom, and clarity; all of these terms share a certain identity of meaning, and in the angels, they signify a multifaceted knowledge of the divine word. Lastly, the effect of perfection is goodness, mercy, love, perfection,<note>“The effect of perfection is . . . perfection.” It should be noted that this imprecise expression exists in the original Latin.</note> and charity. 

In us also, these operations have their proper effects, modeled after the likeness of the unity, truth, and goodness that are in the angels. For our unity, which is the goal aimed at by our purification, is hope, which is our simplicity and stability. Our truth is faith in the all-encompassing truth that was in Jesus Christ. Our goodness and perfection is a love of what is good. Every office and function in the Church of Christ is directed toward these results. And among them, we may conceive of three kinds of those who hope, three of those who believe, and three of those who love. "Now," says St. Paul, "having been made free from sin and having become servants to God, we have our fruit leading to holiness, and the end is everlasting life."

In Dionysius, three ecclesiastical orders are named: those of Bishops, Priests, and Deacons.<note>The terms in the original are *Hierarchai*, *Hiereis*, and *Leitourgoi*.</note> The office of the Bishops is the most extensive, possessing all the power of the lower orders and having, moreover, its own proper duty, which is to make the offering of the Eucharist. From this, it appears that Dionysius calls those "Bishops" whom the later Church calls "Priests," and generally calls those "Priests" who are now called "Deacons." The office of the Priests, according to him, is to initiate into the sacred rites those who have been purified by the ministers and to provide those things which are customary, but to leave the setting forth of the mysteries to the Bishop. For he would have the ministers sift and separate men from the world and from themselves, as if to strip and divest them entirely of their old habits. This is so that they may have nothing to hinder them from being illuminated by the sacraments; instead, having the dust shaken and wiped off, they may be as pure as a fresh-scoured tablet, now fit to have the heavenly image depicted upon them. When they have been prepared in this way by the ministers, they are offered to the priests. When the priests discern that they have no spot and no trace of their old foulness, and are most fully prepared 
<margin>1 Corinthians 15:49</margin>
to be fashioned and formed in Christ—so that "as they have borne the image of the earthly, so they may also bear the image of the heavenly"—the priest depicts that image upon them, as it were, with the sacraments. He initiates them also into the most holy symbols, with which, at least for the faithful,

All completion is the responsibility of the Bishop, who is considered complete himself. By the deacons and ministers, men are brought in purity into the Church; by the priests, they are advanced with enlightening sacraments; by the Bishop, they are perfected with perfecting mysteries. The Bishop, skilled in all the sacred rites and a contemplator of heavenly things, duly orders the Church under him, so that everyone within it may perform some divine work in imitation of God and the heavenly spirits. For, as Dionysius teaches, every action in the Church on the part of everyone ought to imitate the action of God. For, as he also says,<note>“Because, however, the priestly distinctions are images of divine actions,” etc.—Ch. 5, Part 1, § 7.</note> the functions of priests are symbols of the divine workings. For the society of the Church is founded on earth by Christ after the pattern of the heavenly hierarchy of angels, who unceasingly imitate God; and in Christ, all things are arranged by the office of the Bishop. The Bishop represents the role of God and Christ upon earth, to the end that everyone in the Church may diligently occupy himself to the best of his power in some divine work for the common good of the whole society; so that he may be recognized as one sound and perfect member in the mystical body of Christ.

## CHAPTER V.

### PART II.

->*On the Consecration of Bishops, and Ordaining of Priests and Deacons.*<-

WHEN someone is being consecrated a Bishop, he prostrates himself before the altar, and the Bishop’s right hand and the Gospels¹ are laid upon his head. There is an invocation of the Trinity over him; he is signed with the cross, named by the whole priesthood, and kissed. According to ancient custom, the same ceremonies take place at the ordination of a priest, with the exception of placing the Gospels upon his head. The same applies to a deacon, except that he bends only one knee when submitting himself for consecration. <margin>Pencil mark resembling a tick</margin> Only the Bishop bears the Gospels on his head. Once consecrated, everyone is invited to the communion of the Eucharist.

---

¹ This custom seems to have been practiced in all the ancient Churches. In the *Apostolical Constitutions*, two deacons have the duty of holding the Gospels over the head of the person being consecrated as a Bishop; from this, Vasquez infers that this ceremony—unlike the laying on of hands—is not an essential part of the Sacrament. His argument is provided in the notes of Corderius. The ancient form of the ordination of priests is thus gathered by Bingham from the Canon of the Council of Carthage and from Dionysius:—"The person to be ordained knelt before the Bishop at the altar; and the Bishop, laying his hand upon his head, consecrated him with a holy prayer and then signed him with the sign of the cross; after which the Bishop and the rest of the clergy gave him the kiss of peace. The author of the *Constitutions* also speaks of the imposition of hands and prayer, but nothing more. From this, we may reasonably conclude that the words which the Roman Church considers the most necessary and essential part of a priest's ordination—namely, *Receive power to offer sacrifice to God, and to celebrate Mass for both the living and the dead*—were not in any of the ancient forms of consecration."—*Eccl. Ant. Bk. ii. c. xix. § 17.*

Now, so that we may state the meaning of these visible signs, and so that our spiritual eye, as well as our bodily eye, may perceive its proper objects, this first approach to the Bishop and prostration at the altar tells and warns us of subjection to God and to His Bishop. For bending the knee is a sign of subjection and obedience; and by the use of this term, St. Paul, in his Epistle to the Philippians, affirms that all things are subject to Jesus, saying: “Therefore God also has highly exalted Him, and given Him a name which is above every name: that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of things in heaven, and things in earth, and things under the earth. Therefore, my beloved,” be obedient. There is, and ought to be, in the Church this subjection of every inferior person to a higher one for his own sanctification to God in Christ; in whom also resides the supremacy of the higher for the sanctification of the lower, under whatever name they rule—whether among the people as fathers, masters, or husbands. This is the teaching of St. Paul in his Epistle written to the Ephesians and in the one to the Colossians, in which he enjoins subjection and obedience on sons, servants, and wives; which we must understand in all cases to mean with a view toward their sanctification by the former. Therefore, St. Paul notably uses and enforces the expression “in the Lord” in all these instances. To the Ephesians it is: “Let the husbands love their wives, as Christ loved the Church, and let them give themselves for their wives, that they may sanctify them with the washing of grace by the word of life; that they may present them to themselves as glorious wives, not having spot or wrinkle or any such thing, but holy and without blemish.” The laying of the Bishop’s hands upon his head sets forth that

Oh, this wicked generation! These abandoned principles! This madness of princes! This blindness and folly of ecclesiastics! Unless Christ has pity on His Church, death, which is already almost at the door, will seize everyone. For how can that which is managed with destructive plans and murderous hands endure? The gracious Jesus, ascribing nothing to Himself, humbly referred all things to the Father.

God will one day repay them according to their works. But let me now return to the matter of which I was speaking.

In the common salutation and kiss given to him who is elected, there is implied the sense of likeness, love, and joy felt by all for the one thus made like themselves, and by him also for those to whom he is made like.

But what is the meaning of laying the holy Gospels on the head of the Bishop at his consecration? It is this: that the Bishop should keep in his head and in his heart all things sacred and divine, and should be by far the wisest of all in heavenly things, and far the most excellent in every virtue and holy act, after the pattern of Christ set forth to us in the Gospels. In a manner, he points to Christ when he receives the holy Gospels upon his head, so that, as far as he can, he should show forth Christ within him in his life through sanctity and goodness, and by the wisdom of His truth and religion.

The Deacon, bending one knee, receives the sacrament of the purified; the Priest, bending both, receives that of the purified and the enlightened; the Bishop, besides the bending of both knees, has the holy Gospels placed on his head as a sacrament of purification, enlightenment, and perfection; because he rightly

Thus does the whole system of the Church—through purification, illumination, and perfection in Christ—labor for man’s steadfast simplicity, wise order, and perfect goodness, after the glorious example of the angels. This is so that, above the chaos of confusion and of the world, there may arise a bright array of some simple and perfect men in God, to be a city set on a hill, the light of the world, and the salt of the earth. These are such as may shine beneath Christ, their sun, with faith, hope, and charity, and may enlighten and give life to the world.

But alas! Smoke and foul blackness have now for a long time been rising upward in such dense volume from the valley of darkened men as to nearly overwhelm the light of that city. So now churchmen, shrouded in darkness and not knowing where they go, have foolishly blended and confused themselves with everyone; so that in the world again there is nothing more confused than the mass of humanity. In this world, the seal that Christ stamped upon it has been almost erased and destroyed by the indiscriminate jostling of mankind in this universal disorder. I beseech you, merciful Jesus, by your almighty power, to quickly scatter the darkness in your Church and restore order and peace!

Thus, against the Apostates on the one side, there are the Catechumens of the Church; against the Energumens on the one side—whom the enemies vex so that they may fall—are the Church's Penitents, whom her ministers are purifying so that they may stand. In truth, the Church labors with ceaseless toil to preserve what is her own and to regain what was recently hers—meaning the Apostates and Energumens—just as, on the other hand, the Adversary strives to retain what is and was his own, namely the Catechumens and Penitents.) But because, by our Savior's testimony, "the spirit is willing, but the flesh is weak," therefore at times the flesh is overcome by the spirit, darkness by light, death by life, and the Devil by God; through our Lord Jesus Christ, to whom "all things shall be subdued, so that God may be all in all."

The Church of God, which is situated on a hill above the world in pure air and a bright atmosphere, is also supplied from the valley of this world's misery with the material of its own happiness. For the Church, through its lower portion (which is masculine and active), raises up the higher and more passive portion of the world; (much as the rays of the sun refine and thin out the surface portions of water by their heat and so raise them on high, so that the sun may first fashion for itself a body, as it were. This body, though coarser and more physical than the spiritual part of the Church, is nevertheless more spiritual than the worldly body as a whole. From this body in turn, and from the more material part of the Church when it is sound and healthy—I mean from the highest and clearest part of it, and from among those who have now almost become spiritual, who are, as it were, the purer, clearer, and more vital blood of the Church—from among such men, I say, in the passive portion of the Church, the most advanced are drawn into a spiritual state, so that they may eventually become active portions and spirits. But at first, they are only purifying spirits, whose whole duty and office consists of the purification, renewal, and support of the body—that is, the humbler and more material portion of the Church. For between the body and God, who is the soul of the Church, come spiritual men as the agents and workers of all. (Just as in man, between his soul and body, there intervene pure, subtle, bright, and fiery spirits generated from the heat of the heart and the more refined blood; so in like manner between God and the Church of assembled men, there are some of the assembled men themselves, from the clearer portion of the Church and its more divine blood, so to speak. These are spiritual men, begotten of the warmth and love of God, who stand midway...

...between God and the rest of mankind, depending on God through them. And moreover, just as the spirits that come between man’s soul and body are of a threefold kind—for some are natural, others vital, and others animal <note>I use the word here in its strict sense, as Johnson explains it: "Animal functions, distinguished from natural and vital, are the lower powers of the mind, as the will, memory, and imagination."</note>; of which the last are chiefly subservient to the soul, the vital to the senses, and the natural to generation, nutrition, growth, evacuation, restoration, healing, and suchlike processes in the more physical part of man—so, after almost the same manner, there are in Christ, between God and the portion formed by the people, three kinds of spiritual men. Some of these, like the animal spirits, are the special servants of God, the Church’s soul; some chiefly tend the lower part of the Church, to repair, nourish, foster, increase, cleanse, and preserve it; some, midway between these, are the ministers of the senses, as it were, and enlighten them to perceive the spiritual properties of the sacraments in the Church. The first in order <note>That is, the first when counting upwards; in reality the lowest.</note>, which are nearer to the fleshly portion of the Church, give life to the flesh by their purifying virtue, washing away men’s sinful mortal nature, so that they may live by hope in God. The second, who spiritualize the senses, enlighten those who are purified and hoping in God; so that with faith they may have a perfect sense of the sacraments of the Church, and every sacred symbol, and rejoice in them with the fullest belief. The third and highest are those who are intent upon God with all their affections, and have a clear understanding of the simple meaning of each sense. These take such as have by faith had a sense of the symbols and sacraments; and as many of them as come to them in a fit and ripe state they make contemplators of the simple meanings of the mysteries and objects of sense, according to their capacity; in order that, what they have long experienced through the senses under priests, they may now, under the Bishop, understand with the most perfect love; and being filled with the mysteries, so far as they are capable, may be made perfect.

For light is an ethereal and unsubstantial thing, unless it is intensified, as it were, with heat; and the sight of things perceptible to the senses is almost empty and vain, unless men are filled with a love of the mysteries that are made intelligible. By the vision of the mysteries, by love and worship, all things are completed and perfected. Therefore the highest ones, who perfect, are the Bishops; the intermediate, who enlighten, are the Priests; the lowest, who purify, are the Deacons. Those who are purified so as to have a single hope in God, that they may support themselves on him, and in hope may now breathe again in him, and—being born again in a new creation—may now [become] an entirely new creature <note>This may have been what Colet meant; but I think the repetition of "creatura nova" in the Latin, taken along with the occurrence of the gap in the text, indicates some error in transcribing.</note>; those, I say, are in the lowest degree and vestibule, as it were, of the Church. They are as its bones: thick and solid, possessing but little feeling in themselves, but steady in hope, depending on God in all that they do in the world for the support of the higher Church.

<margin>Psalm 24:1</margin>
These are in truth purveyors of the necessities of life, ministers of no learned understanding, but receiving that which is supplied by a gracious God and bountiful nature, and ministering that supply to others, so as to be ministers in temporal things to the ministers of spiritual things; for all things are God's. No one has anything of his own to give; no one possesses what he has not received. "The earth is the Lord's, and the fullness thereof." All things, temporal as well as spiritual, are rightfully his who created them all. What is temporal is of the earth; what is spiritual is from heaven. The humbler and more earthly portion of the Church is the minister of temporal things, by the help of God and in a simple manner; the heavenly and more exalted portion bestows what is spiritual, as a minister of the great bounty of God. These latter are ministers to the physical and duller portion of the church for their eternal life, liberally affording them spiritual food; while the humbler portion, depending in hope on God, and as—

<summary>This page defines the ecclesiastical hierarchy, identifying Bishops as perfecters, Priests as enlighteners, and Deacons as purifiers, while comparing the laity to the "bones" of the Church who provide for its temporal needs. It emphasizes that all resources, whether spiritual or earthly, belong to God and are distributed through his ministers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ecclesiastical hierarchy, shifting focus from the spiritual roles of the clergy to the essential temporal roles of the lower members of the Church and how they support the spiritual body.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>CH. VI. I.    INTROD. TO INITIATION.</header>

...assiduously gathering from the earth the means to feed the Church, ministers temporal life to the more spiritual portion. Thus, by mutual services and benefits, the Church—while militant here, and while partly temporal, and partly and chiefly spiritual—is sustained on each hand, in both the higher and lower regions, in the riches of God, both temporal and spiritual. The servants and ministers of God religiously exert themselves to sustain it in each kind of life, temporal and spiritual; the higher portion receiving temporal life from the lower, and the lower receiving spiritual life and strength from the higher. Thus, the whole Church, seeking only what is necessary in this world, and doing so without excessive worry and only for the passing day, may strive for spiritual life here and for eternal life hereafter. In this lowest part of the Church are the Catechumens, as they are called, who are now under instruction; the Penitents who have been recalled; the Energumens <note>Those possessed by spirits or under intense spiritual struggle.</note> who have been exorcized; and the Apostates who have been reclaimed. It is the task of these individuals, through physical work and labor among the earthly bounties of God, to earn the physical sustenance of the Church, so that they may engage in earthly matters in all simplicity and truth. They are now ministers of God in these terrestrial things for the refreshment of the perishable and physical part of the Church. These are the ones whom St. Paul, in his 

<margin>1 Cor. xii. 23.</margin>
first Epistle to the Corinthians, when recommending harmony to the whole Church, compares to the less honorable members of the body; which, in proportion as they are less attractive than others, are all the more necessary, and receive greater care for their protection from injury. From among this sort of men, if at any time any of those who form the Church's higher and clearer region have at length, through the diligent labor of the deacons, become more godlike—every stain of sin being thoroughly washed away, so as now to be judged by the discerning deacons to be open and transparent, and capable of receiving a measure of light—they are then presented to the priests, so that they may be filled by them with fuller light and shine with greater faith. The things perceived by faith are now offered to their view so that, being now purified, they may discern with an unclouded eye...

What the Bishop is among spiritual persons, such are the Monks—single and solitary—among the laity; and just as the priests are among the one, such are those who are numbered among the holy people among the other. Such, lastly, as are the Deacons among the one, such among the other are those who are under the hands of the purifiers.

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. VI. 2, 3.</header>

## CHAPTER VI.

### PARTS II. III.

->*On the Consecration of the Monk.*<-

The monk originates from the purest portion of the people, being now wholly single and at one within himself, and altogether and thoroughly given up to the one God, as far as anyone of the people can be given up and surrendered.<note>1) “Strange inversion! that gives the largest share of heavenly favor to one who lives for himself, and struggles with himself alone; that allots to a monk, in the esteem of God and man, a place higher than to him who builds his own good upon the good of others.” Wordsworth, *Ecclesiastical Sketches*.</note>

Just as those who are enlightened by baptism have their own rite of consecration, so that they, the holy people, may behold the sacred emblems; so those of the people who are made complete in unity, and raised to another degree of perfection, have a signing of that event with ceremonies. This is so that an outward and perceptible form may be a reminder and token of spiritual progress. Therefore, in order that any monk may make profession of the unity which he has lately attained and his undivided principle of living, he goes to someone of the priestly order (who seem to me to have held the position in the early Church which Deacons do with us), and stands behind him before the altar, with his mind upraised toward God; one, single, and solitary. After the priest has given thanks to God, he turns toward the man and hears him renounce forever, by a most rigid vow, all division and dissipation of life. He hears him promise to center himself on the One, and to keep a single eye so that his whole body may be full of light; to strive toward one goal only; and to serve God alone. Upon this great profession, he receives the sign of the holy cross; so that what he declares himself to renounce—

<summary>This page describes the spiritual status of monks as the most perfected group among the laity and details the beginning of the ritual for their consecration, including their vows of unity and devotion to God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the ritual for the consecration of a monk, explaining the symbolism of its various elements and lamenting the loss of these ancient apostolic traditions in the contemporary church.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>CH. VI. 2, 3. CONSECRATION OF MONKS.</header>

<margin>1 Cor. v. 4.</margin>
may be felt to be dead in the cross of Christ, never more to live for himself again. His hair is shorn,¹ so that by shaving off everything divided and superfluous, he may appear as clean as possible in the sight of God. He changes his clothing upon changing his life, so that a simpler garment may correspond to a simpler life. This custom was observed in every holy change: when the inner man was changing from one spiritual state to another, the outer man should also do the same, so that the outward habit might correspond to the inward. Hence the life of man is called a garment and clothing by the holy Apostles. The kiss signifies a sweet union. Finally, he is admitted to the Eucharist, but in a more sacred manner than the rest of the people, as he is now acquainted with the mysteries. Thus the monk, as a unit of the people, is duly completed and perfected.

I do not know by what rashness of the Bishops in later ages this ancient custom fell into disuse—the custom of initiating and consecrating not only monks but all other orders whatsoever. This custom, owing to its apostolic origin, had the highest authority and was most religiously observed in the early Church. Nor do I know by what rashness on the part of mankind another custom, with new signs and ceremonies, grew and prevailed so greatly that now, in the present Church, not even a trace appears of a matter of venerable antiquity and of the sacred institution of the Apostles. And had it not been for St. Dionysius (who seems to me to be to our Church what Ezra was to the synagogue of Moses, who willed that the mysteries of the Old Law should be committed to writing, lest in the confusion of affairs and of

---

¹ The reason given for this practice by Dionysius is:—"Furthermore, the tonsure of the hair indicates a most pure life, unadorned by any artifice, which does not decorate the deformity of the soul with false colors; but in itself, not by human charms but by singular and unique ones, it hastens toward the most exact likeness of God."—*Eccl. Hier. vi. p. iii.* It should be kept in mind that the shaving—or, more strictly, the cutting off—of the hair referred to by Dionysius was a very different thing from the formal *tonsure* of the Latin Church in later times. Short-cropped hair was seen as a decent middle ground between long, flowing locks on one hand and the shaved heads of the priests of Isis on the other.—See Richardson's *Ecclesiastical Lectures* (1726), vol. ii. p. 291.

<meta>This page continues the author's lament regarding the abandonment of ancient apostolic traditions, crediting St. Dionysius for preserving these rites in writing and warning that current human inventions are leading the Church toward ruin.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. VI. 2, 3.</header>

[among] men the record of so much wisdom should perish)—had not St. Dionysius, I say, in like manner, as though divining the future carelessness of mankind, left written down by his productive pen what he retained in memory of the institutions of the Apostles, in arranging and regulating the Church, we should have had no record of the ancient custom. Now to have thus departed from ancient usage is a thing truly to be mourned; since we ought to be much more zealous in preserving the institutions of our forefathers than the Jews were in retaining their traditions, in proportion as ours are clearer and more excellent than theirs, and symbols of greater and purer truth. For it would be strange if the Apostles did not best know their meaning and fitness, having all things revealed to them by the Spirit; so that it could not be otherwise than that they wisely understood what to adjust fittingly, each to each, and how, by a just propriety, to portray spiritual truths under their special signs. How it happened, without grievous guilt, that these became afterwards wholly changed, I do not know; since we must believe that it was by the teaching of the Holy Spirit that they ordained all things in the Church. For the words of our

<margin>John 16:13.</margin>
Saviour in St. John are these:—

> "However, when he, the Spirit of truth, is come, he will guide you into all truth: for he shall not speak of himself; but whatever he shall hear, that shall he speak; and he will show you things to come."

It is because their most holy traditions have been superseded and neglected, and men have fallen away from the Spirit of God to their own inventions, that, beyond doubt, all things have been wretchedly disturbed and confounded. And, as I said before, unless God shall have mercy on us, all things will go to ruin.

<summary>The author laments the abandonment of apostolic traditions, noting that only the writings of St. Dionysius preserved the original divine institutions. He argues that replacing these Spirit-led rituals with human inventions has led the Church into a state of confusion and impending ruin.</summary>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>CH. VII. I.</header>

# CHAPTER VII.

## PART I.

### *Introduction to the rites of the holy Dead.*

<margin>Mark 12:25</margin>
It is to be believed without doubt that those who have been born again in Christ, whose life of sin has been destroyed, and who have lived righteously so that they may die in righteousness—just as Christ died in righteousness—will, in return for their rejection of iniquity and their maintenance of the righteousness of God within themselves, receive the reward of their righteousness at their departure from this life. This reward is given first to their holy soul, and then, in due time, to their flesh as well, which was the soul’s companion on the journey to God and its comrade in the war; so that every righteous man, in his full individuality in Christ, may be blessed in both body and soul in glory. 

Jesus, who knew all truth, declared this to the Sadducees, who did not believe in a resurrection, saying, “When they shall rise from the dead, they neither marry nor are given in marriage; but are as the angels which are in heaven.” Supported by this hope, the good, who have led a holy life in Christ here, die willingly and joyfully. They are glad, in part, to have escaped the danger of falling into sin, which they might perhaps encounter if their life were further prolonged; and they rejoice in the highest degree at the prospect of the reward for their completed labor and the crown of glory for their Christian warfare, which is undoubtedly stored up for those who strive lawfully. At the happy departure, and indeed the death, of these saints, their friends and relatives rejoice together with many congratulations, and pray for a similar completion of their own lives in Christ; so that, having kept the path of righteousness even until death, they may in death itself exchange a temporal and troublesome life for a happy and immortal one, and may live everlastingly in Christ. Rejoicing through this hope for the departed, and being fully persuaded that he has journeyed

<summary>This chapter introduces the theology of death for the faithful, explaining that those who live righteously in Christ will be rewarded with a glorious resurrection of both soul and body. It describes the death of a saint as a joyful occasion for the community, marking a transition from a troublesome temporal life to an eternal, angelic existence.</summary>
<keywords>righteousness, Christ, resurrection, Sadducees, Christian warfare, holy Dead, immortality, rewards, afterlife, salvation</keywords>

to Christ, they carry the body forth with exultation, and bear it to the priest, and set it before him, to be committed to holy burial.

For nothing is more unbecoming our Christian profession, in which there is a most sure hope of resurrection and of glory after the likeness of Christ, than, in the manner of the heathens, to bewail the dead with so many tears, as if our belief were that it had gone poorly for them, and not that they were living in the highest bliss with Christ. Yet this too is what they now practice almost everywhere in the Church, to its discredit; but most of all in Italy,¹ where men shamefully give utterance to womanish outcries at the funerals of their friends and relatives. [But this is doubtless the custom of those only who have lived a life here so unlike that of Christ that, through consciousness of their abandoned course, they have nothing to look forward to at his hands; and so they are pained and grieved that they must depart from here, either in despair of any future life or in expectation of eternal misery. These are addressed by St. Paul in his manner, in his Epistle to the Thessalonians:

<margin>1 Thess. iv. 12, 13.</margin>
> "I would not have you to be ignorant, brothers," he says, "concerning those who are asleep, so that you do not sorrow, even as others who have no hope. For if we believe that Jesus died and rose again, even so those also who sleep in Jesus will God bring with him."

---

<note>¹ This is interesting as one of the very few allusions this treatise contains to the personal history of the writer; as there can be little doubt that Colet is here speaking of what he had witnessed with his own eyes during his travels in Italy. The remark in the text is confirmed by the opposing opinion that Polydore Vergil, an Italian, formed regarding funerals in England. He was struck by their quietness. After speaking of the custom of the people of Marseilles in ancient times, who observed the day of the funeral without indulging in lamentations, he goes on to say that a similar custom then prevailed in England, where the people seemed to reflect "that it was useless to give free rein to their natural sorrow as men through any foolish lamentations regarding God's pleasure to share His immortality with us. This was especially true since by death we say goodbye to the toils and dangers we enter upon from our very birth." — "*Quod Angli hodie*," etc.—*De Rerum Invent.* (1644) p. 448.
In the *Funus* and *Exequiæ Seraphicæ* of Erasmus, there are several allusions to the excessive outward display at funerals in his time.</note>

<summary>The author argues that Christian funerals should be joyful celebrations of the hope for resurrection, condemning the excessive mourning practiced in Italy as more fitting for those without faith. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the appropriate behavior and mindset during funeral rites, specifically contrasting the joyful death of the righteous with the despair of the wicked and various philosophical errors regarding the soul.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>CH. VII. I. INTROD. TO RITES OF DEAD.</header>

Bad Christians indeed despair of this; and, despairing, they die in misery. But the good, for many reasons, die joyfully; both because they are weary of this stormy and troublesome life, and wish for security, desire rest, and long to be with Christ: as St. Paul said in his own case, 
<margin>Philippians 1:23.</margin>
“I have a desire to depart, and to be with Christ.” Likewise the brave martyr Ignatius was accustomed to say, “My Love is crucified;” meaning Christ, with whom he desired to be in heaven.¹
<margin>x resp</margin>
The absurd notions that have arisen among men, even since the time of Christ, from hearing the teaching of philosophers, such as Dionysius mentions, are to be utterly rejected by Christians of lofty soul and who hope for better things. I mean, either that souls perish along with their bodies; or that they survive, but are destined not to resume their bodies; or that they survive and migrate into other bodies; or exist in some Elysian fields or other, full of all pleasantness and delight.² All these poor and hazy notions were dispelled by the brightness of Christ, which revealed the truth for us to believe in.

---

¹ <note>The passage occurs in the Epistle of Ignatius to the Romans, and is quoted in the *De Divinis Nominibus*, where it would seem to be understood by Dionysius in the same sense as Colet employs it here:—“Atqui visum est plerisque ex tractatoribus nostris divinius esse amoris quam dilectionis nomen. Scribit enim divinus Ignatius, Amor meus crucifixus est.”—ch. 4. § 1. In each case then the *Amor* of Ignatius is referred to Christ. Yet this does not seem to be the natural sense of the passage:—“Meus enim amor crucifixus est, et non est in me: aqua autem alia viva manet in me, intrinsecus michi dicens, Veni ad patrem.” Thus it stands in the Latin Dionysius of 1498, f. 115. It follows the striking sentence in which Ignatius calls himself, in view of his approaching martyrdom at Rome, “the Lord’s corn, soon to be ground by the teeth of wild beasts,” and would have his disciples even caress the beasts of prey, so that they might give him a speedy tomb. And he presently adds, “For my love is crucified, and is not in me. But there abides in me instead a stream of living water, that says to me from within, ‘Come to the Father.’” Hence by “my love,” he would seem naturally to mean all human passions. But the reading and sense of the passage are much disputed, as may be seen from the notes to the Venice Dionysius of 1756.</note>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. VII. 2.</header>

## CHAPTER VII.

### PART II.

->*On the ceremonies of Christian Burial.*<-

If the dead body is that of a priest, it should be placed before the altar during the time that the funeral rites and funeral service are being performed. But if it is that of a layperson, let the corpse of the deceased be placed before the chancel while the funeral rites and sacred services for the dead are occurring; it should be in the spot where it is customary for the people to stand, in the middle of everyone, so that all alike may witness the sacraments of God. The chancel or sanctuary is the enclosure that is partitioned off by rails and separates the priesthood from the people; it is the place where the choir of priests celebrates the holy services—the place which those of our own time also now call the Choir.

The chanting and giving of thanks is begun by the priests. Passages to assure men of a resurrection are read by the deacons. A eulogy is spoken over the departed, after the Archdeacon¹ has first dismissed the Catechumens. The one who has just died in Christ is also pronounced blessed.

---

<note>...soul and body were forever separated by death, for which Plato is held responsible; (3) that of metempsychosis, and (4) that of those who imagined a sort of material paradise for the blessed (which Colet perhaps summarizes with the term "Elysian fields"); this last opinion is thought by Pachymeres to have been that of Papias, Bishop of Hierapolis.</note>
<note>¹ The office of Archdeacon appears originally to have been nothing more than that of a Deacon. Whether the name at first indicated the senior Deacon in a church, or one elected by the others to a kind of presidency, is disputed. The admonition or address mentioned here as being delivered by him to the people formed a proper part of his duty. "It was his business also, as the Bishop's substitute, to arrange all things relating to the lower clergy and their ministries and services in the church: such as which Deacon should read the Gospel, who should lead the prayers, which of them should guard the doors," and the like.—Bingham's Antiquities, Book ii, chapter xxi.</note>

<summary>This page describes the protocol for placing the deceased during funeral rites, distinguishing between priests and laypeople, and details the specific liturgical roles of the clergy. It also clarifies the historical duties of the Archdeacon and continues a refutation of philosophical misconceptions about the soul.</summary>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>CH. VII. 3. RITES OF THE HOLY DEAD.</header>

All who are present are urged by the Archdeacon to take care to die well in Christ. The Bishop offers pious prayers and supplications over the departed, as if exhorting him with words whispered in his ear, and commends him to God. He then gives him the kiss of peace along with his relatives and the whole Church, and anoints him with oil.<sup>1</sup> <insert>When the funeral rites have thus been duly performed, and the customary service completed, the remains are then committed to burial among the other saints of his own order.</insert>

In these funeral rites, there is nothing but hope, joy, gladness, and congratulation over those who have died in Christ Jesus; for it is believed that he has entered the assembly of the saints and the life of the blessed in heaven, where in Christ he will happily enjoy everlasting life.

## CHAPTER VII.

->PART III.<-

*On the spiritual contemplation of the rites of the holy dead.*

<margin>Matthew 16:19</margin>
But now let us see what the meaning of the sacred rites and ceremonies of the dead is. Every action of ours here in the Church is a sign of the things that are done in heaven. For we are established in faith and believe that as we act on earth, in our imitation of the arrangement of divine things, so it is in heaven. Christ said to St. Peter, who was eminent in faith, that whatever he loosed or bound on earth should be loosed and bound in heaven. Our whole Christian life is a struggle so that we may be made like God, as much

For, as St. Paul writes, he “made himself of no reputation, and took upon him the form of a servant,” so that he might make mankind like his Father. And when Jesus taught his disciples heavenly piety on the mount, he ended his sermon with this sentence, namely, “Be therefore perfect, even as your Father who is in heaven is perfect.” But in this striving toward God through Christ, each one attains as far as the strength given to them allows. As the Apostle wrote to the Corinthians, “Every man has his own gift from God; one in this manner, and another in that.” Our merits are according to the measure of our faith; our rewards are in proportion to our merits.

The diversity and order here in the Church militant is an image of that order which the Church triumphant is destined to have in heaven. And so, because the priest is considered more righteous than the layman, he is placed after death, during the funeral rites, in the middle of the choir among the priests. A deceased layman, or even a holier monk, is set among the lay people outside the chancel rails,<note>¹ As early as the time of Eusebius we have mention of the chancel rails, *cancelli*, as separating the clergy from the rest of the congregation. In the strength of God, we who have been enrolled in the Christian army fight, conquer, go forth, and die righteous in Christ; then we receive the glory of the righteous, each according to his own righteousness. The songs that the Church sings at the services of the dead are songs of joy, for the consolation and encouragement of the living; so that they, through the hope that the dead have journeyed to God, may long for a similar end for themselves; so that they themselves may have glory, and the Church may rejoice. As for weeping, tears, and wailing, they are a disfigurement in the Church and are suitable only for those men who have little faith that the righteous after death will share the glory of Christ, who rose from the dead. Now, if they had believed that Jesus Christ—because he died in righteousness—rose again in glory, and that in the same likeness all the righteous will rise again in him through the power of God, they would certainly not mourn the death of friends so piteously. <margin>Ps. 116:15.</margin> For “precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of his saints.” But the death of sinners is the worst death. Therefore, if there were anything to be grieved over, one ought to grieve over the death of the unrighteous, <margin>Mark 9:44.</margin> since it fares worst with them whose “worm does not die.” But in the Christian Church, which through faith and hope is of good courage in God, nothing is baser or more full of disgrace than excessive weeping for the dead—at least for those whom people have known to be not unrighteous in Christ.

The Church, in her customary rites, recounts the praises of departed saints; gives thanks for the one who is dead; brings forth from the Scriptures the joyful hope of a resurrection; exults in the present gain of a friend and brother, in that by a holy death he is released from evils; and rejoices in hope of the reward that the just shall have. The Church—chosen, wise, and dutiful in Christ—does this to show Christian men that no day can be more joyful than the one in which any righteous man dies, especially if he dies even while suffering violence for the sake of righteousness. That man will have such great glory that the whole church exults with gladness. <margin><insert>St. John 14</insert></margin> When his own most holy death was at hand, Jesus addressed his disciples concerning it, as St. John relates:—

Now the reason that the Catechumens are sent away and excluded from holy funerals is that they lack the light of regeneration, which is given by God in baptism through the ministry of priests, and they are still blind, so as to be unable to gaze on the spectacle of the mysteries. Others however, who have been enlightened and yet have blinded <insert>X</insert> themselves by their own wickedness—Apostates and Penitents; and also Energumens, who, being still too weak, are unable to bear the malicious assaults of the devil—these, although they are not permitted to be present at other sacred rites, may still be present at obsequies and funeral rites. This is so that by the service of the Church, and by the hope of a future life which they perceive in the ceremonies, they may be encouraged to repent and desire the life to come. It is the custom also, as I have said, in the ceremonies and services for the dead, for the Bishop at a funeral to pray for the deceased by the side of the bier, and to greet him as though he were still conscious, and for all the others in order to join in the greeting. He prays to God that He would forgive the departed for whatever sins he fell into while alive through human weakness, and mercifully pardon him, and receive him into His own bright region of the living; that he may rest in happiness in the bosoms of the patriarchs, where there is joy and glory everlasting, and that too so great and substantial, that St. Paul asserted, when writing to the Corinthians, that neither “eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man, the things which God has prepared for those who love him.”<insert>)</insert> Oh! how great

But someone asks,<note>In what follows on the subject of prayers for the dead, Colet fairly represents the cautious language of Dionysius, though, as usual, he discusses it at greater length. Dionysius (Chapter 7, §§ 6, 7) distinctly lays down the two great principles: that the prayer of the Bishop over the departed Christian is declaratory, rather than intercessory; and that it benefits only those who are fit objects of God’s mercy. The Roman doctrine of Purgatory, it hardly needs to be said, is not in Dionysius. Thus Stillingfleet can assert, in his argument with T. C., that the one who is prayed for in Dionysius "is said to be filled with divine joy, and not fearing any change for the worse, but knowing well that the good things possessed shall be firmly and everlastingly enjoyed; as he speaks at his entrance upon that discourse. And if this be in effect to teach Purgatory, as you would have it, you must set your Purgatory a great deal higher than you do."—*Rational Account* (1665), p. 648.</note> what need is there for prayers to be made for someone who has departed? For all is over for them, whether for good or for ill. For, according to the merits of their life, they go by the just judgment of God either upward or downward. After death, there is no opportunity for anyone to gain further merit.

<margin>intercessory prayer</margin>
Dionysius shows in answer first that prayer does not benefit anyone, whether living or dead, unless both the one who is prayed for and the one who prays are worthy. The one who is worthy to pray is the saint. The one who is worthy to be prayed for is none but he who wishes and uses every effort above all things to be holy, and who—acknowledging his own unworthiness—fears to approach the divine majesty with prayers and to appear in his sight, unless there intercedes one whom he thinks has more influence with God, who may supplicate along with him; and who finally, in all humility, seeks for himself one from the higher orders in the Church to be a mediator between God and himself, relying on
