<margin>Luke 11:5; 18:2</margin>
...through whose intercession, and by the help of whose prayers, he himself, while also employing the strongest and most earnest entreaties, may at length be heard. Prayer undertaken in this way and method is the most powerful and effective thing of all; so that, if supplication continues without ceasing, with hope and in humility (according to the teaching of our Savior himself, through the example of the man who asked for loaves at midnight, and of the poor woman who sought a judgment from the unjust judge), it certainly cannot fail to be heard. But if anyone neglects his own cause, and is cold at heart regarding his own salvation, and lives wickedly in his usual manner—not beginning or striving first and foremost to regain the favor of God himself, but instead entrusting to others the task of praying for him (as is the current custom of worldly men) upon payment of a small fee,¹ with an expectation that he can be saved by the prayers of others—he certainly is among those who are unworthy of being prayed for, and is also the most foolish person of all mankind. For he thinks that, while he is negligent himself, the diligence of others can be of service to him, and that he who takes no pains himself to be saved will be saved by means of others. He acts just like a sick man who, while living excessively, wishes to be cured by a physician; and, to use the simile of Dionysius, like one who, having put out his own eyes, would gladly gaze upon the sun.² He is foolish and mad who does nothing himself when he has the power, and yet hopes that he can obtain benefit through the work of others; and who trusts that he can, by the help of others, be reconciled to the Prince from whom he has been estranged, though doing nothing himself to win back the Prince’s favor toward him. Whoever, therefore, is of such a mind as to not first and foremost use their own efforts so that it may be well with them, is among those

...whom prayer in no way profits, and who are unworthy to be prayed for. But for those who strive with all their might to be saved, the prayer of saints cannot help but be profitable. This opinion is supported by St. John in his canonical Epistle:—"If any man sees his brother sin a sin which is not unto death, he shall ask, and He shall give him life for those who sin not unto death. There is a sin unto death: I do not say that he shall pray for it." He who prays is undoubtedly aided by the prayer of others; but he who does not pray, though he can, is aided not at all. He who is himself an evil liver trusts in vain, as Dionysius judges,¹ in the prayers of the good.

<margin>Malachi 2:7.</margin>
<margin>John 20:22.</margin>
The prayer of the Bishop over the departed, that he may be in glory, is not so much a petition that it may be so, as a declaration that it is so. For the Bishop, as Malachi calls him, is "the messenger of the Lord of hosts," and the interpreter of the will of God, moved by the Divine Spirit. Accordingly, the prayer in question is a declaration, from the sacred mouth of the Bishop, of how it beyond doubt fares with the dead; in order that the Church may learn by the words of the Bishop what reward there is for the righteous who have died in Christ, and may believe the Bishop, who relies on the words of God and who possesses the Holy Spirit ("Receive," He says, "the Holy Spirit"), when he faithfully says something in his ministry concerning God. For he speaks not of himself, but of the Spirit; and that which he believes in the fullest faith to be the case, and testifies to on earth, is done in heaven, where it takes place before it is revealed for human belief. He, <insert>as</insert> Dionysius says, is the messenger and interpreter of the will of God, and says and does all things as he is counselled by God. Those on whom God would have compassion, the Bishop in God, as the interpreter of His will, has compassion on. As for those on whom the Bishop does not have compassion, it is a sign that the Spirit of God counsels him not to have compassion on them;

For all things are derived from heaven and from God; and He is the one who works in everyone. Men are true ministers of His will, from whom they receive the Spirit, so that they are like instruments for God's work. They do nothing by themselves, but God does all things through them. It was for this purpose that when the God-man Jesus Christ, after his resurrection, came to his chosen disciples while the doors were shut and said to them twice, "Peace be with you." It was for the purpose, I say, of making them fit instruments of his will—to execute on earth what he did in heaven—that "he breathed on them and said to them, Receive the Holy Spirit: as my Father has sent me, even so I send you.

Whosever sins you remit" (as executors, that is, of the will of God, whose Spirit they have received) "are remitted to them; and whosever sins you retain, they are retained"—provided it is done according to the Spirit given to them and to a just administration of God's will. Otherwise, that which we are called to believe is the work not of God, but of man. Therefore St. Paul, when writing to the Corinthians, after saying that they, the Apostles, and all who hold such an office, are "ministers of Christ and stewards of the mysteries of God," adds immediately after, "Moreover, it is required of stewards that a man be found faithful." 
<margin>1 Cor. 4:1, 2.</margin>

These are the declarers, manifesters, and executors of the will of God and of His mind, which first determines and fashions all things. These are to be esteemed and reverenced in the place of God, as God himself; that is, not for themselves, but for God in them; in whose Spirit they are counseled from above how to think and do and say all things. Concerning men of this kind is that saying of Jesus in St. Luke,

<margin>Luke 10:16</margin>
"He who hears you hears me; and he who despises you despises me; and he who despises me despises him who sent me." For God is in Christ, and Christ in those who are truly his; especially in the heads of the Church, if they are truly Christ's.

<margin>2 Cor. 13:3</margin>
"Do you seek proof," says St. Paul, "of Christ speaking in me?" These do all things by the Spirit of Christ, as Christ by the Spirit of God; these, being exalted in God, receive the truths revealed to them, and believe what is revealed; and that which has been shown to them by the secret promptings of the Spirit of God (whose movements none know except those who are elevated in God) they do and say in undoubting faith.) This is the noble faith by which St. Peter acknowledged Jesus to be "the Christ, the Son of the living God," and made confession in words.

<margin>Matt. 16:16</margin>
This is the faith to which God assigns such great power, that whatever it has bound on earth shall be bound in heaven; and what it has loosed on earth shall be loosed also in heaven. Now all things no doubt have been bound in heaven before they are bound on earth; and those things are loosed there first which are afterwards loosed here.) For God depends not on men, but men on God. Nor does he afterwards ratify our deeds, so to speak, but men execute the previous decisions of God; and by the ministry of men that is at length disclosed on earth, which had been before secretly determined by God in heaven. That which, when revealed and believed, is published by the lawful minister of God, has been disclosed at the fitting time and by the office of God's servant; and we must believe him as the messenger and interpreter of God. Hence it is that Dionysius calls the Bishop, who is highest and nearest to God in the Church, the messenger and interpreter of God; <note>“The venerable bishop (as scripture says) is the interpreter of divine judgments; for he is the messenger of the Lord almighty.” — § 7.</note> who,

in faith in God who reveals it, binds on earth that which has been bound in heaven, and looses on earth that which has been loosed in heaven: that is, he shows to the Church the binding and loosing of God, as far as it is revealed to him, and gives notice of it according to the will of God.

For it must be carefully noted, lest Bishops should be presumptuous, that it is not the role of men to loose the bonds of sin; nor does the power belong to them of loosing or binding anything. Since God alone looses and binds; <margin>x</margin> and in His own sight in heaven He looses and binds all things. <margin>x</margin> Those who are leaders in the Church, such as the Bishops, receive by revelation what has been loosed and bound there, and declare what they have received, and by their words execute the design of God, not their own. And if they do not proceed according to revelation, moved by the Spirit of God in all things that they do or say, then they are necessarily foolish and mad in themselves, and they abuse the power given to them, leading both to the blaspheming of God and the destruction of the Church. Hence we may see how high, how exalted, and how completely at home in heaven the Bishop should be—especially he who is the highest, whom we call the Pope;¹ that what he spreads through the Church by his authority, bringing it to eternal life, he may draw entirely from God. Once drawn, he should digest it and rightly <margin>3</margin> and lawfully distribute it through all its members, so that these, being refreshed by divine nourishment, may live together in the Bishop, who lives in God to the fullest degree. And this is done so that all things should go forth from God to His Church, for the purpose of calling all things back to Himself—He who is all in all in a sound and holy Church. He brings to life, enlightens, and perfects the Bishop, especially the one who is the highest, so that he in turn may faithfully and sincerely be a minister of the wisdom and the will of God in all things, for the life and salvation of all; seeking

nothing but the benefit of men in God, and the approval of himself by God, in his stewardship of the mysteries of God. If he is a lawful Bishop, he of himself does nothing, but God does it in him. > “And whoever finds himself free of such blame,
> He will come up and offer in God’s name,
> And I will absolve him by the authority
> Which by the papal bull was granted to me.
> By this trick have I won year after year
> A hundred marks, since I became a pardoner.”

And after Colet’s death Erasmus continued the attack upon it in his Colloquies *On Vows Rashly Undertaken* and *The Military Confession*. John Vitrier, the Franciscan, whose portrait is made by Erasmus to be the companion to Colet’s, was of the same opinion:—“He condemned the foolish confidence of those who, having thrown a coin into the chest, thought themselves free from sins.” —Erasmus, *Letter to Jodocus Jonas*.</note> so that, unless that Mediator who alone can do so, who created and founded the Church out <margin>Rom. viii. 19.</margin> of nothing for himself (therefore does St. Paul often call it a “creature”)—unless, I say, the Mediator Jesus puts his hand to it with all speed, our most disordered Church cannot be far from death. Men slacken and pull tight, loose and bind, not through faith in God what has been bound in heaven, but what they themselves wish; and hence all things on earth are in confusion. They are not executors of the will of God, but doers of their own. They do not testify what God wills, as they ought to do (for their office is nothing else than a testimony of the will of God); but they set forth what they themselves desire. They do not consult God on what is to be done by constant prayer, but take counsel with men; whereby they shake and overthrow everything. “All” (as we must own with grief, and as

<summary>Colet warns that Bishops who act on their own will rather than as God's instruments are poisoning the Church, leading to widespread disorder. He suggests that only the intervention of Jesus can save the Church from its current state of corruption and human-driven governance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bishop's role as a divine instrument, specifically focusing on the Funeral Service and the significance of anointing the dead as a sign of their transition to eternal life.</meta>

<header>ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY. CH. VII. 3.</header>
<page-num>152</page-num>

<margin>Phil. ii. 21.</margin>ul say that all (I write this with both grief and tears) "seek their own interests, not the things which belong to Jesus Christ;" not heavenly things, but earthly ones; things that will lead them to death, not what will lead them to eternal life.

But, to return to my subject, the true Bishop, the lawful overseer, and the faithful steward of the mysteries of God, does nothing except according to God’s will. He is the hand, the minister, and the instrument of God, whom we must believe even as we believe God himself. Therefore, in the Funeral Service, while he is praying for eternal life for the saints who have died in Christ, he is reverently speaking the sure will of God. He is faithfully and effectively asking for that to be done which he fully knows will be done, so that by words of intercession—which at all times befit a Bishop—he may testify to the gathered Church the justice and grace of God toward men, specifically toward those who die well in Christ. For the Bishop is the witness of God's will and, as it were, the mouthpiece and voice of the mind of God, expressing the things that have been foreordained by God, which he perceives by faith. It is God himself who does all things well in everyone.

The custom of the Bishop and the whole Church next greeting the departed and bidding him, as it were, a last farewell, is a sign that he is not entirely dead, but is one who is passing to another region, to his own true and proper country. The body, when kissed, is anointed by the Bishop with oil to signify that he has finished his struggle. And here we must note the ancient custom, established by the Apostles themselves, that those who were to be initiated were first instructed in the creed, were prepared, and stripped; and, once initiated, were anointed with oil for the struggle; <note>¹ See above, p. 68.</note> while finally, as noble warriors at their death, they received their final and completing anointing.

There is frequent use of ointment in the Christian Church, which takes its name from anointing and is called "Christian." Now the <term>Chrism</term> is a mixture of different perfumes, with the addition of oil, or balsam, or some other oily substance.

<summary>This page describes the Bishop as a faithful instrument of God's will, particularly during funeral rites where he testifies to the hope of eternal life. It also explains the symbolism of anointing the deceased with oil as a final completion of the spiritual struggle begun at initiation.</summary>
<keywords>Bishop, Funeral Service, anointing, Chrism, Apostles, eternal life, rites of the dead, Philippians 2:21</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the rites of burial and the symbolic use of anointing, emphasizing that the final anointing signifies the completion of the soul's spiritual warfare.</meta>

<header>CH. VII. 3. RITES OF THE HOLY DEAD.</header>
<page-num>153</page-num>

The Church in this particular matter has its own unique composition. And this Chrism is a sign of the Holy Spirit,¹ by which all are strengthened in Christ; and it is designed for that conflict which we enter into, under Christ our leader, against “spiritual wickedness in high places;” with which we contend when anointed and strengthened by the Holy Spirit. Of this Holy Spirit, the anointing applied to the body is a sacrament, and symbolic of the anointing of the soul, by which we—made spiritual in the Spirit of Christ—wage war in Christ in a spiritual battle, with spiritual weapons, and against spiritual enemies, so long as we live here lawfully. At the last, when we ourselves, undefeated, have come away safe in Christ, and our warfare is finished, we are anointed at our departure; this is so that men may know that we have finished the fight by the grace of the same Holy Spirit, in whose strength we began the contest.

Finally the body is buried, enclosed either in earth or stone or some other material, along with the men of his own rank; there it is reserved for the day of resurrection. It was an ordinance of the early Church that the corpse should be placed by itself and separate; order being preserved unbroken even among the dead, so that order might appear everywhere. And as these bodies shared in the toil and in the contest, by the endurance of hardships and abstinence from pleasures, so are they laid down in a sure hope that they will be

---

Yet one more doubt. They have two sorts of oil to anoint with, differing in holiness, consecration, and use, and yet both hallowed by the bishop. One is of oil and balsam blended together, which is commonly called oil and cream, wherewith bishops and priests in their consecrating, and children, are anointed in baptism: and that is more holy than this for sick persons is, for this is oil alone without other things blended into it, as their master teaches. By obedience to God, by good hope, by faith in the signs, and by constancy in well-doing, the multitude of Christians will be saved, although through this ignorance of theirs they will not reach such a high place in heaven, in Christ all-glorious, as those who through a more abundant measure of the Spirit see further, and have a deeper insight into the mysteries.<insert>]</insert> Since, just as men are promoted here—I do not mean by their own strength, but through humility and by the guidance of the Spirit—so in all cases will they be placed in the same order <margin>X</margin> in heaven.

As to the sacramental words used at the sacred rites, St. Dionysius, when treating of the sacraments,¹ is purposely silent and would not discuss them in writing, lest he should scatter God’s pearls before swine. As I have said,

And I must say again, the sacraments ought to be guarded in holy keeping from the unworthy multitude; for when handled by them, they come to be held cheap. Places, vessels, robes, and whatever else pertains to the priests, being of greater sanctity, may not be looked upon by the ignorant and uninitiated multitude, except at a distance, and that too with great fear and reverence. But alas! in the unhappy state of this our age, all things are in such shameful disorder that nothing is now more profane than what ought to be most sacred.

# CHAPTER VII.
## PART III. SECTION II. <note>In Dionysius, this subject is treated in a single section (§ 11) of the Third Part, or Contemplation, of the Seventh Chapter, on the rites of the holy dead. As it appears to come somewhat abruptly there and is expanded to much greater length by Colet, I have thought it better to distinguish it as a separate section of the present part of the chapter. The reason for its introduction in this supplementary manner is probably because the baptism of adults alone was discussed in Chapter 2.</note>
### *On the Baptism of Infants.*

From the discourse of Dionysius, one may see that from the very beginning of the rising Church, provision was made by Christ himself and his Apostles for the salvation of infants. And in the early Church, I find that newborn infants were not only washed in the holy washing of regeneration and enlightened, but also through participation in the most holy Eucharist (without which in the primitive Church not even the baptism of infants could take place), were joined and incorporated into the mystical body of Christ. And this was because at that age they are far from any fault of their own and clear of sins; provided only that someone took charge of them as a sponsor and promised on their behalf that, if they lived, they would truly seek the sacraments. And although this was then held in derision by worldly men, who do not appreciate the things of God, and who received and approved

<summary>This page argues that sacraments must be protected from the unworthy and introduces a section on infant baptism, noting its apostolic origins and the early practice of providing infants with both baptism and the Eucharist.</summary>

...of nothing except what their grovelling <gloss>our carnal</gloss> reason <gloss>is the driving force</gloss> was persuaded of; yet to faithful and spiritual men the dedication of infants to God appears a great and admirable sacrament of divine compassion. By the Church it is also esteemed a matter full of piety, provided it is done duly and in a lawful manner. And though the meaning of it cannot be seen by men, that is no wonder; seeing that not even the chief angels themselves know all things. But pious faith accepts and reverences all. And those who first appointed not only that sacrament, but all the rest besides—the Apostles, I mean—either possessed the reasons for them, or believed one who did. That which has arisen, and is now performed, at the prompting of the Spirit of God, cannot be devoid of the very highest reason; and though this is not attained by poor human <gloss>our</gloss> reason <gloss>mind</gloss>, yet it is by faith which is above reason,¹ a light surely given to us in Christ, and capable of the highest reason. Now if we are weak in this matter of faith, which <margin>⚓</margin>even <gloss>Peter</gloss>, the very chief of Christ's Apostles, sought to have increased, let us not on that account despise the great Sacraments of God. Rather, let us acknowledge and truly and humbly confess the narrowness of our reason, and let us ascribe to our own perverseness our lack of power to comprehend the things of God, rather than disdainfully spurning what our puny reason does not comprehend; yes, and let us seek to become something that is above reason, namely great in faith; and above the flesh, that is spiritual; and above men, that is divine; and let us wholly and without doubt believe that divine things cannot be received except by divine men. But now let us see, with respect to the sacred rites of infants born in the Christian Church, what was the order and ceremony of initiating them, practiced by the <gloss>early Church</gloss> early men of our

Above his pantheism there is the intense belief in one God; above his positivism there is the trustful aspiration of faith."—Westcott, in the *Contemp. Rev.* No. xvii. p. 27.

religion. In the early Church, as I have said on the testimony of Dionysius, they were admitted both to Baptism and to the Eucharist on certain conditions and rules.¹ The manner of introducing them, so that they might be numbered in the Church, was, according to Dionysius, as follows. A parent, when a child has been brought into the world, aware that it would have been better for a son or a daughter not to be born,
<margin>X</margin>
than not to be born again in Christ; also not ignorant that the sacrament is of little benefit—indeed, it is certainly injurious—unless the infant’s life, if he grows up, shall correspond to the sacred rites received; a parent, I say, in order that his child may be admitted by the Bishop to participate in the sacred rites, goes about and anxiously inquires where he may find a good man, and one skilled in Christian truth, to take upon himself and promise the instruction and training of his child in all things that pertain to salvation, and to be a sponsor for him before the Bishop, so that, if the latter signs him and endows him with the heavenly rites, the infant, if he grows up, shall himself live according to those sacred rites, in holiness and purity in Christ. And when the father has found such a man and entrusted his little son to him in honest faith, as to a second and greater parent in God (for the fatherly duty of begetting an infant is an office preparatory to the regeneration of the child), the man then who has taken upon himself the bringing forth of the infant in Christ—so that at length, if he lives, he may be formed and fashioned into a perfect Christian—on this great profession of fatherhood in Christ, goes to the Bishop, shows him whom he has taken to himself for regeneration, or rather for a full fashioning in Christ, and reverently asks the Bishop to admit him into the body of Christ by assimilation and sealing of the sacraments, so that thereupon he may, through his sponsor’s care and diligence, grow to live a life worthy of those sacred rites. The Bishop then, on hearing the man promise and make a holy vow for the child, and perceiving that he is not unfit to perform his promise, accepts his sponsorship, in reliance on the divine compassion; and, after the

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the rite of infant baptism and the role of the sponsor (or foster-father), following the account of Dionysius the Areopagite.</meta>

<page-num>158</page-num>

...manner and form instituted by the Apostles, provides the infant with the customary ceremonies and signs, both illuminating him with baptism and <gloss>perfecting</gloss>perfecting him with the Holy Communion. Meanwhile, the <gloss>sponsor</gloss>foster-father, who has undertaken the rearing of the child in Christ, gives a pledge and sacred promise on behalf of the infant regarding all things that true Christianity demands: namely, a renouncing of all sin, faith in the sacraments, and a life of perseverance in Christ worthy of such a profession. And he does not say this in the child’s place, since it would be a foolish thing for another to speak in place of someone who is ignorant; but when he speaks of renouncing in his own person, he professes that he will ensure, as far as he can, that the little infant—as soon as it is capable of instruction—shall in reality and in its life utterly renounce every principle contrary to the Christian character. He will show himself throughout his whole life as worthy of the sacred rites, being at length in more mature years aware of and voluntarily professing what he accepted when an unconscious infant. Then, on these terms and conditions—that the foster-father perform what he has promised—the Bishop receives the infant. And when the Bishop hears him (whom I call *foster-father*, though Dionysius calls him at one time *sponsor* and at another *godfather*; he is called *god-sib* by some, and by others a <gloss>co-father</gloss>*surety*¹)—when the Bishop, I say, hears him saying, “I renounce”—which means, as Dionysius explains it: “I will take care that the infant renounces and abjures all that is offered to him from a lower realm and by the devil, and desires nothing except what shall be supplied to him from heaven above by God himself”—he, being not ignorant of the gracious mysteries of God and looking forward to what the sponsor has promised, joyfully signs the infant with the mark of Christianity, so that he may be recognized as having come forth like a shoot from a faithful tree. To this scion, the faith of his parents contributes something;

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the roles of the biological father versus the spiritual godfather, emphasizing that spiritual rebirth through baptism is superior to physical birth.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>CH. VII. 3. § 2. BAPTISM OF INFANTS.</header>

...though it does not contribute much, since the Christian tree grows and multiplies its branches not through carnal generation but through spiritual regeneration. From this it is clear that in terms of true fatherhood, and in Christ, the godfather is more truly a father—who completes the human being in Christ once born—than the father who provided the substance for his flesh. Furthermore, although the son respects each parent—first the one who made him a child of man, and second the one who was the instrument of his being begotten also as a child of God—he has undoubted reason to owe more to the second and to accept him in the place of a parent. For it is surely a greater thing to be perfected in God than to be begotten by man.¹ Therefore, the sponsor is of more importance and in a higher position than the father; his office and work are far more excellent and meritorious, deserving far more from both God and the small infant than anything done by the parent in begetting. For the parent performed only the work of the flesh, rather for the sake of his own pleasure than for the welfare of any offspring. But the sponsor, without pleasure to himself—and even with effort—brings a man anew to Christ, being inflamed with the love of God for the salvation of a human being.

Thus we see how in the early Church, by the ordinance of the Apostles, infants were not admitted unreservedly to the sacred rites, but only on the condition that someone would be a surety for them. This person ensured that, when the infants reached the age of discretion, they would from then on truly set before themselves the pattern of Christ. And this surety and sponsor will consider it his responsibility to educate and bring up the boy in such learning and behavior that, ac-

I have written this much, following in the path of <term>Dionysius</term>, concerning our Ecclesiastical Hierarchy; from the beautiful pattern of which we have far degenerated. But I pray that God, the Creator of all things Himself, will reform, out of His own great goodness, what has become deformed in us; through Jesus Christ our Lord.

# JOHN COLET, PROFESSOR OF SACRED THEOLOGY,
## EXCERPTED AND WROTE THESE THINGS
## FROM THE READING OF DIONYSIUS:

I recognize your sublime and angelic mind, excellent man and dearest friend—a mind truly worthy not only to hear about angels but also to be joined together as one with them. Therefore, what I have read and recalled from memory during the last few days from Dionysius the Areopagite, in his book entitled *The Celestial Hierarchy* (in which he discusses angels in a magnificent and divine manner), I wish to share with you. For among the things recorded and learned in that book, the first and greatest principle is this: that whatever good we have received from any source, we should then kindly impart and communicate it to others. In this, we imitate the inestimable goodness of God, who generously <note>Bestows—C.</note> bestows Himself and communicates in an ordered way to all things. Whatever He gives, He gives so that the recipient may immediately hand it on to another, as far as is appropriate; so that as the gifts of God are distributed and derived from one to another, all may recognize that God is good, and they may all be joined together by that same Divine goodness. Therefore, receive now what my memory has preserved from that reading of Dionysius; following the order of the chapters, I intend to touch upon them briefly and in summary.

### CHAPTER I.

In the first chapter, it is stated: from that Father of lights, a certain pure and spiritual light goes forth and emanates through all created things, as far as the nature of each allows,

Moreover, that light is one through all things and is entirely the same, not varied indeed by the variety of things; but rather, it draws those things which are various and diverse, as far as <margin><unclear>mostly</unclear></margin> possible, into a certain likeness and unity with itself. In this unity and identity of light, there is entirely a variety of things, and the same light remains one and simple in various things; so that without confusion, you may always attribute variety to things and identity to the light. <margin>of God</margin>

<margin>(</margin>But that wonderful irradiation of the divine sun, catching hold of rational creatures who are capable of His divine nature, refines them, <margin>light</margin> as it were, and once they are made light, it lifts them upward intimately into itself, attracts them, and even makes them one with itself. All those spiritual natures whom we call by the single name of angels are in such great happiness; that pure and naked light itself is infused into them. For, because of the simplicity of their nature, they are not unfit to be drenched in open and pure truth.

Men, however, who hold the lowest place among rational creatures, and who are not themselves simple and of a single nature—since a heavy and tedious bodily mass clings to their souls, in which the soul, descending from its simplicity, becomes somewhat corporeal, so that now it is truly inept and altogether powerless for beholding pure spiritual things, and is so weakened by the mixture of the body that it cannot bear any powerful light, but like a sickly and weak person, it shudders at it, flees from it, and is offended by it—these men, I say, <margin>||</margin> since the best and most kind God did not wish them to be entirely devoid of His light, truth, and grace, it was brought about through the ministry of angels that, according to their nature and capacity, the ray of the divine sun and the truth of God should, as it were, descend slightly from its simplicity into that condition—namely, the corporeal and sensible. Thus the human mind, having become nearly corporeal within the body, does not shudder at the vibration of such great light, but admits it with a now kindred and more tolerable appearance, and perceives the truth (which has also now become nearly corporeal) under the senses as far as possible, with bodily signs and likenesses indicating it.

Since this naked truth could not instill itself into the minds of men on its own, and since it recognized the familiarity which the soul has with the senses, it undertook another path, and through these senses, as suitable means, it attempted to introduce itself into souls: so that, what could not be accomplished...

...either from the cause, or at least from the effect, by some reasoning (so that man might be a participant in truth), it might be accomplished. Therefore, that which flows from God into the angels—into their stability, order, and perfection—pure, simple, and sincere, that same thing, proceeding into men and into their senses, falling from its purity and simplicity, becomes through angelic ministry to some extent sensible; with the sensed things themselves reflecting the truth as closely as possible, so that man, placed in the middle between sensible and intelligible things, might be led through bodies to spirit, through senses to intellect, through shadow to light, and through image to truth; so that having eventually attained it, having then entirely despised the flesh and being drawn into the spirit, he may rest in spiritual truth alone.

In this way and by such paths, by His wonderful kindness, the good God wished to recall humble men to His own height, and at last to restore them to truth and to the light itself, who is our Jesus, who is blessed forever. For that Creator, and the ineffable cause of every order, is the glorious and effective exemplar of the first hierarchy and of that sacred principality which burns with such great love, which strives with all its might to reflect God, and to urge those things that are below it to reflect Him in turn. Thus, just as God teaches the first hierarchy, so that one teaches the second, and the second teaches the third. 

Then the third hierarchy of angels, seeing the ignorance of men on earth, who are in the fourth place, and how unfit they were to conduct divine matters, purposefully established for a time, by God's will, that men should imitate the angels with their bodies and bodily actions; so that those who had been unable to do so in spirit, because of their corporeal thickness, might at least in the flesh and body itself, by a certain method of similarity (as far as is possible), reflect God. <margin>Vertical pencil bracket in margin from here to the bottom of the page.</margin> This corporeal and carnal hierarchy and order among men, purifying, illuminating, and perfecting—or rather, signifying purification, illumination, and perfection—was the Synagogue under the master of the Hebrews, Moses, imitating the angels with bodies and sensible signs, not with understood and spiritual ones. Yet Moses himself was not ignorant of the truth, according to whose pattern he organized the Hebrews in order. Nor was Moses himself ignorant that one day there would be a man in whom not an angel but truth itself would speak, who would advance men by the spirit to the truth which he himself had foreshadowed with bodies. This is our most pious Jesus, the sun of justice, who not only...

He indicated what those shadowy <term>Mosaic</term> institutions meant for themselves, but moreover, he illuminated the mind so that it might believe the things revealed. Meanwhile, it was brought about that only that empty shadow of order and of some <term>hierarchy</term> appeared on earth, so that by a just order, man's promotion and translation into the angelic state might seem to proceed—namely, from the shadow to the image, and from the image to the truth. The shadow was under <term>Moses</term>; the illustrated image now exists on earth in <term>Christ</term>; the truth will one day be ours in heaven. Under <term>Moses</term>, the condition of the <term>Hebrews</term> and the <term>synagogue</term> was such that there was a little light within much shadow. In the <term>church</term> of <term>Christ</term>, however, while we are here, there is abundant light, but at the same time some opacity and shadow; yet it is so overcome by light that it is called "color" rather than shadow. For color consists of opacity and light.

Indeed, the <term>church</term>, while we are still in our bodies, neither lacks sensible signs nor can it do without them. But here they are few, and very necessary for retaining the weaker members in the <term>church</term>, so that they might be admonished through their senses when they grasp the spirit in their minds. This spirit does not descend into men, as long as men are sensory beings, unless sensible signs are employed; while we live here, these are the foundations and essential matter of the <term>Christian</term> <term>sacraments</term>, without which they cannot be initiated or imbued with the truth of sacred things. This is the reason, as <term>Dionysius</term> teaches, for the most holy mystery of <term>communion</term>, in which the holy society of <term>Jesus</term> is happily recognized in the spirit. All the other <term>sacraments</term> also have their underlying materials appropriately and fittingly applied. In these, while living here, those to whom the spirit is ministered learn that they are human, and that they are not yet altogether capable of receiving the naked spirit.

A time will indeed come in which, the opacity and death of our body being utterly extinguished, neither shadow nor color—but that pure and glowing light, simple and naked in itself—will be perceived by us, when what we believe will be realized in fact and effect. But to return to <term>Dionysius</term>, it is (to speak briefly) dealt with and shown in this chapter that a certain common spiritual and divine light flows from God and spreads through all things sweetly, received by each according to his own capacity. In those clear and translucent angels, it is infused simple and naked; but to men, through the wonderful kindness of God, it is ministered through certain coverings and inte-

### CHAPTER II.

Theology, which is the revelation of divine truth and the speech of the prophets, takes great account of human weakness. Since it wishes to regenerate this weakness into a heavenly condition, it somehow causes those heavenly and spiritual natures to descend and brings them down to the senses of humble men to some degree. It describes the state of divine things and the orders of blessed spirits through poetic fictions. This is not done through entirely fictional matters—lest there be too little authority in a story—but through truly living men and their mutual actions. This is done so that a rough stage and spectacle might first exist as an obscure representation. If this is not the absolute truth itself, it is certainly a future image that will later appear more illustrious and reflect the truth itself more clearly, once the shadow has emerged into the light for a while and the opacity has been illuminated with colors, as it were. For as we said above, the Jewish synagogue is like a shadow; the church, under Christ the sun, is like an opacity illuminated in various ways. Just as something becomes beautifully colored when it receives the sun's light, so the whole institution and order established among the Hebrews by Moses foreshadowed this church on earth. <note>A pencil bracket on the right margin highlights the text from "Since it wishes" to "mutual actions"</note> The Sun of Righteousness, Jesus Christ, having risen, has variously illuminated this foreshadowing by sending rays of his truth according to various capacities. He caused it to be clearly visible in the light of day, with the shadow being brought forth into a clear image and the synagogue transformed into the church. Thus, the day disperses the darkness of night, so that men no longer walk and live in the darkness and shadow of night, but under Christ the Sun in the light of God, in the spirit. Eventually, once translated into the truth itself, they may rest happily in the "day of days" and the "Sabbath of Sabbaths." Just as those Jews exercised themselves with their bodies and actions <note>A small circled letter "D" in pencil appears in the right margin</note> arranged in a certain order, so Christians must exercise themselves in mind and spirit in whatever was signified there...

...follow, so that not even one iota <gloss>of the Law</gloss> passes away.) These spiritual actions—the image of the preceding shadow, the following truth, and the future truth—proceed from faith; namely, from the fact that men believe in Jesus Christ. He finally revealed openly what that Mosaic foreshadowing meant and signified, as well as where that whole synagogal hierarchy (conducted with bodies and not minds) was tending.

Those who believe in his gospel and good news, and who simultaneously perform in mind and spirit what was signified through this faith, are truly in the day. Like Jesus, who taught more expressively not only with words but also by deeds, they walk as if more brightly colored. By a beautiful order among themselves and by mutual actions that are quite spiritual and clear, they represent the true face of the heavenly spirits both more closely and more clearly.

Into this face, as Paul says, they are transformed from glory to glory, as if by the spirit of the Lord. In this they will finally exist in the truth itself, which they now reflect in spirit as best they can through an order of images and actions, believing in Christ and eagerly awaiting that full and perfect truth together with the angels. This is what Paul writes to the Galatians: "For we," he says, "through the spirit, by faith, wait for the hope of righteousness; meanwhile we live by faith, which works through love." In this is the completion of the law. This is the "shortened word" that God has placed upon the earth.

Thus, by God’s ineffable distribution of things and his care for the human race (so that it might be reformed into happiness), first there preceded a certain light shadow of the divine countenance, enticing men into a closer image of that same countenance. This was so that they might then be transferred into the truth of that same countenance, so they would no longer look upon God in shadow or in image, nor in the church here on earth as if through a mirror dimly, but behold the true face of God himself face to face. This will happen, specifically, when it appears what men shall be (as John says), and they will see him as he is.

But now, no one sees God or divine things. We believe in Jesus Christ, the Angel of Great Counsel; the only-begotten Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, has himself made him known. He declared that it is time for the image of the Mosaic description to appear more clearly in men, reflecting the divine countenance and the trinities of angels. <margin>With Jesus as leader</margin> Offering himself as leader, he established a kind of dance and a more brilliant celebration, so that, with joined hands, they might mutually follow in his footsteps, <gloss>the gospel</gloss>.

When Moses learned these things from the angels, who indicated to him by what means they themselves reflect the form and order of God within themselves, he attempted to fashion the relation of God among men, after the likeness of the angels, according to the standard and rule of the shown pattern; <margin>x</margin> so that by a certain institution and order, having imitated the angels in life and actions, men might represent something of the divine reason in themselves; even though, as was appropriate for the beginning of the matter, they did so more crudely.

...and more obscurely. But that father was content with any beginning whatsoever, looking forward to those who would both polish and illuminate his obscure roughness. Therefore, he led those Hebrew men—who were little refined—into the school like children; so that, playing as if <gloss>with the tricks of</gloss> dolls and fictional things, they might represent in shadow what men would one day do more truly. They imitated little girls, who play with dolls in their early years—images of children—who will later give birth to real children in their mature age. They also imitated little boys, who ride toy horses and are armed with toys, who will one day ride real horses with real arms. <margin>1 Corinthians 13:11</margin> When I was a child, says Paul, I understood as a child; when I became a man, I put away those things which belonged to the child.

<margin>2 Corinthians 3:18</margin>
<margin>2 Corinthians 4:6</margin>
From childhood, images, and fictional things Christ has drawn us—He who shone upon the darkness, who taught the truth, and who made those believing in Him into men. Thus, with unveiled faces beholding the glory of God, we are transformed into that same image, from glory to glory, as if by the Spirit of the Lord. God, who commanded light to shine out of darkness, has Himself shone in our hearts to provide the illumination of the knowledge of His glory in the face of Jesus Christ. By faith, we may see His truer countenance, for He revealed the things that Moses depicted concerning Himself and the church. Whoever believes in that word believes in the word which God placed as a summary upon the earth. For in Christ, all things are gathered together in abundance, whatever that famous Moses wished either to teach regarding justice or to establish regarding religion. These things had to be foreshadowed first, and then later illuminated in their own time, so that eventually, at the end of the age, all things might be perfected. In those foreshadowings and significations by Moses—a most sharp-sighted theologian and observer of nature—metaphors are taken from everywhere, because there are no proper words for describing divine things. Nothing is suitable for signifying God Himself, who is not only entirely unspeakable but also unthinkable. Therefore, He is most truly expressed by negations; since you can say what He is not, but not what He is. Whatever you affirm about Him, you lie, since He is none of the things you can name. However, because the hidden reason of God resides in all things, on account of that slight similarity, theologians have tried to signify Him by the names of all things—not only by the better things, but even by the worse. This is done so that the duller sort of people, captured by the appearance of beautiful things, should not think that God is exactly what He is called. Thus it is necessary...

...you speak metaphorically, so that you seem to have sought the word from elsewhere (rather than speaking from the essence of the thing itself; and so that it may be openly recognized that you do not use proper terms, but analogies. Therefore, divine things are spoken of not only by the names of the sun, heaven, light, life, intellect, mind, or whatever might be better in that category; but even by the names of birds, four-footed animals, plants, stones, elements, and reptiles.) For He is all in all; and lower things reflect better things obscurely, and all things reflect God as much as they are able; from whom, through whom, and in whom all things exist. This style of writing was used by the ancient theologians, patriarchs, and prophets, out of both necessity and usefulness. It was necessary because they could not speak of God in any other way; and it was useful because, by it, even a weaker vision is attracted and led to behold clearer things, while the truth is hidden from the impious and profane. That woven Mosaic veil is of such a kind that it hides the precious things of God from the wicked, and yet teaches the pious something, even if (not the truth itself, at least some shadow of the truth. Indeed, regarding the things Dionysius discusses about divine matters, he solemnly charges Timothy to keep them sacredly hidden and covered, and not to communicate them except to those who are worthy, lest they be violated by profane eyes.)

### CHAPTER III.

The one God, beautiful and good—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—that Trinity which is the creator of all, is Himself the purgation of things into unity, the illumination into beauty, and the perfection into goodness. That God, by His poured-out light—purging, illuminating, and perfecting—ineffably deifies and makes <term>deiform</term> (god-like) that triple hierarchy of angels. In these angels, although all things are shared by all (for purity, light, and <term>deiformity</term> are inseparable), there are nonetheless something like personal attributions: such as assigning perfection to the first hierarchy, illumination to the second, and purgation to the third. Likewise, within the first hierarchy, it is attributed to the first order that it perfects most; to the second that it illuminates; and to the third that it purges. By this threefold reasoning, the triple hierarchy is made like the Triune God.
<margin>X</margin>
The entire effort of all spirits is to reflect God. God, in the first place, powerfully assimilates those things that are near to Him; those that are assimilated...

Thus the <gloss>motions</gloss> derivation of the deity proceeds from order to order, and from hierarchy to hierarchy, and from superior creatures to inferior ones, according to the capacity of each, toward the deification of all; to use the words of Paul, "to the praise of the glory of God's grace." In this lot also (so that they may be reformed in a hierarchy reflecting God) are human beings. When the angels were exhausted in bringing them into order—He who is order itself and the incarnate beauty of God—<margin>of Christ</margin> beautiful in form above the sons of men, Jesus Christ, came to their aid. He established within himself three hierarchies among chosen men: the highest, the middle, and the lowest. And in each of these, he established <gloss>1</gloss> the purgative, <gloss>2</gloss> the illuminative, and <gloss>3</gloss> the perfective stages. Thus, as <margin>fellow workers with Christ</margin> men also cooperate in Christ toward the glorification of God, they may eventually complete a perfected human hierarchy on earth, which will one day be equal to the angels in heaven.

### CHAPTER IV.

<margin>of Loving</margin> God created all things because He is good; and because He is good, He calls everything back to Himself according to its capacity, so that He may kindly <gloss>communicate</gloss> share Himself with them. There is nothing that does not share in God to some extent, since they have their existence and their well-being from God. To the first and highest men, visions are more open, and a simpler <gloss>shining</gloss> irradiation of the deity enters their minds. For the remaining, denser people, all things are suitably indicated through signs: both the <margin>beauty of Christ</margin> fashioning of <gloss>conduct</gloss> character, the worship of God, and the expectation of truth. <header>CH. V. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

The Mosaic law contains obscure signs of these things <gloss>the core</gloss>. Therefore Dionysius calls it the image of a more sacred law. <margin>Section 3.</margin> It is the derivation of the poured-out Deity, which, proceeding outward, deifies from one hierarchy to a lower hierarchy, and within each hierarchy from one order to another by a reciprocal ministry, so that they may become the souls and spirits of God. The angels first learned that mystery of the incarnation; <margin>The Incarnation.</margin> they then taught it to men: to <margin>Zachary</margin> Zachariah, Mary, Joseph, and the Shepherds. Among men, there is then a reciprocal instruction from member to member in Christ, which proceeds from the first through the middle to those who are last in the church (as Paul says) through the <gloss>joint and habit</gloss> joint of supply. <margin>Eph. 4:16</margin> Thus, a certain impartation of the Deity is derived from God through the angels—that is, from angel to angel—and from them to men. God Himself also, coming as a man, Jesus, took on the role of an angel and is called the "Angel of Great Counsel"; and, insofar as He was a man, He continually received the ministrations of angels at His conception, birth, youth, and when He was about to die and ascend. <margin>X 86</margin> For before the resurrection, He was a mortal man, as it were, lower than the angels; yet after the resurrection, that same man (being an immortal <gloss>heavenly</gloss> man, was the God of all).

---

### CHAPTER V.

Angels are called messengers [nuncii] from the act of announcing [nunciando]; because they announce the things of God from the higher to the lower beings, all the way down to men. Truly, the greatest messenger to men was Jesus Christ; hence He is called by Isaiah the "Wonderful Counselor" and the "Angel of Great Counsel." Although the lowest spirits are called "angels" by their specific name, because their duty can be performed by any superior being (for a superior can do whatever an inferior can), therefore the names of the lower beings are suitable for the higher ones; however, the names of the higher ones are not at all suitable for the lower. These names were not invented by men, but were taken from the Holy Scriptures. These various names in the sacred text necessarily indicate various spirits in the heavens, whose order Dionysius arranged. The lowest angels stimulate our pontiffs and prelates, refining them upward into the spirit, so that they may become entirely spiritual; so that they, in turn, may lead other men under them, each according to his capacity,

It also defines the term "angel" as a messenger and discusses the nomenclature and spiritual refinement within the celestial and ecclesiastical hierarchies.</summary>

<header>CONCERNING THE CELESTIAL [HIERARCHY], CH. VI.</header>

...they may be spiritualized; so that from men (as far as possible) a fourth hierarchy in Christ may be truly spiritual. These men will one day be true spirits and angels, as their spiritualization by God in Christ is continued through angels and angelic men.

### CHAPTER VI.

God, who is one and three, according to the pattern of His own internal order, has arranged triple hierarchies among the most blessed spirits; Dionysius calls these sometimes functions, sometimes distinctions, and sometimes <term>hierarchies</term>. God alone, the creator of all things and orders, undoubtedly knows everything exactly. Through angels, He revealed certain things to the prophets (whom Dionysius rightly calls "theologians," since a true theologian is one who speaks the revelations of God). To those prophets, I say, God indicated certain truths which they afterwards, as far as was permitted, committed to writing. These writings both are and are called "sacred," for they contain God's holy revelations, in which the names of the nine orders of blessed spirits are heard. 

Paul, enlightened by the Spirit of God, fully understood what these names meant and signified. He taught Dionysius the interpretation of the divine word regarding these matters. Based on Paul’s instruction, the revealing of the scriptures, and the authority of the scriptures themselves, Dionysius professes that everything he says regarding the celestial spirits comes from these sources. Indeed, he calls and names the Apostle Paul his master, guide, and teacher. 

He writes that in each <term>hierarchy</term> there are three equal orders, which reflect the Persons in God—the Hierarchy of hierarchies. Moreover, there are three hierarchies which, when tripled, form nine orders called by these names: <term>Angels</term>, <term>Archangels</term>, <term>Principalities</term>, <term>Powers</term>, <term>Virtues</term>, <term>Dominions</term>, <term>Thrones</term>, <term>Cherubim</term>, and <term>Seraphim</term>. These names were not invented by humans but were drawn from the sacred scriptures by God's prophets and theologians.

<summary>This page describes how God established three celestial hierarchies, each containing three orders, totaling nine ranks of angels. It emphasizes that this knowledge was revealed to the prophets and interpreted for Dionysius by the Apostle Paul.</summary>

<header>CH. VII. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

### CHAPTER VII.

After the Trinity, the first are the Seraphic spirits, who are entirely fiery and burning, full and perfect in their reception of God. For "Seraphim" signifies a burning fire. These are the greatest loves, resting most sweetly in divine beauty, joined in happiness with the most loving Jesus, who is God himself forever. Contemplating His blessedness, they love, venerate, and adore Him; indeed, they even learn from our most glorious Jesus things they do not know by their own nature. These most blessed loves are filled with ineffable pleasure in their adherence to divine beauty: in the contemplation of Jesus, they feel themselves flooded with all joy. This is what Peter says in his Epistle: *Things into which angels desire to look.*

Then, in the second place after these, are the Cherubic spirits, who are certain most brilliant illuminations, shining—far beyond what can be imagined—with the all-encompassing wisdom of God. These can be called "lovers of wisdom," just as those first ones were "wise lovers." For in both there is both love and wisdom. But in those first ones, because they alone are closer to God, the Truth itself, it is far greater. Therefore, what is in them is named Love. In the second ones, everything is lesser than in those others; compared to the first, they seem to be merely lights. For this reason, they have the title of Knowledge. Thus these orders differ, so that in these closer ones [the Seraphim] there is the knowledge of love, and in those [the Cherubim] the love of knowledge: in the former, love is knowledge; in the latter, knowledge is love. Among the angels, intense knowledge is love, while less intense love is knowledge. Do not think either that the highest do not know, or that the second do not love; but rather that these [the Cherubim] know lovingly, and those [the Seraphim] love knowingly. Think also of a certain compensation and equality in their difference and personal property; so that, just as those first ones subsist by their fire of love, so the second ones subsist by their light of knowledge, reflecting the wisdom of God just as the first reflect His love.

In the third place are those who, because of their unity, simplicity, constancy, and firmness, are called sometimes "Thrones" and sometimes "Seats." These also possess wisdom and love. But to them, out of their simplicity, are attributed unity, power, might, strength, and stability—qualities which are also present in the Cherubim and Seraphim. However, this is assigned to them by a peculiar attribution, because

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>## DE CAELESTI CAP. VII.</header>

They relate to the first person of the Trinity and the power of God, whose reach in all things is seen to be the most extensive. For in these resides the greater power of God, as well as His authority, severity, and—as it were—His firm and established judgment and the immobility of His decree. This is why God is said to sit on a throne, to exercise judgment, and to bring forth an irrevocable sentence. For through these angels, who are the images of His power, He will accomplish at the final judgment that which will remain as an immovable sentence. Stability comes from simplicity, and simplicity from purgation. For when a thing is purged into its own simple nature, then, being uncompounded, it remains indissoluble in its unity. From this it is clear that purgation is attributed to the **Thrones**. A thing that is purged is then illuminated, and once illuminated, it is perfected. This perfection is given to the **Seraphic** spirits, and that illumination to the **Cherubic** spirits. In all things, by every threefold reason, there is a striving with all one’s strength to imitate God; He is the very purgation that produces unities, the very illumination of those unities, and finally the very perfection of those who are illuminated. Power purges, clear truth brings serenity, and consummate 

<margin>x</margin>
love perfects. Dionysius relates that even those supreme **Seraphic** angels (which he considers a miracle) look up to **Jesus** at the right hand of the Father, and humbly and reverently adore Him. They signal by angelic signs that they gladly wish to learn from Him, and to know more fully the reason for His mysteries—especially those concerning God made man—and thus to learn from the most kind **Jesus** what they may then teach to other, inferior angels. For he understands that the angels introduced in the prophets are those where some teach others the majesty of the glorified **Jesus Christ**, having themselves as teachers learned from **Jesus**. They teach that He who ascended into heaven as a glorified man after the resurrection from the dead is the **Lord of Sabaoth** and of hosts, and of heavenly virtues, and the Lord of glory Himself. For, "Who is this king of glory?" is the question the lower angels ask of those middle ones above them in the writings of **David**; and those middle ones, commanding as **Christ** ascended, cried to the lower ones: "Lift up your gates, O you princes, and be lifted up, you eternal doors, and the King of glory shall enter in." And they replied to those who asked: "The Lord strong, the Lord mighty in battle, the Lord of hosts; He is the king of glory." He is the one who ascends the mountain of the Lord, innocent of hands and pure of heart, who has not received his soul in vain, nor sworn deceitfully to his neighbor, whom the generation of those seeking Him follows.

...of those seeking the face of the God of Jacob. Those supreme spirits, contemplating Jesus, ask one another hesitantly, "Who is this who comes from Edom, with dyed garments from Bozrah?" To them Jesus says: "It is I, who speak justice." Then the angels, encouraged by the kindness of Jesus, ask him: "Why then is your clothing red?" To which he replies: "I have trodden the winepress alone." Thus they learned the secret of the incarnation more fully from the glorified Jesus, and they taught the lower orders, simultaneously purging, illuminating, and perfecting them. For these operations occur at once. Indeed, those supreme spirits, resting in the most blessed happiness, sing together most sweetly:
> Holy, Holy, Holy, Lord God of Sabaoth.

## CHAPTER VIII.

There are nine orders of angels, which are nine images of the patterns in God. Each receives a name according to what it reflects in God. Hence, the first is characterized by love, the second by knowledge, and the third by the throne; reflecting stability, wisdom, and love in God. In the Second Hierarchy, the first order is named **Dominions**, an expressed image of the true and exemplary dominion in God. For indeed, dominion in them is simple and pure, and entirely free from servitude; it presides over all, is useful to all, and is a true and pure liberty of ruling, according to the form and pattern of the Lord. They not only strive to reflect this divine dominion within themselves, but also most earnestly endeavor to draw all things everywhere into a true dominion and into an imitation of the dominion in God; so that whatever takes on the role of dominion may truly rule in God. The Lord Jesus Christ himself, in his human form, clearly taught how we little men should imitate the dominion of God: in him we learned that dominion among men is a free servitude and a spontaneous obedience to God and to men, so that they too may obey God. To the second order, the name of **Virtues** is assigned; by which word Dionysius means that there is signified in them a certain virile, masculine, and unshakable strength, an invincible and insuperable health, and a marvelous imitation of the divine power itself:

<header>CONCERNING THE HEAVENLY HIERARCHY CH. VIII.</header>

...in which nothing is done weakly, feebly, or sluggishly. In these strengths and virtues there is zeal, care, and action; so that all things that are in God may prevail strongly and manfully by a certain chaste and masculine virtue, so that God may be represented everywhere even in virtue. Jesus Christ, the very virtue of God and his wisdom (as Paul calls him), has taught us, his little humans, health, strength, and invincible virtue, having been strengthened in him in our human form. He taught, I say, that true virtue and strength in men is patience; as an example of which Jesus, the very virtue of God, gloriously suffered even to the death of the cross. This weakening unto destruction was the strength and fortitude of God. Wherefore Paul wrote to the Corinthians:
<margin>1 Corinthians 1:25</margin>
> The weakness of God is stronger than men.

To the third order, the name of **Powers** is attributed, because they represent within themselves divine unity, simplicity, power, and authority; and in a way, they display within themselves an omnipotent state and a paternal majesty, always imitating God in producing everything toward that which is more magnificent. They not only strive for this themselves, but they also call all things in God toward a fertile and productive majesty (if I may call it such). First, they call those things nearer to them, which then derive what they have received to their neighbors in turn. For what the nine orders of angels drink in is diffused through a nine-fold progression into the entire universe, so that there is nothing so base among things that it is entirely devoid of this nine-fold generosity. For as there is an outflowing of the Trinity of God into all things, so simultaneously there is a pouring out of the three persons—as it were, triplicities—even to the smallest things; so that by a nine-fold reckoning, everything may consist in the tenth, which is God. Jesus Christ, a fertile and magnificent power, produced by paternal authority a state among men—a power, so to speak, out of all powers—each of which might daily procreate in itself a better state after the example of Jesus. Thus, by divine power, the church in Christ may consist within itself, reflecting in virtue and presiding in dominion. So God shines back as stable, wise, and loving in the Thrones, Cherubic, and Seraphic spirits. This threefold nature of the divine ray, proceeding onward, causes a divine and stable power, a wise virtue, and a most loving dominion to shine out in the Powers, Virtues, and Dominions. Thus the Trinity of God, co-equal in itself, now shines forth as a second hierarchy under that first one; which, although from that...

# CAP. IX. HIERARCHY.

...the first should degenerate, from which it receives new names, yet always remember that in every degeneration there necessarily arise at the same time all those things which are compensated; so that in the series of things, even if dissimilar and unequal, the providence of God is a likeness and equality in a wonderful way. Just as in the dissimilarity of likeness there is a certain variety and pleasant beauty, so in the likeness of dissimilarity there is at once a grave and solid constancy of all ordered things; so that beauty does not waver as if inconstant, and a constancy that is not beautiful does not displease. Moreover, that the illumination and revelation of God proceeds in order from angel to angel is taught by the prophet Zechariah; in his discourse, a certain angel learned from another angel what he had taught the prophet.
<margin><unclear>Sub angelo</unclear></margin>
> Behold, he says, the angel who spoke within me went out, and another angel went out to meet him, and said to him: "Run and speak to this boy, saying: Jerusalem shall be inhabited without walls," etc.

For purgation, illumination, and perfection descend from on high through the middle to the lowest ranks. The same Zechariah saw that the restoration of the Hebrews to their homeland from the Babylonian captivity—which he learned from an angel—that angel had learned from another more excellent one. Thus, by the testimony of those theologians, Dionysius proves that angel ministers a triple ray to angel.

### CAP. IX.

The LAST hierarchy, indeed, is that of the Principalities, Archangels, and Angels. These orders also have their own patterns in God himself, which they reflect. For what is a principality other than a certain image of the true and exalted principality in God, and a spiritual effort of drawing all things that rule throughout the world into imitation of that true and primary principality of God? This is so that those who are in a principality may rule with all love, and love with dominion.
<margin>q.</margin> Moreover, the Angels, who hold the last place in the series of divine spirits and are closer to worldly and bodily things, and who receive the name "Angel" more uniquely because they are most greatly and most openly involved in the care and announcement to the world, <margin>x</margin> reflect most of all the kind and useful announcement of God, and in...

<meta>This page continues the examination of the third hierarchy, specifically focusing on the roles of Archangels and Angels in mediating between the divine and humanity, and the establishment of a human hierarchy modeled after the celestial one.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>ON THE CELESTIAL [HIERARCHY] CHAPTER IX.</header>

By announcing these things among themselves and finally to men, those things which are derived from God through a long series of angels, they exercise themselves (as far as they can) truly and purely. <margin>8.</margin> (As their nature is more related and akin to our human minds, so their life is more familiar to us.) Between these orders is placed the middle one, which is called the Archangels. These are consistent in a middle state between the Principalities and the Angels—as in position, so in nature, strength, office, and action. They represent in God and imitate a certain principal power, both wise and full of virtue, for the purpose of announcing. These orders, like the others in the remaining hierarchies, are distinct in their own properties, yet at the same time (as Dionysius constantly asserts) co-equal. <margin>X</margin> I believe this is because of the representation of the Trinity, in which the three persons are equal. In the order of Angels, which is the final expression of the Deity, they act diligently to bring that same thing into men, and to lead men into the imitation <gloss>of</gloss> themselves and of God; so that under them also there may exist a certain triple hierarchy, namely of men on earth, alluding to that which is in the angels.

The angels long struggled to achieve this in vain, because of the resisting foolishness of man, until there came to help that great angel (as Isaiah calls him), the Angel of Great Counsel, Jesus Christ. Regarding his birth, the angels sang exultantly: "Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace to men." This son of the Virgin established a hierarchy according to the form of the angels and after the pattern of his own truth. <margin>X -12 c</margin> He built it out of those living stones he chose, whom he not only arranged in order but also gave life to within himself, so that as living beings they might be arranged in that very order. <margin>X</margin> (Furthermore, the writings of the prophets <margin>Daniel 10:20, 21</margin> testify that angels are the governors and stewards of men in the service of God.) For in Daniel, there is a Prince of the Persians and a Prince of the Greeks, and Michael is named as the Prince of the Jewish nation. For the Most High established the borders of the nations according to the number of the angels of God. Michael, as the governor of the Hebrews, had a province more obedient to him. In all other nations, however, human wickedness so resisted, and each person followed his own specific lust and perverse will, so that nothing could be compelled into order, nor even into the image of order. The cause of this evil (as Dionysius says) was both the self-love and pride of each person. Yet Michael at least achieved some shadow of the truth among the Hebrews, whom he trained in certain figures, <margin>Revelation 12:7</margin> so that afterward, at some time, they might rise higher...

<header>CHAPTER IX. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

...might be promoted. Finally, the magnificent Christ, the Lord of all, not only called people from every nation into the love of God and obedience, but he also caused those who love him to obey all the angels then serving more eagerly for the sake of human salvation in the Savior Jesus, so that everywhere people might be converted and live. Indeed, let them be converted to the life-giving ray of God, now that the sun of justice itself, Jesus, has risen. <margin>512!</margin> As Dionysius says, the spiritual light of God has always shone upon the minds of people in the world, and people have always had the choice to turn toward the light; nothing stood in the way <margin>x o</margin> except a perverse will. 

<margin>𝓍, x</margin>
Before Jesus was born, Melchizedek from among the gentiles turned toward God and earned the title of priest of the most high God.

<margin>(2) Luke 2:14</margin>
When Jesus was born, the angels also announced peace on earth to people of good will. But consider this: God’s help works together with the power of the will, and within grace people act freely, so that from both together is born the freedom for good. Thus, unless a person wills it, they do not admit the light; and unless they are illuminated, they do not will to admit it. The will is the reason why the soul admits the light, and the light is simultaneously the reason why the soul wills it. The soul, warmed by grace, chooses the good by its own freedom—the same freedom by which it can refuse it. Without grace there is truly no freedom, and within grace, there is nothing but freedom. In the radiating light there is help, so that whoever wishes to do so may turn to God. Those who turn themselves are those who are sufficiently helped so that they desire to turn. Since the birth of Christ, the Sun has come closer to us, and the light poured into humanity is more effective; therefore, the conversion of people has been more abundant—specifically those who, placed in freedom of choice through grace, have willed to turn.

But let us set these matters aside, for the mind’s edge grows dull when blunted by the difficulty of such a question. Let us return to Dionysius, who affirms that God’s power and kindness are equal everywhere, but the human will is not good everywhere. He states that the gracious ray is spread equally to everyone, but people do not relate to it equally. God has an equal concern for all people, and there is one God for all; likewise, equal angels are appointed over all their places, managing their provinces with equal care. For what of Pharaoh, the king of the Egyptians? Did not a good angel, the prince of that region, indicate the dream to him—which Joseph, having been instructed by an angel, then explained as he intended? Likewise

<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine light and human will, transitioning from the universal availability of grace to the specific roles of angelic "proconsuls" and the structure of the celestial hierarchies.</meta>

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>184 ON THE CELESTIAL [HIERARCHY] CAP. X.</header>

Was not Daniel the interpreter of the vision for the King of the Babylonians, which the angel-proconsul of that nation put into his mind? What else, then, can be said but that there is one God of all, who is equally good to everyone? For he is no respecter of persons; rather, some people are rejectors of God. Likewise, he asserts that angels are distributed everywhere as administrators of the world, with equal power, zeal, and kindness. He claims that those ancient Hebrews were in no way chosen above the rest; the lot of all is equal, and there is one common freedom for everyone. The Hebrews were more acceptable to God only because they obeyed their proconsul, Michael, more easily. That Michael held the leadership of the Jews, just as other nations had other archangels, is clearly shown by the speech of Daniel; in his tenth chapter it says: *And there is no one who helps me in all these things except Michael, your prince.* But that God is equally God to all is also testified by David, saying: *God is the King of all the earth.* And Paul asks: *Is he the God of the Jews only? Is he not also the God of the Gentiles? Yes, of the Gentiles also.* But because the ancient Hebrews were more obedient to angelic inspirations, it is written in David: *Since the Lord has chosen Jacob for himself, and Israel for his possession.*

That hierarchy especially works out stability, knowledge, and love in the human hierarchy, so that the foremost among men, drawn up on high together with them, may purely, clearly, and ardently gaze upon all things.
