### CHAPTER XII.

<margin>charity</margin>
**LIKEWISE**, the priest of the human church is called an angel by Malachi; which is (as Dionysius says) because of the imitation of angels, the announcement of truth, and the zeal and duty of purifying, illuminating, and perfecting those who are committed to his care. For as we said above, according to the opinion of Dionysius, all things are in all things, but in those that follow they appear with a more degenerate mark, and in humans most degenerately. Yet in these very humans, especially those who are better, there is such an imitation of the angels that humans also seem to be able to be called angels in a certain way by right; [to these humans there is in some way a cause from imitation why they are called angels.<gloss>1</gloss>] <margin>W</margin> Furthermore, they are even called Gods in *Holy Scripture* from their change and relation to the deity as far as they are able. Those who are carried toward this with all piety along with the angels are called Gods together with the angels. Moreover, that angels are called Gods is testified by that passage in Genesis, spoken to Jacob by the man wrestling with him: "If you have been strong against God, how much more against men." That angel called himself God. And David: "A great King above all Gods." Regarding humans; in Exodus, the Lord says to Moses: "Behold, I have made you a god to Pharaoh." And David: "I said, You are gods, and all of you are children of the Most High." Which the Savior himself teaches in John, is to be explained regarding the humans to whom the word of God was spoken. Let us conclude, therefore, that just as things are from above, so the names are derived at the same time to humans.

<header>CHAPTERS XV, XVI. ON THE HIERARCHY.</header>

## CHAPTER XV.

ANGELS, having been taught, in turn teach; they turn toward God, they stand firm in themselves, and they pour down their influence below. These things are indicated to us through the figures and likenesses of angels by (prophetic theologians), who most freely use (the image of fire). The powers of fire known to us are very similar to those of the angels, and it is by this means that the celestial spirits are best demonstrated to us. (From this sensible world of darkness, therefore, fire is primarily chosen so that what takes place in that luminous world of the angels may be taught;) for this fire, by its own light, greatly imitates that true light which is inaccessible. Likewise, they mostly use the excellent nature of man, because this is most familiar to us; and, seeking similarities from all its parts, they represent angels as if they were men. Furthermore, from many other and various things familiar to us, they signify to humans the natures, powers, actions, properties, likeness, difference, simplicity, constancy, stability, strength, obedience, order, beauty, light, wisdom, sweetness, kindness, vigor, speed, subtlety, freedom, presidency, power, authority, equity, governance, fruitfulness, fruit, life-giving power, holiness, religion, the worship of God, happiness, blessedness, kingdom, abundance, plenty, riches, clarity, and finally contemplation and joy of those blessed spirits. Indeed, (ancient theologians) and (those great prophets) have sought these sensory things and metaphors from everywhere and from every part, with (wonderful wisdom and charity, so that by this way and method they might both teach lowly humans the things of God and preserve the majesty of divine things.)

## CHAPTER XVI.

GOD, the infinite good, who entirely fills from within and infinitely surrounds from without, is light itself, in whom there is no darkness at all. Therefore, when He shines in the darkness, the darkness did not comprehend Him. He is also absolute in Himself, apart from matter, place, time, degrees, or cause.

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>188</page-num>
<header>ON THE CELESTIAL [HIERARCHY] CHAPTER XVI.</header>

All things desire that absolute end so that they may be perfected, from which all things were made <insert>and by whom all these same things are arranged according to his own pattern. He is therefore the beginning from which all things exist;</insert> and the medium through which all things are beautifully ordered; and finally the end by which all things are completely perfected. This is what Paul says in the Epistle to the Romans in these words: *From him and through him and in him are all things: to him be glory forever.* Because out of himself, according to his own pattern, and for his own sake, he makes, arranges, and perfects all things. Inasmuch as he creates powerfully, he is the Father; inasmuch as he arranges wisely, he is the Son; inasmuch as he perfects kindly, he is the Holy Spirit. From this comes existence, order, and desire in things; from this all created things both exist, are beautiful, and are good. 

This being, beauty, and goodness are entirely in the beginning, and there they are a certain unity in which all things are the same; which, because of its infinite power, propagates within itself an infinite offspring, which is infinite within the infinite. This is the eternal, beautiful Son of the eternal, omnipotent Father; between whom an eternal and infinite love necessarily flourishes. These three, because of their shared infinity, are both equal to each other and most similar, and their substance is unique and most simple. Regarding these, it is not lawful for man to speak otherwise than of three persons and one God: the beginning, the middle, and the end of all things. In whom, except for their <term>relationships</term> to one another, whatever belongs to one person belongs to all. 

For the Father is power that is wise and good; the Son is wisdom that is powerful and good; the Holy Spirit is goodness that is powerful and wise. From that triple unity, the universe is established powerfully, wisely, and well; in which there is likewise propagation and love, essence, sense, and desire in all things. All of these consist in the beginning, middle, and end; they are stable in themselves, shine beautifully, and indeed thrive well <note>They act—C.</note> in him from whom they received their being, form, and goodness. Toward that ineffable Trinity, those most blessed spirits whom we call angels constantly direct the eyes of their minds. All of them truly and attentively gaze upon those three persons, but in different ways for different reasons; yet all contemplate all things and all the principles of things.

# CHAPTER XVI. THE <term>HIERARCHY</term>.

The <term>Seraphim</term>, who aim more closely and attentively at the end, contemplate all things lovingly and sweetly in the end—the beginning, the middle, and the power and wisdom of God. Yet they contemplate the end itself more attentively than the other things. The <term>Cherubim</term> also contemplate the end with the <term>Seraphim</term>, but in the end they look more at the middle; the <term>Thrones</term>, in the end, contemplate the beginning more. Therefore, these three orders of the first hierarchy strive more toward the good end and the <note>Holy Spirit</note> of God. Truly, among those who are caught up by the force of the end and the good so that they are occupied with God alone—although in the consideration of that end, the <term>Seraphim</term>, contemplating the end itself, are most of all called "flames" and "loves"; the <term>Cherubim</term> are said to be "knowing" of the middle, order, and beauty in the end; and the <term>Thrones</term>, in the end, are named the "standing" because of the beginning, power, and stability—nonetheless, that entire third and supreme hierarchy, because it aims mostly at the third person <note>the Holy Spirit</note> in whom each sees most clearly what belongs to them, relates to the other two hierarchies just as the first order in a single hierarchy relates to its other two orders. And although in that sacred governance there are those who are loving, wise, and standing in the end and goodness of God, yet that entire trinity of orders, compared to the other hierarchies, can and certainly should be thought of as "loving."

Likewise, the second hierarchy, which tends entirely toward the middle and perceives all things in the middle and in order and <term>wisdom</term>—though in a threefold and varied way, which is why three orders are placed in it as well—that second hierarchy, I say, compared to the others, can be called "knowledge" in its entirety. Finally, the third hierarchy, which is carried entirely and chiefly toward the beginning, in which they see and worship all things (though through a different reasoning within its triple order), this whole hierarchy, compared to the others, can rightly be called <term>Thrones</term> and "those who are seated."

In the second hierarchy, however, although all the spirits wonder at all things, they look upon the middle and the pattern through which all things are made more closely and, so to speak, more willingly. But the <term>Dominions</term> see the end in that middle; the <term>Virtues</term> see the middle in the middle; and the <term>Powers</term> see the beginning in the middle. This middle hierarchy contemplates all things in the middle; and compared to the others, it can be called "knowledge" as a whole: it is concerned mostly with that reason and wisdom through which the universe is crafted orderly and beautifully. The third hierarchy, which depends most on the beginning, in which there are the <term>Principalities</term> aiming at the end within the beginning;

<summary>This page explains how the nine angelic orders are divided into three hierarchies, each corresponding to an aspect of the Trinity: the highest hierarchy focuses on the "End" (Holy Spirit/Love), the middle on the "Middle" (Son/Wisdom), and the lowest on the "Beginning" (Father/Power).</summary>

# CHAPTER XVI. CONCERNING THE CELESTIAL <term>HIERARCHY</term>.

...the <term>Archangels</term> tend to the middle in the beginning; the <term>Angels</term> to the beginning in the beginning. Thus, the last order of each hierarchy tends toward the beginning: the <term>Angels</term> toward the beginning itself; the <term>Powers</term> toward the beginning in the middle; and the <term>Thrones</term> toward the beginning in the end. The middle hierarchy tends toward the middle, and in every hierarchy, all the middle orders tend toward the middle; and the last orders in each tend toward the end.

Therefore, it seems that the first hierarchy <note>the lowest</note>, being more remote from God, cares for the existence of everything through the power of God; the middle hierarchy for the form of everything through the reason of God; and that last and supreme hierarchy for their perfection. Truly, in the first hierarchy, those who are of the first order care most for existence; those of the second, mostly for the form in existence; those of the third, mostly for the perfection in the existence of everything. In the middle hierarchy, those of the first order care most for existence in form; those of the second, mostly for the form itself; and those of the third, mostly for perfection in form. In that third and supreme hierarchy, those who are first care most for existence in perfection; those of the second, mostly for form in perfection; and those of the third, mostly for perfection itself. Thus, by the ministry of the <term>Angels</term>, all things exist, are formed, and are completed.

In these matters, the angels act diligently so that man may go forth toward God—from multiplicity into simplicity, and from foul despair into pure hope—and that he may stand firmly in that hope. Through hope in the powerful ray of God, man may now be spiritually in God and Christ. In the all-encompassing wisdom of God, those knowing <term>Cherubic</term> spirits, and within them the <term>Virtues</term>, and within those the <term>Archangels</term>, reveal the reasons for things and the beautiful truths of the Word of God. By revealing these, they illuminate the man who already hopes in God through faith in Jesus Christ, who is the very Reason, Word, and Messenger of Truth. In Him, every declaration of light flows in order from the middle angels to men, so that those already purged into hope may believe in that Word of God.

<note>The Jews seem to have had hope more than faith before the coming of Christ.</note>

<note>Likewise, Christians under Christ himself while he lived on earth had faith more than charity, which is the Holy Spirit.</note>

Finally, in the goodness, love, and Holy Spirit of God, the <term>Seraphic</term> spirits—burning entirely with that love—and the <term>Dominations</term>—burning entirely with wisdom—and finally the <term>Principalities</term>—burning entirely with the power of God—each in their respective order (the lower in the superior, and all in the heat of the Spirit of God), men clearly shining in faith are solidly, seriously, sweetly, and perfectly filled with the love of God. Thus, having been purged by the <term>Angels</term>, <term>Powers</term>, and <term>Thrones</term>; illuminated by the <term>Archangels</term>, <term>Virtues</term>, and <term>Cherubim</term>; they are at last perfected by the <term>Principalities</term>, <term>Dominations</term>, and <term>Seraphim</term> in the Spirit of God.

Just as there are nine orders of angels, so similarly there are nine orders of men [who are in God through Christ and by the ministry of angels. All things <margin>x</margin> in these are in all. Indeed, all hope, believe, and love God in Christ. <margin>X Left hand margin.</margin> Otherwise, in this triple ecclesiastical hierarchy, it does not nor can it exist. <page-num>194</page-num>
# ON THE CELESTIAL [HIERARCHY] CHAPTER XVI.

He who lives forever created all things for Himself. Those empyrean spirits, inhabitants of heaven, were created where it shines like fire above the foundation. They were indeed created good; but a great part of them, by their own will not long after, wanting to exalt themselves through wicked pride, fell from the highest place to the lowest. Our Savior Jesus testified to this when He said: "I saw Satan falling like lightning from heaven." Likewise, he did not stand in the truth. The remaining part, looking up to God, partly by their own will and partly by assisting grace, remained happy in the truth; they were made blessed by God, not by their own merits, but by grace. On the other hand, that Lucifer with his followers did not fall because of God's disdain, but by his own fault. These wretched and lost angels inhabit this thick and misty air around us as testers of men, so that they do not become sluggish through negligence and sloth; they will be even more miserable sometime after the final judgment. Paul teaches us that we have struggles with the princes and powers of this air. For they continually try to make us like them, so that we may feel a punishment equal to theirs. But for the protection of men while they live here, angels are assigned by the mercy of God. For everyone has his own guardian angel and preserver, and likewise the same angel as a constant advisor and stirrer toward the good; otherwise, weak man would neither be strong enough to persist in good nor to resist evil. It is the opinion of some that if those who labor make progress, they will also progress in blessedness; which these debaters call <term>accidental merit</term>. We are a concern to all the angels; but those to whom we are entrusted as guardians watch over us with more intensive care. For this reason, those who honor and worship their guardian angel daily with some special prayer act most rightly.

Demons—impure spirits, rational, subtle, and envious—strive to harm the human race, desire to deceive, delude the senses, and infect the affections of men. Whether people are sleeping or awake, they disturb the world and trouble the elements. As hypocrites, they often transform themselves into angels of light; in idols, they claim the majesty of God for themselves; in their pride, they aspire to power. They continuously wage war against men, sometimes attacking them openly, sometimes lying in wait for them secretly. When they conquer, they exult; when conquered

# CHAPTER XVI. THE HIERARCHY.

Indeed, they depart in confusion, so that they do not dare to tempt further in the same way and toward the same sin. David incessantly complains about these evil natures in his hymns; and, as if always in danger, he implores help for himself and calls down harsh curses upon those demons.<insert>)</insert> For he says in a certain psalm: "May it please You, Lord, to rescue me; Lord, look to help me. Likewise, in another: "O God, attend to my help; Lord, look to help me. Let those who seek my soul be confounded and put to shame." Indeed, he does almost nothing else in nearly all his psalms than pray to God in Christ, so that he might emerge victorious among so many and such great enemies, and be made safe in God.<insert>)</insert> Men are called by God through *Jesus Christ* to this dangerous war. One must not go into it rashly or without consideration, but one's strength must be weighed beforehand, and the dangers provided for. And before anyone becomes a Christian, or a priest and religious person in Christianity, let him first prudently consider within himself what he will be able to do; lest the devil, as an open enemy, should he be victorious, act more harshly and unhappily with him than if he had remained a subject and tributary to *Satan*. 

For this reason indeed, *Jesus Christ*, the leader and commander of this war, when He called His fellow soldiers to Him (as *Luke* reports)—although He wants all men to be saved—nevertheless, so that those fleeing Scylla do not fall into Charybdis, nor those seeking salvation fall into greater damnation, He warned the crowds to be careful and look ahead considerately at what they were about to take upon themselves. He preferred that they live as servants of the devil rather than be killed as enemies in war, and that they be condemned by a lesser condemnation rather than a greater one. Therefore He said to them: "What king, going to engage in war against another king, does not first sit down and think if he is able with ten thousand to meet him who comes against him with twenty thousand? Otherwise, while the other is still far away, he sends a delegation and asks for terms of peace." When we have professed some better life in Christ, then the devil prepares himself with a more abundant and better-equipped army to attack and harass us; if this tyrant overcomes us as rebels, he will torture us more cruelly, just as *Paul* writes to the *Hebrews*: <insert>(</insert>It would be better not to know the way of jus-

# CHAPTER XVI. ON THE CELESTIAL HIERARCHY.

...rather than being turned backward. Moreover, demons are subtle by nature, observing natural things with a sharp insight; they are clever from long experience and practice, diviners of future events, and sometimes they are even very knowledgeable regarding higher revelations. But being evil themselves, they use all things for evil purposes and plot the eternal destruction of men above all else; in their envy, they cannot endure the glory that humans are to have in Christ. They urge evil under the appearance of good; and if they ever urge something good, they do even that for an evil end. They always discourage true good, and if they ever discourage evil, it is only to lead someone into a greater evil. Sometimes they harm the body and break limbs, or bring on diseases; they present empty forms to the senses and overwhelm the mind with false images. For this reason, Peter wrote to us: "Be sober and vigilant, because your adversary the devil walks about like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour." By divine grace and the help of the angels, and through continuous prayers and acts of penance, we are able to resist the devil and overcome him—especially by imitating our leader, Christ, who conquered when He was tempted and broke the power of the devil.

## JOHN COLET ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY OF SAINT DIONYSIUS.

### CHAPTER I.

<margin>To all of us [the universal body of priests]</margin>
Sacred scripture teaches us that the human priesthood possesses within itself both divine wisdom and action from on high. Within the human priesthood are indeed all those who are consecrated in Christ God. The meaning of the sacred scriptures is entirely spiritual, and Jesus revealed it in its entirety to his own followers. From this, it should be believed that the sacrifices, rites, and ceremonies <note>Reading "ceremonias" for "ceremoniæ"</note> in the church grew up together with it, according to the institutions of the Apostles. After his resurrection, Jesus himself revealed <note>Reading "prodidit" for "prodibit"</note> what the scriptures intended regarding the condition and state of the new church within him. Therefore, that spiritual sense—which requires a prophetic spirit to be understood—is the wisdom and rationale of our entire established Christian church. For Moses spoke of Christ; a fact to which Pope Leo, a venerable authority who held the apostolic helm, also testified in a sermon on the Fast of Pentecost, with these words:

> "It is not to be doubted," he said, "dearly beloved, that every Christian observance is of divine instruction, and whatever has been received into custom by the church through devotion proceeds from apostolic tradition and the teaching of the Holy Spirit. He now also presides over the hearts of the faithful through his instructions, so that all may both keep them obediently and understand them wisely."

These are the words of Leo the First, who sat on the pontifical throne four hundred and fifty years after Christ. Regarding this illumination of the scriptures—conveyed not so much by words as by deeds, not so much by doctrine and writings as by reality, and the institution and command of the apostles—Saint Dionysius was about to write to Timothy...

<summary>John Colet introduces the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy, explaining that the Christian priesthood and church rituals are divinely instituted and possess a deep spiritual meaning revealed by Jesus. He cites Pope Leo I to affirm that church traditions and observances proceed from the Holy Spirit and apostolic tradition.</summary>
<keywords>John Colet, Dionysius, Pope Leo I, Ecclesiastical Hierarchy, priesthood, Sacred Scripture, Apostolic tradition, Holy Spirit, church rites</keywords>

<meta>This page continues John Colet’s commentary on Dionysius's "Ecclesiastical Hierarchy," focusing on the necessity of keeping divine mysteries secret from the unworthy and the structured transmission of divine light through the hierarchy.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>

<header>ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL [HIERARCHY] CHAPTER I.</header>

Timothy, first he solemnly and repeatedly charges him not to wickedly betray the <term>rationales</term> of the sacred rites, the holy of holies, and the <term>mysteries</term> of God and divine worship to the common people, nor to share them except with the most holy and those like himself. He is mindful of that precept of Jesus Christ: *Do not give what is holy to dogs, nor pearls to swine.* Instead, he should guard everything holily, soberly, and in seclusion, and store and worship them most religiously in the secret chamber of his mind with all love and reverence. He imitates that Jesus, the author of all priesthood and sacred things, who taught mysteries only to his own disciples, and this in private. Even to them, he did not teach everything, for they were not yet perfected and spiritual, nor could they bear everything. 

That same ineffable light now at the right hand of the Father shines more clearly and fully upon the angels, who are by nature far purer than men. It reveals itself more abundantly to them, and then through them to those men who, having been called away and separated from the body, stand out higher and approach closer to the angelic nature, being purified, illuminated, and perfected. 

In this class, that eminent man <term>John, son of Zebedee</term>, was one who, by the revelation of an angel on the Lord’s day, saw many things which he described to the seven churches. To be rightly understood, these things certainly require the same angel as a revealer; these things that scribe of God called the <term>Apocalypse</term>. Revelation to men through angels is done under the beauty of figures, so that <unclear>* * * *</unclear> the recognition of the degree and devotion to God through which men are established in one or another state in Christ, so that they may be differently illuminated in him and perfected once illuminated. 

In all these things in Christ, there is truly one principle in a way, and (as <term>Dionysius</term> says) one intention, and everyone strives to do the same according to the strength of <term>grace</term>. For there are many distributions of graces, but one Spirit; to each is given the manifestation of the Spirit for their benefit, to one in this way, to another in that. Indeed, the effort of all is to be perfected by divine rationales and to be made like God as much as possible. He who achieves this most is the <term>pontiff</term> and rightly holds the first place of ministry, so that he may then pass on what he has received; the greater he is, the more he should serve many by the office of charity and by ministering. Then, those who are second perform this and pass on what they have received to those who are third. And so the perfecting of men in Christ proceeds and advances, with everyone striving upward and handing down what they have drawn from above to those below, so that

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy, moving from the necessity of keeping divine mysteries secret to the active role of members within the hierarchy, specifically focusing on the flow of divine love and the imitation of Christ.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I. HIERARCHY.</header>
<meta>catchword: In this</meta>

...by the desire and love of God and neighbor, they may be perfect members in Christ; receiving indeed through the love of God, and giving also through the love of neighbor. Thus, being fashioned as just by love on both sides, they may be ministers of divine grace, from whom all things come in Christ. In him, everyone ought to be so ready to give all that he has received, that he seems to have received it for no other reason than to give; so that in the act of giving, the receiving is revealed, and in the love of neighbor, the love of God. We declare that we love God (as John teaches in his Epistle) only if we love our neighbor.

> If anyone says that he loves God, and hates his brother, he is a liar.

In this reciprocal charity there is order, duty, and office in the church, and in this, the whole ecclesiastical hierarchy consists: in the love of God and neighbor, in receiving and giving, in the imitation of Jesus the head, who is charity itself, and in the effort of each person to sanctify himself with the fire of charity, so that he may then sanctify others. Thus, through the office of sanctifying, the whole church may imitate Jesus, so that they may eventually become entirely like him; as John writes:

> All who have this hope in him sanctify themselves, even as he is holy.

And the same says:

> He who says he abides in him ought himself also to walk just as he walked.

That Jesus began the dance in this ecclesiastical choral dance, so that everyone might follow him in a line with joined hands. He was the fullness of justice, so that all might imitate his justice in him—a justice which consists in the giving and the love of what you have received out of love. For what other perfection is there in the angels than the giving of what has been received? In this giving, they resemble God more than in their receiving. For in receiving there is no perfection. God, indeed, receives nothing. But God gives most of all, and is most truly represented by giving. Therefore, Paul says:

> it is more blessed to give than to receive.

In Christ, therefore, in whom are all things, who is the supreme reason and definition of our order and hierarchy; who received nothing from us, who gives all things, who established us in the course of truth, who raised us above the Mosaic law into the light of the gospel and revelation, who through faith made us wise and taught us to act from faith, who by his spirit led us toward improvement, so that by the law of the spirit we might rightly imitate those most blessed spirits. In his power, we begin to be spiritual, so that we may eventually be perfect spirits, whose most blessed heavenly life we strive to reflect; therefore Paul said:

> our citizenship is in heaven.

<page-num>200</page-num>
# ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY, CHAPTER I.

...nevertheless, in a truly happy state in Jesus Christ, because we are all still visible humans, our new nature, its powers, and its actions are usefully indicated and strengthened by certain underlying sensible signs. These serve as monuments for us and as invitations to those things which are not seen. For as long as we rely on symbols and see through a mirror darkly, none of our instruction, fashioning, formation, action, or imitation of the spirits can lack physical images. For as long as we are bodily, it is necessary that our sacraments and our ecclesiastical order be somewhat physical. Under Moses, the legal hierarchy was entirely physical and symbolic; under Christ (in whom we now imitate the truth of the spirits), the ecclesiastical hierarchy is established, being partly physical and partly spiritual. 

In that triumphant hierarchy, however, it will be entirely spiritual, when our natural bodies are completely transformed into spiritual bodies. From these enticements of grace and signs, we will then break forth into the presence and the truth; so that, with the angels, we who are then true may truly see the truth itself face to face. Everyone ought to strive toward that One, and into His form, as much as they possibly can. 

Jesus is the one prize <note>Latin: *bravium*, a prize or reward in a race.</note> of our church toward which we all run, so that we may fully grasp Him. Each person ought to reflect Him in themselves, and to smell and taste of Him—though certainly not all in the same way or equally, but each according to their ability and what has been given to each, just as God has distributed a measure of faith. Let everyone, as they judge themselves able, strive to reflect that which is the head and the One. Even if one does not reflect Him in the highest degree, let them reflect Him in as high a degree as possible, so that they do not seem unwilling to do what they can, and thus abuse their grace (which is given to each for the manifestation of the spirit); nor should they seem to languish by choice when they are able to be strong, for such a voluntary sickness is damnable. 

For he wickedly erases the image who does not represent it as far as it is imprinted. Since we are all sealed by one seal and have received the character of one king, like His coinage, so that we may reflect the One on whom all things depend; we must act according to the nature of that sign and impression. In that first One who is Jesus Christ, in whom we are sealed with His figure and character—so that we may be <term>Jesuits</term> <note>Literally "those belonging to Jesus" or followers of Jesus.</note> in Him—we are sealed to Him, consecrated in the priesthood in Him, so that to God in—

# CHAPTER I. HIERARCHY.

...we ourselves may be priests, so that we may sacrifice ourselves to God in Him, and offer a holy sacrifice, just as He offered—who died for us, as Peter writes, so that we might follow in His footsteps. If we are in Him, as John testifies, we ought to walk just as He walked. Let everyone offer himself as a burnt offering to God in Christ, so that he may burn entirely with charity on the altar of the cross. In this we know the charity of God: because He laid down His life for us; and we ought to lay down our lives for the brothers. — But now let us discuss the ecclesiastical hierarchy.

<margin>Vicar of Jesus Christ</margin>
EVERY hierarchy is the system and comprehension of sacred things. In the Christian hierarchy, it is the pontifical duty and office to contain all sacred things within itself and to possess all things. For the <term>pontiff</term> is the sacrament itself, and the very comprehension in himself of all things which follow in the church. He knows and represents the whole priesthood fully and clearly within himself, so that there is nothing in any subordinate which is given by God that is not also in the Christ-like pontiff—both more solidly and more clearly, and even in a more perfect manner. Indeed, through him all spiritual and divine things ought to be poured into all the orders of the church; nothing should proceed in the church which is not first contained in the pontiff, nor should anything praiseworthy and divine exist in anyone under the pontiff that is not recognized as having come from him. If he lacks what is in any subordinate that proceeds from God—such as holiness, wisdom, and justice—he is certainly not the one who should occupy the pontifical seat. The creator and founder of our hierarchy is that same one, most almighty Jesus. The Father through Him with the Holy Spirit—that adorable Trinity—out of His goodness, is certainly the cause for this human hierarchy to exist, militating under the leader Christ, under the banner of truth, with spiritual weapons, and also that it may exist well and perfectly. For the good God willed that man should be saved; which certainly cannot be unless men are deified and reformed into a divine state, becoming gods assimilated to God, so that they may reflect God. For all things strive most earnestly and intensely toward that end: to be fashioned into the divine form. To that end, all who are called into Christ are consecrated to God, so that while they live here, they may reflect God as much as they can. However, as much as humans are able, Jesus—God and man—taught, who...

He willed to become man so that He might demonstrate the divine way of living among humans. Toward His likeness, all who are in Him ought to strive. For he who says he remains in Him ought to walk just as He Himself walked. He is the head of both the celestial and human hierarchy; in Him, the earth found a model to imitate, and now in heaven, the angels also have a model to imitate. Everyone ought to burn with love for Him, so that, being drawn toward Him, one gazes upon nothing other than Him, turns one's eyes to no other direction, and looks back at nothing at all. Instead (as Paul writes concerning himself to the Philippians), *forgetting those things which are behind and reaching forward to those things which are ahead, pursuing the goal of the prize of the upward call in Christ Jesus*, one should imitate Him to gain Him, being conformed to His death so that, by rising, he may be conformed to Him in glory. Thus, at last, in dying, he may take hold of that for which he was taken hold of by Jesus. 

Let all who are in Him strive with all their might <note>Literally "with hands and feet."</note> toward the unique principle of their perfection. Being formed by Him, they should reflect Him to the highest degree possible—constantly raised up, nourished, refreshed, and recreated by Him daily, transferred from glory to glory, until they are thoroughly transformed into Him. Toward Him, I say, let them be carried, eagerly burning with charity, so that in Him they may be offered and sacrificed like burnt offerings and sweet-smelling incense to God by the fire of charity. Moreover, let the flight from these lower things be so sharp and anxious that one always thinks enemies and death are pressing from behind. Let him always set before himself the truth of the goal toward which he directs himself as his highest aim; let him rise up, run, and fly toward Him, take hold of Him, consume Him, be nourished by Him, and be thoroughly transformed into Christ Himself in God. 

This wholesome struggle should truly belong to everyone in the church, so that in all things everyone may appear, sound, smell, taste, and act like Christ. God deifies <note>Meaning the process of "theosis" or becoming god-like through grace.</note>, imparting Himself to the angels directly and simply, but to humans indirectly and through a complex system of images. The Holy Scripture is full of these images (which <term>Dionysius</term> calls the "substance of our priesthood"), especially the Old Testament. To this is added the New Testament, written by the evangelists and apostles, which is not without such symbols. In the time of Dionysius, these books were included among the holy and canonical writings by the authority of the early church and approved with all reverence. To these was added a certain more secret system of wisdom not committed to writing by...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmission of divine mysteries, explaining that the apostles did not commit the inner reasons of the sacraments to writing but entrusted them to the hearts of their successors to protect them from the unworthy.</meta>

<language>Modern English (translated from Latin)</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I. THE HIERARCHY. 203</header>

...from the apostles, but preserved holily and religiously in their minds and hearts, things which those worthy men learned from Jesus Christ. They subsequently handed these down only to those who were worthy and like themselves, adjuring them not to commit these matters to writing, lest divine things become common, and *holy things be given to dogs, and pearls to swine*. The sublimity of John’s mind shadowed forth what he saw in that genre with his own figures, so that almost no one understands it except himself, unless he is moved by the same prophetic spirit. Furthermore, the <term>institutions</term> celebrated in the church and derived from the apostles themselves down to us were not handed down by them in writing, but were entrusted to the actions of the common people. The <term>reasons</term> for these actions were held only by the primary men in the church; they did not reveal them except to those whom they recognized as more purified and promoted to the <term>pontifical</term> dignity, who understood the naked reasons of things and of the sacraments.

It was by the counsel of the apostles that these same things were offered to the common people through a certain imaginary method, so that the unrefined might drink and be nourished by what is coarser. For, as Paul says, *knowledge is not in all*. The holy bread, by the Savior’s testimony, is not to be given to dogs. The bread of the priests is prepared only from the fine flour of sincerity. However, that which the common people are fed must have some bran of images and likenesses, lest their eyes be either too much dazzled by the truth itself, or they be stirred up into vain thoughts.

Hence the sacraments and ceremonies—whether expiating, illuminating, or perfecting—were established for the common people by the apostles themselves with the best reasoning and described through the most beautiful likenesses. The reasons for them, however, were not committed to writing but were entrusted to and retained in the minds of the holy <term>pontiffs</term>, so that just as the signs succeed among the common people, so the reasons succeed in the minds of the <term>pontiffs</term>. <note>The word "pontiff" here refers generally to high-ranking prelates or bishops.</note> William, the Bishop of Mende, <note>Referring to William Durand (Gulielmus Durandus), author of the *Rationale Divinorum Officiorum*.</note> attempted at last to extract these many years later; in this study, he prophesies and divines as best he can, not unlike those who speak on what the Revelation of John signifies. In all such matters, whether written with such a thickness of signs or entrusted to custom and use, there is need either of a revealer to whom it was revealed, or of the same prophetic spirit by which they were established, so that he may point it out; otherwise, human vision must necessarily grow dull. But whatever things are in celebrated use in the church as symbols and seals of divine reasons, it must be believed that they were instituted by the apostles; whose...

<summary>The author explains that the apostles intentionally withheld the deep "reasons" behind church sacraments from the written word and the common people to prevent the debasement of holy things. These mysteries were instead preserved in the minds of bishops and presented to the public through symbols and ceremonies.</summary>
<keywords>apostolic tradition, sacraments, John the Apostle, Paul the Apostle, William Durand (Bishop of Mende), symbols, hierarchy, sacred mysteries</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmission of divine mysteries, explaining how the apostles and early church fathers used visible signs and oral tradition to protect and convey spiritual truths to different levels of the faithful.</meta>

The learned held the reasons from God himself and (to use the words of Dionysius) covered the heavenly sacraments with visible signs, placing for the unrefined the simple within the complex, spiritual things in corporeal forms, divine things in human, remote things in the near, and foreign things in the familiar. Concerning these things, as they wrote very little at the beginning, they committed the greatest part to the memories of men. For as they went out preaching and announcing Christ, they shaped those they found willing to believe with further doctrine as it seemed fit. They arranged them in order, placing priests and elders over that church, whom the younger should obey. They also left certain sacramental seals in appropriate words and signs, which were to be for the people like supports, aids, and timely reminders of a higher and simpler truth; by these, the Christian masses are kept in religion, and religion is protected from the profane. The <term>pontiffs</term> <note>high priests or bishops</note>, however, are drawn into the light itself, for they hold the truths of the signs; the Christian common people are given the outward color, whereas the Jewish people were merely shadowed. In the Christian religion, the <term>pontiff</term> Dionysius was skilled in the mysteries of the sacraments. Writing to Timothy, he adjures him not to share them except with holy men like himself—learned and good men of the <term>pontifical</term> order—on the condition that they also only share them with such individuals, so that holy things (contrary to the Gospel precept) are not given to dogs, nor pearls to swine.
<margin>What is that Gospel precept?</margin>

## CHAPTER II. 1.

### *Concerning Baptism.*

Our Christianity desires nothing else except that we be made like God. *Be perfect*, says the Savior, *even as your Father in heaven is perfect*. We are made like Him by doing and achieving what He commands. We will clearly be made like Him if we love Him; indeed, we will love Him if we keep His commandments. *If anyone loves me, he keeps my word.* Love is the beginning of all things. From love you believe, and from faith you hope. Love precedes faith; hope follows. As Polycarp writes to the Philippians: By love we are born again into a new

<summary>The author explains how spiritual truths are hidden within physical signs to accommodate the common people while being fully understood by the high clergy, and introduces the concept of Baptism as the beginning of becoming like God through love.</summary>
<keywords>Dionysius the Areopagite, Baptism, ecclesiastical hierarchy, sacraments, spiritual vs. corporeal, Polycarp, imitation of God</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual rebirth and the role of the sacraments as mediators of divine truth, transitioning into a specific examination of Baptism.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER II. 2. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

...is spiritual in God. Being loved by God, we are reborn in Him as faithful and hopeful people, so that in Christ we may cry out, "Abba, Father." First, it is necessary that you are reborn in God, so that you may then grow into something better. We are reborn into God by the Spirit of God, who, warming us, purifies us so that we may believe in Christ and hope in Him. When we believe out of love and hope for what we have believed, we are reborn as children of God; this is baptism by spirit and fire. This spiritual logic is foreshadowed and signified by images, so that the spirit may be embodied by them and bodies may be spiritualized by them. By these signs, we are reminded to be mindful of divine regeneration, through which we are born not of blood, nor of the will of the flesh, nor of the will of man, but of God; so that we may consider that we must live not according to man but according to God the Father, in whose house we are. Since we are not of the world but of God, let us live in this world not to please men, but God. For what else does our new birth intend than that we should not be what we were? Is not the birth of one thing the destruction of another? Are we not reborn so that we may die to the world and to men, and live for God? We are reborn so that in the house of God our conduct might be in heaven, and that in this world we should be content with food and clothing. The apostles intended this perfection of the new life, into which we are reborn by the Spirit of God, to be signified by images; so that through images men might approach the truth, and truth might descend into men through the same. Thus, images and sacraments are certain mediators of the truth, and of the men who are to be made true by the Spirit of truth.

...of the cross. He who knew no sin, he made to be sin for us, so that we might become the righteousness of God in him. This was the eternal Son of God, whom the Father, sending in the likeness of sinful flesh, condemned sin in the flesh on account of sin; so that the justification of the law might be fulfilled in us, who do not walk according to the flesh, but according to the spirit, in the knowledge and worship of the true God. Everywhere on earth, the bishops <note>Lat. *pontifices*</note> act in his place, and they perform in him what he himself performed. With equal devotion, they strive for the purification, illumination, and salvation of men through the constant proclamation of the truth and the radiance of the gospel, just as he strove. Paul says: God was in Christ reconciling the world to himself, not counting their sins against them; and he has placed in us the word of reconciliation. Therefore, we serve as ambassadors for Christ. Acting in the place of Christ, they fan the fire that Christ came to send upon the earth, so that they might increase it. "I have come to send fire on the earth," he says in Luke, "and what do I desire but that it be kindled?" "I have a baptism to be baptized with, and how I am constrained until it is accomplished!" According to the testimony of John, he baptized with the spirit and with fire. For fire purifies, illuminates, and perfects. That spiritual fire does these things in the souls of men. So that they may increase this saving fire in the forest of humanity, the bishops <note>Lat. *pontifices*</note> are the ministers and vicars of Jesus, seeking the kindling of men in God. This fire is indeed the holy love of God, which the beloved bishops channel into the world, loving men themselves in God, so that those men may then love God in return, and being reborn by the love of God, they may live in God, believing in the Son who was sent, and placing all their hope in God. For love is the principle of begetting; holy love is the principle of holiness, and the love of God is the principle of divinity. Moreover, the messenger of this goodness, mercy, love, and piety of God was his own beloved son, Jesus Christ, who wisely brought that primary love down to men, so that, being reborn by it, they might love their heavenly Father in return. To those who acknowledge, admit, hear, and receive Jesus, the messenger <note>Lat. *angelum*</note> of great counsel, he gave the power to become children of God; so that those born of men, now believing in Christ, may be happily reborn as children of God. While he lived as a mortal in the flesh, he himself performed the office of the high priesthood <note>Lat. *pontificatum*</note>, teaching the duty by his actions. And at that time, salvation lay in believing in him while he was present. But when, having shown the form of the high priest, he himself as a good and patient priest offered himself to God as a propitiatory sacrifice, then

...the same salvation began to be for those who believed those who performed the vicarious work of Jesus, who in the place of Jesus announce him just as he announced himself. For, as Paul says: 

> We do not preach ourselves to you, but Jesus Christ our Lord; and ourselves as your servants through Jesus. 

Therefore the Savior, as Luke reports, said to those he sent to evangelize: 

> He who hears you hears me, and he who rejects you rejects me. He who rejects me rejects him who sent me. 

For God was in Christ, and Christ was in the apostles and in the successors of the apostles, for the reconciliation of the world to God. Therefore, each bishop <note>Lat. *pontifex*</note> acts in his own church (as the blessed martyr Ignatius testified in his epistle to the Magnesians) in the person of God and Christ; he commands that everyone obey him as the Lord himself. Under the bishop, the priests hold the place of the apostles. Under these, the deacons are ministers to the faithful people. The bishop’s office is, after the pattern of Christ, to constantly and assidueously evangelize the truth he has received. For he is like a mediating angel between God and men who announces divine things to men, just as Christ did, so that, just as God made him, he might make other men like him; so that just as he imitated God, others may in turn imitate him; let him incessantly cry out that apostolic word: 

> Imitate me, as I imitate Christ. 

For imitation and progress toward the likeness of God, who is in Christ Jesus, is the life of man upon the earth. The bishop, carrying the form of Christ within himself, and preaching, exhorting, and admonishing all so that they might desire to be molded into that form—so that, being like Christ, they might be saved by him—must necessarily move some because of the power of the divine word; which (as Paul writes to the Hebrews) is living and effective and sharper than any double-edged sword, reaching even to the division of soul and spirit, of joints and marrow, and is a discerner of the thoughts and intentions of the heart. He who is truly moved by the spiritual ray of God, which travels with the word of God, begins to be reborn in the spirit as a child of God. For he begins to repent of the life lived without God; indeed, the first effect of the Holy Spirit is repentance for that life which is being abolished, and the laying aside and casting away of it forever. Hence that forerunner John cried out: 

> Repent. 

Then Christ himself proclaimed to men even more magnificently and loudly: 

> Repent; for the kingdom of heaven has drawn near.

...for he who does not repent of past evil is unable to desire future good. And he who has not laid aside that which he repents of cannot put on that which he desires as better. He who wants to change his clothing must strip himself in order to put on new clothes, and must abolish all oldness within himself so that he may possess the fragrance of novelty. What is preached by the bishop <note>Lat. *pontifice*</note> is new, and once the old odor is thoroughly scraped away, it demands new vessels; otherwise, like new wine poured into old vessels, it will burst them and the wine itself will flow out. He who wishes to walk decently in Christ, wearing the new and immaculate tunic which the hand and finger of God wove, must have the old and filthy garment of mortality—which man fashioned for himself—completely stripped off and cast away. For no one puts a patch of unshrunk cloth onto an old garment; or, as Mark says, *No one sews a piece of unshrunk cloth on an old garment.* Indeed, as Luke adds, *the new patch does not match the old.* 

If a man journeys toward immortality, he should do nothing except what looks toward immortality. Let him cast off the works of darkness and put on the armor of light; let him follow the bishop as his leader; let him be a soldier enlisted by an oath <note>Lat. *sacramento*</note> in the Christian army. When he has departed from the world, marked with the cross of Christ, let him declare battle against the enemies of truth and the prince of darkness. Our struggle, as the apostle writes to the Ephesians, is not against flesh and blood (that is, against men, whose salvation should be sought, not their death—for "I do not desire the death of a sinner," says the Lord in Ezekiel, "but that he be converted and live"). Instead, our struggle is against principalities and powers, against the rulers of the darkness of this world, against spiritual wickedness in high places. 

Whoever declares this war against the enemy of light, out of love for the reward offered to the victors, should approach the church humbly and with great courage, and ask someone to lead him to the commander of the army (that is, the bishop), so that he may be bound to the fight in Christ by a military oath <note>Lat. *sacramento*</note>. Let him profess himself an enemy of the world and a companion, servant, and soldier of Christ. Having sworn by his oath (Paul calls those to whom he writes his fellow-soldiers), let him ask for spiritual weapons and request to be trained in lawful combat. Then, whoever it may be to whom such a person comes by the guidance of the divine spirit (for the Savior says, "No one comes unless he is drawn"; and at the preaching of Paul in Antioch of Pisidia, as Luke writes, "as many as were ordained to life believed")...

...eternal) — he, I say, to whom he is preordained and drawn, comes into the true church when he is asked by that same person to lead him to the Bishop <note>Lat. *pontificem*</note>, so that he may be admitted into the number of those fighting in Christ Jesus. Truly, it belongs to the man who hears the person's vow that, even though he desires nothing more than for as many as possible to be faithful and undertake spiritual warfare in Christ, he should nevertheless reflect and consider at the start how great a thing the profession of warfare and the Christian name is, and also how great human weakness is. He should shudder and fear for that man, lest he rashly take upon himself more than he can perform. 

For it goes poorly for those who do not fight in Christ Jesus; but it goes much worse for them, and they are a thousand times more unhappy, who do not act according to the persona they have assumed—those who languish, who grow cold, who desert their post, and who slip back to where they came from. Then, as the Savior says in Matthew, *the last state of that man becomes worse than the first.* As it is in the second epistle of Peter: *If, fleeing the defilements of the world in the knowledge of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, they are again entangled in them and overcome, their later state has become worse than the former. For it would be better for them not to know the way of righteousness than, after knowing it, to turn back from the holy commandment delivered to them.* For that true proverb happens to them: *The dog returns to its own vomit*, and, *The sow that was washed returns to wallowing in the mud.* 

It was indeed the custom, and all those who introduced someone into the church of Christ, into the heavenly warfare, and into the temple of God, used to first shrink back hesitantly. Yet they finally introduced them in good faith and with hope in God, together with many prayers poured out that the matter might succeed well, and that all things might turn out well and fortunately for that man who wished and desired to be initiated into the Christian sacred rites. The Bishop <note>Lat. *pontifex*</note>, however, rejoicing within himself over the gain in Christ, gives thanks to God and, together with the rest of the priesthood, sings hymns joyfully. 

And then, when the Bishop asks what he wants, the person introduced responds—detesting the way in which he strayed—that he wishes to be instructed in the path of righteousness and the way of truth; he deplores his former state and desires the new state of Christ Jesus. Recognizing this as true repentance and approving his sincere will, the Bishop first asserts to him that if he wishes to take the journey toward God, he must commit himself there entirely and inwardly, being puri—

<meta>This page continues the description of the baptismal rite, focusing on the bishop's instructions to the candidate and the symbolic actions of renouncing the devil and professing Christ.</meta>

<language>Latin</language>
<page-num>210</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>CONCERNING THE ECCLESIASTICAL [HIERARCHY] CHAPTER II. 2.</header>

...completely and perfectly. Then, explaining what the right way of living is, and the path which he should enter, and then asking whether he wishes to undergo it freely and of his own accord—when he sees the man offer himself entirely to that path—the bishop, having laid his hand upon him and signed him according to custom, commands the priests to enroll his name in the catalog of Christians. He also commands that the name of the one who introduced him (whom Dionysius calls a sponsor; I believe he is the one we now call a godfather) be recorded. He then immediately hands the man over to the priests and ministers to be stripped of his clothing.

Once stripped and nearly naked, they place him facing toward the West. They command him to breathe out against Satan three times, and with his mouth, his face, his spirit, and his hands, to profess a perpetual renunciation of all things belonging to the devil; and finally, that he should utterly renounce himself. Whoever has done this—who has breathed out against Satan, renounced all that is his, and finally denied himself (according to that saying of the Savior, who said to all his followers, as Luke reports, *If anyone wishes to come after me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow me*)—then they turn the man toward the East. They command him to profess Christ and the things of Christ three times, both in his mind and with his words.

The bishop blesses the man as he does this and commends him to God in prayer, with his hands again laid upon his head. Then, after the remaining covering is removed, when the bishop has marked him three times with the sign of the cross using holy oil, he then hands over the rest of the man’s body to the priests to be anointed.

Meanwhile, the bishop himself—when he has sanctified the water, the mother of adoption, according to the custom instituted by the apostles and the legitimate rite—takes the man into his hands. His name is read from the register, and while the priests acclaim his name, the bishop invokes the Trinity and pours water over the man, covering him three times so that he may emerge three times. 

Once he has emerged and been brought forth into the light, as if reborn to God, they clothe him in a new and white garment. Once clothed, they lead him back to the bishop; once led back, the bishop marks him again with ointment and makes him a participant in the sacred communion. This is done so that, being now in the mystical body, he may go toward Christ daily and advance further forward, so that he may not be judged lazy in the journey he has undertaken and the profession he has made. This was the custom and rite of baptism and the washing of regeneration in the early church, instituted by the holy apostles, by which the more excellent baptism of the inner man is signified. This form differs greatly from the one we use in this age; at which I truly wonder...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the baptismal rite, moving from the physical actions of renouncing the devil and professing Christ to a deeper spiritual interpretation of the sacrament and the necessity of maintaining apostolic tradition.</meta>

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER II. 3. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

This is why there is such a disparity of sacred rites in one and the most ancient religion; for it seems we ought to be more diligent in preserving our own rites than the Hebrews were in theirs, especially as ours are much more perfect than theirs. For as Paul writes to the Ephesians, "There is one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one body." Thus, there certainly ought to be one similar mode and rite for the sacraments, with nothing added and nothing taken away from the ancient and venerable institution of the apostles. To change their statutes in sacred matters is surely a wicked crime. For they, having been taught by Jesus Christ, thoroughly knew the mysteries which are the appropriate symbols and suitable signs; so that one may suspect either the rashness or the negligence of posterity in whatever has been added to or taken away from their institutions.

### CHAPTER II. 3.

### *A Spiritual Contemplation of Baptism.*

Besides the cleansing of life, which that washing with water appropriately signifies, there is also an <term>Anagoge</term> <note>A spiritual or uplifting interpretation that leads the mind from the literal to the divine</note> and a significance of higher understanding in it. For just as invisible things are the beginnings of sensible things, so the sensible sacraments are signs of insensible things, which are applied to spiritual sacraments as the body is to the soul. The good and kind God is the sun of justice, irradiating spiritual natures, pouring himself out similarly and equally upon all without ceasing, and standing at the doors and windows of the mind, knocking to be received. Under these rays of the divine sun, there are two kinds of men. One is he who does not wish to know as much as he can; the other is he who wishes to know even more than he can. The former, wickedly and voluntarily delighting in evil and darkness, refuses the light; the latter, more proudly, overruns the measure of his light. From this it follows that not only does he not receive what he desires, but he also loses what he had. For, as <term>Paul</term> warns, we should not seek to know more than is proper, but to know with sobriety, lest while saying we are wise, we become fools. If, having received the divine light, you trust in yourself and begin to strive for things by your own power, then you extinguish everything. Increase what you have by the same means through which you received it; that is, by believing, trust in God and not in yourself. Allow the divine ray to increase within you; be humble and patient subject to it: this indeed

<meta>This page continues the spiritual interpretation of the baptismal rite, focusing on the movement from darkness to light and the significance of being "sealed" and "enrolled" within the community of the faithful.</meta>

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY, CHAPTER II. 3.</header>

...is to open yourself to the light. Within that very light, if you become proud by trusting in yourself in any way, you close yourself so that the light cannot enter; and walking in your own darkness, you do not know where you are going. The <term>Bishop</term> <note>Latin: *Pontifex*</note>, whom Christ has illuminated, shines from the pulpit like the sun with the light of truth, and radiates the gospel word equally to everyone. He cries out:
> "Awake, you who sleep, and arise from the dead, and Christ will illuminate you."
His oratory consists of pure words, like silver tested by fire and purified seven times. The Bishop himself has been made into fire by God; he shines with truth, warms with goodness, and teaches everyone with kindness. But he who has learned that he is in darkness (for all things that are rebuked are made manifest by the light; everything that is made manifest is light); when he is illuminated by recognizing himself, he then seeks to be carried higher into the light, and approaches a faithful person for illumination. But in this sensible order, consider at the same time the spiritual order. This approach to the faithful person represents the humility and submission of the soul to the divine ray. When this is done humbly and sincerely, that person, touched by the divine light, is immediately marked for the lot of the saints. For he receives a certain light, a sign and mark that he belongs to the Lord’s flock. Paul, in the epistle to the Ephesians, calls this marking by God the Shepherd—as if of a sheep—the "pledge of inheritance"; in which, he says, 
> "and you also, when you had heard the word of God and of truth, the gospel of your salvation, received it, in which also believing, you were sealed with the Holy Spirit of promise, who is the pledge of our inheritance for the redemption of the acquisition, to the praise of His glory." 
The Bishop's laying on of hands denotes this marking. For as he is touched by the Bishop’s hand, he is simultaneously marked by being touched by the right hand of God, who is the Son of God, whose "finger" upon the man's head is the Holy Spirit. Then that man is happily among those enrolled for salvation, the image of which is the recording of his name in the catalog of the faithful by the priests. In that early church, all who were marked for faith through grace were enrolled. This practice (after a crowd of people poured in, taking the name of "Christian" more than the reality of it) began to be reserved only for the more sincere part of the church, as were many other things that used to be common. The <term>sponsor</term> <note>Latin: *susceptorem*; the godparent who presents the candidate</note>, whom Dionysius calls the sponsor, is also recorded: he who offers himself is written down; he who offers another to God is written down; because the Father in heaven rejoices over the recovered sheep.

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>CAP. II. 3. HIERARCHIA. 213</header>

...[the Shepherd rejoices more over] the hundredth sheep than over the ninety-nine which did not go astray. He who has now been received into the <term>simplicity</term> of Christ must profess a certain single and undivided way of living, looking only upward. Because, as <term>Dionysius</term> says, it is not right that he who has entered the fellowship of the One should have divided lives. For it is necessary that he move toward only one part. For "no one putting his hand to the plow and looking back is fit for the kingdom of God." No servant can serve two masters: for either he will hate the one and love the other, or he will cling to one and despise the other. He cannot be both divided and simple.

Christianity is a profession of simplicity, into which man is drawn so that he moves from the multitude into simplicity. The simple Christ does not allow a double tunic. If you wish to be in His wedding garment, you must approach naked in order to put it on; and you must lay aside your former way of life so that you may enter that which is Christ's. This is the meaning and significance of why he who turns to Christ strips off all his clothes, why he spits and breathes toward the West, and with a great protest renounces whatever is iniquity.

The fact that he now presents himself entirely naked toward the East, to the rays of the rising <term>Sun of Justice</term> <note>Latin: *solis justiciæ*</note>, signifies, I say, that being purified and simple, he may receive the simple and pure divine ray, and put on the garment of light and justice which the grace of the Holy Spirit has woven in Christ. Thus, shining in the light with the noble garment of justice, he may walk with heavenly majesty in this dark valley, through the weapons of justice on the right hand and on the left, extending his white hands, full of goodness, to all. He himself remains always good and consistent, unable to do anything but good, innocent toward those who harm him, and possessing unconquerable patience in the face of evils; so that by a continuous action of good, he may be seen to have kept the garment of justice inviolate. 

This is that white garment which everyone who has been washed in the pool of holy regeneration puts on; the garment of holy and stainless justice. In this garment, one must always move toward the East, more and more into the increase of light, without looking back toward the West and the region of death, lest, like the wife of <term>Lot</term>, we be turned into a pillar of salt. Furthermore, the fact that he is anointed all over by the priests signifies the contest and struggle which we have—not against flesh and blood, but against the rulers of the darkness of this world, against spiritual wickedness in high places—with the armor of God, which...

<summary>This page explains the baptismal symbolism of moving from worldly "multiplicity" to "simplicity" in Christ, represented by stripping off old clothes and facing East to receive a garment of light. It emphasizes that a Christian must maintain a single-minded life of justice and goodness, even when facing evil.</summary>
<keywords>simplicity, Sun of Justice, baptismal robe, renunciation of the West, Dionysius, spiritual warfare, regeneration, armor of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the spiritual interpretation of the baptismal rite, focusing on the symbolism of anointing as preparation for spiritual warfare and the meaning of the triple immersion as a participation in Christ's death and resurrection.</meta>

# DE ECCLESIASTICA CAP. II. 3.

That anointing signifies the Holy Spirit, by whom the members are made more vigorous. You must fight so that you may conquer; you must conquer so that you may be crowned. Fight in Him who fights in you and conquers, Jesus Christ, who declared war on death and fights in everyone. He is the one who conquers in us, and we in Him, anointed by the Holy Spirit, so that, once the empire of death has been defeated and overthrown, He Himself may be all in all in the kingdom of light. The rule of fighting is to imitate the Leader, who is the Lord strong and mighty in battle. Nor is it lawful for Christians to undertake any other kind of warfare than what He Himself taught; for we have no enemies and foes except sin (which is always against us) and the evil spirits who persuade us to sin. Once these are conquered within us, then, armed with the armor of God, let us through charity help others—even if they do not endure it, even if in their foolishness they do not see their own slavery, even if they wish to kill their liberators. To love a person so much that you would die to ensure their salvation is most blessed.

Indeed, that triple immersion while the Trinity is invoked is a beautiful representation of death, and of the laying aside of the flesh, of carnal thoughts from the mind, and of all sins, from which he who believes in Christ professes to be separated and to die forever. He who has died to sin has died once; but he who lives, lives for God. Just as death is the laying aside of the body, so spiritual <term>baptism</term> is the laying aside of the bodily life. This is signified by that overwhelming of the whole person in the waters; by this they are reminded that they are dead with Christ, and as if buried for three days with Christ. This is what that triple immersion means: that just as Christ's body was laid aside, our whole bodily life may be laid aside, so that we may rise again as righteous; dying in the righteous Christ, so that eventually we may also rise in the same body into glory.

First we must emerge into a righteous life, then we must rise into a glorious one. Death destroys death; <term>grace</term> produces justice; justice gives birth to glory. So that we may die to sin, it is necessary that the Righteous One die for us. But so that we may live for justice, it is necessary that we be sustained by the grace of the righteous rising One. Finally, so that we ourselves may rise as the righteous dead, it is necessary that we be resuscitated by divine strength and power. Those indeed rise again who, in the washing of regeneration, having laid aside their vices in the death of Christ, walk persevering until the end in the white robe of justice without stain; and who do not fail the anointing of confirmation, which is finally applied by the <term>pontiff</term> <note>Likely referring to the Bishop acting as high priest in the Dionysian hierarchy</note>, so that, confirmed in grace and perfected...

<summary>This page explains the spiritual symbolism of the baptismal rite, describing anointing as preparation for spiritual warfare against sin and triple immersion as a participation in Christ's death, burial, and resurrection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sacraments of initiation, detailing how baptism and confirmation lead to the Eucharist as the final act of spiritual perfection and union with the Body of Christ.</meta>

# CAP. II. 3. HIERARCHIA.

...signifies. To a member of Christ who is thus perfected in his own kind, the sacred food of the Eucharist is administered for union with the body and for the nourishment of the member. By this, it is understood that he is both in the body and is nourished and spiritually fed within the body. No one is perfectly a member of the body of Christ until he has partaken of the sacred communion and vital nourishment. By participating in this, he grows together into the body. In the early church, all who were baptized and simultaneously confirmed by the holy anointing were admitted without delay to the communion of the divine food.

It should be noted that Dionysius speaks of confirmation in such a way as to teach that it is not a distinct sacrament, but rather something for the completion of baptism, so that baptism and confirmation are only one sacrament. It is also no less noteworthy that it was the custom of the ancient church for all the baptized to receive communion immediately, so that they might be considered part of Christ's mystical body through their common nourishment. Otherwise, although baptized, they would seem not to be of the body. For that communion binds and unites through common nourishment, and it achieves the final completion. 

Therefore, it was once also given to baptized infants, of whom it is read in the ancient books of the mysteries: if the bishop is present, the infant ought to be confirmed immediately, and afterwards receive communion. If the bishop is not present, before the infant is suckled or has tasted anything, the priest should give him the communion of the body and blood of the Lord before Mass—even if an emergency arises—so that the person may already be purged, illuminated, and perfected in Christ. The washing purges; the subsequent anointing with chrism illuminates and makes one bright; the Eucharist fills and perfects in the perfect Christ, in whom all things are perfect, and in whom nothing can exist that is not perfect.

> O good God, here one may see how purged and how pure he who professes Christ ought to be: how intimately and thoroughly washed, how white, how bright, how completely without blemish or stain, and finally, according to his capacity, how full and perfect in Christ himself—in whom he may henceforth flourish and be strong as His whole and healthy member.

For Christ is the head of the church, the very essence of integrity and perfection. From Him, as Paul writes to the Colossians, the whole body—supported and joined together by joints and connections—grows with the increase of God, so that God may shine forth in each simple, serene, and solid member; so that constantly

It also references historical practices such as infant communion and the early church's view of confirmation as part of the baptismal rite.</summary>
