<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual perfection through Christ, moving from the general effects of baptism to a specific examination of the Eucharist as the completion of all sacraments.</meta>

...so that they may clearly and perfectly acknowledge and worship God in Christ, and so that each person may so carry the fragrance and reflection of Christ within themselves alone, that Christ may be seen to be all in all. Therefore, if we have risen with Christ through baptism, let us seek the things that are Christ’s, where Christ is sitting at the right hand of God; let us fix our minds on the things that are above, not those on earth. Since we are dead, let us seek our life, which is hidden with Christ in God, and which is the glory that will appear to us when Christ—in whom we have hope—appears in glory. Meanwhile, for those of us who profess Christ, may Jesus Christ himself cause us to be, to think, and to do everything in a manner worthy of our profession.

### CAP. III. 1.

->*On Holy Communion. The <term>Synaxis</term>.*<-

This communion in the body and blood of Jesus Christ is the fulfillment of all the sacraments. Indeed, all sacraments lead toward communion; but none can compare to this one, in which the fellowship and living together are wonderful, as many people become one through the sharing of one thing. Men are prepared for this by the other sacraments, which come before so that they may be completed by this one. All sacraments work toward the goal that unity, likeness, and simplicity may exist in men. We are advanced toward this by the other sacraments; we are perfected by the eucharist and the <term>synaxis</term> <note>A Greek term for "assembly" or "gathering," synonymous here with the Eucharist.</note>. Therefore, whatever great mystery is performed, it is usually completed by this sacrament; without it, all the mysteries of initiation are begun but remain imperfect. Since all these sacraments ensure that men are formed into one, being united to the One themselves, and since the <term>synaxis</term> achieves this most of all, it was specifically given that name—just as the name "illumination" particularly fits the sacrament of regeneration. In all sacraments these three stages exist: to purge, to illuminate, and to perfect; but perfection is attributed to the <term>synaxis</term>, while illumination is attributed to regeneration. For in this first sacrament, a person receives the light by which he begins to perceive the truth. Because of this, Dionysius calls baptism the "sacrament of illumination." For, having been touched by the divine ray and called back, the person looks toward God. The laying on of the bishop's hands signifies this. He looks indeed at the light reflecting back toward God; as David says, "In your light we shall see light."

<vocab>Christus, baptisma, Communio, Synaxis, sacramentum, eucharistia, Dionysius, David</vocab>

<summary>This text explains that the Eucharist (Synaxis) is the ultimate fulfillment of all sacraments, bringing believers into a perfect unity with Christ and one another. It distinguishes between the stages of purging, illumination (associated with baptism), and perfection (associated with communion).</summary>

### CAP. III. 2.

->*Mass for Communion.*<-

The Bishop first offers a holy prayer at the altar, which I believe was the Lord's Prayer. He perfumes the altar with incense, and then the entire temple. Then, returning to the altar together with the rest of the priesthood, he sings hymns and the Psalms of David. Next, the ministers and deacons, one after another, recite from the Holy Scripture those readings they call "lessons." At this point, they dismiss from the temple the <term>catechumens</term>, the <term>energoumens</term>, and the penitents, since they are not worthy to be present for the mysteries.

<term>Catechumens</term> are those who, not yet initiated into the sacred rites nor baptized, are being instructed so that they may be baptized. The word *catecheo* means "to instruct"; therefore, those who are instructed so that they might properly approach the mysteries are called <term>catechumens</term>, a name derived from the Greek. Paul mentions them in the Epistle to the Galatians: "Let him who is taught the word," he says, "share in all good things with him who teaches him."

The <term>energoumens</term> are indeed those driven and troubled by evil spirits. The power of darkness dwells heavily within them, and they are called <term>energoumens</term> because of that power's force, operation, and action. For *energeo* is a Greek word meaning "to act" or "to work." Under this name are included not only those who are miserably insane after being seized by demonic fury, but also every wicked person who either feigns their religion or rejects it, such as apostates. It also includes those who, having professed Christ, nevertheless lead a most disgraceful life defiled by notorious crimes—such as fornicators, sorcerers, murderers, blasphemers, and others of that kind. Although they are called Christians, they lead the lives of impious pagans. While they take their name from Christ—whom they falsely profess—in reality they belong to the kingdom, or rather the slavery, of darkness. In them, the princes of darkness and the spiritual forces of wickedness in high places have more power than Christ. These individuals are only a few degrees removed from those who are driven into madness by evil spirits and the devil's servants. Indeed, they are in the same condition and under the same diabolical influence; the life of any such wicked person in the Devil is a form of madness. For them to become clearly insane and entirely lose themselves, nothing is lacking except for a few degrees of total madness to be applied by that...

<summary>This page describes the opening rites of the Mass, including the Bishop's prayer, the use of incense, and scriptural readings. it defines catechumens and "energoumens," explaining that the latter includes both the demonically possessed and those who live wickedly while claiming the name of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Mass, liturgy, Bishop, incense, catechumens, energoumens, spiritual darkness, Galatians, ecclesiastical hierarchy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definition of the groups excluded from the mysteries, specifically focusing on the spiritual state of the energoumens and the process of repentance for the penitents, following the dismissal of the catechumens.</meta>

### CAP. III. 2.

...by the evil spirit who began that falling away from the light. In a descent toward what is worse, the beginning is from the author, the devil, and it moves toward a final conclusion; just as in the perfection toward what is better, the author is God. And just as this perfection results in divine zeal, so on the other hand that descent results in diabolic frenzy. But divine zeal is indeed wisdom and health in Christ. Those who fall away from him into themselves, into a state of apostasy and darkness, and who delight in their own ways, are then deserted by God. They are immediately and easily led to every kind of wickedness by evil spirits and can be called <term>energoumens</term>, that is, the "driven." He who is not driven by the Spirit of God to be a child of God is necessarily driven by the spirit of the devil to be a child of the devil. For man is constantly pulled from both sides, so that he may follow what he wills. To whichever side he leans, there are spiritual beings standing by who diligently promote that pull and bring it to completion once it has begun.

<margin>free will</margin>
It is up to the man himself to choose which way he wishes to go, to listen to advisors—whether good or evil—and to follow either these inspirations or those. But in whichever direction he tends, spiritual natures are all around who lead him where he wishes to go. Therefore, when the divine mysteries are being performed in Christ, no one is allowed to be present except he who is moved by the Spirit of God, which is a holy life. The profane—as all <term>energoumens</term> may be called—should be driven far from the sacred rites and mysteries. For, as David sang, "Holiness becomes the house of God." Therefore, all those whose wicked lives have shown them to be in the power of the devil (for "by their fruits you shall know them") were kept far away from the sacred rites in the early church. This was done so that no honor would be granted to the devil—namely, that any of his followers should be present at the divine services.

The penitents, however, were those who repented of this wandering and lost life. These were the ones who, coming to their senses, returned to the church, prostrating themselves humbly at the feet of the church, imploring divine mercy and grace. They wept for their sins and demanded the punishment and penalty for their offenses, so that they might publicly mourn their errors as an example to everyone. Through temporal punishment, they sought to escape eternal death and regain eternal life. The church, which acts in the person of Christ and for which whatever it binds or looses on earth is bound or loosed in heaven, does not desire the death of a sinner, but rather that he should be converted and live. Therefore, it graciously receives such people back into the church and nurtures them once they have completed their assigned penance and have repented sufficiently...

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual state of energoumens, who are driven by evil spirits through their own free will, and contrasts them with those led by the Spirit of God. It also describes the early church's practice of excluding the wicked from the mysteries and the process by which penitents are restored through public repentance and penance.</summary>

### CAP. III. 2. HIERARCHIA.

...and preserves them in that same state as much as possible. Meanwhile, however, while they exercise themselves in the commanded penance, still being regarded as not sufficiently purified, they are driven far away and excluded along with the <term>catechumens</term> and <term>energoumens</term>. Once these have been excluded and the temple doors are vigilantly watched by the <term>porters</term> and <term>gatekeepers</term>,
<margin>mass of the catechumens</margin>
lest any profane person should enter, then—with the <term>deacon</term> and minister performing their duties—the principal deacons who are around the <term>pontiff</term>, together with the <term>priests</term>, continually serve the pontiff and place the holy bread and the cup of blessing upon the altar. Meanwhile, as the entire clergy chants the praise and confession of the sacred rite, the <term>pontiff</term>, with the bread and cup now present, pours forth that most holy prayer, which I believe to have been the Lord’s Prayer. Then, after peace has also been announced by the pontiff to everyone, they all greet and kiss one another in turn.

After the mystical words of the saints have been recited, and then with the <term>priests'</term> hands washed, the <term>pontiff</term> stands in the middle of the altar, closely surrounded by the <term>priests</term> and chosen ministers, and proceeds to offer the remaining words for the praise and confession of the sacrament, finally completing the divine and saving rite with the consecrating words. Once this is done, he shows the completed mysteries to everyone with all reverence; and then he, first turning toward the altar, partakes of it himself, and then exhorts all the others who are present to share in the participation and communion of the same sacrament, so that all may be united in the one sacrament with the <term>pontiff</term>.

When everyone has participated, the <term>pontiff</term> then brings the rite to an end with a prayer of thanksgiving, clearly understanding what those sensible signs signify and perceiving the reason for the whole matter—though others who are less learned are unable to look beyond the physical senses. However, the fact that they act in good faith is for their salvation. Knowledge does not lead to life, but <term>charity</term> does. If anyone loves God, he is known by Him. Ignorant love is a thousand times more powerful than cold wisdom. Yet for the <term>pontiff</term>, and for whoever is to perform the divine rite, it is necessary both to love supremely and to know supremely, so that he may intimately understand the reasons for the sacred things he performs. To this, all <term>priests</term> whose duty it is to perform the divine sacrament are undoubtedly bound and constrained: that in that divine mystery, they should understand more than the common people. In the early church (as <term>Jerome</term> proves with good evidence), those whose office it was to consecrate the Lord's body were...

<summary>This page describes the sequence of the liturgy, from the exclusion of the unpurified to the consecration and participation in the Eucharist, emphasizing that while the laity may participate through faith, the clergy must possess both supreme love and deep understanding of the mysteries.</summary>

--- Page 276 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the roles within the ecclesiastical hierarchy, specifically addressing the original equality of priests and the functional origin of the office of bishop, before transitioning to the spiritual significance of the Eucharist.</meta>

...were the first of all; and in the beginning all were equal in this office and likewise equal in dignity. For dignity is recognized by one's office and actions. The most excellent office, which requires the most excellent and perfect people, is to consecrate the Lord’s sacrament. No one can be exempt or set apart from the priestly order for a more excellent office. However, among the priests—immediately after the apostles, starting from those first disciples and followers of the apostles—one was chosen from the number of priests (who were equal in office and dignity) and placed in charge to settle disputes, calm discords, and terminate dissensions by his opinion and judgment, so that the church might remain in harmony. To this person, authority was delegated by the universal church, so that those who did not agree among themselves might find rest in his judgment. This leader does not excel the other priests so much in office and dignity—since he performs nothing more excellent than any other priest—as he does in a certain administration and authority in settling disputes. In this way, all who act as priests together with him might find rest in his judgment as if in a single point, once all discords are ended. This person then began to be specifically called a <term>bishop</term>; a name which, under the apostles, belonged to all priests until that one was chosen whom I just mentioned, and for the reason I stated. But now the wisdom and contemplation of the truth must be brought forth, along with the very reason for the inner secret, of which no priest should be ignorant.

---

### CAP. III. 3.

->The meaningful truth of the sacred Eucharist.<-

First of all, the songs and readings of the sacred scriptures are heard by the common people and by the whole church together, so that they may learn from the things in which they are instructed to lead a holy life. This is what Paul writes to the Ephesians: that they should be filled with the Holy Spirit, speaking to one another in psalms, hymns, and spiritual songs; singing and making melody in their hearts to the Lord, always giving thanks for all things to God the Father in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ. And to the Colossians: "Let the word of the truth of Christ dwell in you abundantly, in all wisdom, teaching and admonishing one another."

The text then begins to describe the liturgy of the Eucharist, focusing on the use of scripture and song for the moral instruction of the people.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exposition on the ecclesiastical hierarchy, specifically focusing on the spiritual and communal significance of the Eucharist as a means of unity and life-giving nourishment in Christ.</meta>

### CAP. III. 3. HIERARCHIA. 221

...yourselves in psalms, hymns, and spiritual songs, singing with grace in your hearts. From this it has been derived that passages taken here and there from <term>Sacred Scripture</term> should be recited in the church, so that men may learn from them how to live well. For the <term>Sacred Scripture</term> is the teacher of life and the rule of truth. Likewise, the common people and the more uneducated multitude, through the presentation of the **one bread and one cup** and the participation of everyone in that same nourishment, are beautifully and suitably reminded that, being fed and nourished by one thing, they should all grow together into one in harmony and brotherly peace, under God the Father at the table of God. They should understand that, just as there is one bread and one cup which all taste and which is shared by all, so they too should all be a certain unity among themselves and be similar to one another, bound together in the **unity of charity**, which is the bond of peace. 

This <term>communion</term> also contains a venerable representation of that holy Lord’s Supper with his disciples, in which he gave himself to them to be eaten, so that all might be united in him and, being incorporated into one with him, might be made complete in that same unity. <term>Matthew the Evangelist</term> describes this most sacred and life-giving supper, signifying that Jesus presented the bread and the cup of the <term>Passover</term> to his followers during the feast. 

> For he says, while they were eating, Jesus took bread, blessed it, and broke it, and gave it to his disciples, saying: "Take and eat; this is my body." And taking the cup, he gave thanks and gave it to them, saying: "Drink from this, all of you; for this is my blood of the new testament, which will be shed for many for the remission of sins." 

What was given by Jesus Christ to his disciples is thereafter distributed by priestly persons to all believers according to the same rite, so that, mindful of that supper, they may be united in Christ with those who were first united in Christ. Thus, the begun <term>life-giving work</term> may continue and proceed, through the operation of the sacred mysteries, toward the fulfillment of Christ—in whom are both the dead and the living, and all who will believe in the future. All these are sustained in Christ by holy communion and heavenly food in the high society of God, living no longer at the filthy table of demons but at the immaculate table of God. Indeed, this <term>sacrament</term> holds other and much deeper thoughts; but because it offers the Lord’s Supper, common upbringing, and brotherly unity, even if there were nothing else in it, it nonetheless serves as a very useful memorial for people of simpler understanding toward honorable society. But now let us look at deeper things. The <term>bishop</term>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the liturgy, specifically the symbolic actions of the bishop or pontiff and the essential role of the Holy Scriptures and Psalms in the life of the Church.</meta>

### ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY, CHAPTER III, SECTION 3. 222

He acts in the person of God, who, entirely at the altar in the sight of God, burns with the sweetest scent of incense, which is a sign of his most fragrant charity. For just as God pours out His sweet goodness, and as Jesus largely pours out His mercy so that He might return with the great salvation of men to the same source—He who, remaining in Himself, wonderfully magnifies Himself toward others so that His fragrant grace might draw them back into Him—it is exactly like that net of Peter, which, having been let down into the sea by him, is brought back to the ship full of fish. In almost the same way, so that small things may imitate the greatest, the <term>pontiff</term> and vicar of God, the head of the church and its more vital part, imparts himself and his divinity—indeed, the deity of Christ within him—while losing nothing of himself nor diminishing his majesty. He shares this kindly and divinely with others, so that as many as possible might be deified with him in Christ. 

Certainly, this burning of incense, begun at the altar and then proceeding through the entire temple and returning to the same place from which it started, signifies in a holy and venerable way to the simpler folk that from the height of God, a fragrant grace is spread far and wide upon everyone. It is intended that, being captured by the scent of divine sweetness, they might eagerly follow the returning grace back to that very place from which the grace itself went out, so that it might return to the same. Now, the grace in the <term>Eucharist</term> is what is called "good grace," which, ministered by the <term>pontiff</term>, passes from him to others so that it may return to him as others are drawn into him. The <term>pontiff</term> himself also, as if going forth, imparts himself through other signs; nonetheless, he withdraws back into himself and remains consistent with himself in the absolute and simple contemplation of the mysteries. 

The singing of psalms and the recitation from the sacred codex of those scriptures which are canonical neither can nor should be absent from the handling of the mysteries in the church. Indeed, in them is contained all wisdom and goodness, all knowledge of nature, all metaphysical speculation, all institution of good morals, all remembrance of God's past deeds, all expectation of future events, and finally, all faith and love of the true and the good; so that beyond what is recited for listening and instilled in the ears of men, nothing remains. Not even the apostles wished the church to hear anything else. 

For <term>Paul</term> commands time and again, writing to both the Ephesians and the Colossians, that they should admonish and comfort one another in psalms, hymns, and spiritual songs, sing-

### CHAPTER III. 3. HIERARCHY. 223

...singing in their hearts to God. Therefore, the melody and singing of psalms in the church during the performance of the mysteries and the sacrifice ought to be included appropriately, so that the minds of the listeners may be soothed by the harmony and sweetly drawn by the composed music and divine harmony into the logic of all beauty and concord; thus, being at peace in their minds, they may be more capable of receiving the grace of God. Indeed, the recitation of the psalms and scriptures while the sacred rites are performed seems to have been instituted by the apostles themselves, so that by learning from the Old Testament that the coming Christ has arrived, we may rejoice and, being prepared by this faith, proceed toward a further increase of grace. This grace works secretly and wonderfully in those who believe piously through the performance of the sacred rites. <term>Dionysius</term> says that this is the reason for the singing of psalms and the reading of scripture while any of the mysteries are being handled. For this reason, one may see that chanting in the church is a most ancient practice in our religion.

There are, moreover, four kinds of people: first, those who refuse the word of truth; second, those who admit it but are not yet initiated, who are called <term>catechumens</term>; third, those who, though initiated, have not sufficiently freed themselves from the power of demons, whom the ancients called <term>energumens</term>; fourth, those who have repented for not living according to their profession of faith. All of these hear the recited scriptures and the chanted psalms so that, being admonished by heavenly doctrine, they may recover their senses and return to perfection. However, when the handling of the sacred rites is close at hand, they are driven out of the temple. For the ancient institution required that no one take part in the sacred rites unless they were entirely perfected. For Christ was perfect, from whom all things flow for the consecration of men to God in Him; these things do not descend except upon those who are perfected in the holy mystical body of Christ. 

Therefore, all who are profane, imperfect, diminished, fallen, and the <term>catechumens</term>—whom Mother Church still labors to bring forth like unformed fetuses and has not yet delivered into the light—are kept far from the mysteries. So that their eyes are not too greatly overwhelmed by the brilliance of those mysteries, the church looks out for their weakness and commands them to be absent until they have become stronger. For the holy things of God require no small light of faith. Those who have been initiated into the sacred rites, such as apostates, bad Christians who by no means live according to their profession, and those properly called <term>energumens</term>—who are agitated by an evil spirit, by which the perfected are in no way troubled (indeed, they command every evil spirit)—and finally all...

### ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY, CHAPTER III. 3.
# 224

The profane crowd is dispersed and driven away from the sight of the sacred rites, including even those who have repented of their sins, until they are fully purified. Truly, only those who are pure, distinguished, and entirely perfect without blemish are left in the temple: that most holy Bishop, the priests, the deacons, the ministers, and the holy people. All of them, with their eyes fixed on the sacred objects, sing together the symbol of our religion: "I believe in one God." For in that Creed are contained all things pertaining to our salvation in Christ, which must be recited with the highest devotion so that, in giving thanks for our salvation, we may be mindful of divine mercy and appear devoted to God.

When the covered holy bread and the cup of blessing have been placed upon the altar, they then greet one another with a mutual kiss and call to mind the names of the saints who have died in Christ. In the offering of the one bread in which all participate, that mutual kiss of the living and the memory of the dead signify the unity, concord, likeness, and uniform way of living according to the image of Christ—which all who worthily participate in his sacred rites profess to imitate. While reciting those names of holy men, we remember how great their virtue was, so that by their example we may be stirred to an equal virtue. 

In those few whose names are heard, all the rest are understood, whose names cannot be recited because of their great number. They are certainly recited as those who are living with unwavering hope; who (as the Savior testified) have passed from death into life <note>Read: from death into life</note> and who remain in the mind of him who well knows his own, as it is written in the book of life. Once the Savior has been set forth under the forms of bread and wine, the commemoration of the saints follows immediately, so that, being united in Jesus, they may be understood to be one with him. 

Moreover, the fact that the Bishop with the priests washes his hands signifies that they are entirely clean and do not need washing except in their extremities—as the Savior said, "He who has bathed does not need to wash except for his feet, but is entirely clean." The tips of the hands are washed so that if even a slight scent of sin remains, it may be wiped away completely, and the Bishop with his attendants may enter the Holy of Holies entirely sweet and simple, so that he may purely and religiously perform and complete the divine rite in the mystery.

It also explains the symbolic meaning of the Bishop washing his hands before entering the Holy of Holies to complete the sacrament.</summary>

...for to live so purely and uprightly that not even a slight shadow of affection appears in our brightness. For this reason, Jesus washed the feet of Peter. For this reason, the Bishop, before he is about to perform the sacred rites, washes the tips of his hands—a practice derived from that washing of the feet—so that whatever remains of the scent of sin on the fingertips, whatever it may be, may be removed in the water of grace. Regarding which it is now <margin>customary</margin> the custom to say, *I will wash my hands among the innocent.* But no one is so innocent that, if they perform any work, they do not find it necessary to wash at least their fingertips; that is, to set aside those light affections which are difficult to avoid, and the remnants of sins that have been uprooted. Here, let every priest see through that sacrament of washing how clean, how pure, and how bright he ought to be who handles the divine mysteries, and especially the sacrament of the Lord’s body. They ought to be so washed, cleansed, and polished inwardly that not even a shadow remains in the mind by which the incoming light might be obscured for a time, nor any trace of sin remain that would prevent God from walking in the temple of our minds.

O priests, O priesthood, O detestable audacity of wicked men in our time! O cursed impiety of those little priests, of whom our age has a great multitude; who, from taverns and brothels, from the lap of a harlot and a stinking whore <margin>unholy crime</margin> (O unspeakable crime!), do not fear to thrust themselves into the temple of the church, to the altar of Christ, and into the mysteries of God! <margin>divine vengeance</margin> Most wretched little men, upon whom God's heavier vengeance will eventually fall, the more impudently they have mixed themselves in divine matters.

Jesus Christ, wash not only our feet but also our hands and our head. The bronze basin and the washing instituted for the priestly class in the Old Testament (as described in Exodus) refer to these mystical washings and signify them, just like other symbolic purifications there. By these we should be warned, and by our own holy signs as well, how purged and purified in mind we should stand at the altar of God, where God Himself is present as the spectator and judge, Jesus Christ, with the angels as witnesses, in whose sight <margin>angels</margin> <margin>witnesses</margin>—while Jesus perceives all things—we perform His most sacred business. If we do so rightly, it is for our eternal salvation; if unlawfully, it is an unspeakable insult to Him and for our perpetual damnation. Then the Bishop, completely and thoroughly...

### CHAPTER III. 3. THE HIERARCHY.

<margin>X</margin>
Having been washed, he returns to the more sacred praises, to the consecration of the sacrament, to its public display, and finally to communion in it—which is the very thing that everyone who belongs to the church shares together with the bishop. To show what these things mean and signify, let us look back a little further.

<margin>X</margin>
When, indeed, immediately after creation, foolish human nature was enticed by the smooth lures of the adversary and fell away from God into a weak and mortal condition, and when it was dragged headlong in a swift course toward death itself—exchanging all good things for all evils at once—then eventually, in His own time, the good, pious, kind, gentle, and merciful God willed to take man back into Himself, to join Himself to man, and to rescue him from the power of the adversary, having overthrown and destroyed the adversary’s rule.

For, as Paul writes to the Hebrews, *Because the children (that is, the servants) shared in flesh and blood*—that is, men deserting God—*so God Himself, emptying Himself, took the form of a servant and similarly shared in the same flesh and blood* (that is, in man), *so that through death He might destroy him who had the power of death, that is, the devil; and He liberated those who*, through fear of death, were subject to slavery throughout their whole lives. He destroyed that enemy indeed, not by force, but <margin>Elsewhere</margin> (as Dionysius says) by judgment and justice, which he calls hidden and mystical.

For it was a wonderful victory: that the victorious devil, by the very fact that he conquered, was himself conquered; and Jesus, by the same means by which He was conquered on the cross, was victorious. Thus, in the reality of the event, the victory was different on both sides from how it appeared. Therefore, when the adversary (who had conquered man) was conquered by God, man was restored <margin>X</margin> to the freedom and light of God without any just complaint from the devil. To man was shown the way to heaven, trodden by the feet of Christ, whose footsteps we must follow if we wish to arrive where He has gone.

The suffering and dying Christ (a miracle!) conquered as if He were defeated, and being cast out, He cast out the enemy, when that voice of the Father was heard: *I have both glorified it and will glorify it again*; which was spoken for the sake of others. Then Jesus, showing what kind of glorification that was, said: *Now is the judgment of the world; now the prince of this world will be cast out. And I, if I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all things to myself.* He said this, however, signifying what kind of death He was going to die. For by that death, snatched from the dead, we serve God, and the memory of that death...

This victory, achieved through justice rather than mere force, provides the "mystical" foundation for the church's sacraments.</summary>

### CHAPTER III. 3. THE HIERARCHY.

<margin><unclear>g. ...</unclear></margin>
<margin>1 Cor. 11. 26</margin>
<margin>XX</margin>
We celebrate these things, having been initiated into the sacred rites of him who, when he was about to die, commanded: "Do this in memory of me." Likewise Paul says: *As often as you shall drink this cup, you shall proclaim the Lord's death until he comes.* During the sacrifice, that remembrance of his death truly shapes us into the image of Christ; the sacraments draw us and lead us away from the world, and they dispose and bind us to God so that the form of God may shine within us. Therefore (to return to the matter), when that bishop and the priests have commemorated what Jesus the Son of God did for us, he then proceeds to complete the rest using suitable signs instituted by God. He sincerely excuses himself and proclaims himself unworthy of such a great mystery; he prays that he may perform it worthily and distribute it rightly to the gathered church, and that the church may participate in it correctly. In the early church, during the mass, those who looked to the priest shared together with him in the sacrificed Christ. The priest presents the completed sacraments before their eyes, breaks the holy bread into pieces, and distributes it along with the cup. Thus he multiplies the one for the many, so that he may unify the many in the one. Christ willed, while remaining undivided, to multiply himself in a certain way, so that the many who share in him—the one Word of God—might be united in him, made like him, and become entirely holy in him.

As Dionysius writes, we must look at the life of Jesus in the flesh—which was lived entirely for our sake—and we must imitate that example and that life if we wish to be sharers of his glory. He emerged from the hidden into the open for humanity; the invisible was made visible, and the one became, in a sense, many, so that he might draw all things into his own order. This is the mystery signified by the single bread and cup being brought out from what was secret and hidden, and then shown, distributed, and shared. Just as these physical things are multiplied to create a union, we should understand that the invisible and unique Jesus is multiplied so that as many people as possible may be united in him—specifically, those who imitate the <term>sinlessness</term> <note>Dionysius uses the word "impeccantia."</note> of Jesus and who do not sin, just as he did not sin. The bishop is the first to participate, because he is closer to God and ought to be by far the holiest and wisest of all. For the unworthy handling of such a great sacrament stems from ignorance. Let bishops, therefore, learn the mysteries of God and promote only worthy priests to these sacred offices, lest God’s rightful vengeance be finally provoked against us due to long-standing unworthiness. May God, in his supreme piety, make us worthy of his mysteries.

<header>DE ECCLESIASTICA CAP. IV. 1, 2.</header>

### CHAPTER IV. 1, 2.

->*Concerning the Consecration of the Ointment.*<-

In all sacraments, there is a leading down of the one to the many, of the simple to the complex, and of invisible unity to sensible signs, so that by these related methods, men who are captured by multiplicity may be drawn back toward unity. Thus it is clear that all sacraments are suitable means between the one God and many men, so that the many may be deified in God. Moreover, the ointment is consecrated, which the priestly office uses for almost everything; and it has nearly the same ceremonies as the <term>Synaxis</term>, such as incense, psalms, readings, and songs. While it is being consecrated, the catechumens, apostates, the possessed <note>Those influenced or occupied by evil spirits; the <term>energoumenoi</term>.</note>, and penitents are driven away.

The covered and protected ointment signifies the secret guarding of divine inspirations within the holy mind, which should neither be shown too pridefully nor profaned rashly, lest they then lose their pleasant fragrance. The fragrance of the thing and the brightness of divine grace must be protected by the veil of holy guarding. For nothing is more precious or more fragrant than the gift infused into the mind of man by God. If a man persists in this infusion, the degrees of grace are increased, so that finally the mind may turn out to be not unlike the likeness of God. 

God depicts His full image if the whole man looks toward Him constantly. He should not make public what he has received of the Deity, lest the fragrance of the divine odor grow cheap and vanish. By their own right, divine things wish to be hidden in secret; if they are made public, they lose their divinity. For God does not wish to communicate Himself to many, because there are not many to whom He can communicate Himself. Brightness, beauty, power, and fragrance are absent if you make secrets public; you ought to hide these in such a way that you not only keep from publishing your wisdom and what you know through revelation, but also—following the Savior’s command—do not perform your works publicly to be seen by men. 

For as God is secret, so divine men are withdrawn, secret, and solitary, and inward within themselves, separated far from the common crowd; and their entire wisdom, life, and works are hidden, witnessed by God and the angels, but not by men. They seek and savor the things that are above, where Christ is sitting at the right hand of God, not the things

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the holy chrism, focusing on the necessity of spiritual secrecy and how the material elements of the sacraments serve as mediated light for those unable to behold God directly.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>

# CAP. IV. 1, 2. HIERARCHIA.

...upon the earth. They are dead, and their life is hidden in Christ. For this reason they love solitude and, like God, they desire to perform all their good deeds in secret, so that God may be praised and not themselves; so that their works may shine before men, but not they themselves, and that their heavenly Father may be glorified, and not themselves. If you wish to be divine, be hidden like God: appear in heaven, and let your way of life be in the heavens. If you are in the truth itself, you do not need sensible sacraments; you possess the very grace of God, toward which all sacraments tend. This was the state of the monks in Egypt long ago, who did not regularly use all those sacraments that we have to draw us to God; because they had already been drawn into God, they did not require them. 

To help us understand that divine secrets ought to be hidden and covered, those wings cover and protect the holy <term>chrism</term>, by which every priestly office is perfected and completed. For the holy <term>chrism</term> is almost equal to the <term>Eucharist</term>, and equal in both use and necessity. In its consecration there is the same ceremonial solemnity, in which men are also drawn through many-colored forms toward that simple light which shines inwardly within itself, and does not become colored even in the opacity of signs. In this ointment—just as in the <term>Eucharist</term>, where the truth of God is seen in signs by the eyes of the body—that simple truth is perceived by the spiritual sight as it is poured out from the sun of justice. Those who cannot behold the sun itself, whose powerful vibration of light shatters a delicate gaze in the shadows, can nonetheless endure the colors. 

Moreover, that ointment and holy <term>chrism</term> is prepared from many, varied, and sweet-smelling materials, so that men imbued with it may smell good and pleasant, and refresh the senses most sweetly. This ointment is Jesus Christ himself, the divine anointing and fragrance; in whom, from the harmony of all virtues, there breathes for spiritual men an ineffably delightful odor, refreshing the senses of the mind. This Jesus, our <term>chrism</term>, imbues us with himself and anoints us with his own life-giving oil of gladness, so that we may be vigorous, strong, radiant, and sweet. This is the oil about which Paul writes in the Second Epistle to the Corinthians:

> It is God who confirms us with you in Christ, and who has anointed us; God has also given us the pledge of the Spirit in our hearts.

This Spirit of Christ is what the <term>chrism</term> itself is; wherever it is present, it breathes the sweetest fragrance. This anointing is from that Holy One who teaches all things, as John writes:

Christians we are in Christ; that is, anointed in the anointing itself.

<page-num>230</page-num>

# DE ECCLESIASTICA CAP. IV. 1, 2.

God is total softness, gentleness, sweetness, pleasure, delight, flavor, scent, harmony, light, and exulting joy. Jesus, the God-man, drenches all spiritual senses with every pleasure; and the soul, the bride who unites with Him, is snatched into ecstasy through pleasure, so that in the pleasure of God she is entirely pleasure—no longer merely feeling pleasure, but becoming pleasure itself, providing pleasure to others. This softness filling the touch, this flavor refreshing the taste, this scent delighting the smell, this harmony soothing the ears, and this light refreshing the eyes, is Jesus, our bridegroom, who is altogether delightful. Whoever stands closer to Him smells more fragrant. We all ought to be scent-seekers and scent-followers, so that we might be a fragrant temple of God and be sweet-smelling, so that in Christ, who is fragrant with pleasant scent, the whole temple might smell of God. Therefore, our <term>chrism</term> is Christ, with whom we are imbued so that we may be fragrant. Twelve seraphic wings cover Christ and the <term>chrism</term>, protecting them from the profane. Those primary spirits also draw their scent from Jesus without ceasing. Therefore, they are depicted around Christ and the <term>chrism</term>. All things then come to us from Jesus through the angels, who, constantly and incessantly refreshing themselves in the fullness of Jesus, exult without ceasing, singing together without end: "Holy, holy, holy." Because of their ineffable joy, they are unable to restrain themselves; they rejoice so supremely in the Supreme Being. When you read of the twelve wings, understand them to be the two <term>Seraphim</term>, whose six wings each hide the high and deep things, reveal the middle things, and are carried into the midst of God. They call to one another so that they may communicate glory to each other. They stand by the <term>chrism</term>-anointed Jesus, so we may understand that the incarnate Christ did not depart from His majesty or from the angels; and that He, anointed above His fellows and sanctified, is the same one who, in anointing, sanctifies. (Jesus Christ is the very anointing itself, which the angels venerate; therefore, it is rightly used in all sacred rites. He is to spiritual nostrils what the Word of God is to the ears and the <term>Eucharist</term> is to the eyes.

<header>CHAPTER IV. 3. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

And the signs of the cross made with the holy ointment are always a sacrament of Christ and of our <term>chrism</term>, who was crucified and died for us. In the baptistery, oil is poured in the shape of a cross, signifying that the Anointed One Himself was crucified and died for those baptized in Him, and that those baptized on the cross of Christ die and are submerged in the death of Christ, so that they may be immersed into His life. This is indeed what Paul writes to the Romans: "Are you unaware, brothers, that whoever of us were baptized into Christ Jesus, were baptized into His death and cross?" He was in the flesh for us so that we might lay down our sinful life in it and be buried by the waters of His death, buried together with Him through baptism into death. In this baptism, those of us who believe in the cross of Christ, as if crucified together with Him, die to that life for which Christ died. Therefore he says: "buried together with Christ through baptism into death; so that, just as Christ rose from the dead into the glory of the Father, so we, emerging cleansed, might walk in the newness of life." That Jesus is our sanctifying death; therefore He is present, crucified, in our waters. He is also our altar; therefore the <term>chrism</term> itself is poured upon the altar while it is being consecrated. Christ is in all things, He who is our everything, who became for us wisdom and justice, sanctification and redemption; so that (as it is written) he who glories, let him glory in the Lord. He is truly all our sacraments—the purging, the illuminating, and the perfecting ones. He is our temple, altar, priest, sacrifice, and sanctification; He who sanctified Himself for us, so that we might be sanctified in Him, our Lord Jesus Christ.

### CHAPTER IV. 3.

God is indeed in Christ, Christ is in the sacraments, and the sacraments are in humans, leading to their temporal death here, so that humans initiated in these sacraments may be in Christ and God for eternal life. Every descent of the deity is for the ascension of humanity: every descent to mortification is so that there may be a resurrection to life. God became man so that He might die for humans, and that He might establish the mor-

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion of the sacraments as tools for transformation, transitioning from the specific symbolism of the holy chrism to the general function of the sacraments in uniting the believer with Christ through death and resurrection, and beginning a new chapter on the priestly office.</meta>

...sanctifying sacraments in those who believe in Him; I say "mortifying" in that they put to death the death of faithful men, so that they may live immortally for God. All sacraments, as *Dionysius the Areopagite* teaches, bring about our assimilation to God, which cannot happen unless they simultaneously work to put us to death within ourselves, so that God may live in us. Therefore, in the sacraments, we ourselves die so that Christ may live in us: through death, one enters into life; namely, through the death of death into the life of life. Through the mortifying sacraments—in all of which are the death and cross of Christ—one enters into the life-giving Christ Himself. Thus, being crucified, dead, and buried together with Christ in these sacraments, we may emerge purged, illuminated, and perfected in Him, as if rising from the dead, living in Christ, and cleansed by His sacraments. In these sacraments lies the power of Christ's death and cross, through which we crucify the flesh with its vices and lusts, so that the power of the spirit may live in us. All things were first in Christ, and from Him, all things come to us. In Him were humility, death, the sanctification of Himself, and an example to others, so that the righteous might die. In John, He says: "For them I sanctify myself, so that they too may be sanctified in truth." That Holy One sanctified others so that those holy ones might sanctify others; He provided an example of holiness to men. From Him flow all things that are first in Himself. He, having been purged, purges; having been sanctified, sanctifies; and having been perfected, perfects. He is our <term>teleta</term>, that is, our sanctification and perfection. Because the ointment perfects, it was therefore called <term>telete</term> by the ancestors. Extreme unction is the completed Christianization. In anointing, there is the significance of being made a Christian: from the fullness of anointing, Jesus is called *Christ*, in whom all His followers are anointed.

### CHAPTER V. 1.

IT IS the function of the priestly office that all things be drawn toward order, integrity, and beauty. Every hierarchy is divided into three parts. Every function of the sacred order is divided into <term>teletas</term>, that is, into perfections. The entire function of the priestly office consists of the perfectors and the perfected; so that the superior perfected ones may perfect the inferiors, and these in turn, if there are any...

# CAP. V. 1. HIERARCHIA. 233

...lower ones; so that the first are those who perfect, the second are those who are perfected and also perfect others, and finally the third are those who are perfected. In the derivation of the deity and of divine principles to men, every priestly function and labor exists so that what is received may be handed down for the perfection of all. Now, <term>telete</term>, which is that perfection completing all things, is God Himself—one and triune—who effects the perfection of men in a threefold manner. Hence, there are three hierarchies of spirits under the Trinity of God; and each of them is threefold, consisting of three distinct orders. Openly and clearly, all those orders receive the most glorious Trinity, from the first through the middle to the <margin><unclear>but by light</unclear></margin> lowest angels, as the divine light is received and poured down.

This kindly outpouring of divine light and goodness, by which all things are perfected, proceeds through those simple spirits all the way down to men. But in former ages, when men were still children and tender in their senses, that simple divine ray of truth and justice—under the ministry of Moses—passed through many and various shadows. In these shadows, that simple light of God, as if deviating from its true self, turned into many different colors, so that the tender sight, which could not bear the pure and simple light of truth and justice (powerful as it is in force and operation), might for a time look upon clearer and, as it were, weaker colors, until such time as a more vigorous eye, capable of enduring the light, could gaze upon the truth itself without shadows. 

This is indeed the hierarchy and the legal priesthood established under the angels by the holy Moses. On the mountain, Moses was exposed to that simple light under the angels, but he did not dare to commit the truth he received to the weakness of men. Knowing how to accommodate each thing to each person, he laid down what he saw on the mountain for the humble sight of men, though he used veils that were quite thick and woven with dense thread. He placed before their eyes a kind of meadow of truth and justice, so that those who were unable to look up at the supreme Sun of Justice in heaven might look down at the light within the painted Mosaic flowers. 

For when the whole world lay entirely without light—indeed, without an image, or even any shadow of truth at all—the heavenly order had to be foreshadowed, as if first on a bare tablet <margin>blank slate</margin>. Men had to be trained in cold shadows through mutual action, labor, and shadowy worship, which Paul calls "elements" in the Epistle to the Colossians; this foreshadowing was to await illumination.

->God is the cause of all order,<-
->Order itself is the founder of all order.<-

->The world is the material of the work, and as it were the tablet of the painting.<-

The human race is the material—the clay—from which God Himself fashions what He wishes. From these humans He composes and, with Moses as his minister, in a shadowy structure, He sketches the heavenly hierarchy on earth. Then, from those whom He chooses, He established a clear image of that sketch, with Jesus as His minister, so that heaven might evidently appear on earth. Eventually, everything will be heaven and all will be truth. Thus, what fell suddenly is revealed in a long order; and what the creature demolished suddenly, the Creator rebuilds over a long succession of time, so that He might restore all things in Jesus Christ. He promotes from nothingness into shadow, from shadow into image, and from image into truth, so that eventually all things may be made true in the truth. 

Since humans had to be called back to God to contemplate Him in an angelic manner, and being so lowly for such high contemplation, it was necessary first to draw them for a while by certain imaginary reasons; staying there for a time, they might then be lifted higher, from where they might finally be promoted into truth itself. After the fall of man, there was darkness in the world: the sun of justice and truth, directing its rays through the angelic hierarchy, cast a shadow of that same hierarchy onto the earth. Then, in its own time, the sun itself descended, so that by itself it might immediately illuminate that shadow of the angelic hierarchy with colors and infuse the light of its truth, from which a colored image of the angelic city [would appear] more clearly...

From this order, anyone may see the reasons for the four senses in the Old Law that are celebrated in the Church. The **literal** sense is when the actions of those ancient people are narrated. When you think of that image and the Christian Church which it foreshadows, then you grasp the **allegorical** sense. When you are raised on high, so that from the shadow you may perceive the truth of the things themselves, then the **anagogical** sense arises for you. When you notice the instruction of a single person from the signs, then everything sounds **moral** to you.

->**Hierarchy**<-
- Celestial is signified by **Anagoge**, in its more remote withdrawal.
- Christian is signified by **Allegory**. — The Whole.
- Particular man is signified by the **moral sense**. — The Part.

->**The Mosaic system is entirely signifying in its historical events of:**<-
- Celestial truth.
- Christian image.
- The just life of a Christian man.

->**The historical event is a shadow of three things:**<-
- Light.
- The Whole.
- The Part.

In the writings of the New Testament—unless when it pleased the Lord Jesus and the apostles to speak in parables (as Christ often does in the Gospels, and John does throughout the Apocalypse)—all the remaining discourse, by which either the Savior more clearly teaches his disciples or the apostles establish the churches, carries the sense that it presents on its face. One thing is not said while another is signified; rather, that very thing which is said is signified, and the entire sense is literal. However, because the Church of God is an image, always consider the **Anagoge** in what you hear in the teachings of the Church; its significance will not truly cease until the image becomes the truth. From this, also conclude that the allegorical sense is not always present where the literal sense is; but on the contrary, where there is an allegorical sense, a literal sense always underlies it.

<header>CAP. V. I. HIERARCHIA. 237</header>

...through wholesome and divine precepts the multitude is instructed to live well and blessedly, and in the worship of true religion. Therefore, in the written law, one only observes how the goodness and power of God are present in the outcomes of things; how each person should live; and by what reason one should worship God. <margin><unclear>secrets</unclear></margin> But indeed, those more secret mysteries lying hidden under the bark of the law and the rough pretext of words—namely, those secrets of the highest divinity—if those ancient men had revealed them to the foolish common people, what else would that have been, I ask you, than giving what is holy to dogs and scattering pearls before swine? Therefore, those ancient wise men did not communicate the deepest contemplation of the most secret things and the retreat of more abstruse truth found in the law, except privately from the crowd, only to the wise; <margin>1 Cor 2:6</margin> among whom alone Paul writes to the Corinthians that he speaks wisdom. It was a holy practice among all the ancient wise men to keep mystical doctrines unviolated by the profane multitude through the knots of enigmas. The <margin>x</margin> Master of life, Jesus, revealed many things to his disciples which they indeed were unwilling to write down, lest they should become common to the crowd. <margin>x <unclear>secrets</unclear></margin> Dionysius records that the more secret Christian mysteries were poured from mind to mind by the authors of our religion without writing, through the medium of the spoken word alone. In <margin><unclear>nearly</unclear></margin> the same way, by God's command, that true interpretation of the law, divinely revealed and delivered to Moses, <margin>x</margin> was called <term>Cabala</term>, a Hebrew word which in Latin is "reception"; because that doctrine was received from one to another by a quasi-hereditary right through regular successions of revelations, rather than through written records. But after the Hebrews were restored from Babylonian servitude by Cyrus, and after the temple was restored under Zerubbabel, they applied their minds to repairing the law. Ezra, then the head of the church, after correcting the book of Moses, clearly recognized that through the exiles, killings, flight, and captivity of the Israelite people, the custom established by their ancestors of delivering doctrine from hand to hand <note>orally</note> could not be preserved. He saw that the secrets of heavenly doctrine divinely granted to them would perish, as they could not last long in memory without the help of written commentaries. Thus, he decreed that, having called together the wise men who then remained, each should bring forward what he held in memory concerning the mysteries of the law, and with the help of scribes, these should be compiled into seventy volumes (for there were nearly that many wise men in the <term>Synedrio</term> <note>Sanhedrin</note>).

Regarding this matter, Ezra himself speaks thus in the fourteenth chapter of his fourth book <note>The Latin text says "thirteenth," but the marginal note correctly identifies the passage as 2 Esdras 14 (also known as 4 Esdras 14).</note>: "When the forty days were finished, the Most High spoke, saying: 'The first things you wrote, set out in public; let the worthy and the unworthy read them. But you shall preserve the last seventy books, so that you may hand them over to the wise among your people. For in these is the vein of understanding, the fountain of wisdom, and the river of knowledge.' And so I did." These are the books of the Cabala and of "reception," which are honored with such reverence among the Hebrews that no one is permitted to touch them except those who are forty years old. In those books, all our [Christian] doctrines are clearly present. Pope Sixtus IV carefully arranged to have these books translated into Latin through the work of a certain learned man; while he was living, three of these books were being read. <margin>Pico</margin> Therefore, it is certain that besides the law which God gave to Moses on Mount Sinai, and which he left written and contained in five books, the true exposition of the law was also revealed to that same Moses by God himself. This included the manifestation of all the mysteries and secrets contained under the bark and rough surface of the words of the law. Thus, Moses received a twofold law on the mountain: the literal and the spiritual. He wrote down the literal law and delivered it to the people by God's command; but he communicated the spiritual law only to the wise men, who were seventy in number, whom Moses had chosen by God's command to guard the law. He commanded them not to write it down, but to reveal it to their successors by word of mouth, and they in turn to others. From this reciprocal reception, it was called the "Cabala." This knowledge was later put into writing; these books clearly contain all the secrets and mysteries that are veiled in the literal law. <margin>Romans 3:2 of the true OT</margin> These secrets—as Origen believes—Paul calls the "oracles of God," which give life to the law; without this life-giving spirit, the law lies dead. That exposition and spiritual sense of the whole literal law which Moses received from God, as we have said, was what we call the anagogical sense. For just as there is among us a fourfold method of interpreting the Bible and a fourfold sense—literal, mystical and allegorical, tropological, and anagogical—so it is among the Hebrews. But the Cabala pursues the anagogical sense, which was delivered to Moses from the mouth of God. This sense is more sublime and divine, drawing and leading us upward from earthly things to heavenly things, from sensible things to intelligible things, from temporal things to eternal things, from the lowest to the highest, from human to divine, and from corporeal...

<header>CHAPTER V. I. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

...towards spiritual things. The Jews follow earthly, sensible, temporal, lowest, human, and corporal things, all of which are found in the letter that kills. Those who are called into the Christian spirit follow heavenly, intelligible, eternal, celestial, divine, and spiritual things—specifically that which was revealed to Moses by God and delivered by that same Moses to the seventy chosen ones. This sense is heavenly and celestial. Men are called closer to this divine truth by Christ, so that they may have a deeper wisdom than the Jews and live more rightly; this is the <term>anagogical</term> sense. Regarding this sense, Dionysius writes that the leaders are those who learned the holy tabernacle more sacredly from Moses, whom we should understand to be the seventy wise men whom Moses chose to teach the exposition of the law. 

But so that we may believe in heavenly things with faith and charity, our leader will be Jesus Christ, who led us out of Egypt and even beyond the earthly promise and the earthly Jerusalem. He established us under himself in a clearer hierarchy, which stands in relation to the legal hierarchy as its end and perfection. Then, the end and final perfection of this hierarchy of ours is that heavenly and celestial one. As Dionysius teaches, our hierarchy is the middle one between the heavenly and the legal. As befits a middle state, it includes both extremes within itself to some extent, having a spiritual and shining understanding from the higher level, and a carnal, bodily, and sensible meaning from the lower level. For as long as we are human, even the most spiritual among us cannot be entirely without signs. The lower reflects the higher as best it can, in its own way. All things that are in the heavenly hierarchy exist in the Christian hierarchy by way of images; and all things that are in the Christian hierarchy were preceded by the legal hierarchy in a shadowy way.

<language>Latin</language>

<page-num>240</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V. 1. CONCERNING THE CHURCH</header>

...some are sanctifying, some are sanctified, and some are in between these—both sanctified and sanctifying at the same time. All, however, are ministers of God for the derivation of the Deity, leading at last to the full holiness of men. All strive to imitate the angelic orders, so that the first trinity may purge, the second illuminate, and the third perfect and complete; so that the whole church may be purged, enlightened, and perfected in God. Purgation consists in the laying aside of whatever was our own, even of our greatest hope in ourselves, so that, being turned toward God, we may hope in Him. Illumination consists in faith in revealed things, which is our wisdom. Perfection consists in the completion of charity, which is the fullness of the law and of justice. The highest members purge, illuminate, and perfect; the lowest are purged, illuminated, and perfected, having already been initiated into the sacred mysteries, baptized, and confirmed. Those in the middle are also purged, and they themselves purge; they are already shining and illuminate those who have been purged, leading them toward completion. 

The highest members in the church complete and perfect; in them, there ought to be the greatest power for establishing men in hope, illuminating them in faith, and finally perfecting them in charity. There, and by those primary members in the church, the same actions are performed as by those in the middle; and by these, the same actions as by the first. For purgation, illumination, and perfection are carried out by all simultaneously. Indeed, these three operations, worked by a single cause, cannot be separated; but by the middle ranks, those same things are performed with a more notable distinction than by the first; and finally, by the last and highest ranks, with the most notable distinction of all.
<margin>of God</margin>
That whole divine operation in heavenly things tends toward assimilation to God, and toward the representation of His trinity, unity, beauty, and goodness. Hence there are three operations: purgation, illumination, and perfection. The effect of purgation is unity, simplicity, power, constancy, and stability, which in them is a single quality by which they firmly establish themselves in God. The effect of illumination is a beautiful variety, truth, order, beauty, wisdom, and clarity; these words signify the same thing, and in the angels, they signify a certain multiform knowledge of the divine Word. Finally, the effect of perfection is goodness, kindness, love, perfection, and charity. These operations also have their effects in us, in the likeness of the unity, truth, and goodness in the angels. For our unity, toward which our purgation tends, is hope, which is certainly our simplicity and stability. Our truth is faith

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ecclesiastical hierarchy, focusing on how different members participate in the divine light according to their rank and defining the specific roles of the three orders—bishops, priests, and ministers—as described by Dionysius.</meta>

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>241</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V. 1. THE HIERARCHY.</header>
<meta>institutional stamp: THE INSTITUTE OF MEDIAEVAL STUDIES; handwritten pencil notes in margins; several words circled in pencil.</meta>

...to the multiform truth which was in Jesus Christ. Our goodness and perfection is the love of the good. Toward these ends, every office and function in Christ’s church is directed. In those who are hoping, <note>1. Read *of those who are hoping*.</note> three types may be considered: three types of believers, and three types of lovers. This is not because anyone in the church is without love or without faith—for all virtues are in all people—but because these are distinguished by degrees and vary according to a greater or lesser participation in the divine ray. This ray is one and the same, acting in all; yet, due to the distinction of degrees, there is a variety of titles assigned, and each has its own proper role. Each person in their own rank performs the duty according to their given gift; all collaborate in pulling away from this world and in the sanctification of men for God. "Now," says Paul, "being made free from sin and become servants to God, we have our fruit unto sanctification, and the end everlasting life."

According to Dionysius, there are three named ecclesiastical orders: bishops, priests, and ministers. The office of the bishops is the most extensive, capable of whatever the lower orders can do, and having besides its own proper duty, which is to perform the Eucharist, to consecrate the holy ointment, and to ordain priests. From this, it is evident that he calls "pontiffs" those whom the later church calls "bishops"; and he calls "priests" almost those who are called "deacons." According to him, the office of the priests is to initiate those purified by the ministers into the sacred rites and to provide those things which are customary, leaving the explanation of the mysteries to the bishop. For he intends for the ministers to separate and detach men from the world and themselves, and as it were to strip them of their former habits entirely and lay them bare; so that they have nothing contrary to being illuminated by the sacraments, but instead, having shaken off and wiped away the dust, they are as pure as a <term>tabula rasa</term>, ready for the heavenly image to be painted upon them. 
<margin>X</margin>
When they have been so prepared by the ministers, they are then presented to the priests. <margin>1 Cor 15:49</margin> When the priests see that they have no stain and smell of nothing from their heavy past, and that they are highly disposed to be molded and formed in Christ—so that, just as they have borne the image of the earthly, they may now bear the image of the heavenly—the priest, as it were, paints them with sacraments, initiating them with the most sacred signs, through which (at least for believers) the Spirit of God operates. <page-num>242</page-num>
<header>OF THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY CHAPTER V. 2.</header>

...they are enlightened by the hands of the priests, so that they may be perfected and completed by the pontiff. For every completion belongs to the pontiff, who is considered to be "full": he offers the Eucharist, confirms with the holy ointment, and reveals the mysteries of the signs to those who are worthy. Thus, in the hands of the pontiff—if they are found suitable—they are perfected in all things. Once perfected by him, they proceed to the place and rank of their own office, just as the wisdom of the pontiff justly arranges. The pure are introduced into the church by deacons and ministers; they are promoted through the enlightening sacraments by the priests; and they are perfected through the perfecting mysteries by the pontiff. The pontiff, knowledgeable in all sacred rites and a contemplator of divine things, rightly arranges the church under his authority so that each person may perform some divine action within it, in imitation of God and the divine spirits. For (as Dionysius teaches) every action of anyone in the church must imitate divine action. For, as he also says, priestly functions are images of divine operations. For the ecclesiastical society on earth is established by Christ according to the pattern of the heavenly hierarchy of angels, who constantly imitate God. In Christ, all things are arranged by the pontifical office; the pontiff represents the person of God and Christ on earth, so that everyone in the church may diligently apply themselves to some divine operation for the public benefit of the whole society, according to their ability. Thus, each may be recognized as a healthy and perfect member in the mystical body of Christ. As Paul says: "To each one as God has distributed the measure of faith." There are many divisions of graces; to each is given the manifestation of the Spirit for usefulness—namely, so that the church may finally be perfected in spirit and in the immortality to which it is called. It must strive toward this with all its strength, maintaining order with distinct offices under the pontiff, under the angels, and under God, if it ever wishes to be immortal.

---

### CHAPTER V. 2.

When a pontiff is being consecrated, he lies prostrate at the altar with the right hand of the [consecrating] pontiff and the Gospels held over his head. The Trinity is invoked upon him; he is marked with the sign of the cross; he is named by the entire priesthood; and he is kissed. The same things are done

...with the ancient rite for the priest, except for the laying of the Gospel on the head. Finally, the same is done for the minister; but he submits to consecration with only one knee bent. Only the pontiff carries the Gospels on his head. All those who are consecrated are summoned to the communion of the Eucharist.

### CHAPTER V. 3.

To speak of what these visible signs signify—so that the spiritual eye may see its objects along with the physical eye—that first approach to the pontiff and prostration at the altar speaks of and advises submission to God and to one's pontiff. For kneeling is a sign of submission and obedience; Paul used this word in his letter to the Philippians when he affirmed that all things are subject to Jesus, saying: "Because of this, God also has exalted him and given him a name which is above every name, so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, in heaven, <margin><unclear>Note</unclear></margin> on earth, and under the earth." Therefore, my beloved, obey.

This submission exists in the church, and ought to exist: the submission of every lower person to a superior for their sanctification to God in Christ. In this also is the presidency of superiors for the sanctification of their inferiors, under whatever title they preside; even among the common people as fathers, masters, or husbands. Paul teaches this in his letter to the Ephesians and in the letter written to the Colossians, in which he commands submission and obedience from children, servants, and wives; this is to be understood in all things so that they may be sanctified by those superiors. For this reason, Paul notably always adds and emphasizes "in the Lord." To the Ephesians he says: "Husbands, love your wives as Christ loved the church, and give themselves up for their wives, so that they may sanctify them by the washing of grace in the word of life; so that they may present to themselves glorious wives, not having spot or wrinkle or any such thing, but holy and without blemish."

Furthermore, that laying on of the pontiff's hands upon the head signifies that the person consecrated to God, whoever they may be, is entirely encompassed by God, so that He may be in them and perform all things, and so they may hope to be defended and safe by His power. For the hand is the power of God in man. If God is with us...

It also relates these religious duties to social roles, such as those of husbands and fathers, as a path toward sanctification.</summary>

<header>CONCERNING THE CHURCH CHAPTER V. 3.</header>

<margin>Eph 6</margin>
...if God is for us, who can be against us? But we are defended against spiritual wickedness in the heavens. Because, as he writes more clearly to the Ephesians, our struggle is not against flesh and blood, but against principalities and powers, against the rulers of the world of this darkness, against spiritual wickedness in the heavens. Moreover, being marked with the sign of the cross proclaims and declares that we carry the cross of Jesus, so that we may be his disciples. For he said: "He who does not take up [his] cross and follow me, cannot be my disciple." To take up and receive the cross is, in truth, through faith in the death of Christ, to lay down the sinful life completely and forever, and to bury it with the mortal flesh of Christ; and thereafter, with eyes fixed on Christ, to constantly imitate his most holy life; so that, being conformed to his death, they may reach the resurrection which is from the dead, in the same way that Paul writes of himself to the Philippians: "One thing," he says, "I know: forgetting those things which are behind, and stretching myself forward to those things which are ahead, I press on toward the goal for the prize of the upward call of God in Christ Jesus." To the Galatians the same man writes: "Those who are Christ's have crucified their flesh with its vices and lusts."

This is what the seal of the cross signifies, impressed upon a person professing Christ; so that, when he has received the mark of the holy cross from the pontiff, he may say with Paul: "I have been crucified with Christ to the cross; I live, yet no longer I, but Christ lives in me." In him, truly, the person is a member, so that he may be conformed to the head, and a shoot in the tree of Christ; so that in all things he may taste of the root; so that he no longer lives by his own judgment, which has been crucified, but, having drawn the vital sap from the root, which is Christ, he may live in all things (so to speak) in a Christian manner. Therefore, let each one seriously reflect within himself what the impression of the sign of the cross means, which is the very death of the sinful person within the man forever, so that it may never live again thereafter. For, as that teacher of life taught, his cross must be taken up, and he himself must be followed; that is, our own life must be abandoned, and his must be imitated. Then, he is announced by name as consecrated by the pontiff, which certainly indicates divine election and <term>lot</term> <note>sors</note>, by whose judgment and will all things that are done rightly in the church are performed. But here there is some space for us to say something about divine lot-casting, if we first say what a lot is; a name which is used much in the scriptures...

It explains that consecration represents a divine election and "lot" that aligns the individual with Christ's life and the church's order.</summary>
