<meta>This page continues the discussion of ecclesiastical selection and the divine role in the church hierarchy, transitioning from the symbolism of the cross to the practice of choosing leaders through the "sacrament of the lot."</meta>

<page-num>245</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V. 3. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

A lot is indeed the accidental outcome of some matter, from which the word *sortior* [to cast lots] is derived, which means to make lots, or to receive lots, or to decide or choose something by lot. Those who choose anything by lot are called <term>sortilegi</term> <note>diviners or lot-casters</note>. Likewise, those who foretell the future by lots are called the same. However, a lot is sometimes taken to mean fatal necessity, and sometimes judgment. A lot is also understood as a patrimony <note>an inheritance</note>, from which the words "consorts" and "consortships" are derived. Among Christians—certainly those ancient men and apostles—the sacrament of the lot was of great importance; and the casting of the lot among men indicated the most certain election by God. Indeed, for men who expect all things from God and believe that God works through their signs, the lot is an argument, a sign, and as it were a sacrament, in which one recognizes upon whom the divine will and most certain election has fallen.

> The lot, says Solomon, is cast into the lap, but it is directed by the Lord.

And so Matthias was elected to the apostleship by lot, as Luke writes; with much preceding prayer, which invoked the Holy Spirit, so that through the sacrament of the lot, the divine will in the election might be reverently approved. The words in the Acts of the Apostles are these:

> And they appointed two: Joseph, who is called Barsabas, who was surnamed Justus, and Matthias. And praying, they said: "You, Lord, who know the hearts of all, show which one of these two you have chosen to take the place of this ministry and apostleship, from which Judas went astray, that he might go to his own place." And the lot fell upon Matthias, and he was numbered with the eleven apostles.

"Lot" also has its own meaning in the scriptures sometimes for fatal necessity, for judgment, and for inheritance, from which "consorts" and "consortships" are named. For those whom divine predestination identifies as sons and heirs of His patrimony are said to be in the "lot" of God and in fellowship with Christ. However, everything in the church of Christ—of which God himself is, as it were, the soul—ought to be acted upon by God himself, while humans meanwhile believe and wait. Whatever person is called into the church, or promoted to any rank or place of dignity within it, or whatever is done by that promoted person by virtue of his office—whatever (I say) is done either in the construction, ordination, or completion of the church, if it is not done by the instinct of the Divine Spirit, it is nothing.

> Unless the Lord, says David, builds the house, in

<summary>The author explains the theological significance of "the lot" (sors) as a means of divine election, citing the biblical examples of Solomon and the appointment of Matthias. He emphasizes that all church governance and appointments must be directed by the Holy Spirit to have any validity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine guidance in the church, specifically focusing on the use of lots in the early church as a means of discerning God's will for ecclesiastical appointments, and contrasting this with the corrupt practices of contemporary princes.</meta>

<page-num>246</page-num>
<header># DE ECCLESIASTICA CAP. V. 3.</header>

Those who build it have labored in vain. Unless the Lord guards the city, they who guard it watch in vain. In His work, which is the church, it is right that He should govern everything. But if human ingenuity rashly meddles, it is inevitable that everything will be disturbed and confused. Among men, nothing else is required except persistent prayer and petition, indubitable faith, and the firmest hope, which (as *Paul* says) does not disappoint. 

Because the actions and operations of God are hidden, it must be believed that the apostles and apostolic men in the early church sometimes used lots, not without divine command. They did this so that through those lots they might be taught the judgment of God, and whom they should name to any gift or office according to the will of God, believing clearly that the will of God was present where the lot fell. As soon as the lot fell upon someone, everyone named him as if with one voice from God, believing without doubt that he who was named on earth was also named by God in heaven. For what is loosed and bound on earth is, by the *Savior's* testimony, loosed and bound in heaven; because when people are gathered in Him, He is in their midst. This is what *Dionysius* says: that the sacrament of nomination signifies the choice and approval of God in heaven. 

Indeed, such a nomination of anyone—whether as a member or into some ministry in *Jesus Christ*—is a great sin to perform in His church unless it is done by the highest and holiest of men, and in the most venerable places, and furthermore with long and devout fasts, vows, sacrifices, and prayers; so that it may appear to have been done not by mere mortals <note>The Latin "homunculis" literally means "little men," used here disparagingly.</note>, but by the great God. 

Therefore, one may here curse that detestable custom which has long grown within the church and is now so ingrained it nearly brings the *Christian* commonwealth to its end. By this custom, temporal princes—insane indeed, and though they bear the *Christian* name are enemies and foes of God, blasphemers of *Christ*, and destroyers of the church—nominate bishops to govern *Christ's* church. They do this not with humble and pious minds, but with proud and rash ones; not in sacred and pure places, but in bedrooms and at banquets. And they nominate (O unspeakable crime!) men ignorant of every sacred matter but knowledgeable in all things profane, for whom they have already shamefully claimed those same bishoprics. O wicked age! O ruined morals! O madness of princes! O blindness and folly of churchmen! Whether this is to be mocked or mourned...

<summary>The author argues that only God should govern the church and its appointments, using the apostolic tradition of casting lots as evidence of divine selection. He vehemently condemns temporal princes for corruptly appointing ignorant men to bishoprics in secular settings rather than through sacred prayer.</summary>

<keywords>ecclesiastical appointments, divine providence, apostles, lots, Matthias, Paul, Dionysius, corruption, temporal princes, bishops, Christian commonwealth</keywords>

<page-num>247</page-num>
<header>CAP. V. 3. HIERARCHY.</header>

I am even more ignorant of it: order is perverted, the flesh swells, the spirit is extinguished, and all things remain deformed and foul. Unless Christ has mercy on His church, death, which is now close at hand, will take hold of everything. For in what way will that endure which is handled with pestilential counsel and poisonous hands? The good Jesus, ascribing nothing to Himself, humbly referred all things to the Father.

> "If I glorify myself," He says in *John*, "my glory is nothing. It is my Father who glorifies me"; of whom not He himself but the Father said, *You are a priest forever, according to the order of Melchizedek.*

But those princes of wickedness, Christians in name but in truth ministers of the devil, arrogantly ascribing to themselves the things that are God's, demolish the work which the blood of Christ built and the temple of God with wicked lust; to whom God will eventually give according to their works. But let us return to what we were speaking of. All those sacred things, as we have said, are the <term>sacrament</term> of nomination in heaven. By the name <term>sacrament</term>, not only are those seven most well-known in the church so called; but furthermore, whatever is signified remotely by a sensible sign and visible marks is called a sacrament. In the lot of *Matthias*, *Dionysius* notes a great sacrament, when he says that in it there was a sign for the apostles as to whom the hidden divine election had fallen upon. So in the sacrament of joint-naming, it is revealed—as is believed without doubt, if it is done by suitable men—that divine nomination in heaven; by which, whomever He wills, that person is named in the church. He who is not named by Him should believe that he is not what he professes to be; which I wish the bishops would hear, who are named as such by temporal kings. In the salutation and kiss of him who is named, likeness, love, and joy are implied, which exists in all toward the one made like them, and in him also toward those to whom he is made like. But what is the meaning of that imposition of the most holy Gospels upon the head of the consecrated bishop? Truly, it is so that the bishop may contain all sacred and divine things in his head and breast, and be by far the wisest of all in divine matters, and the best by far in every virtue and holy action, after the example of that Christ who is preached in the Gospels. He somehow signifies Him when he receives those most sacred Gospels upon his head, so that as far as possible he may represent Christ himself within him through his life, holiness, and goodness, and the wisdom of that truth and religion. But the minister, with one knee bent, represents the <term>sacrament</term> of the purified—

<header>ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY CHAPTER VI. 1.</header>

the priest has two knees bent, representing those who are purged and illuminated; the bishop, besides the bending of two knees, has the holy gospels upon his head, as a sacrament of both purgation, illumination, and perfection; because he shows the evangelical mysteries in the proper rite, so that men filled with them may be perfected according to their capacity. Thus the entire ecclesiastical institution, through purgation, illumination, and perfection in Christ, strives for the constant simplicity of men, a wise order, and perfect goodness, according to that clear model of the angels; so that above the chaos and confusion of the world, a bright order of men, simple and perfect in God, may stand out, which is the city set on a hill, the light of the world, and the salt of the earth; which, shining under Christ the sun with faith, hope, and charity, may illuminate and give life to the world. But, alas, a smoke and foul darkness from the valley of shadowy men has for so long and so thickly breathed upward that it has almost overwhelmed the light of the city; so that now churchmen, wrapped in darkness, not knowing where they go, have foolishly mingled and confused themselves with every <unclear/>, so that now again in the world there is nothing more confused than the crowd of men. In this world, because of the turbulent collision of men in such great confusion of things, the seal of Christ which was impressed is nearly obliterated and destroyed. Merciful Jesus, by your omnipotence, I pray you, quickly scatter the darkness and restore order and serenity in your church.

JUST as there are those instituted by God who purge, illuminate, and perfect, who are the active causes and as it were men of
<margin>2</margin>
masculine virtue, so on the opposite side it is necessary that there be those who are purged, those who are illuminated, and those who are perfected; who are as it were the matter of the church, and like the woman to be placed under the masculine part, so that she may be perfected in the man. In this part are the catechumens to be assisted in spiritual birth, apostates to be recalled, the possessed to be exorcised, and penitents to be cleansed. In these offices are the ministers, whose duty is to purge and to produce, as far as they can, a simple unity in men; so that those sliding down and flowing away

<page-num>249</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VI. I. THE HIERARCHY.</header>

...and having been tossed about, they may establish themselves in God, at the point of hope, so that they who hope in God may now be rooted. But as there are various people to be purged, so there are various ministers. To <term>catechumens</term> striving to depart from the world, the catechizing ministers reach out a hand and draw them toward God with constant instruction. The Adversary incessantly attacks the church, so that he may demolish what God builds through the ministers. Therefore, he overthrows and destroys some as apostates; some he shakes and vexes as <term>energumenos</term> <note>those troubled or possessed by evil spirits</note>. On the other hand, the ministers of God must be anxious with labor and diligence, so that they may repair the damage inflicted by the enemy and restore the church with all their strength—both by calling away those from the city of the devil, by recalling those who have fallen from the city of God, and by strengthening any who have been shaken. 

Therefore, against their apostates are the church’s <term>catechumens</term>; against their <term>energumenos</term>, whom they disturb so that they may fall, are the church’s penitents, whom the ministers purge so that they may stand firm. Truly, the church strives with unceasing labor to preserve what is its own and to recover what was recently its own—I mean the apostates and the <term>energumenos</term>—just as, on the other hand, the Adversary strives to retain what is and was his: namely, the <term>catechumens</term> and the penitents. Thus, with mutual excursions and frequent conflicts, these armies of God and the devil fight for dominion with alternating losses: spirit against spirit, and spiritual men against carnal men. Evil spirits love the flesh; good spirits desire the spirit. *The flesh*, says Paul, *is against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh.* But because, by the Savior’s testimony, the spirit is willing but the flesh is weak, therefore eventually the spirit overcomes the flesh, and light overcomes darkness, and life overcomes death, and God overcomes the devil, through our Lord Jesus Christ, to whom all things shall be subjected, so that God may be all in all.
<margin>1 Cor 15:28 <br/> <unclear>I for prayer</unclear></margin>

The church of God, which is situated above the world on the mountain of God, in clear air and a bright spirit, has the material for its own happiness supplied to it from the valley of the misery of this world. By its lower masculine and active part, the church lifts up the higher and more passive part of the world—almost exactly as the rays of the sun, thinning and rarefying the surface of the waters with their heat, lift them on high—so that from it the church may first construct for itself a kind of body. This body, though it is thicker and more corporeal than the more spiritual part of the church, is nevertheless more spiritual than the entire worldly body. From which body then, and

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ecclesiastical hierarchy, comparing the structure of the church to the physiology of the human body, specifically the role of intermediary spirits that connect the soul and the body.</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VI. I. THE CHURCH HIERARCHY.</header>

From the coarser part of the church—that which is healthy and whole, namely, from its highest and elder region—and from those who have already nearly become spiritual, who are as it were the purer, clearer, and more vital blood of the church's body; from such men in the passive part of the church, who are more mature, they are drawn <gloss>into the spiritual order</gloss>. This is so that they may now be active parts and be spirits; but first they are purgators, whose task and duty is entirely focused on the cleansing, restoration, and nourishment of the body, which is the lower and coarser part of the church. Between this part and God, the <margin>acting cause</margin> and soul of the church, are spiritual men, the workers and effectors of all things. Just as in a human being, <margin>so</margin> between his soul and body there are intermediary spirits that are pure, subtle, bright, and warm, produced from the very heat of the heart and the finer blood; so similarly between God and the church of gathered people, there are spiritual men from among those very gathered people—from its more sincere part and, as it were, its more deified blood—produced by the heat and love of God. These men are intermediaries between God and the rest of the people, who depend on God through them. 

And also, just as <gloss>between the soul</gloss> and body of a man there are three kinds of spirits—for some are natural, some vital, and some animal—so these animal spirits serve the soul most highly, while the vital serve the senses, and the natural serve generation, nutrition, growth, cleansing, restoration, healing, and similar works in the more corporeal part of the man. In almost the same way in Christ, between God and the common people, there are <margin>the</margin> three kinds of spiritual men. Some of these, like the animal spirits, serve God (the soul of the church) most directly. Some take care of the lower part of the church especially, so that they may restore, nourish, cherish, increase, cleanse, and preserve it. Some are intermediate between these, who serve as if they were senses, and who illuminate people so that they may recognize the sensible qualities of the sacraments in the church. 

These first ones, who are closer to the carnal part of the church, give life to the flesh through a purgatorial power, washing away their foul death so that they may live in the hope of God. The second ones, who <margin>2</margin> create spiritual senses, illuminate those who have been purged and are hoping in God, so that the believers may perfectly perceive the sacraments of the church and every more sacred sign, and delight in them with the greatest faith. The third and highest group are those who are focused on God with all their desire; <margin>behind</margin> they clearly understand the simple principle of every sense, leading those who have perceived the signs and the sacraments through faith, who are fit among them

<summary>The author describes a hierarchy within the church where spiritual leaders act as intermediaries between God and the laity, mirroring the function of natural, vital, and animal spirits in the human body. These leaders are divided into three groups dedicated to purging and nourishing the lower church, illuminating believers through the sacraments, and contemplating God directly.</summary>

<page-num>251</page-num>

### CHAPTER VI. I. HIERARCHY.

...and they approach as mature ones, (they make them observers of the simple reasons of mysteries and sensible things, so far as they are capable; so that what they sensed long ago through faith under the priests, they may finally, under the pontiff <gloss>Augustine</gloss>, understand with the greatest love, and according to their capacity (being filled with mysteries) they may be perfected. For light is a rare and subtle thing if it is not, as it were, condensed by heat; and the contemplation of sensible things) is almost empty and void if they are not filled with the love of the understood mysteries. The vision, love, and worship of mysteries complete and perfect all things. Therefore, those supreme ones who perfect are the pontiffs; the intermediate who illuminate are the priests; the first who purge are the ministers. <margin>X</margin> <margin>①</margin> [But those who are purged into a simple hope in God, so that they may present themselves in him, and hoping may now breathe in him, and (being reborn as a new creature [they may become] <margin>generate</margin> now entirely a new creature, these are in the first grade and vestibule of the church, and like its bones in their thick firmness, having little sensation in themselves, but stable in hope, depending on God in all things which they do in the world for the support of the superior church; <margin>X</margin> <margin>-> profit</margin> they are certainly the providers of the necessities of life, ministers not of a clever wit but of the good God and of skillful nature <margin>ε</margin> † receiving the supply of God and nature † and administering it to others; so that in temporal matters they are ministers to the ministers of spiritual things. For all things belong to God. No one has anything of his own to give; no one possesses what he has not received. <margin>X</margin> The earth is the Lord's <margin>Psalm 24 (</margin> and its fullness. Both all temporal and spiritual things are his by right, for he created all things. Temporal things are from the earth; spiritual things are from heaven. <margin>2</margin> The humbler and more earthly part of the church is the minister of temporal things through God's work and simple reason; but that celestial and more sublime part bestows spiritual things, as the minister of the great kindness of God. These are ministers to the more corporeal and coarser part of the church for eternal life, generously providing them with spiritual food. <margin>1.</margin> But the humbler part, which depends on God through hope and diligently draws from the earth that by which the church may be fed, this part supplies temporal life to that more spiritual part. Thus, by mutual duties and benefits, while the church fights here, and while it is partly temporal and partly (and mostly) spiritual, it is supported from both sides and from both a higher and a lower place in the riches of God, both temporal and spiritual; with God's servants and ministers holily exercising themselves in sustaining both the temporal and spiritual life; the superior part supporting the inferior which lives temporally, and...

<header>ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY CHAPTER VI. I.</header>

...the lower part living and thriving spiritually from the higher; so that the whole church in this world, having acquired only its bare necessities—and those <margin>into</margin> acquired only sparingly for the day—may strive here for the spiritual life, for eternal life. In this lowliest part of the church are those called catechumens who have already been instructed, the recalled penitents, the exorcized <term>energumens</term> <note>those troubled by spirits</note>, <margin>thus</margin> and recovered apostates. It is their duty to seek the physical sustenance of the church through the work and labor of the body among God’s earthly riches; so that they may exercise themselves in earthly things in all simplicity and truth, now acting as God's ministers in these terrestrial matters for the nourishment of the frail and bodily part of the church. These are the ones whom Paul—urging harmony upon the whole church in his first epistle written to the Corinthians—likens to the lowlier members of the body; though these are less attractive than others, they are all the more necessary, and greater care is taken for them so that they may be preserved whole.

From this class of men, if any of them—through the diligence and labor of the ministers from the highest and most serene part—finally emerge as <term>deifications</term> <note>those being made god-like</note>, <margin>X</margin> with every stain of sin thoroughly wiped away so that they are judged by the perceptive ministers to be transparent and capable of receiving some light, <margin>2</margin> they are then offered to the priests, so that, being bathed by them in a more abundant light, they may shine with a greater faith. To these are offered the spectacles of faith, so that with a clear eye of the mind they may now, being purified, discern the sensible sacraments into which they were initiated by the priests. Having been initiated into the Christian rites and marked with the most sacred signs, <margin>X</margin> they may be observers of the divine and illustrious images in Christ without stumbling in their spiritual perception. They themselves also now shine with those same likenesses <margin>resembling</margin>, showing in all humility the signs, marks, and characters within themselves—impressed by human ministry but <margin>X</margin> with a divine seal—standing as most worthy witnesses of a surely more sublime and simpler light and truth.

These have only just entered the door of the temple of God and contemplate the more common furnishings of the house of God; they are not yet admitted to those riches which are more secret <margin>ark (3)</margin> and hidden in the inner depths of God, which the pontiff guards holily and religiously in his <term>ark of wisdom</term>. <margin>thus</margin> It is not lawful to reveal these except to those Christians who are perfected and completed by the love of God and of divine things. Dionysius includes under the name of the "holy people" those who have been illuminated by the sacraments of the priests, <margin>certainly</margin> who are now permitted to be present at and observe the sacred rites, and who are most vigorous in faith. Meanwhile, others, such as those who are in the process of purification...

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the church's hierarchy, moving from the lowest members (the catechumens and penitents) to the "holy laity" and the "monks," who represent the bridge between the common people and the clergy.</meta>

<header>CHAPTER VI. 2, 3. HIERARCHY.</header>

If they themselves cannot be present for the sacred rites—even if they are not the kind of people who can easily be absent—then let those initiated into the rites and the <margin>holy laity</margin> holy laity occupy themselves in preparing the sustenance of the church. For a superior in the church can do whatever an inferior can, but the reverse is not true. Indeed, the highest and most serene part of the <margin>highest laity</margin> holy laity—which has now risen as if into the region of fire and glows with love, ready to be set ablaze—should be delivered by the priests to the <term>pontiff</term>. This is so that he may opportunely lay his finishing hand upon them for their perfection, and fill them, as much as they can hold, with the simple and unveiled mysteries, which, once seen, cause them to burn with love. These are above the common people, and Dionysius calls them <term>Monks</term>. They are the more refined part of the laity, <margin>clergy</margin> already so inflamed with love that, like fire, they cling to the celestial spheres. In this state, they may progress so far that they can be promoted into the order of <term>ministers</term>, so that those who are the highest among those receiving the rites might be the lowest among those performing them. For such as the pontiff is among the spiritual ranks, such are the unique and solitary monks among the physical ranks; and as the priests are among the former, such are those numbered among the holy laity among the latter. Finally, as the ministers are among the former, so among the latter are those who are in the hands of the purifiers.

<header>ON THE CHURCH, CHAPTER VI. 2, 3.</header>

<margin>monk</margin>
...should advise and instruct. Therefore, so that a monk may profess the unity he has just attained and his indivisible way of living, he approaches someone of the priestly order—who seems to me to have held that place in the early church which deacons hold among us—and stands before the altar, upright towards God, as one who is single, simple, and solitary. The priest, after giving thanks to God, turns to the man and hears him <gloss>Vows</gloss> renounce, by a most solemn profession, all division and distraction of life forever; he vows to keep himself gathered into one with a simple eye, so that his whole body may be full of light; to strive toward one goal only; and to serve God alone. During this great profession, he receives the sign of the holy cross, so that the things he professes to renounce may be understood as having died on Christ’s cross, never to live again in him. His hair is shorn, so that, with everything divided and superfluous shaved away, he may appear as pure as possible in the sight of God. He changes his garment, changing his life; so that a simpler garment may match a simpler life. This is observed in every holy change: that when the inner man transforms himself from one spiritual state to another, the outer man should do the same at the same time, so that the outward appearance corresponds to the inward. <margin>IMP</margin> Hence, the garment and tunic are called by the holy apostles the "life of man." The kiss, moreover, signifies a most sweet union. Finally, he is admitted to the Eucharist, but in a more sacred manner than the rest of the people, since he is now knowledgeable of the mysteries. 

Thus the monk is duly completed and perfected as the <term>monad</term> (unit) of the people. This ancient custom of initiating and consecrating not only monks but all other orders—which, by the institution of the apostles, held the highest authority and was most holily observed in the early church—was abolished in later centuries by some custom of the popes, and another custom then grew, through I know not what human rashness, with new signs and ceremonies. It has now grown so strong that not even a trace remains in the present church of that most venerable antiquity and the most holy institution of the apostles. But if Saint Dionysius (who seems to me to be in our church what <margin>Ezra</margin> Ezra was in the Mosaic synagogue—who wished the mysteries of the old law to be committed to writing, lest the memory of such great wisdom be lost through the confusion of things and men)—if, in the same way, as if divi-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the apostolic origins of church traditions and the lamentation of their neglect, transitioning into Chapter VII which discusses the rewards of the righteous in the afterlife.</meta>

<language>Latin</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>255</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII. 1. THE HIERARCHY. 255</header>

Had Saint Dionysius not foreseen the future negligence of men, and had he not recorded with his blessed pen what he held in memory from the statutes of the apostles regarding the arrangement and ordering of the church, we would have no recollection of the ancient custom. It is truly to be lamented that this has now departed from common practice, since we ought to be much more diligent in preserving the institutions of our fathers than the Hebrews were in retaining their traditions; for ours are much more excellent and clearer than theirs, and signify a greater and purer truth. The apostles, to whom the Spirit revealed all things, surely understood their reason and fitness best; it could not be otherwise than that they wisely understood how to adapt each part fittingly to the other and to depict spiritual truths in appropriate signs with a just harmony. I do not know how it happened that these were afterwards changed without a wicked crime, since it must be believed that the apostles, taught by the Holy Spirit, instituted everything in the church. <margin>John 16:13</margin> For in John these are the words of the Savior: *But when that Spirit of truth comes, he will teach you all truth: for he will not speak of himself; but whatever he hears he will speak, and he will announce to you the things that are to come.* Because their most holy traditions have been disregarded and neglected, and because men have fallen away from the Spirit of God toward their own inventions, everything has indeed become miserably disturbed and confused. And, as we were saying before, if God does not have mercy, all things will perish.

---

### CHAPTER VII. 1.

It is to be believed beyond doubt that those who are regenerated in Christ—whose sinful life has died, and who have lived justly so that they may die as just men, just as Christ died as a just man—shall have the reward of justice upon departing from this life for their defense of iniquity and for the protection of the justice of God within themselves. They shall possess this reward first in their holy soul, and afterwards in their own time in the flesh as well, which was a companion on the journey to God and an ally in battle; <margin>Note</margin> so that every just man may be entirely blessed in glory in Christ, both in soul and body.

<summary>The author laments the loss of apostolic traditions recorded by Dionysius, attributing the church's confusion to the neglect of these Spirit-led institutions, and begins discussing the eternal rewards for the righteous in both soul and body.</summary>
<keywords>Dionysius the Areopagite, apostolic traditions, Holy Spirit, John 16:13, ecclesiastical order, regeneration in Christ, resurrection, justice of God</keywords>

# CONCERNING THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY CHAPTER VII. I.

<margin>Mark 12:25</margin>
<margin><unclear>I Cor 15</unclear></margin>
To the Sadducees, who do not believe in the resurrection, Jesus—who is conscious of all truth—affirmed, saying:

> When they rise from the dead, they will neither marry nor be given in marriage, but will be like the angels of God in heaven.

Sustained by this hope, the good, who have led a holy life here in Christ, die willingly and joyfully; partly rejoicing that they have escaped the danger of falling into sin, into which they might perhaps have slipped if their life were prolonged further; and partly, even most joyfully, expecting the reward of their completed labor and the crown of glory of the Christian warfare, which is undoubtedly reserved for those who strive lawfully. Regarding the happy departure and death of those saints, their friends and relatives also greatly rejoice and offer congratulations, wishing for themselves a similar completion of life in Christ, so that, having kept the path of justice until death, they may in death itself exchange this temporal and sorrowful life for a happy and eternal one, and live in Christ without end. Rejoicing in this hope for the deceased, and having the full conviction that they have departed to Christ, they carry out the funeral exultantly and bring it to the bishop, offering it to him to be committed to holy burial. For nothing is more unworthy of the Christian profession—in which there is a most certain hope of resurrection and glory in the likeness of Christ—than to mourn the dead with such tears after the manner of pagans, as they now shamefully do almost everywhere in the church, and especially in Italy, where men at the funerals of friends and relatives most foully utter <insert>womanish wailings</insert>, weeping for the dead as if we were of the opinion that it had gone poorly for them, and that they were not living most blessedly with Christ.

But without a doubt, this is only the custom of those who have lived a life here so unlike the life of Christ that, because of a conscience <insert>of a wasted life</insert>, they have nothing to expect from Him; wherefore they greatly grieve and are pained that they must depart from here, either despairing of a future life or expecting eternal misery. Paul addresses them in this manner in his letter to the Thessalonians: <margin>570</margin>

> I would not have you ignorant, brothers, concerning those who sleep, so that you do not grieve as the others do who have no hope. For if we believe that Jesus died and rose again, even so God will bring with Him those who have slept through Jesus.

<margin>2</margin>
Indeed, wicked Christians despair of this, and in their despair they die miserably. <insert>The good, however, for many reasons</insert> meet death joyfully; and because they are weary of this...

Likewise, that most brave martyr Ignatius was accustomed to say, "My love is crucified," meaning Christ, with whom he desired to be in heaven. The delusions that have arisen among men even after Christ from listening to philosophers (which Dionysius mentions) are to be far rejected by magnanimous Christians who hope for better things; namely, that souls either perish with the bodies; or survive, but without resuming their bodies; or as survivors migrate into other bodies; or live in some Elysian fields (I know not what) full of every pleasantness and delight. The clarity of Christ has far dispelled all these cloudy little opinions and has opened the truth to be believed.

->CHAPTER VII. 2.<-

If the dead body is that of a priest, let it be placed before the altar while the funeral rites and the funeral office are being performed. But if it is of the common people, while the offerings and holy rites for the dead are performed, let the body of the deceased be placed before the sanctuary, in that place where the people stand, in the midst of everyone, so that all may behold the sacraments of God. Now the <term>sacrarium</term> or sanctuary is that enclosed area separated by screens which divides the priesthood from the common people, in which the choir of priests celebrates the holy offices; this place is also now commonly called the "choir" by our people. The singing and giving of thanks begin with the priesthood. Lessons that confirm the resurrection are recited by the ministers. A eulogy is spoken over the deceased, provided the archdeacon has first dismissed the catechumens. That dead person is also declared to be blessed in Christ. All who are present are exhorted by the archdeacon to take care that they die well in Christ. The bishop, as if whispering into the ear of the deceased to remind himself, prays and petitions well, and commends him to God; he salutes him with his clergy and the whole church, and pours oil upon him. When the funeral rites have been performed correctly in this manner and the proper ceremonies completed, the remains are then committed to burial among other saints of their own order. In this funeral rite there is nothing but hope, happiness, joy, and con-

### CHAPTER VII. 3.

But now let us see what the sacred rites and ceremonies for the dead signify. Every action of ours here in the church is a representation of those things which take place in heaven. For we are founded in faith and believe that things are in heaven as we act on earth, imitating the arrangement of divine things. He said to Peter, who was distinguished in faith, that whatever you loose or bind on earth shall be loosed and bound in heaven. Our whole Christian life is a striving so that we may be made as like to God as possible. God himself was made man, becoming in a way unlike himself. For, as Paul writes, he emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, so that he might make men like his Father. And when Jesus taught his disciples heavenly piety on the mountain, he concluded his sermon with this statement, <margin>Matt. 5:48</margin> saying: "Be you therefore perfect, even as your Father in heaven is perfect."

But in this striving toward God through Christ, each person attains what their given strength allows. As <margin>1 Cor. 7:7</margin> the apostle wrote to the Corinthians: "Every man has his own proper gift, one after this manner and another after that." Merits are according to the measure of faith; rewards are according to the proportion of merits. The diversity and order here in the Church Militant is an image of that order which the Church Triumphant shall have in heaven. 

Therefore, because a priest is considered more righteous than a layman, when he is dead and his funeral rites are performed, he is placed in the middle of the choir among the priests. However, outside the screens, before the choir in the area of the common people, the deceased layman—or even a more holy monk—is placed among the laypeople. By this arrangement, we are reminded to believe that a different place in heaven, and one much more sacred, is given to priests than to laypeople. For this order in the church is derived from that super-heavenly order, since the apostles, endowed with the Divine Spirit, properly and from heaven arranged all things, having learned all truth from the Spirit, the <term>Paraclete</term>. Chosen by the power of God into the Christian army, we fight, we conquer, we escape, we die as the righteous in Christ, [attaining] the glory of the righteous

<summary>This page explains that earthly church ceremonies and hierarchies, such as the placement of bodies during a funeral, are direct reflections of the divine order in heaven. It emphasizes that the distinctions between priests and laypeople in the Church Militant mirror their relative rewards and positions in the Church Triumphant.</summary>

...going to receive, each according to the measure of his justice.) The church rejoices over the righteous dead, and with a festive procession they deliver the deceased victor to God, with certain hope that he is to be crowned. What the church sings in the sacred rites for the dead are songs of joy, for the consolation and exhortation of the living; so that, hoping the deceased has passed to God, they might desire a similar <gloss>death</gloss> departure for themselves, so that it may be a glory to them and a joy to the church. Truly, weeping, <gloss>tears</gloss> tears, and wailing are a <gloss>shameful</gloss> foulness in the church, and clearly belong to those people who believe too little that the glory of the Christ rising from the dead will belong to the righteous after death. For if they had believed that Jesus Christ, because he died righteous, rose in glory—as an example of the resurrection of the righteous, and that by the power of God all the righteous will rise in him according to that same form—they would undoubtedly not mourn the death of their friends so miserably. For "precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of his saints." But the death of sinners is the worst. Therefore, if there were anything to be grieved, it would certainly be the death of the unrighteous; for it goes worst with those whose worm will not be extinguished. 

Truly, in the Christian church, which stands with magnanimous faith and hope in God, nothing is more shameful or full of disgrace than the weeping for the dead—at least for those whom they knew were not unrighteous in Christ. The church sings praises for the righteous dead; she congratulates the deceased; she brings forth the pleasant hope of resurrection from the scriptures; she rejoices in the present benefit of a friend and brother, that he is liberated from evils by a holy death; and she exults in the hope of the future good promised to the righteous. The chosen, wise, and pious church in Christ does these things to show Christian people that no day can be more pleasant than that on which some righteous person dies—especially if they die while suffering violence for the sake of justice. For such a person, the glory will be so great that the entire church exults with joy. When Jesus’s most holy death was imminent, he spoke to his disciples about it as John records:

> "You have heard that I said to you, 'I go away and come back to you.'"

And because he saw they were sorrowful, he added:

> "If you loved me, you would surely rejoice because I go to the Father";

he spoke thus so that they might rejoice at Jesus's death. In the same way, the whole church and every individual in it should rejoice for the righteous person who has died with Christ into glory, so that they may be seen to have fulfilled the duty of Christian piety and to have truly loved the deceased brother. Let him rejoice, I say—whoever—

...and should greatly rejoice for his brother's benefit in death; otherwise, he may be judged not as a hopeful friend of a brother in Christ, but as a despairing **friend and lover** of himself and of the world. <insert>[The reason why catechumens are excluded and driven away from the sacred funeral rites is that they exist without the light of regeneration, which is given in baptism by God through the ministering priests; they are still blind, so that they cannot look upon the spectacles of the mysteries.]</insert> <margin>✕</margin> There are others who had been enlightened but blinded themselves by their own wickedness—apostates and penitents; likewise the <term>energumini</term> (those possessed by spirits), who, being still too weak, cannot bear the evil agitations of the devil. Although these are not permitted to be present at other sacred rites, they may nevertheless be present at the funeral commemorations and rites, so that they may be admonished by the office of the church and by the hope of the future life which they see in the sacred rites for the dead, so that, coming to their senses, they may desire the future life. Furthermore, as we have said, it is the custom in the ceremonies and sacred rites of the dead for the bishop to pray near the bier for the deceased and to greet him as if he were still sensible; then all the others greet him as well.<note>The manuscript reads "Consultent" instead of "Consalutent" (greet together).</note> The bishop prays to God that, if there are any sins into which that man fell while alive through human weakness, He might acknowledge it and mercifully grant pardon, and receive him into that bright region of the living, so that he may rest happily in the bosoms of the patriarchs, where there is eternal joy and glory. This glory is so great and so solid that, as Paul asserts when writing to the Corinthians, no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man, what things God has prepared for those who love Him. O how great will be the pleasure and how exultant the delight for those sons of men, O good God, who have hoped in the shadow of Your wings, as Your royal prophet David sang. They shall be intoxicated with the richness of Your house, and You shall give them to drink from the torrent of Your pleasure, for with You is the fountain of life, and in Your light we shall see light. The Lord Himself, as it says in another psalm, is our hope and our portion in the land of the living. But someone may ask: what is the need for prayer for the deceased? For his case is decided, for better or for worse. Indeed, by the just judgment of God according to the merits of his life, he goes either upward or downward. After death, there is no place for anyone to earn merit. Dionysius, in answering him, first indicates that prayer does not benefit anyone—

...not for the living, nor indeed for the dead, unless the person is worthy and the prayer is offered by one who is also worthy. He who prays must be worthy—that is, he must be holy. The only person worthy of being prayed for is one who desires it and, above all, exerts every effort to be holy himself. Such a person, recognizing his own unworthiness, fears to approach the divine majesty with prayers or to appear in His sight (unless someone he believes has more influence with God intercedes and supplicates together with him). Finally, he most humbly seeks out someone from the primary orders in the church to be a mediator between God and himself. Relying on that person's support and aided by their shared prayer—while he himself prays most earnestly and intensely alongside them—he may at last be heard. Prayer entered upon in this way and manner is truly the most powerful and effective thing of all; provided that the humble entreaty proceeds incessantly with hope (according to the teaching of the Savior himself, in the example of the man who asked for loaves at midnight, and that of the woman who demanded a judgment from the unjust judge), it certainly cannot fail to be heard. 

But if anyone neglects his own cause and is inwardly cold regarding his own salvation, living wickedly as he was accustomed to do, and does not begin or act himself first of all<note>The manuscript reads "imprimens" (pressing/imprinting) instead of "imprimis" (first of all).</note> to return to grace with God, but instead commands others (as is now the custom of worldly men who give a small fee) to pray for him, hoping that he can be saved through the prayers of others; such a person is indeed among those who are unworthy to be prayed for. He is also the most foolish of all men, for he thinks that in his own negligence, the diligence of others can benefit him, and that he will be saved through others while he himself cares nothing for his own salvation. He acts just like a sick man who, while living a life of excess, expects to be cured by a physician; or, to use the example of Dionysius, like someone who has had his eyes gouged out and yet wishes to gaze at the sun. He is insanely foolish who, doing nothing himself when he is able, thinks he can gain some benefit from the external actions of others; he is like one who trusts he can return to the favor of a prince from whom he was alienated through the efforts of others, while he himself does nothing to reconcile that prince's favor to himself. Therefore, if there are any such people who do not first and foremost act so that it may go well with them, they are numbered among those whom prayer does not benefit, and who are unworthy of being prayed for. But for those who themselves act with all their strength to be saved, the prayer of the holy ones cannot...

...is not of the greatest benefit. The apostle John agrees with this opinion in his canonical epistle, saying, "He who knows his brother to be committing a sin that is not unto death, let him ask, and life shall be given to him who sins not unto death. There is a sin unto death; I do not say that anyone should pray for that." Prayer certainly helps the one who prays; but for the one who does not pray, if he is able to pray, it helps nothing. According to the opinion of Dionysius, he who lives wickedly himself hopes in vain for the prayers of good people. That prayer of the <term>bishop</term> over the deceased, that he may be in glory, is not so much a petition that it might happen, but rather an indication that it is so. For the bishop is, as Malachi calls him, an angel of God and an interpreter of the divine will, moved by the divine spirit. Therefore, that prayer is a declaration from the sacred mouth of the bishop regarding what is undoubtedly being done with the righteous dead; so that in the words of the bishop, the <term>church</term> may learn what reward there is for the righteous who have died in Christ; and so that the church may believe the bishop—who depends on the mouth of God and who possesses the <term>Holy Spirit</term> (for He said, "Receive the Holy Spirit")—when he speaks anything by faith in the <term>ministry</term> of God. 

He does not speak of himself, but from the Spirit; and what he believes to be true with the highest faith and testifies to on earth, that thing has been done in heaven—where it is performed before it is revealed so that it may be believed. He is, as Dionysius says, an angel and interpreter of the will of God, and he speaks and performs all things just as he is prompted by God; and those on whom God wishes to have mercy, on them the bishop in God, as a diviner of that will, has mercy. Regarding those on whom the bishop does not have mercy, it is proof that the Spirit of God does not prompt him to show mercy, and that God Himself in heaven does not have mercy on such people; for He moves His minister, the bishop, by the Holy Spirit according to His own will, so that what has been decreed in heaven may be carried out on earth through the bishop's office. 

For all things are derived from heaven and from God; and He is the one who acts in all things; and true men in Him are ministers of His will from whom they receive the spirit, so that they might be like instruments of God's action; indeed, they themselves do nothing, but God does all things in them. For this purpose, after His <term>resurrection</term>, the <term>God-man</term> Jesus Christ—in order to make His beloved disciples (to whom He entered while the <term>doors were closed</term> and said to them once and again, "Peace be with you") suitable instruments of His will, so that they might carry out on earth what He Himself has done in heaven—breathed on them and said, "Receive the Holy Spirit. As the Father has sent me, so I send you. Whose sins you forgive" (namely, carrying out the vo-

...the will of God, by having received this Spirit, sins are forgiven them; and whose sins you retain, they are retained: provided it is done by the Spirit given, and by the just dispensation of the will of God. Otherwise, it is done not by God but by man, in which case it is not to be believed.

<margin>1 Cor 4:1–2</margin>
Wherefore Paul, writing to the Corinthians, when he called the apostles and those who hold such a position "ministers of Christ and stewards of the mysteries of God," added immediately: "Moreover, it is required among stewards that a man be found faithful." For not all are righteous ministers, nor do they dispense correctly, nor are the prudent ones led by the divine Spirit of God; instead, relying on themselves, they perform what they themselves want by their own spirit, which is an enemy to God. But those who are chosen by God through Christ and drawn upward into him depend on him, and like instruments in his hand, they are always turned in that direction and toward that affection where God directs them. They are the signs, declarations, and executors of the will of God and of his mind, which defines and forms all things beforehand. Certainly, these do not act of themselves, but God acts in them; nor are they the authors of any deed, but God is. Whatever they have done, it must be believed that it was done by God. They are truly to be held and revered in the place of God, as God himself—not they themselves, but God in them, by whose Spirit they are moved so that they may think, act, and speak all things divinely.

Regarding this kind of men is that saying of Jesus in Luke: "He who hears you, hears me; and he who rejects you, rejects me; but he who rejects me, rejects him who sent me." This is because God is in Christ, and Christ is in those who truly are his; especially in the leaders of the <term>church</term>, if they are truly of Christ. "Do you seek proof," says Paul, "of Christ speaking in me?" These men do all things by the Spirit of Christ, just as Christ did by the Spirit of God. These, being raised high in God, receive revealed truths and believe what is revealed; and whatever has been indicated by the Spirit of God through secret prompting—whose movements only those sublime in God can know—they both do and say with indubitable faith. This is the magnificent faith by which Peter recognized that Jesus Christ was the Son of the living God and confessed it in words. This is that faith to which God attributed such power that whatever it binds on earth shall also be bound in heaven; and whatever it looses on earth shall also be loosed in heaven. All things are undoubtedly bound in heaven before they are bound here, and those things are loosed there first which are afterwards loosed here. For God does not [depend] on men...

# CHAPTER VII. 3. ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.

...but men depend on God. Nor does He, as if after the fact, confirm our actions; rather, men carry out those things which were previously God's. And through the ministry of men, that which was previously defined more secretly by God in heaven is finally made manifest on earth. What has been revealed and believed by a lawful minister of God comes into the open; it is revealed at the appropriate time and through the duty of God's servant, who, as a messenger and interpreter of the divine will, must be believed. 

This is why <margin>X</margin> Dionysius calls the <term>pontiff</term>—who is highest in the church and nearest to God—an "angel" and an "interpreter of God." <margin><unclear>of ... and Law</unclear></margin> Believing in God who reveals it, he binds on earth what is bound in heaven, and looses on earth what is loosed in heaven. That is, he demonstrates to the church the divine binding and loosing as far as it is revealed to him, and makes it known in God who wills it. 

Because <margin>X</margin> it is very much to be noted—so that pontiffs do not become arrogant—that it is not within the power of men to release the bonds of sins; <margin>X</margin> nor does the power of loosing and binding anything belong to them. For God alone looses and binds, and He looses and binds all things within Himself in heaven. Those who are first in the church, such as the pontiffs, receive by revelation what is bound and loosed there; and having received it, they announce it, and in their words they carry out the divine mind, not their own. But if they do not proceed from revelation, being moved by the Spirit of God in everything they do or say, they then necessarily rave as fools out of their own selves, and they abuse the power given to them, both to the blasphemy of God and the destruction of the church. 

From these things we may see how high, how sublime, and how entirely centered in heaven the pontiff ought to be—especially the one who is supreme, whom <margin>We, the Author, call the Pope</margin> we call the <term>Pope</term>. This is so that whatever he pours into the church by his authority, giving it life toward eternal life, he may draw entirely from God. Once drawn, he may digest it and distribute it rightly and lawfully through all the members; so that these members, refreshed by divine food, may live together in that pontiff who lives most fully for God. Thus all things may proceed from God into the church, for the calling back of all things into Him, who Himself is all in all in a healthy and pure church. He gives life, enlightens, and perfects the pontiff, especially the supreme one, so that he may then faithfully and sincerely administer the wisdom and will of God in all things for the life and salvation of all; seeking nothing but the gain of men in God, and his own approval by God in the dispensing of God's ministries. If he is a lawful one <gloss>thus</gloss>, he is not the one

This has indeed been done for many years now; it has increased so much and now occupies all the members of the church so powerfully that unless the Mediator—who alone is able, and who created and founded the church for himself out of nothing (which is why Paul often calls it a "creature" or "new creation")—unless, I say, the Mediator Jesus quickly sets his hand to it, the extremely sick church cannot be far from death. Men relax and hold back, they loose and bind, not out of faith in God regarding what is bound in heaven, but according to what they themselves desire, which is why everything on earth is disturbed. <margin>Question of <unclear/></margin> They are not executors of God's will, but actors of their own. They do not testify to what God wills, as they ought to do (for their office is nothing other than the testimony of God's will), but they demonstrate what they themselves crave. They do not consult God in their actions through constant prayer, but take counsel with men; by this, they weaken and demolish everything. They all seek (a thing to be lamented, and which I write both grieving and weeping) their own interests, not those of Jesus Christ; they seek earthly things, not heavenly ones; things that will lead them to death, not to eternal life. 

But, to return to the subject: the true <term>pontiff</term> and lawful bishop and faithful steward of God's ministries does nothing except from God; he is the hand, the servant, and the instrument of God, to be believed as if he were God himself. Therefore, in the funeral service, while he prays for eternal life for the holy who have died in Christ, he reverently speaks God's certain will. In faith and purpose, he asks for that to happen which he clearly knows will happen, so that with the prayerful words which are required of a <term>pontiff</term> in all things, he may testify to the gathered church concerning God's justice and grace toward men—specifically to those who die well in Christ. For the <term>pontiff</term> is a witness to God's will, serving as the mouth and the word of God’s mind, expressing those things prescribed by God which he perceives through faith. It is God himself who does all things well in everyone. 

Furthermore, when the <term>pontiff</term> and the whole church salute the deceased and say a final farewell, it signifies not a person who is entirely dead, but a person migrating to another region and to their own true and proper homeland. <margin>thus <unclear/></margin> After this salute, he pours sacred oil over the corpse...

<margin><unclear>Notat...</unclear></margin>
...the pontiff, so that he may indicate that he has finished this contest. <margin>x</margin> A traditional custom instituted by the apostles themselves should be noted: those to be initiated were first instructed in the creed, taught, and stripped; and those already initiated were anointed with oil for the contest; finally, the noble fighters who had died received the final and perfecting unction. The use of ointment is frequent in the Christian church, which takes its name from unction and is <margin>x</margin> called Christian. Ointment is truly a mixture of various scents, with oil, balsam, or some other similar fat added. The church has its own proper composition of this kind. This ointment signifies the Holy Spirit, by whom all are strengthened in Christ, and it relates to the contest we enter under Christ as our leader against the spiritual forces of wickedness in the heavenly realms, with whom we fight, being anointed and strengthened by the Holy Spirit. The sacrament of this Holy Spirit is the unction applied to the body, which signifies the unction of the soul; by this, spiritual people—in the Spirit of Christ, in a spiritual fight, using spiritual weapons against spiritual enemies—contend legitimately in Christ for as long as we live here. <margin><unclear>Sn</unclear> x</margin> Finally, when we have escaped as undefeated and safe in Christ, having finished our military service, we are anointed in death, <margin>x</margin> so that it may be understood that by the grace of the same Holy Spirit by whose power we began to fight, we have finished the war. Finally, the body is buried, enclosed by either earth, stone, <margin>x</margin> or any other material, with men of its own order, reserved for the day of resurrection. It was the institution of the early church <margin>x</margin> that the corpse should be placed separately and distinctly, so that order remained undisturbed even among the dead, and <margin><unclear>Cam</unclear> x</margin> order might appear everywhere. Just as these bodies were participants in labor and the contest through the endurance of evils and abstinence from pleasures, they are placed in certain hope of participating in glory, when they shall be reunited to their souls, restored by the power of God. For <margin>x</margin> if the whole person were not to be glorified following the example of Christ, then sacraments would not have been instituted for the body and flesh by <margin><unclear>ad apu...les</unclear></margin> Christ himself and the apostles, from whom every institution of the sacraments proceeded; <margin>x</margin> these sacraments certainly relate to the body for its immortality, just as the spirit relates to the immortal glory of the soul. For the bread and the blessed cup—internal <margin>x</margin> food—and unction—an external¹ <note>Read: external.</note> warming—what else do they mean...

...and enlightened, but furthermore were joined through participation in the most holy Eucharist (without which, in the early church, not even the baptism of infants could occur) and were grafted into the mystical body of Christ.<note>¹ I find that "I find" or something similar is missing from the original text.</note> Although carnal men, who do not appreciate the things of God, then held this in ridicule—admitting or approving nothing except what their lowly reason persuaded them of—nevertheless, to faithful and spiritual people, the consecration of infants to God seems a great and admirable sacrament of divine mercy. And it is held by the church as a matter full of piety, provided it is done rightly and in a lawful manner; even if the reason for this cannot be discerned by men, it is no wonder, since not even the highest angels know everything. Truly, pious faith admits and honors all things. And those who first instituted not only that sacrament, but all the rest besides—namely, the apostles—either understood the reasons for them or believed the One who did. 

Whatever has arisen and is done by the instinct of the divine Spirit cannot lack the highest reason, which humble human reason does not reach; instead, faith stands above this reason—a light certainly given to us in Christ that is capable of grasping divine reason. In this matter and faith, which even the chief disciples of Christ themselves asked to be increased for them, if we are small, let us not for that reason despise the great sacraments of God. Rather, let us acknowledge and truly and humbly confess the narrowness of our reason; let us attribute our inability to comprehend the things of God to our own fallen nature, rather than immediately and disdainfully rejecting what our puny reason does not grasp. Let us strive to become greater through faith above reason, spiritual above the flesh, and divine above human nature; and let us believe entirely and without doubt that divine things cannot be grasped except by divine men. 

But now let us see, concerning the rites of the little ones born in the Christian church, what the order and ritual for their initiation was among those ancient men of our religion. In the ancient church, as we have said by the testimony of Dionysius, they were admitted under a certain condition and law to both baptism and the Eucharist. The manner of bringing them in so that they might be counted within the church, as Dionysius reports, was indeed this. A parent, once a child was born into the light, understanding that there is no birth for a son or daughter...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of infant baptism, specifically explaining the role of the sponsor (godparent) and the bishop in the sacramental process, following the previous page's assertion that we should not despise these mysteries even if they exceed human understanding.</meta>
