<header>CAP. VII. 4. HIERARCHY.</header>

...it would have been better to have died than not to be reborn in Christ. He is not ignorant of the fact that the <term>sacramentum</term> is of little benefit—indeed, it is certainly harmful—unless one's life, if the infant grows up, corresponds to the sacred mysteries received. 

Therefore, so that the child may be admitted by the bishop (<term>pontifex</term>) to participate in the sacred rites, the parent goes about and solicitously seeks to find a good man, skilled in Christian truth, who will take it upon himself to promise the instruction and education of the child in all things that pertain to salvation. This man promises the bishop that if the bishop marks and invests the child with the divine sacraments, he will see to it that the child, upon growing up, will live a holy and pure life in Christ. When the father has found such a man and has in good faith entrusted his little son to him as to a second and greater parent in God (for the paternal duty and the physical generation of the <term>infans</term> is a ministry preceding his regeneration), that man who has taken upon himself the spiritual labor of birthing the child in Christ—so that at last, if he lives, he may be formed into a perfect Christian—approaches the bishop with this great profession of fatherhood in Christ. 

He shows the bishop the child whom he has accepted for regeneration, or rather for a full formation in Christ. He reverently petitions the bishop to admit the child into the body of Christ through the likeness and sealing of the sacraments, so that the child may grow through them into a life worthy of the sacred rites by the sponsor’s diligence and care. When the bishop hears the man solemnly promise on behalf of the child, and sees that he is fit to fulfill these promises, he accepts the pledge. Trusting in divine mercy, and following the custom and rite instituted by the apostles, he adorns the infant with the proper formulas and signs, both enlightening him through baptism (<term>baptismate</term>) and perfecting him with holy communion (<term>communione</term>). 

Meanwhile, the sponsor who has undertaken to raise the child in Christ gives his word and solemnly promises on the infant's behalf all that true Christianity (<term>Christianitas</term>) demands: namely, the renunciation (<term>abrenunciacionem</term>) of all iniquity, faith in the sacraments, and a persevering life in Christ worthy of such a great profession. This does not mean he speaks in the infant's place, for it would be ridiculous for one person to speak for someone who is ignorant; rather, when he speaks the renunciation, he himself professes that he will ensure, as far as he is able, that as soon as the child is capable of being taught, the child will in reality and in life renounce every way of living contrary to a Christian person, and show himself throughout his entire life to be worthy of the sacraments.

<summary>This page explains the role of the sponsor in infant baptism, who acts as a spiritual parent and promises the bishop that they will ensure the child is raised in the Christian faith. It clarifies that the sponsor's vows are a personal commitment to guide the child's development rather than a literal substitution for the infant's own voice.</summary>
<keywords>infant baptism, sponsor, godparent, bishop, regeneration, Christianitas, abrenunciacionem, communion, sacraments, spiritual education</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of infant baptism, focusing on the role of the sponsor (the spiritual father) and the bishop's requirements for admitting a child into the sacred mysteries of the church.</meta>

<page-num>270</page-num>
<header>ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY, CHAPTER VII. 4.</header>

...knowing eventually, in a more mature age, and voluntarily professing what he himself received as an unconscious infant. Then, on this law and condition—that the <term>alumnus</term> <note>Colet uses this term to refer to the sponsor or nurturer of the child</note> performs what he has promised—the bishop admits the infant to the sacred rites. When the bishop hears him (whom I call an <term>alumnus</term>, but whom <term>Dionysius</term> calls sometimes a <term>susceptor</term> [sponsor] and sometimes a divine father; while others call him a <term>patrinus</term> [godfather], and others a <term>compater</term>)—when, I say, the bishop hears him saying, <gloss>"I renounce"</gloss>—which means, as <term>Dionysius</term> explains: "I will myself ensure that the infant renounces and rejects everything offered to him from the lower world and the devil, and desires nothing except what has been brought down to him from above, from heaven, by God Himself"—the bishop, having faith in that promise and not being ignorant of the good mysteries of God, and expecting what that <term>susceptor</term> has promised, gladly marks the infant with the sign of Christianity. He does this so that the infant may be recognized as a shoot coming forth from a faithful tree.

The faith of the parents contributes something to this, though it does not contribute much; for the Christian tree rises and multiplies into branches not by physical generation, but by spiritual regeneration. From this it is clear that in terms of true fatherhood, and in Christ, that sponsor is more truly and more fully a father—one who completes the man born in Christ—than the biological parent who supplied the matter for the flesh. Although the son should honor both parents—both the first, who made him a son of man, and this second, who as a minister procreated him as a son of God—it is certainly true that he owes more to the second and should regard him in a higher place of parenthood. For it is indeed a greater thing to be perfected in God than to be produced by man.

Therefore, the <term>susceptor</term> is of more value and in a higher position than the father; and his office and action are far more excellent and meritorious. It is a role that deserves far more from both God and that infant than anything done in the physical generation by the parent, who performed the work of the flesh more for the sake of his own pleasure than for the benefit of the child. The <term>susceptor</term>, however, without any pleasure of his own, and even with the pain of laboring to bring the man to Christ, is set on fire by the love of God for the sake of the man's salvation. 

Thus we see that in the early church, by the institution of the apostles, infants were not simply admitted to the sacred rites, but only on the condition that someone would stand as a <term>fidejussor</term> (surety) for them. This was to ensure that when they reached an age where they could use reason, they would in reality demonstrate the form of Christ. This <term>fidejussor</term> and <term>sponsor</term> will take it upon himself as a duty to instruct and educate the child in such doctrine and morals that, in exchange for the sacrament...

<summary>This page explains that the spiritual sponsor (godparent) holds a higher and more meritorious position than the biological parent because they facilitate the child's spiritual rebirth and growth in Christ. It also outlines the sponsor's duty to ensure the infant eventually renounces the devil and lives a life reflecting the Christian sacraments.</summary>

<keywords>infant baptism, sponsor, susceptor, alumnus, Dionysius, spiritual regeneration, godparent, bishop, fidejussor, sacraments</keywords>

--- Page 327 ---
<meta>This page continues Colet’s interpretation of Dionysius, focusing on the heavy spiritual responsibility of the sponsor (the godfather or "alumnus") in the rite of infant baptism and the dire consequences of neglecting this duty.</meta>

<page-num>271</page-num>
<header>CAP. VII. 4. HIERARCHIA. 271</header>

...so that by the logic of these materials he may show himself to be a true Christian; for he is an infant marked with sacred rites so that he may be educated in them. If there is someone who solemnly vows and promises that this will happen in the child, then the pious church, with good hope, joins the infant to itself, so that by the sponsor's teachings and warnings he may grow into a man in the profession of Christ. 

See, then, how great a burden he takes upon himself who promises to be a <term>sponsor</term> <note>Colet uses "alumnus" here to refer to the nurturer or guardian</note> to infants; how much he has to perform for the child; and how much both the parents and the child himself owe to the sponsor and his <term>godfather</term> <note>Latin: "susceptori"</note>, provided he acts according to his duty and fulfills what he promised to do. I say he owes far more to him than to the biological father who begot him.

Here also we may see how, through our ignorance and the confusion of the times, those who undertake this duty impiously neglect what they are obligated to do, resulting in the greatest loss for both themselves and the infants. If an infant dies physically through the neglect of a biological father, that is imputed to the parent as a grave crime; how much greater then is the crime committed by him who, through his own negligence, allows a person to perish eternally in spirit? How great a murderer is this man, and what a death does he deserve himself, by whose treachery a person dies an everlasting death? 

Therefore, let every sponsor acknowledge who he is, what he promises, and what ministry he performs in Christ. Let him recognize how great a father he is in the ministry of the fatherhood of God—who is the true regenerator of all—and how much he must provide for the child he has taken on. For it is no light matter to demonstrate oneself to be a truly just Christian according to the logic of the sacred rites; nor does he who promises this on behalf of another promise something small or easy to do. 

If he performs this task according to his declared duty and office, he does something most pleasing to God, most useful to the child, and most delightful to the parents; he also brings gain to the church and earns for himself a reward of glory. But if he neglects his duty and allows the person to misuse the sacred rites he has received, then he heaps up eternal death and misery for himself on every side. 

Just as nothing is more beautiful, better, or more fruitful than such a sponsor if what is promised for the child is faithfully carried out in reality; so also, surely nothing is more harmful or damnable if he treacherously neglects what he promises to the <term>bishop</term> <note>Latin: "pontifici"</note> at the <term>sacred font</term> <note>Latin: "sacrum lavachrum"</note>. Because of the neglect of these people, who are like the doors of the church, there have been introduced into Christian society those who possess nothing of Christianity except its signs. From this it happens that under the name of "Christian" there is a surging tide of sinners, who under a beautiful...

<summary>Colet emphasizes the immense spiritual duty of a godparent, arguing that neglecting a child's religious education is a greater crime than physical murder. He warns that the failure of sponsors leads to a church filled with people who are Christians in name only.</summary>
<keywords>baptism, godparents, sponsors, spiritual education, spiritual fatherhood, ecclesiastical hierarchy, John Colet, Dionysius</keywords>

--- Page 328 ---
<meta>This page concludes Colet’s commentary on Dionysius's Ecclesiastical Hierarchy, finishing the discussion on the responsibilities of godparents and offering a concluding prayer for the reformation of the Church.</meta>

<page-num>272</page-num>
<header>CONCERNING THE ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY.</header>

...in [the Christian] name they practice every kind of foulness. Indeed, the greatest cause of this horror and shame are those <term>godparents</term> <note>Latin: "susceptores"</note> and sponsors for the children, to whose care the education of the little ones in Christ and in living in the truth is committed. For as the planting is, so is the tree; and as the education of the children is, so is the city. Parents are also in the height of folly and are partly the cause of this plague in the Christian community if they do not carefully seek out and provide such godparents by whom they know their children can be properly instructed in Christ. It is also the priest's duty to have a sharp eye in this matter, so that no one is admitted to the office of godparent to whom an infant is entrusted, except those who are worthy, holy, learned, and excellent men. These men, being of such a character themselves, may give spiritual birth to the infants as the same; so that the infants, reflecting the holiness and goodness of their holy godparent, may finally emerge as worthy members in Christ—men who fail neither the sponsor's promise, nor the bishop's hope, nor finally the sacred rites they have received.

Following these footsteps of Dionysius, we have written concerning our ecclesiastical hierarchy, from whose beautiful form we have far degenerated. But we pray to God, the creator of all things, that out of His great mercy He may reform what is deformed in us, through our Lord Jesus Christ.

---

->THE END OF THE WRITINGS OF JOHN COLET ON THE<-
->ECCLESIASTICAL HIERARCHY OF DIONYSIUS.<-

<summary>Colet concludes his treatise by emphasizing that the corruption of the Christian city stems from negligent godparents and parents, and he prays for God to reform the current, degenerate state of the Church.</summary>
<keywords>John Colet, Dionysius, ecclesiastical hierarchy, godparents, sponsors, education, spiritual growth, reformation</keywords>
